PREFACE 

The authors are in agreement with those who emphasize the need for education for complete living. They are of the belief, however, that the term education for complete living does not suggest that one's study should be limited to the traditionally cultural subjects, but rather that the proper attitudes, interests, and ideals cannot be obtained unless a citizen has a proper economic understanding and the ability to function in an economic community. 

Education, from whatever agencies it is obtained, is becoming increasingly important in furnishing high school students with proper and well-planned equipment necessary for earning a living. Purposive participation in social and economic life will be much more effective if society's citizens are to be independent economically as well as politically. 

In common with the prevailing practice through the country, the secondary schools of New York offer economic citizenship in the ninth year. This course is planned to give high school students vocational guidance directly rather than indirectly. Not only should the course give information that is important in itself, but it also should serve as an orientation course in any social studies sequence. The New Economic 

Citizen is designed to satisfy this need. 

The metropolitan area will be the field of occupational activity for ninety per cent of the present student body. 

What are the vocational opportunities in this metropolis and its environs? What talents and characteristics does the growing boy or girl possess? Finally, how can these characteristics be turned to the utmost profit for the student as well as for the community?

This book is the result of many years of teaching the subject from its beginning as a course in local industries, then as vii

viii 

PREFACE 

a course in vocations and industries, up to its present form, a text in economic citizenship. During this period, the authors developed a set of mimeographed study sheets, which have served as an outline for this text. Frequent revisions of these study sheets, as a result of actual classroom practice, have helped to evolve a content both specific and concrete, together with language that is within the learning capacity of ninth-year students. The need of teachers and students for an all-inclusive understanding of the wide field of vocational opportunities has caused the authors to present a rather unique treatment. Some industries, as, for example, the clothing industry, have been treated in great detail. 

Using this industry as a type, the authors have developed basic opportunities in all industries of the same nature. A study of basic vocations in basic industrial groups has resulted in a comprehensive analysis of vocations. In this way, not only has actual work in an industry been presented, but the possibilities of the entire field have been encompassed. 

Valuable realia, such as census reports, excerpts from aptitude tests, classifications of business groups, diagrams, and photographs, have been included to paint a complete picture for teachers as well as students. The authors feel that this book is both outstanding and unique in the treatment and organization of the teaching aids at the end of the separate chapters and units. Both the self-testing and thought questions, as well as the projects, have emerged from the crucible of classroom experience.

J. V. M.

UNIT I WHY WORK? 

INTRODUCTION 

Try to recall the scene at your home early this morning. 

Perhaps when you awoke, your father was shaving hurriedly. Your older brother and sister were already hastening to the breakfast table, while your mother finished the preparations for breakfast. Everyone was busily getting ready for the day's work. All was hustle and bustle. 

Soon all was quiet. Your father, sister, and brother had gone their various ways. But follow them in your mind's eye. Focus it on Pennsylvania Station, Wall Street, Union Square, or some other important business section. 

See thousands of fathers, sisters, and brothers milling in and out of subways, elevated trains, and buses. All are hurrying like bees to office, factory, and store.

As you dressed slowly for school, you might very well have asked yourself, Why all this hustle and bustle? Why all this apparently meaningless activity? Why didn't Dad take his golf clubs instead of his tool kit or his brief case? 

Why didn't Brother and Sister take their tennis racquets? 

Why are they all hurrying so much? Why work?— 

Let us seek the answer. 1

CHAPTER I  What Work Means. Before we attempt to find out why people work, we must be sure that we understand what the word really means. Work, as we are to understand it, is any purposeful activity.

This definition of work is general. To some people a certain activity may be play, reserved for leisure time. To others the same activity may be their life's work. To you, for example, a game of baseball may be a means of spending a pleasant afternoon, as a diversion from study. To your brother, playing baseball with a semi-professional team may be a source of additional pocket money. To Babe Ruth, Frankie Frisch, Mel Ott, and scores of other professional baseball players of the past and present, baseball means even more. To them, it means or has meant making a living. They have devoted nearly all of their time in training and playing.

Work does not always consist merely of physical activity. Work is involved in waiting on customers in a store, in solving engineering or architectural problems on the drafting board, in entering figures in the ledger of a big business enterprise. Every purposeful activity, mental or physical, or both, is work.

Classifications of Work 

We must study the various activities from the point of view of purpose in order to understand the different kinds of work going on about us. 

Vocation. A vocation is an occupation in which one engages as a life's work. A person plans to earn his living 2

Ch. 1] 

permanently by doing this work. Your father, let us say, has been a plumber ever since he began to work. He has continued in this field until he has become a master plumber with his own staff of workers. Plumbing is his vocation. 

It is important to distinguish the word "vocation" from the word "job." Sometimes these words are used interchangeably, and confusion results. A vocation is a career, a regular occupation. The word job means simply employment, or a piece of work done or to be done. For example, Mary Smith is a milliner. She has worked as a milliner one year for Mr. Jones, six months for Mr. Brown, and two years for Mr. Black. In all, she has had three jobs, all in the one vocation.

Avocation. An avocation is a temporary vocation, or better, an occupation in which one engages temporarily. 

Your brother may be a bank teller, earning twenty-five dollars a week with the hope of becoming a cashier or a branch manager. In order to earn additional money, he plays baseball with a semi-professional team. On the other hand, your sister may have attended college, studied diligently, taken examinations, and may now be prepared to teach in the elementary schools of New York. Unfortunately, however, the list of teachers is long and she must patiently await her turn until she is appointed. While waiting for her appointment as a teacher, she may accept employment as a sales girl in a department store. Selling is her avocation because she works at it until her vocation, teaching, begins to pay. Baseball-playing is your brother's avocation because it supplies him with additional funds. 

Frequently, an avocation becomes a vocation, either through necessity or choice.

Hobby. A hobby, another form of occupation, differs from a vocation and an avocation because one engages in a

[Ch. 

DAIS BASEBALL AS A HOBBY 

When baseball is played by an amateur for the pleasure derived from it, it is a hobby. 

YORK 

By Arrangement with Christy Walsh 

BASEBALL AS A VOCATION 

Lou Gehrig, the "Iron Man of Baseball," plays baseball as a vocation. hobby purely for the pleasure to be derived from it, without regard for monetary return. Would you stop playing baseball because you weren't paid for it? Would your sister 

Would your father stop playing the piano for that reason? stop playing golf? Would your friend stop tinkering with his amateur radio broadcasting station? Would your uncle stop the scenic painting he is doing? Would you stop collecting stamps? Would you stop dancing? Probably you would not.

But if no payment were forthcoming, would your brother play baseball so regularly? Would the owner of the radio store repair your radio? Would the big stamp-collecting businesses continue? Would dancing schools continue? Here, too, the answer is, Probably not, because these represent vocational or avocational activities.

Ch. 1] 

Set down the various activities in which you engage. 

Some activities you follow because you must, others because you expect to be paid. Some of these you follow only because you "like" to do them. This last group will most likely contain your own hobby.

Monetary Motives for Working 

While we have examined and classified the various forms that work may take, we have not yet answered the more important question, Why work? This question may be broken up into two parts: (1) Why choose a particular kind of work? (2) Why work at all? The answer to the first part is interlocked with the answer to the second. 

Such answers as "I like to be a nurse" or "I work because I have to" are weak and cannot withstand the test of careful examination. The first answer does not explain why the person likes to be a nurse; the second does not explain why one type of work instead of another was chosen. 

Various urges have always driven people to work. When we study the history of man's progress, we find that even primitive man had to work if he was to exist. We discover also that the work of some of our leading figures in history was motivated by various urges. These urges or motives manifested themselves in the fields of art, politics, social service, and many others.

Let us examine a few motives to see which ones fit you. 

Desire for Gain. This motive may be described as the desire of an individual to earn money for the purpose of satisfying his wants.

At this point let us consider what our wants consist of. 

From early childhood you have probably wanted something: a rattle, a toy, candy. Your father may want a suit, an automobile, food, a house of his own. Your mother may want an electric refrigerator, a new dress, a hat. Wants

[Ch. 

may vary from the want for a simple but comfortable home to a mansion, or from the want for simple but wholesome food to more expensive and sometimes less digestible, imported foods.

Elsewhere in this book, we shall discuss the merits and demerits of purchasing these goods. For the present, let us content ourselves with the fact that these wants exist and that they may be satisfied only by the payment of sums of money. To have money, one must work. 

The desire for gain may, therefore, be divided into two types.

Desire for Moderate Gain. The desire for moderate gain is the urge to provide enough to live comfortably without aid from others. Mr. Smith, an electrician, wants to earn enough to take care of his needs and those of his family. 

In addition, he wants a comfortable sum set aside for the future. He exemplifies a person motivated by the desire for moderate gain. The financial condition that he seeks is called economic independence. We shall deal with the subject of economic independence at greater length later in our study.

Desire for Excessive Gain. The desire for excessive gain is the urge to amass great wealth, so that one may live in luxury. In many cases it is difficult to draw the line between this motive and the desire for moderate gain. If we contrast extreme cases, we shall understand the essential difference. Mr. Jones may desire a mansion, but Mr. Smith will be satisfied with an apartment or a small house. Mr. 

Jones hopes to buy a Lincoln, while Mr. Smith would be satisfied to own a Ford. Each has a different goal, although both are working to earn money. 

Some vocations lend themselves more readily to the satisfaction of the desire for excessive gain than other voca-

Ch. 1] 

tions do. The income-tax returns for a recent year show that the person who received the highest income for that year was an actress. Many other entertainers are listed among the highest income group. The owners of businesses and corporation executives may also receive high incomes. 

On the other hand, it must be remembered that the income-tax list included men and women in all. walks of life. All types of business and professional people were represented. Likewise, there were probably many actors and businessmen not listed in these returns who suffered losses and who were not able to earn enough money to live even 

The fact that a particular vocation offers the possibility of a higher return than does some other vocation should not, therefore, be the only reason for your choice of that vocation. There is no assurance that any vocation is a "gold mine.” There are poor doctors and rich doctors, poor actors and rich actors, poor butchers and rich butchers. Rather than choose a potential "gold mine," seek a vocation that will give you a moderate income, sufficient to permit you to live comfortably without aid from others and, at the same time, give you genuine pleasure in working. Happiness and success in your work cannot be measured in terms of dollars and cents.

Nonmonetary Motives for Working 

Many of us who are constantly in need of money forget that any other reason for working exists. Yet if we examine the lives of some of the leading figures in history, we find that other motives caused these people to accomplish work that gave them greater happiness than the gold hoard of Croesus. If we observe the work carried on by many people about us, people who have given of their time and, in many cases, of their lives, we may well pause in shame at our efforts to earn more money than the next fellow. Other

[Ch. 

urges exist within us which, if satisfied, will give us greater happiness.

Altruism. In our course in civics we learned the meaning of terms such as pasteurization (the process of destroying bacteria in milk so that our milk supply will be safe) and the Pasteur treatment for hydrophobia. For these aids to humanity, we owe a debt of gratitude to Louis Pasteur, the great French scientist. 

PAUL MUNI DEPICTING LOUIS PASTEUR 

The life of Louis Pasteur is an example of altruism.

Ch. 1] 

Those of us who have read the story of his life or who have seen and heard it in the motion pictures recall that his home life was far from luxurious. Why was he willing to sacrifice material comfort? Why was M. Curie willing to devote his life to his work? Why did his wife after his death continue with his experiments with radium? Why are thousands of scientists today poring over test tubes and microscopes, foregoing all other pleasures, in order to find a way to lengthen lives and to relieve the pain of the sick? 

History provides many outstanding examples of people who found happiness in giving up their own comforts for the benefit of others. Florence Nightingale, the English nurse whose life, too, has been portrayed on the screen, exemplifies the true spirit of the nurse. Lillian Wald, founder of the 

KAY FRANCIS DEPICTING FLORENCE NIGHTINGALE 

The life of Florence Nightingale is an example of altruism in its highest form.

[Ch. 1 

Henry Street Settlement and other social-service centers, has done much to make the lives of the poor in the slums a little happier. By her Visiting Nurse Service, she has helped to relieve the pain and suffering of the poor sick people of the city; by her summer camps, she has helped to give our less fortunate boys and girls a little more sunshine, fresh air, and good food so that they might stand a better chance in their later lives.

It is this motive, too, which is responsible for the work done by those people of ample means who, if they chose, would not have to work at all. A glance at the membership list of any charitable organization or social center will reveal the names of many people of wealth in the community. Why are they happy to contribute so freely of their money instead of buying another automobile or yacht for themselves? Why do they spend day after day at these centers, when they could live a life of ease at home or among other pleasant surroundings? The desire to help others is the urge that prompts them.

This motive is not necessarily reserved to those who are in social service, in science, medicine, or nursing, or in the clergy. This same urge is found in others who are engaged in other vocations. In all walks of life, the desire to help the other fellow must be present. Your father and mother may be working, not only for their own comfort, but also to provide you with good food and comfort, or even to set a sum of money aside for your future schooling. The telephone lineman, who keeps communities together, the milkman, who delivers the milk for baby's formula, the "whitewing," who sweeps the street, all serve the public interest. 

Not only does each receive his share of that genuine pleasure which comes with giving, but such givers find that service is often repaid in dollars and cents. Many grocers have found that by installing a free delivery service for housewives, they have increased their sales tremendously.

Ch. 1] 

The stenographer who is willing to stay after hours to type a few letters for her employer will find, in most cases, that bonuses and increases in salary are the result. 

Self-Expression. To this motive more than any other do we owe thanks for the great works of art in all fields, such as in sculpture, books, and paintings. This motive exists within nearly all of us in varying degrees and causes us to try to create something that will express our own individuality. Rembrandt, the great Dutch artist, living in a garret, spending his money for paint instead of food, worked industriously to paint pictures, not for the fame that came to him 

GENE AIELLO Courtesy United Artists Corp. 

CHARLES LAUGHTON DEPICTING REMBRANDT 

Rembrandt was urged on by the motive of self-expression.

[Ch. 1 

later, or for great wealth, but because the motive of selfexpression urged him on. Beethoven, although deaf, had this urge to compose music. Despite his blindness, Milton kept alive the urge within him and gave posterity "Paradise Lost."

This same spirit lives today. Many unknown artists, living in poverty, are working industriously to perfect their technique so that they may better express themselves in paint or in music that will thrill the hearts of future generations. 

AN ARTIST AT WORK 

An artist satisfies the urge of self-expression in the creation of his work. 

This motive is not, however, the special property of actors, authors, painters, musicians. Even in the more prosaic vocations we find this motive asserting itself. To the

Ch. 1] 

homemaker, it may be in the choice of draperies or in the baking of a cake. The janitor may take pride in the spickand-span appearance of the building he supervises. In the typist's work this motive may be revealed in the particularly neat report that she has completed. Even your little brother has this motive. Watch him at the seashore, and you will discover that he will busy himself by building a castle or by digging a tunnel in the sand. These are all evidences of the urge to create.

Joy of Work. Closely allied with the motive of selfexpression is another motive, sometimes called the joy of work. Many students of this subject have attempted to analyze the satisfaction that one receives in doing a particular type of work, as distinguished from the satisfaction derived from the contents of the pay envelope. As a result, many names for this same motive are found in books dealing with economics. Thorstein Veblen, a well-known economist, called it the instinct of workmanship. Another economist, Frank Fetter, called it psychic income. However, the most striking, if not scholarly, description of this motive is "getting a kick" out of a job well done. 

This motive explains many actions that take place in the world of business. Old-timers rush back to their jobs after a vacation, happy to be back at the office "with the crowd." 

Elderly men, financially well off, fear retirement, or, after having retired, go into business again. If this motive were not present, how could you explain the glow of pride that is yours when your father compliments you upon the spickand-span appearance of the cellar that you have just cleaned or the lawn you have just mowed?

Desire for Reputation. Man is a social being and likes to mingle with other people. The sociologist calls this characteristic of man the gregarious instinct. In addition, many

[Ch. 1 

of us also wish to be well known by our fellow men and to be respected by them. This desire we call the desire for reputation.

In nearly all vocations there is an opportunity for the building of a reputation for those who reach the top. While it is true that plumbers or electricians are known in relatively small groups, there nevertheless is an opportunity for building a good reputation in those limited groups. 

Some vocations lend themselves more readily to satisfying this motive. A glance through the morning newspaper will disclose many opportunities for building a reputation. 

Lawyers' names frequently appear. The names of actors and actresses of stage and screen, of baseball players, and of politicians are on every tongue.

Some human beings, in order to become known, will go to extremes to have this desire satisfied. Criminals, on being questioned as to the motive for their crimes, will often state that they wanted to see their names in print. What a pity that they could not have selected more honorable pursuits to satisfy this desire! 

Publicity vs. Notoriety. The urge to reach the public eye may be employed for the purpose of making money. After a particularly great feat, the person who has become known may be offered contracts to write, to go on the stage, or to play in the movies. Charles A. Lindbergh, who flew the 

Atlantic alone, was given these opportunities. The name of 

John Dillinger also stood out in the headlines of the daily papers for many weeks. But the publicity given to Lindbergh was much more palatable than the notoriety accompanying Dillinger's efforts.

Desire for Power. A step removed from the desire for reputation is a more extreme form of this same motive, the desire to control the rest of the community. This extreme

Ch. 1] 

motive may be called the desire for power. Sometimes this motive is used to serve the community, to raise the standard of living of the group, and to better general conditions. At other times this motive is strictly personal in nature and has as its purpose the satisfaction of one's own personal vanity. 

Some people achieve their goal by force of arms. Napoleon, Hitler, and Mussolini are representative of this group. 

Others may achieve the same goal indirectly by commanding huge sums of money.

To distinguish this last group from the groups motivated by the desire for gain as described previously, it should be noted that the individuals in this group already have more money than they can use. Nevertheless they continue in their businesses and seek to accumulate more and more. 

Some members of this group wield great power in American life. To attempt to evaluate the good or evil of this power is not within the province of this book. Suffice it to say that this desire for power does exist and is an important motive for working. 

Affection. John Black's parents were poor, hard-working citizens. From the day of John's birth, his mother lived in the hope that her boy would become a great doctor. During the boy's childhood she constantly made mention of this desire. Although John was discouraged by others from studying medicine, when the time finally came for him to select a career, he chose the vocation of a doctor. 

If we examine this actual case, we find that none of the motives we have listed so far seems to fit. Yet John must have had some reason for his choice. His affection for his mother and his regard for her opinion overcame his objections to medicine and strongly influenced him in his choice of a career.

Let us examine another actual case. When Mary Smith was attending high school, she met a teacher whom we shall

[Ch. 1 

call Miss Wilson. During her entire high-school career, Mary continued her friendship with this teacher. She modeled all of her actions upon those of her teacher-friend. When Mary decided upon her career, she chose teaching as her vocation. 

What helped to motivate her choice? 

One more case study will conclude our investigation.

Frank Brown had no goal. His high-school career consisted of a series of "just passing" his subjects. He was more capable in physics than in his other subjects, but even in that subject his interest was only lukewarm. Unfortunately for 

Frank, his father was wealthy enough to keep him well supplied with funds so that work to support himself was not absolutely necessary.

One day he met a young lady. From then on, things took on a different view. From hints that she tactfully directed, he knew that in order to earn her respect he would have to stop living on his family and begin earning his own living. 

However, he knew of no work in which he was sufficiently interested.

One evening in the midst of a party, the radio in his friend's home stopped working. Here was an opportunity for him to show that there was something worth while that he could do. This incident opened a new world to Frank. 

Special ability in this field of work and a desire to please the young lady caused him to study diligently. He now occupies a position of importance in a large radio company. 

In all of these cases we notice that the choice of vocation was influenced by the desire to please some person whether that person was a parent, a relative, or a friend. This desire to please we call the motive of affection.

It should be remembered, however, that if this motive is influencing your choice of vocation, it should be considered with great care. Fortunately for Frank, his abilities cut him out for the vocation he chose. Fortunately his choice was that of an honorable vocation. It might have been a very

Ch. 1] 

serious mistake to have chosen a field not so honorable, or one for which he was not fitted. 

Let us hope that Mary Smith had the abilities required for success in teaching, and that she was not confused between her affection for Miss Wilson and Miss Wilson's vocation. Let us hope, too, that Mrs. Black's hopes for her son were not blighted. John may or may not have had the talents that a good doctor must possess. Mrs. Black would have been wiser if she had watched her son develop before trying to influence his vocation. It would have been better for John to be a happy carpenter than an unhappy doctor. 

Motives and Vocations. It is important to determine in your own mind what motive is strongest within you. As you have noticed, certain vocations lend themselves more readily to the satisfaction of certain motives than to the satisfaction of others. You must analyze yourself and decide what your motives are and then seek a vocation in which these motives will be satisfied. This is an important first step in determining your vocation so that you may avoid the unhappiness that comes with being a "vocational misfit." 

A girl whose main aim in life is to become very wealthy should think twice before undertaking to become a nurse. To be happy in nursing, one must be devoted to a lifetime of service. To such a person, the fact that she has relieved a patient of pain should bring greater happiness than the purchase of a new dress. The smile of an invalid whose suffering has been eased by his nurse's care should be a greater reward than the cash that she receives for this aid. Otherwise nursing may prove to be drudgery rather than a source of happiness.

Law may bring huge sums of money to a lawyer but, unless he is prepared to serve society as well as his clients, he may not be happy in his vocation.

[Ch. 1 

Teaching may be a haven of safety for those who seek economic security. However, if the teacher does not possess the personal attributes of patience, tolerance, and the desire to serve her pupils and the community, she too may find her work extremely distasteful. 

Social Motives for Working 

So far all of the motives that we have discussed are personal, i. e., they spring from within us as individuals without regard to the rest of society in which we live. However, we are not hermits. As we have mentioned before, man is a social being. If we broaden our outlook, therefore, we shall discover that there are still more motives in existence that inspire man to work.

Civic Co-operation. Because our goverment has continued to "step into business activity," we find that Americans have divided themselves into two groups. One group believes that the government should take more of a hand in business; the second group, on the other hand, believes that society can progress better by individual effort. The members of this second group are sometimes referred to as rugged individualists. They believe that business activities should not be too closely regulated by the government. Notwithstanding this firm belief, even the most rugged individualist will admit that certain activities are better handled by a government than by individuals. For example, he will admit that if his house were on fire, a trained community fire department could extinguish it more rapidly than he himself could do so. He realizes also that he himself could not withstand the onslaughts of an invading enemy army. Not even the most rugged of rugged individualists would want to cut himself off from the community and live as a hermit. 

Man has discovered that it is to his advantage to live with others. However, with benefits come corresponding

Ch. 1] 

Courtesy Nationa 1Board of Fire Underwriters AN EXAMPLE OF CIVIC CO-OPERATION 

A trained fire department serves the community. obligations, which must be paid. Thus arises the community motive for working. 

Co-operation among the members of society is the very foundation of modern community life. 

If you will recall to mind the functions that are carried on by our city government, you will find that these consist of certain necessary jobs that our citizens as individuals could not handle themselves. Your mother could not ordinarily take a trip to Chicago to examine a steer in order to be certain that the meat she is serving to you is pure and wholesome. Even if she could take this trip, she could not, without training, be prepared to pass upon the health of the animal. Thus, we leave work of that kind to trained government officers. In like manner, it has been found that a community police force is more efficient and less costly than

[Ch. 1 

individual policemen hired by individual citizens to guard their lives and homes.

Conjure up in your mind the sight of a street paved in sections by individual landowners. Try to envision the result if each housekeeper disposed of garbage in her own way. Imagine the numerous fatalities that would result if there were no municipal traffic regulations or if there were no central agency to prevent the spread of disease. 

For these conveniences and safeguards that man has in an organized community he must be prepared to pay in the form of taxes and services.

Industrial Co-operation. Aside from the civic aspect of co-operation, there is still another view, which further proves the importance of co-operation. When we study the history of man's progress in getting goods to satisfy his wants, we shall find that with each step in his progress has come a greater dependence of man on his fellow men. 

Tracing briefly the history of one good that man requires for living in a cool climate, that is, shelter, we can see this 

STEEL IRON 

GLASS 

RUBBER

An automobile is one result of industrial co-operation.

Ch. 1] 

wider circle of dependence as man has progressed through the ages.

At first, man probably satisfied this need by using shelter such as foliage, caves, and other natural forms. This type of shelter was unsatisfactory for those who traveled from place to place, and so we find in our next phase of development the use of a portable shelter. This is best exemplified by the wigwam of our own American Indians.

Those of us who are acquainted with our own colonial history will recall that when our settlers came to live in this country, the entire group helped in the building of more substantial homes for its members. 

Speeding quickly through time, we arrive at the modern methods of housing. We find now that man must depend completely on his fellow men for this one want. Even if an individual is a carpenter and is therefore skilled in building, he too must depend on others for the raw materials that he uses, that is, nails, lumber, etc., which are obtained through the work of others.

If we consider the great number of wants that man has besides that of shelter, we cannot help but realize how complicated our economic system really is and how necessary it is for each man to co-operate with others in order to promote the general welfare of society. 

Assuming that all of this is true, you may well ask “Why must I work; why not let the others do it?" If inanimate objects were given the power of speech (and of wanting), the wheel of your father's automobile might well ask the same question of the axle. The axle might well respond, "I am supporting you and guiding you while you turn, so that both of us will get somewhere. If we decided to part company or one of us decided not to work, you still might continue to turn but would soon find yourself in the ditch. I, on the other hand, would drop to the ground and remain motionless."

22 [Ch. 1 

This fanciful tale illustrates a truth that is equally applicable to members of society. Working together, they progress; separated, they achieve little success. Interdependence is both the cause and the effect of progress. 

Enduring Monuments of Civilization. Another motive in our list of social motives may best be exemplified by the story told of three bricklayers who were employed in the construction of the beautiful Cathedral of St. John The 

Divine. An observer asked each one the question "What are you doing?" One answered, "I am laying bricks." The second answered, "I am making fourteen dollars a day."

The third looked up from his work and, wiping his brow, replied to the questioner, "I am building a cathedral.”

Let us consider the full meaning of the statement made by the third worker. Here was a man like the others, a bricklayer. Yet he saw beyond the comparative smallness of his own job, saw beyond his pay envelope, and envisioned a great edifice within whose walls people would come to pray, a building whose beauty would give pleasure not only to his own children but also to generations to come. 

Many a riveter and others who played a part in the construction of the Triboro Bridge must have shared this feeling of satisfaction when their work was done. They saw before them another enduring monument of civilization, which would save travel, not only for them but also for their children and grandchildren as well.

These so-called "enduring monuments" are not made only of stone, steel, and cement. Other works of man, which have lasted through the centuries, are also enduring monuments. How barren our life would be, even with our modern conveniences, if our radio could not give us the great musical works of Beethoven, Bach, Chopin, and the other great masters who wrote not only for their own generation but for all time. How empty our minds would be if there had been

Ch. 1] 

CATHEDRAL OF ST. JOHN THE DIVINE 

This cathedral is a fine example of an enduring monument of civilization.

[Ch. 1 

no writers like Milton, Shakespeare, Bacon, and others whose thoughts, beautifully expressed, will endure forever.

How devoid of beauty our lives would be if we could not view the great masterpieces of Rembrandt, Whistler, and others. 

In all fields the opportunity is present to carry onward the torch of progress by creating such monuments. Sometimes the desire to do certain work is a personal one, which, when completed, serves the world. Perhaps the blueprint of an enduring monument is made with another, more personal, motive in mind; but it is more beautiful and more lasting when this personal motive blossoms into a wider social motive, which includes our fellow human beings of the present as well as generations after us. This desire then becomes one to erect a monument for the years to come and to leave our community a better place in which to live.

UNIT II YOU AND YOUR VOCATION INTRODUCTION 

In the preceding chapter we attempted to find out why people work. In our investigation of the lives of people in the working world, we discovered that each one had some reason for working.

Yet if we were to ask many of the people about us whether they were happy in the work they were doing, only a few could answer truthfully that they really were.

The chief reason for this lack of happiness is due to carelessness in selecting the work in which they are engaged. 

Choice vs. Chance. After leaving school, many people drift into jobs. Often the job has been obtained through the efforts of relatives, friends of the family, or chance acquaintances. The workers drift from job to job. No plan of action has been worked out in advance. 

Later, that worker assumes the responsibility of supporting a family. He must take any job that comes along. 

It is then too late to choose.

The Vocational Misfit. Very often pupils who do not plan their vocation find that they have embarked on a vocation for which they are not suited either by ability or by temperament. A high-strung individual who embarks on a career in the assembly line of an automobile plant will soon find that he will be unable to cope with the conditions of the work. A mentally sluggish person who engages in a profession will discover that he cannot successfully compete 27

[Unit 2 888 

Courtesy Richard Blasej

ONE RESULT OF NOT CHOOSING A VOCATION WISELY 

with others in his profession. A person who lacks the abilities required in a vocation will be a failure. The vocational misfit, as he is called, is a very unhappy person. Avoid being a vocational misfit.

The Financial Failure. Another important disadvantage that may result from carelessness in the selection of a career is the failure to achieve economic independence. This, more than any other, is the cause of greatest unhappiness in one's work.

As we shall observe later, various factors play a part in determining our financial success. Many of these obstacles may be surmounted by one who selects his vocation wisely

Unit 2] 29 

YOU AND YOUR VOCATION 

29 and prepares himself adequately for it. A recent investigation of those who are on the brink of poverty indicates that many of these people had not carefully considered the vocations that they had chosen. Others had no vocation at all. 

Avoid being an "odd-jobs" man.

Advantages of a Wise Choice of Vocation. A person who selects his vocation after careful study of both the vocation and himself will find that (1) He will most likely be happy in his work because he will be well suited to it. (2) He will have a better opportunity to achieve economic independence. (3) He will be a worthy member of society, doing his part to make life happier for others as well as for himself. 

The Economic Citizen Plans His Career 

In order to achieve success in selecting the proper vocation, one must weigh carefully two important problems. (1) What one has to offer to a vocation. (2) What the vocation offers to him.

Let us consider each of these aspects separately. The two chapters that follow are an analysis of each of these problems.

CHAPTER II  FINDS OUT ABOUT HIMSELF 

Taking an Inventory. Every business, whether it is run by a single individual, by partners, or by a corporation, takes an inventory at regular intervals. By this we mean that the businessman counts all items in the store or the warehouse to determine the number on hand. For example, a rubber corporation finds out how many automobile tires, raincoats, feet of garden hose, or rubber boots it has on hand. A grocer counts his boxes of cereals, his canned goods, and all other items offered for sale. Even the housewife may be said to take an inventory when she checks up at intervals on the number of sheets, pillow cases, tablecloths, shirts, and socks that are still suitable for use. 

Taking an inventory is useful to the merchant or the manufacturer because it shows where his stock is too large and where it is too small. To the businessman a stock that is too large is undesirable. Boxes of goods on the shelf or commodities in bins are useless to him until they are sold. 

On the other hand, a stock that is too small is likewise undesirable. If the supply is too small, the businessman may not be able to fill the orders that he receives from his customers. A business cannot long exist if its customers find that it cannot be depended upon to have the goods they have ordered.

Taking an inventory discloses to the businessman all of the "dead wood" in his stock, that is, the items he does not need in operating his business. At the same time it shows where he should build up his stock in order to take care of 30

Ch. 31 

the orders that he will receive. Equipped with this information, he is prepared to conduct his business properly. 

Every young man or woman who is planning to embark on a career is faced with a problem somewhat similar to that of the businessman and can therefore learn a great deal from the businessman and his methods. As human beings we, too, have a stock of merchandise to sell or to "build up." This stock we call our abilities, our characteristics, our traits, or our talents. The sum total of these we call our personality.

Just as a customer orders or requires certain merchandise from the businessman, success in vocations requires the use of our abilities. Everyone has some abilities. It is not sufficient, however, to possess certain abilities. These abilities must be directed into the proper channels. 

Abilities. As the merchant builds up his stock so that he will be able to fill the orders of his customers, we must develop our abilities so that we shall be able to meet the demands of vocations.

Before we can discuss intelligently how we can use abilities, we must first get a clear picture of what we mean by an ability. Everything we do or can do may be said to be an ability. Our skills in walking, dancing, and speaking are all abilities. Likewise our skills in hitting a baseball, in sewing a dress, in repairing a machine, or in writing short stories are abilities.

It should be noted that the definition of ability includes not only what we are able to do at the present time but also what we can do in the future. We are all born with certain abilities that are inherited from our parents. These qualities are called capacities or native abilities. Sometimes with adequate training these abilities are developed to the fullest. 

Often, however, we neglect these native abilities and do not permit them to develop as they should.

1242 [Ch. 2 

Not all men are born equal. Since such a statement is opposed to all you have learned of American freedom, it demands further explanation. The statement that all men are born equal refers to the fact that all men have equal legal rights. Not all people, however, are born with the same capacities. There are boys and girls about you who find it difficult to solve many of life's problems because their capacities are limited. On the other hand, some boys and girls have unlimited capacities but are too lazy to develop them. 

In your own experience, like as not, you may have had an algebra problem that, after a few attempts, you gave up as being too difficult for you. Did you strain yourself in the attempt to solve it? Determination to solve the problem might have paved the way to the solution. Did you use your capacity to the utmost?

It is interesting to notice, in our study of abilities, that not all abilities are inherited. We inherit our capacities or native abilities, but not the abilities that are developed. 

Simply that a person is born with the capacity to become a good pianist does not mean that he will become one. The ability to play the piano, like many other abilities, is one that must be developed with great care. 

General Abilities and Special Abilities. At this point it might be well to distinguish between general abilities and special abilities. As we shall see later, general good health, that is, freedom from bodily ailments which may keep us from our work, is needed in all lines of work. Good health may, then, be said to be a general ability. 

Certain bodily characteristics also play an important part in determining one's career. For example, if a person is planning to be a policeman or a teacher, he will have to meet certain minimum height requirements. No matter how healthy he may be, unless he can meet these requirements he cannot enter either of these vocations. We may

Ch. 2] 3332 

Courtesy National Broadcasting Company, Inc.

ARTURO TOSCANINI 

This incomparable Italian maestro developed with great care his capacity to become a musical genius. say, then, that our abilities consist of both general abilities and special abilities. General abilities are those that are required by almost all vocations, while special abilities are those required by specific vocations. 

Classifications of Abilities

Returning once more to the businessman, we find that we can adopt another of his methods. Your local grocer carries a large variety of products. In order to simplify his listing problem, he resorts to some kind of classification. 

He may decide to list all canned products in one group and

[Ch. 2 

his loose products in another. Inasmuch as we, too, have a large variety of abilities to offer to an employer, we, too, must classify our abilities. Some traits defy classification, but many of them can be separately grouped for study. 

Physical Qualities. All those abilities that have to do with the body can be listed here. Without health few people can do their work well enough to obtain a satisfactory income. In your own experience you have probably found that it is almost impossible for you to do school homework while you are suffering from a toothache or other ailment. 

An intelligent employer recognizes that the good health of his employees is important to produce highest efficiency. 

It is for this reason that some employers erect gymnasiums and pools, conduct periodic health examinations, and use other devices to develop the health of their employees. 

Likewise, when an employer examines an applicant's school record, he is interested in finding out how often the pupil has been absent from school because frequent absences are often an indication of poor health.

The establishment of good health habits must be started in early life. Leaders in every walk of life realize this. Bobby 

Jones was sent out on the golf links at an early age to improve and develop his health. In 1930 the same Bobby Jones captured the four most important titles in professional and amateur golf. Lou Gehrig of the New York Yankees did not miss a ball game in twelve years a tribute to his well developed health habits. Franklin D. Roosevelt might never have been President if, in spite of a serious illness that rendered him partially paralyzed, he had not taken such excellent care of his body. Theodore Roosevelt developed himself from a sickly boy to a sterling example of good health. 

Nor should care of health cease as one grows older. 

Former President Hoover tossed the medicine ball around

Ch. 2] 

BOYS KEEPING IN CONDITION BY PLAYING BASKETBALL 

daily, while Senator William Borah keeps in condition through horseback riding in Rock Creek Park near his Washington home. Until a few years ago Pope Pius XI, now over eighty years of age, climbed mountains to keep his mind and body alert. Samuel Vauclain, President of the

Baldwin Locomotive Company, paid a doctor, not when he was sick, but only as long as he retained good health. Truly, health has been and will continue to be of vital importance to the successful man and woman. 

Specific vocations require, in addition to general good health, certain special physical abilities. Some special physical requirements, i. e., those of the policeman and the teacher, have already been explained.

[Ch. 2 

Motor Skills. For other vocations other special physical abilities are needed. These are called motor skills and are the ability to co-ordinate mind and muscle to perform certain manual operations. The typist must be able to manipulate her fingers in a certain fashion. The automobile mechanic, the accountant, the surgeon must all acquire certain of these motor skills. 

The secret of acquiring these skills lies in practice. You cannot learn to swim by reading about it. You must get into the water and swim. You cannot become proficient in pitching a ball unless you practice. One must "learn by doing." 

These special physical requirements are extremely important. Upon them, to a great degree, rests success or failure. Those who possess them to a high degree are careful to preserve them. The late Christy Mathewson, as great a 

BOY WORKING AT LATHE 

Motor skills can be developed early in life.

- 

pitcher as ever lived, conserved his pitching arm by constantly carrying his valise in his left hand. Rogers Hornsby, the great right-handed hitter, had the newspaper read to him in order to conserve his batting eye. The Mayo brothers, those extraordinary surgeons, have had their hands and fingers insured for millions of dollars. 

Mental Qualities. As we have seen before, not all of us have been gifted with an equal amount of mental ability, which we call intellect. Few of us can hope to equal Einstein or other brilliant men of our times. Many of us may not even possess the general mental qualities required of those in professional pursuits such as teaching, law, and medicine. 

Yet this is no cause for discontent. Each of us can achieve success in his own field with the intellect with which 

HIGH SCHOOL LIBRARY 

Mental qualities can be developed through reading.

[Ch. 2 

he has been endowed. Each of us has a place in the business world and a part to play in making the world a better place in which to live. In fact, one who possesses this quality to a lesser degree is more likely to obtain a job in certain fields than one with a higher intellect. One of the leading department stores refuses to employ highly intellectual people for certain jobs because it feels that these people will not be happy and, as a result, will not do their best. The Ford 

Motor Company will not select a highly intellectual person for work on its assembly line because such work may have a disastrous effect on that person's mind. Each job, whether it is that of a physician or a ditchdigger, a teacher or a street cleaner, is important if society is to progress. 

A far more important question, which each of us must answer, is "Am I developing to its fullest extent the intellect given to me?" Many of us fail, not because we lack ability, but because we are too lazy to use it. 

Here, too, special mental abilities should be noted. Special mental abilities in certain subjects are required for certain types of work. A boy who hopes to become an engineer must be highly proficient in mathematics. A man who repairs radios must understand the theory and practice of radio. The lawyer must know his law. Each of these people must have a special kind of knowledge. 

There are certain mental qualities that are not taught as subjects in school but are, nevertheless, important for success. The lawyer must be able to think fast when he is faced by an objection from his adversary. The grocery clerk must be able not only to add but also to add quickly when serving his customers.

Still another important quality that we should all try to develop is the ability to think "straight" or logically. This quality is important in all walks of life. No matter how much law the lawyer knows, he must be able to marshal and organize his facts to prove his clients' cases. In the

Ch. 2] 

world at large we must all be prepared to think straight when faced with the important economic and political problems that the citizen must solve.

Moral Qualities. Moral qualities are concerned with one's conscience. These qualities, such as honesty, loyalty, kindness, and obedience, play an important part in the success we attain. All of these qualities have an important bearing upon other people's attitude toward us. It is selfevident that a person who is loyal, kind, and honest will have more friends both in business and outside of business than a person who does not have these qualities. Possessing the proper moral qualities is, therefore, important since it helps a person in his relations with other people. 

In business the quality of honesty is especially important. 

Businessmen must to a large extent depend upon the honesty of their employees. Many business transactions are based upon oral agreements, but all parties to the transactions feel perfectly secure because they know that those with whom they deal are honest. A reputation for honesty is therefore one of the most valuable assets that a person in business can have.

Much has been written about the great fortunes that have been built on dishonesty. Some people conclude, therefore, that dishonesty is profitable. It is true that some dishonest people do escape detection. But a tremendously greater number are caught in their crimes or are suspected so strongly that others no longer place confidence in them.

Even if the dishonest person escapes detection, his lot is not enviable. The time and effort required by his dishonest activities, the effort required to escape detection, and the worry that is necessarily his, occupy him so fully that he is not able to give the proper attention to legitimate activities that might in the long run be more profitable. The bank teller who has borrowed funds from his employer can-

[Ch. 2 

not devote his whole attention to doing the efficient work that will give him an opportunity for promotion to a higher position in the bank. The surgeon cannot perform a successful operation with the cloak of guilt obscuring his vision and the weight of his conscience dragging his hands. Honesty is its own reward because it keeps the worker's mind clear to perform his daily tasks.

Social Qualities. Man is a gregarious being. This means simply that human beings rarely dwell alone, but, on the contrary, mingle with other people. The social qualities refer to those qualities that people need to get along with others.

In business the social qualities may spell success or failure, no matter how well endowed we are with other qualities. 

To be successful, a person must be able to mix with others. 

In some vocations a high degree of these qualities is needed. 

For example, salesmanship depends in great part on being able to get along with buyers. In the vocation of law it has been said that the successful lawyer is one who knows people rather than one who knows law. The success of a business executive depends largely on how well he can handle his staff of workers.

It would be well for those who plan to enter vocations of the types just mentioned to develop these abilities. Unless he can develop them, the shy person must look elsewhere for his vocation.

Other Qualities. Many qualities that defy classification are required in vocations. These qualities partake of many of the characteristics listed, and yet are so important that they require separate discussion. Such a quality is that of initiative, the ability to think and act for oneself. This trait is illustrated by the many examples of success that we see about us. John, a young man who had been employed as a

Ch. 2] 

shoe salesman, was discharged during the recent depression. 

Despondent over the loss of his job and his inability to get another, he brooded over his misfortune. Finally he took hold of himself and, investing his meagre savings, went into business for himself. He is now a leading shoe merchant in this city.

Cases of initiative abound. It is this quality that urges your father to try a new method in his business, that causes your friends to organize clubs, that prompted our forefathers to cut the tie from Great Britian and set up a hitherto unknown form of government, under which we live today. 

Another quality akin to initiative is that of perseverance, the ability to stick to a job until you have reached your goal. 

After many years on the New York Police Force, William H. Mulrooney became successively Police Commissioner, Chairman of the State Alcoholic Beverage Control Board, and finally Commissioner of Prisons in New York State.

Mr. Mulrooney had many other qualities, but he is an outstanding example of perseverance and steadfastness. 

Dr. Carlson, basketball coach of the University of Pittsburgh, relates how his son came into the house one day and told his father that he had shot twenty-seven fouls without a miss, thereby breaking his own record. The boy had applied the principle of "learning by doing," which we have already discussed. More than that, he had shown that he possessed the quality of sustained concentration. 

Another bough that sprouts from the tree of initiative is leadership. Leaders are more in demand today than ever before. In government, changes are more rapid and problems are more intricate than they have been at any previous time. A successful government, especially in a democracy like ours, depends upon able, courageous, and far-seeing leaders. In business, leaders are required to guide the affairs of organizations with an annual income and expenditure of many millions of dollars. Leaders are also demanded by em-

[Ch. 2 

ployees to represent them in their negotiations with employers. Often such a leader may represent many thousands of other workers. Leadership is, then, demanded in all fields of endeavor, and as the problems of government and business become larger and more intricate, leadership will be in even greater demand. 

All of the mental qualities in the world are of no avail unless they are backed by the qualities of initiative and perseverance and by those moral qualities of honesty, loyalty, and kindness. "Character above all" adorns the bulletin board of many a high-school classroom. Character is made up of these qualities and is more important than all other abilities.

REASONS FOR DISMISSAL OF EMPLOYEES 

Elsewhere we defined 

Overt Elements of Personality. 

personality as the sum total of all our abilities, qualities, and skills. Let us assume that we have the necessary qualifications and are applying for a job. How can we present these qualifications to our prospective employer?

There are some characteristics and abilities that a person can perceive and others that he cannot. For example, a prospective employer can determine whether you dress neatly, but he cannot tell whether you are honest. He can tell whether your speech is good, but he cannot tell whether you are a good stenographer unless he gives you a performance test.

Ch. 2] 

333333 Certain characteristics can, therefore, easily be discerned. 

From those qualities which he can see, the prospective employer gathers certain impressions concerning your other qualities. To illustrate, a slow heavy walk may indicate to a future employer that you are generally lazy; poor speech may point to lack of intelligence; a high-pitched voice may give the impression that you are exceedingly nervous; a lack of tidiness or cleanliness in your appearance may convince a prospective employer that your work will probably be untidy and dirty as well; rudeness may indicate a lack of all social graces.

It is important therefore to be particularly careful regarding those qualities which are apparent or overt. Otherwise your future employer will form wrong first impressions. 

First impressions are usually strongest.

These overt elements may be grouped under four headings: (1) Your appearance or dress. 

(2) Your carriage or posture. 

(3) Your manners. (4) Your speech.

Your position in this matter is somewhat like that of an actor. He has only these four elements with which to work in order to put over his part, whether it be that of hero or villain. Likewise, you have these four elements at your service to help sell yourself to an employer. 

Habit Formation. There is, however, a considerable difference between your task and that of the actor. He may drop the characteristics he has adopted when the play is over and the curtain drops. You, on the other hand, must continue with yours all the time. To help you to carry on in this fashion, your nervous system is constructed so that you can reduce certain jobs to the habit level; that is, you

[Ch. 2 

can perform certain acts without thinking. Just as you require no careful thinking to put on your coat or adjust your tie, you can develop habits of good speech, manners, posture, and appearance. However, constant practice is necessary. 

It is wise to develop these habits early in life so that they will remain with you until you have finished your last job. 

This explains why your teacher constantly calls your attention to such matters as errors in English or untidy appearance. He is trying to help you to form good habits. 

Man is a creature of habits. Make all yours good ones. • Let us compare what we have just learned about ourselves with the following lists of instructions prepared by the 

Pittsburgh Public School Employment Service. These in structions are issued to boys and girls who are preparing to embark on their first interview for jobs. 

FOR BOYS 

Before you leave home, remember to: 

Make yourself clean and tidy. 

Comb your hair. Shave. 

Clean your teeth. 

Manicure your nails. 

Shine your shoes. 

Wear a clean shirt and collar. 

Press and brush your clothes. 

Replace all missing buttons. 

Remove gaudy pins and advertising buttons.

Wear a hat or cap that goes well with your suit. 

Take any letters of recommendation you may have. 

Carry a clean handkerchief. 

Carry money for carfare and lunch. 

Leave early enough to keep your appointment on time. 

When you arrive, remember to:

Glance at your personal appearance before entering. 

Leave your cigarette outside. 

Kill any tobacco odor on your breath. 

Remove any candy or gum from your mouth. 

Remove your hat or cap on entering. 

Ask only for the person you are to interview.

Ch. 2] 

Remain outside "Private Office" until told to enter.

Refrain from interrupting a conversation.. 

Introduce yourself, state your reason for calling, and present your card of introduction. 

Remain standing until invited to be seated. 

Sit comfortably in your seat. Do not slouch. 

Be willing to take a test to show your ability. 

Be courteous all the time. 

Show willingness to return for a second interview. 

Let the employer do most of the talking.

FOR GIRLS 

Before you leave home to apply for a position or to have an interview with an employer, think about the things necessary to make your personal appearance acceptable in any business office. Remember that you are a business woman and that you should dress accordingly. 

Your clothes should not be elaborate or extreme, but plain, neat, and in good taste. 

The "Do's"

Be sure that your body and clothing are kept clean. 

Brush your teeth at least twice daily. 

Keep your nails well trimmed and clean.

See that there are no spots on your clothes, no torn places, no frayed edges, no missing buttons. 

Carry a clean handkerchief. 

Keep your stockings and gloves mended. 

Brush your clothes and polish your shoes.

The "Don'ts" Don't wear your hair in extreme fashion or fail to keep it tidy. 

Don't wear shoes run down at the heels. 

Don't wear conspicuous stockings. 

Don't wear shirtwaists that are too thin. 

Don't wear a sweater that is conspicuously open.

Don't wear too much rouge and powder. It cheapens your appearance instead of adding to your attractiveness. 

Don't wear earrings or gaudy and cheap jewelry.

FOR BOYS AND GIRLS 

When you arrive at the office where you present yourself for the position, you will make a good impression if you keep these points in mind. Be courteous at all times, learn all you can, and prove your value. 

The "Do's"

Enter the room in a quiet, self-possessed manner. 

Walk slowly; wait until you are requested to approach the desk and be seated. 

Introduce yourself and then present your card of introduction, if you have one.

[Ch. 2 

Sit in the middle of your chair with both feet on the floor. It is not becoming to loll or wrap your feet about the rungs of a chair. 

Look your interviewer in the face, and speak distinctly and without hesitation.

Be modest; aggressiveness is out of place. 

Answer the interviewer's questions in a concise, businesslike manner. 

He is not interested in family history or other irrelevant details. 

Let the employer do most of the talking. 

Be willing to return for another interview. 

Be willing to take a test. 

The "Don'ts"

Don't interrupt the employer. 

Don't have chewing gum in your mouth.

Don't watch what he is writing about you. 

Don't try to read or handle anything on the desk. 

Don't set your salary rating too high.

Don't be overconfident in stating your virtues and abilities. 

It would be advisable for you to use this as a check list when you are ready for your first interview.

Finding Out About Yourself. After having read the preceding pages, you may very well say to yourself, "Even if all that has been said is true about me, how can I find out what qualities I possess so that I may know whether the vocation I select is suitable?"

One answer to this question may be, "Take the job. If you are successful in it, then you have the abilities." 

Unfortunately, this method of finding out is much too expensive in time and money. Preparation for a profession, for example, consumes many years of a person's time and costs a great deal of money. If, at the end of a long period of training, a person finds that he cannot pass the required examinations, he has wasted his time and substance. A more economical method is desirable. 

How then can you find out about yourself? A variety of methods is open to you.

Actual Work. Take a job in the line of work that interests you. However, do this during your school vacation season

Ch. 2] 

or after school hours. Many boys and girls have "found" themselves by trying out the job before leaving school. 

This method has an important advantage over others because it gives you an opportunity to examine your traits right "in the field." If you find that the work requires abilities that you do not possess, you have lost but little time. It is more economical to lose a summer than many years or a whole lifetime working in a vocation for which you are not suited and in which you will probably be unhappy. 

Exploratory Courses. Schools have come more and more to the realization that they must help their boys and girls in the selection of vocations. In your own school a wide variety of courses is offered to permit you to try out your abilities in various lines. Shop courses, business courses, and arts and crafts courses may be followed in most schools. 

These courses, while not actually "in the field," are nevertheless extremely helpful. They should be distinguished from those courses given in the regular vocational high schools which are now being opened. These schools are designed to train the worker for his job after he has selected his vocation. Enrollment in such a school should be made after the boy or girl has reached a decision concerning his vocational choice.

Extracurricular Activities. Each school provides its pupils with opportunities to test after school their abilities in clubs, teams, and other similar activities. One school in this city has about one hundred such activities going on. 

Ample opportunity is given to the future Justice Hughes to test his ability as a speaker in debate or as a "straight" thinker in the Law Club. To the future Jesse Owens or 

Nat Holman, the track and basketball teams are available.

The future Sarah Bernhardt may display her talents under the auspices of the school's Dramatic Society. The Radio

[Ch. 2 

Club offers an interesting program to attract future Marconis. Certainly from the great number of extracurricular activities, the future worker may select one or more to test his abilities under pleasant circumstances. 

Psychological Tests. This relatively new method attempts to find out by means of tests of various kinds, whether or not you possess certain abilities. Among the types of tests are: (a) Intelligence Tests. These tests are of the type that you were required to take upon entrance into high school. 

They are supposed to indicate your capacity for learning. 

While these tests are not absolutely accurate, they serve as a guide in determining the level of job for which you are 

(b) Aptitude Tests. These tests are designed to determine whether you possess certain special capacities which may be developed. For example, there are tests to determine your capacity as a salesman (Excerpts from such a test are given on page 49.) or to determine whether you have the capacity to be a teacher. Such a test is the "Teaching Aptitude Test" by Moss, Hunt, and Wallace.

[Ch. 2 

Of a similar type are the "Minnesota Paper Farm Board

Test" by Likert and Quasha and the "Stanford Scientific 

Aptitude. Test" or the "Stenquist Mechanical Aptitude

Test." Each is designed to discover your capacity in certain lines of work.

While these tests have proved helpful to boys and girls, they are still far from being perfect. But, imperfect though they are, they are valuable when administered and interpreted by a good vocational guidance counsellor. 

Guidance Counsellors. Practically all schools have one or more persons who are trained to help the boys and girls discover their abilities. Take advantage of the opportunity to be helped. Do not feel that your problem is too simple to bring to him or too difficult for him to handle. 

In this connection a pupil seeking such help must be careful to avoid certain outside groups who fraudulently set themselves up as guidance counsellors. Just as the medical profession has its outer fringe of quacks and purveyors of harmful medicine, so, too, this field has its self-styled "experts" who fraudulently extort money from their clients. 

Some use most unscientific methods, such as phrenology (determining one's vocation by the shape of his head) and other methods proved to be ridiculous.

There are, however, many outside agencies that are helpful to boys and girls. A list of such agencies is included at the end of this chapter. 

Self-Analysis. No one knows us as well as we know ourselves. Unfortunately some of us do not know how to evaluate ourselves. We rate ourselves too high or too low. 

Yet only by a truthful analysis of oneself can one learn his own abilities and determine his fitness for a vocation. 

A boy whose name we shall omit is at present in his sixth term in a city high school. He is anxious to become a

Ch. 2] 

health education teacher. He is doing fairly well in that subject, but he is doing poorly in the other subjects on his program. Were he to evaluate his abilities in terms of the vocational requirements, he would discover that he could never reach his goal because his marks would prevent him from entering an institution of higher learning from which he must graduate before he can enter his selected vocation. 

Another boy has become convinced that he would like to be an engineer. His marks in mathematics are very low. 

Were he to analyze his own abilities (or lack of them) in terms of the requirements for engineers, he would see immediately that he lacks an important requirement. 

Socrates, an ancient Greek philosopher, once said, "Know thyself." Only by really delving into your own mind and honestly noting the abilities or disabilities you possess, can you hope to determine your fitness for a particular vocation. 

Your method should be systematic. You will find at the end of this chapter an arrangement that, if honestly carried out, will prove helpful to you. Make use of it. Your friends, your teachers, and your family will be glad to help you.
