CHAPTER III  ANALYZES VOCATIONS 

The Future Worker Seeks Advice. After the prospective worker has taken stock of his abilities, he must determine what opportunities exist for utilizing his talents. He must seek the answer to the question, "Now that I have carefully considered what I can offer to economic society, in what vocation shall I engage?" Unfortunately, the question is more easily asked than answered.

Even among respected businessmen there seems to be little unity in the advice given to the prospective worker. 

A few years ago the advice of successful executives and business owners was sought to help students who were ready to leave college and to enter the working field. Some of the answers were as follows:

August Heckscher, the philanthropist, suggested that every college graduate, man or woman, be required to spend a year working on a farm. 

H. W. Hoover, a vacuum-cleaner manufacturer, advised a period of selling something on a commission basis. 

L. A. Downs, an important railroad executive, recommended that the students take anything they could get for their first job. 

Nellie Taylor Ross, Director of the United States Mint, advised scrutinizing interesting fields to determine which ones are not overcrowded and which promise real opportunity. 

Although these business people tried to be helpful to college graduates on the eve of their entrance into the business world, this advice served only to confuse them. One may readily see that such recommendations would be even more bewildering to high-school boys and girls. 

Even a vocational guidance counsellor, well-trained in his work, is unable to state positively what vocation a 56

Ch. 3] 

student should enter. He can, however, give you a "negative prescription"; that is, he can tell you what vocations you should not enter. Aside from that, he can merely assist you to prepare for, enter upon, and progress in a vocation. The actual choice of vocation is up to you. 

Must we then give up our fight and leave the entire matter to chance? The outlook is not quite so gloomy. 

Fortunately there are some preliminary steps that any high-school student may take before he selects his career. 

Consider them carefully before embarking on a vocation that may result in unhappiness.

Steps in Selecting a Career 

Matching Motives. In Chapter I we discussed various reasons why people work. We noticed also that certain vocations lend themselves to the satisfaction of certain motives while others do not. 

It is important for happiness and success in our work that we select a vocation in which our motive for working will be satisfied. If Rembrandt had selected a clerical position instead of art as his career, he would have been a very unhappy man. If Lillian Wald had decided to become a stock market speculator, she, too, would have been dissatisfied.

Weigh your own motives for working, and try to find out if the vocation you have in mind will satisfy them. 

Matching Abilities. If your motives for working will be satisfied by a certain vocation, you must next find whether your abilities will enable you to fulfill the requirements of the vocation you have in mind.

In civil service vocations, this task is fairly simple. The civil service agencies of the Federal, state, and city governments have prepared a complete list of the requirements for each of the positions for which examinations are given.

[Ch. 3 

CHIEF ENGINEER CEMENT FINISHER 

ABORER BULLDOZER A FEW OF THE VOCATIONS 

Select and define these skilled, and

Ch. 3] 

TRANSIT MAN HODCARRIER 

LANDSCAPE ARCHITECT STONETTER IN HIGHWAY CONSTRUCTION 

vocations on the basis of unskilled, professional. 

Courtesy Department of Parks, New York City

[Ch. 3 

Mental and physical tests must be passed. Minimum height and weight requirements must be met for various positions, and, in addition, minimum age, education, and experience requirements must be satisfied.

In private industry, however, the task is not so simple. 

Here, careful study of the vocation and of the people following it must be made by the prospective worker. Only in professions, such as law and medicine, are educational requirements specifically provided for by the New York State 

Education Department. (See Chapter VI.) 

Nearly all vocations may be classified generally on the basis of mental-physical requirements. Such a classification may be helpful to one who is aware of his own mental and physical abilities.

Unskilled Labor. A typical example of unskilled labor is the ditchdigger. As a general rule, the mentality required for this type of labor is below the average. On the other hand, jobs in this group are generally associated with hard, physical labor and require a fairly high degree of general physical ability.

Skilled Labor. This group of workers requires at least average intelligence. The knowledge required may vary from that of a semi-skilled tender of a simple machine to that of a highly skilled automobile mechanic. 

Within this group many vocations are found. In each, however, a special ability is required. The stenographer, clerk, and bookkeeper are examples of those who possess a special mental skill. The carpenter, automobile mechanic, shoe repairer, dressmaker, and tailor represent skilled workers who possess a special physical skill. All work in this group requires co-ordination between mind and hand, i. e., a special motor skill.

The Professions. This group of workers requires a much higher degree of intelligence than the other two groups.

Ch. 3] 

As we have already mentioned, our state law specifies certain schooling above the high-school level for all those who enter upon a profession. In addition, those who wish to practice a profession in this state must pass certain state examinations. We shall deal with these workers in greater detail in 

Chapter VI. 

In this brief survey of classes of work, we must bear in mind that we have been very general in our analysis. The analysis made should be used merely as a guide to groups of vocations rather than to a specific vocation. Each vocation within each group has requirements peculiar to itself. 

Each must be carefully studied separately because many other factors relating to abilities are also important in determining the requirements of a particular vocation. 

Working Conditions. Some jobs are outdoor occupations, while others are indoor occupations. Some workers, such as policemen and canvassers, are almost always out of doors in all kinds of weather and must be able to resist illnesses that come from exposure to cold or rain. On the other hand, clerical workers, bank tellers, stenographers, and factory employees work indoors and are deprived of the benefits of sunshine and fresh air.

Even within the groups of indoor occupations, differences must be noted. Some people are unable to withstand noises, odors, and other unpleasant conditions of factory work. 

Office work usually provides more pleasant surroundings. 

Some vocations require constant standing, while others may involve sitting for long periods of time.

Another factor that must be considered in connection with your abilities is the length of the working day. Until recently firemen were on a twelve-hour shift, subject to call at any minute of the day or night. Teachers, on the other hand, are required to put in six hours and twenty minutes, although considerable time after school is devoted to school

[Ch. 3 62 

Courtesy The Port of New York Authority WORKING CONDITIONS DURING TUNNELING OPERATIONS 

duties. Doctors are generally subject to call at any time of day or night.

It is important for you to determine whether you are willing to work long hours and whether you are physically able to do so.

Closely related to this point is the tenseness under which work is being done. Although firemen under the new threeplatoon system are on duty only eight hours a day, they are not busy fighting fires all day. On the other hand, teachers conduct classes throughout almost the entire working day. At times a court stenographer has much leisure time, but on other occasions the pressure of work may keep him busy for ten to twelve hours at a stretch. 

Although firemen do not often work during their whole shift, they are, nevertheless, subject to call at any minute

Ch. 3] 

63 

A HAZARDOUS OCCUPATION 

The element of tenseness is present in an occupation similar to this one. of the day or night. As a result, many of them suffer from heart trouble or nervousness because they are usually aroused suddenly. The strain on the heart is severe no matter how well conditioned a man may be.

It is extremely important for you to consider the physical requirements of a vocation in the light of working conditions when you compare the requirements of the vocation with your own abilities.

Educational Preparation. Still another factor must be considered in matching abilities with vocational require-

[Ch. 3 

ments before a choice of vocation may be made. The requirements for many vocations are becoming more and more stringent.

At the present time the educational requirements for policemen are not severe. Graduation from elementary school is all that is required, although it may be only a short time before a prospective police officer will have to be a high-school graduate. Although only graduation from elementary school is required of prospective firemen, 59 of the first 200 men on a recent list for firemen held college degrees, while a total of 180 had a high-school education. 

In the professions, too, the educational requirements are becoming more strict. A prospective physician must undergo a long period of formal schooling. His preparation must be financed by his family, friends, or relatives. In many instances the medical schools in New York State will not admit a college graduate unless his personal characteristics are outstanding. Competition in all walks of life is constantly making better preparation necessary. Be sure that yours can stand the test. Be sure, too, if you choose to enter a profession, that you are in a position financially for the long period of training that will be required. 

Vocational Choice and Economic Independence 

Let us assume that you have selected a vocation for which you are well suited. Before you embark on it, however, you must be sure that another important obstacle to happiness has been hurdled, that is, the achievement of economic independence.

Elsewhere in this book we have mentioned the importance of economic independence. No matter how well you satisfy the requirements of a vocation, you will not be happy in it unless there is some assurance that you will have economic independence as a result of your work.

Ch. 3] 

Various obstacles may be present that will prevent you from achieving economic independence. 

While many of these difficulties may be traced to general business conditions or to carelessness on your part in spending the money you earn, many other obstacles are present because of the peculiar nature of certain vocations. Let us examine a few types and be guided accordingly. 

Low-Wage Vocations. If a person receives a low wage, he will have difficulty in making both ends meet, let alone provide for a rainy day.

Low wages may be the result of different causes. As we shall see later, low wages may be caused by business conditions or a host of other circumstances. One general rule, however, holds true under all business conditions. The less skill required of a worker, the less money he will be paid. An unskilled worker's total earnings during his entire working life are considerably less than those of a worker in a skilled vocation. Likewise, the total earnings of the average professional worker are greater than those of the average skilled worker.

It is for this reason that pupils are urged to continue their schooling as long as possible. If the pupil has the capacity to do work of a more highly skilled nature, he should avail himself of every opportunity to develop his capacity to the utmost. 

What of the pupil who must leave school at an early age because his financial support is needed at home? Is he doomed to a life of low wages? The answer to this question is "No!" Our city provides us with means of educating ourselves even after our formal school days are over. In 

Chapter XV we shall deal with this phase more fully. In 

Chapter X we shall concern ourselves with other efforts of workers to increase their wages.

[Ch. 3 

Let us make use of our capacities to the utmost so that we may rise above the low-wage vocations. 

Overcrowded Vocations. Training oneself for a highwage vocation does not necessarily insure a high wage. 

Many a young lawyer, for example, is now earning a weekly wage that is considerably less than the wage received by the less highly trained stenographer who works in the same office.

While at first glance the preceding statement seems to nullify the rule expressed earlier concerning the relationship of wages to training, this condition is not a negation but rather an exception to the rule. The reason for the condition is that the field of law is overcrowded. In overcrowded vocations, competition among workers results in low wages. 

Should the prospective worker follow the advice of 

Nellie Taylor Ross and avoid entering an overcrowded vocation? This question cannot be answered flatly by a yes or a no. The prospective worker must approach this matter very cautiously.

As has already been pointed out, competition is very keen in an overcrowded vocation. Therefore, only those who excel in the abilities required in the vocation are likely to succeed. Those who merely skim through may find themselves unable to make a living in the vocation. "There is always room on top for a good man.' 

Here also connections play an important part. "Connections," in this sense, means relatives or friends who are in a position to help and who may be of great assistance to prospective workers in an overcrowded vocation. For example, if your father is a successful lawyer, it is probable that you will have a better opportunity than the next fellow since your father will, in all likelihood, make you a member of his firm and in this way aid you in making a start in your life's work.

Ch. 3] 

Those who plan to enter an already overcrowded vocation should be in a position to answer "Yes" to the following questions. Do you have the ability to be among the leaders in the vocation? Do you have any way of making contacts with people who can provide you with a worth-while beginning position?

Blind-Alley Vocations. A blind-alley vocation is one that offers but little opportunity for advancement in wage or in position. As we grow older, our financial responsibilities increase. It is important, therefore, that an avenue be available whereby our earnings will increase correspondingly.

If we examine two types of positions, this point will be clear. Pupil A decides to become an elevator operator. As he grows older, he will find that he will not continue to 

SUPERINTENDENT 

PRINCIPAL FIRST ASSISTANT STARTER TEACHER ELEVATOR OPERATOR VOCATIONAL LADDER 

Note that the position of elevator operator leads only to the position of starter.

68 [Ch. 3 

receive increases in salary. If he is sufficiently ambitious, he may be promoted to elevator starter and may thus obtain a slight increase. Here his line of promotion comes to an abrupt halt. This is a blind-alley vocation. 

On the other hand, some vocations offer unlimited opportunities for advancement. If Pupil B becomes a policeman, he may rise through successive civil service examinations to the positions of sergeant, lieutenant, and even higher ranks on the force. Commissioner Valentine began his career in the Police Department as a patrolman.

Many successful businessmen pride themselves on the fact that they began their careers as newsboys. However, it should be noted that these men did not continue to work as newsboys. To achieve success, they turned to other occupations. Newspaper-selling is a blind-alley vocation. 

While it might be wise to use blind-alley occupations as a means of temporary employment, one should not plan his life's work in a vocation providing only blind-alley jobs. 

Seasonal Vocations. Some vocations offer only seasonal employment, that is, work during only certain months of the year. During the balance of the year the workers in these vocations are unemployed. Examples of workers in this type of occupation are clothing workers, gardeners, and bricklayers.

Our needs, however, continue throughout the year. 

Each of us must provide ourselves with food, clothing, and shelter even during a period when we are unemployed. 

Should we then avoid seasonal vocations? Here a more definite answer can be given. The answer is "No." Certainly no one would have advised Babe Ruth to refuse a $75,000 contract to play professional baseball because this work is seasonal. 

A few words of caution are advisable, however. First, the worker should carefully compute the amount he will

Ch. 3] 

earn during the working period. He should then spread his total seasonal wage over a yearly period to determine how much he really has earned during the year. For example, if A earns $50 a week for six months of the year, his income is equivalent to $25 a week for the whole year. 

Second, a seasonal worker should train himself for a worth-while avocation (See Chapter I). During the offseason period he may obtain work in his avocation and thus increase his total yearly income. Bill Terry, the famous baseball player and manager, is an executive of the Standard Oil Company of New York. He takes care of his work with the oil company during the winter months when he is not engaged in baseball.

Short-Lived Vocations. When people plan for economic independence under normal conditions, they generally assume that they will work for a definite period of time, let us say thirty-five years. Unfortunately, in many cases, this period of time is cut short because of the peculiar nature of the vocation. As a result, workers engaged in these vocations are compelled to retire or seek other employment before the time they had planned. 

Certain vocations are extremely hazardous. For example, policemen and firemen run the risk of death at all times in their efforts to combat crime and fire. Steeplejacks, outside painters, aviators, and window cleaners are in imminent danger of loss of life or limb. High voltage linemen are in danger of electrocution during their work. 

Other jobs wear out a man at an early age. While the worker is not in danger of his life at any one moment of his day's work, his work may be so arduous that he cannot keep up the pace for a long period of time. Examples of this type of work were given when we discussed the problem of matching our physical stamina with the requirements of the job. (See pages 61 to 63.)

[Ch. 3 

Some vocations offer only a short working period because they require special skills that a person may have for only a short time. For example, no one can expect to play professional baseball for very many years. Babe Ruth, one of the best players of all time, found himself without a baseball contract at a relatively early age. Jackie Coogan's career as a motion-picture star was cut short because he grew up. 

Should a vocation-seeker give any consideration to shortlived vocations? Here, again, the answer is a definite "Yes." 

If boys or girls have the ability and the courage to enter a hazardous vocation, they should do so. However, it is advisable to remember that under these circumstances such workers have a special problem to solve. Since their earnings may be cut short at any moment, they must make ample provision for the future. In Unit V we shall discuss various ways of making provisions for needs after retirement. 

Those engaged in short-lived vocations must be sure to consider this most carefully.

Industrial Changes. In the preceding discussion we mentioned vocations that the worker must leave because he becomes physically and mentally unfit to continue in them. 

We now turn our attention to vocations that leave the worker. In other words, certain vocations, or entire industries, may cease to exist or may move and leave the worker to seek other means of earning a livelihood. 

These changes in industry may be caused by a number of factors: 

(1) Secular Trend. Economists have made a study of certain industries in order to determine whether these industries are increasing or decreasing in importance. This study covers a long period of time and indicates what is known as the secular trend of industry.

Ch. 3] 

To illustrate this, let us examine a few industries. Not so long ago, many of the blocks in our city boasted of at least one blacksmith's shop. Today "smithies" are few and far between. In fact, one may travel for many miles in this city without passing the shop of one harness-maker or wheelwright. The entire industry revolving around transportation by horse and wagon is almost extinct. 

Even a more modern transportation facility has suffered severe losses within the past few years. The railroad industry has been forced to compete with the more recent bus, truck, automobile, and airplane industries. As a result, many railroad employees have been discharged and have been forced to seek work elsewhere. 

PHONOGRAPH RECORD MANUFACTURING 

Increased opportunities for employment in the field of phonograph record manufacturing are not likely.

122 [Ch. 3 

On the other hand, other industries are definitely on the up-grade. The President's National Resources Committee, in its report of July, 1937, mentions air-conditioning, aviation, and television as industries that are growing. 

This committee recommends that a permanent planning board be created to study industries as they develop or lag. 

Perhaps we shall soon have a careful study of industries in general so that students may be informed of the secular trend of all industries. 

Young people should avoid an industry that seems to be on the downward path. Some years ago an ambitious young man worked his way, step by step, in the business of manufacturing ladies' cotton underwear. A change in fashion, because of the introduction of rayon, reduced the demand 

Courtesy National Broadcasting Company, Inc.

AIR CONDITIONING CONTROL ROOM 

Increased opportunities for employment in the air conditioning field are likely.

Ch. 3] 

for ladies' cotton underclothing and practically ruined the business built up by him. 

On the other hand, a young man entering the air-conditioning field today can foresee a prosperous future as he advances with the industry. (2) Technological Changes. Changes may occur because of scientific advances. Many industries have suffered reverses because of new inventions or methods. Our earlier example of the railroads is a case in point. Their importance has been diminished to some extent by the advent of the automobile as a cheap method of transportation. 

Referring once more to the National Resources Committee Report, we learn that other industries may soon find their positions shaken. The growing use of artificial cotton and wool may upset the natural cotton and wool industries, just as rayon destroyed the cotton undergarment industry. 

Gasoline produced from coal is something for the petroleum industry to watch. Steep-flight aircraft and other methods of transportation by air are already making inroads in the relatively new automobile industry. 

In many instances, however, even if the industry remains, many changes may take place within the industry itself. These changes may result in the loss of one's vocation.

Chief among such changes is the invention of machinery that replaces human labor. The mechanical cotton-picker, which has recently been invented, is liable to take jobs from millions of cotton-pickers in the South. The invention of the automatic doors used in our city's subways has caused the displacement of many conductors.

While we shall consider the social effects of these changes in a later chapter, we should at this point consider the problem from the point of view of the worker. Needless to say, it would be unwise for one to train himself for a vocation if

[Ch. 3 

he is liable to be displaced because of the invention of machinery.

It is difficult to prophesy which vocations are likely to be mechanized. A worker who does a repetitive job requiring but little skill may be easily replaced by a machine. On the other hand, machines have taken the place of highly skilled workers as well. Among our earliest inventions, the power loom took employment from expert weavers. At the other extreme, the automatic elevator has displaced the elevator operator, a semi-skilled vocation. No hard and fast rule may therefore be set down. Yet a suggestion may be in order.

Modern industry requires a high degree of specialization. 

Yet it is this very specialization that leaves the worker unemployed if a machine takes his place. It is wise, therefore, to make one's background of training as wide as possible. A young girl who has a talent for sewing should not completely neglect this ability in her efforts to become an efficient stenographer. (3) Regional Shifts. Another example of a vocation that leaves the worker is an industry that moves to another city. 

Many unemployed workers in this city are without jobs today, not because of changes we have already noted, but because the factory in which they worked has moved elsewhere. In our study of the geography of New York City, Chapter VII, we shall analyze this condition as it exists in our city at the present time.

Under such circumstances the future worker may pursue one of several courses. First of all, he should bend every energy as a citizen to keep the industry in the city. This he can do by helping to solve the problems that cause industrial removals. 

Second, he must be willing to travel with the industry, although this is a less desirable alternative. While it is difficult to leave your city, your friends, and possibly your

Ch. 3] 

family, this may be necessary if you are to continue to engage in that type of work.

Finally, a prospective worker should apply the same tactics that one uses if he is in danger of being replaced by machinery. He should try to foresee whether the industry will remain in the city. This he can do by analyzing the industry in relation to the advantages the city offers. Again, he should try to make his background of training as wide as possible so that he can take on another vocation if he finds that he is unable to engage in the first he had selected. 

Luck. We could not honestly conclude our study of obstacles in the way of economic independence in one's vocation without mentioning one factor that is present in all of man's efforts, i. e., luck. There are elements of chance in all vocations no matter how carefully one plans his career. 

Some vocations, however, depend more than others on luck. The manager of a hockey team, the owner of a baseball team, or a professional golfer must be successful or the crowd will soon desert him, and with that will come a loss in earnings.

Since luck plays such an important part in determining economic independence, shall we forsake everything and depend on luck alone? The answer is definitely in the negative.

Even in those vocations in which luck plays such an important part, other features are important. The so-called luck of Lindbergh in crossing the Atlantic had a background of courage and careful preparation for the trip. The golf professional spends many hours in practicing to reach his peak. Colonel Ruppert may have taken a chance when he paid huge sums for the services of Babe Ruth. However, he must have studied carefully the ability of Babe Ruth in order to determine if the latter had sufficient drawing power to attract the crowds. While luck does enter into all voca-

[Ch. 3 

tions, we must be prepared for luck to give us the boost to success.

Social Aspects of Vocations Social vs. Anti-Social Vocations. In Chapter I we dis cussed the social motives for working. Among these motives one should be especially recalled, i. e., industrial co-operation. 

We learned that we are all mutually dependent upon one another for the goods we consume. Each of us must produce. 

Yet we discover about us vocations that are not really productive. Those workers who engage in such occupations are called predatory labor. This term refers to workers who depend on others to provide them with the goods they need without themselves contributing their share of the work. 

It is much like one member of a football team who permits the other ten players to do all the work although he shares in the glory of victory. The two general types of predatory labor are discussed in the following paragraphs. 

Legitimate vs. Illegitimate Vocations. The difference between legitimate and illegitimate vocations will be made clear if we contrast two occupations, for example, the automobile mechanic and the burglar. The automobile mechanic contributes his share by repairing automobiles and trucks so that they may continue in their tasks of transporting goods and people.

The burglar, on the other hand, contributes nothing at all. In fact, he takes goods from those people who rightfully deserve the fruits of their toil. It is for this reason that he is condemned by society and considered a criminal. 

Wealth Producers vs. Illth Producers. While the dis tinction between an automobile mechanic and a burglar is quite obvious, there are other occupations similar to the latter that are not so clearly distinguishable. These occupations include the great group of workers who are engaged in

Ch. 3]  

77 the production of goods or services that are useless or harmful to society. These goods, which we call illth, are discussed in 

Chapter XII. In that chapter we shall see why these occupations manage to last for any length of time. Ordinarily they are short lived. The advertiser who lies about his product soon loses his business. The workers in certain undesirable theatres recently banned in New York City are now without jobs. Although such vocations may flourish for a while, society soon tires of supporting able-bodied workers who refuse to share the burden of producing wealth instead of illth.

In selecting a vocation, we must be careful to select one that is truly productive. If all of us do this, we will all benefit as members of society by an increase in the supply of useful goods. On the other hand, if some of us do not aid in worth-while production, as members of society we shall all suffer. Industrial co-operation is the keystone of social progress.

As individuals, each of us must be careful, for our individual security, to select a truly productive vocation. Other vocations are generally not lasting. Society does not tolerate slackers very long. With so many desirable vocations from which to select, it would be a poor plan indeed to select a vocation that society condemns and soon destroys. 

Dignity of Manual Labor. Many prospective workers who have had the benefit of a high-school education prefer not to choose work involving physical labor. Many regard manual work, such as that of an automobile mechanic, with disfavor, while they will do everything possible to engage in an occupation that will permit them to work under conditions in which they will not soil their clothing. This type of occupation is known as white-collar work and includes positions such as those of salesmen, clerks, and stenographers.

[Ch. 3 

Courtesy The Port of New York Authority THE DIGNITY OF MANUAL LABOR 

The cable worker also serves. 

As a result there is a scarcity of skilled manual labor at the present time, although many clerks and salespeople are unemployed.

If we analyze vocations on the basis of productiveness, we cannot fail to recognize that a skilled mechanic is more useful to society than a doctor who is not well versed in his profession. Although society needs good doctors and good salespeople, it cannot do without good mechanics. All labor, if it is productive, is dignified. 

Finding Out About Vocations 

If the selection of a vocation is to be more than a mere guessing game, it is necessary for the prospective worker, before making his selection, to become acquainted with

Ch. 3]  

79 every detail of the vocation in which he is interested. It would be unwise indeed to study carefully the principles we have laid down in determining one's vocation if the facts concerning a particular vocation were not known. In our discussion we have made mention of a few characteristics of some vocations. The prospective worker must know a great deal more of the particular vocation he has under consideration.

Organizing the Information. In order to get help in obtaining the details you will need, you might well follow the guide to a study of a vocation that appears at the end of this chapter. Organizing the data will help to make facts more clear and will assure you that you have not neglected any fact that may be a deciding factor in your final determination.

To help you in your search for facts, a number of sources of information are open to you. (1) Work and Study. On pages 46 to 51 several means are suggested for finding out about ourselves. Actual work, exploratory courses, and extracurricular activities are likewise desirable for finding out information concerning the vocation as well. (2) Interviews. Another source of information is an interview with one or more persons in the vocation in which you are interested. If possible, arrange for a personal interview.

It is important to interview a person who knows a great deal concerning his vocation. It is generally true that many people who rate high in their vocations will gladly give an earnest student their time and attention to discuss the student's problems. (3) Reading. Considerable material has been published with regard to vocations. The following are among the most valuable.

[Ch. 3 

 (a) Government Publications. The Federal Government has prepared information on a large number of vocations. 

The Superintendent of Documents, Washington, D. C., can supply interesting pamphlets dealing with many vocations. 

The State Department of Education has a complete set of pamphlets dealing with requirements for various occupa tions. The U. S. Employment Service of the Department of 

Labor has prepared books listing all of the jobs in large industries, with definitions, requirements, and other interesting information. (b) Books and Magazines. Many books have been written with regard to vocations. Some of them are biographies of men engaged in the vocation that interests you. Other books deal with vocations directly. A partial list of such books appears at the end of this chapter. Many of these and others appear on the shelves of your school library or the public library. Use them.

Magazines often contain studies of occupations. One magazine, Occupations, contains information dealing only with this subject. (c) Newspaper Want Ads. Many interesting facts about your vocation may be obtained by a daily perusal of newspaper want ads. Many important questions can be answered by diligent daily reading. How many times a week have openings in your vocation been announced? Does the demand appear to be for boys or for girls? Does a minimum age appear in any of the advertisements? What other requirements, particularly educational, are common to all? 

A study of advertisements along these lines is very helpful to find out current conditions in the industry. (4) Radio Programs. The modern educational medium, the radio, also plays a part in giving us vocational information. Very often important people will discuss their vocations on the radio. Every Friday afternoon the American 

School of the Air broadcasts vocational information. Other

Ch. 3] 

addresses by prominent people are given under the auspices of our state and city governments. 

Take advantage of this first-hand information. Watch the radio page of your newspaper for announcements of these addresses. 

A Final Note. Unless we carefully consider all of the principles laid down in this discussion, we are in danger of finding only unhappiness in our life's work. Let us avoid being "round pegs in square holes." Instead, let us try to be happy economic citizens because we are doing the kind of work we want to do and can do.

THE STUDY OF A VOCATION 

Each pupil is required to prepare a report dealing with one vocation in which he is interested. This report should be prepared in book form.

Ch. 3] 

This book should be organized in the following manner: 

1. The Cover. The cover of the book should list the title of the occupation, the name of the pupil, and the subject class and official class of the pupil. In addition, an appropriate and attractive picture, or an original drawing by the pupil, of the occupation studied should be mounted on the cover. 

2. The Title Page. This page should contain the same information as that on the cover. 

3. The Table of Contents. Like all books, this book should contain a table of chapter headings and page numbers. The table of contents may be similar to the main headings in the guide to a study of a vocation. 

4. The Body of the Book. The guide given below suggests the essential information you must include in each chapter in your study of the vocation. This outline is merely suggestive. If your vocation permits another organization of these essential facts, you are free to make changes. 

5. References. Keep a careful record of the sources of all the information you have used to prepare this report. For a book, indicate the title, the author, and the pages read and quoted. For an interview, give the name of the person interviewed, his address, position, and company. Clippings and illustrations concerning the vocation should be included. Indicate the source and the date of each. This record should be placed at the end of the book. 

Neatness, accuracy, and completeness are equally important in the preparation of this report.

A GUIDE TO THE STUDY OF A VOCATION 

I. Why I selected this vocation.

A. My motive for working. 

B. Why this vocation above all others will satisfy this motive. 

C. The importance of this vocation to the community. 

II. The nature of the work. 

A. The day's work. 

1. Description of one day's activities of a worker in the vocation. 

2. Equipment used, e.g., machines, books, scientific instruments, office appliances, etc.

[Ch. 3 

B. Analyzing the work. 

1. Manual, mental, or both. 

2. Unskilled, semiskilled, skilled, technical, or professional. 

4. Discretionary automatic. 

C. Conditions of work. 

1. Type of place, e.g., office, factory. 

2. Healthful — unhealthful. 

4. Hazardous - nonhazardous. 

5. Hours of work: day, night.

III. Requirements of the vocation. 

A. Personal qualities required. 

1. Physical, mental, emotional, moral, social qualities. 

2. Proof by reference to the nature of the work. 

3. If you know of a successful worker in this vocation, prove the existence of those qualities that made him successful.

B. Educational requirements. 

1. High school. (a) Most suitable course. (b) Other school activities that will help. 

2. Special training. 

(a) Apprenticeship, training school. (b) Where this special training may be obtained. 

3. Higher education (professional). (a) Kind. (b) Estimated cost. (c) Length of time required. (d) Where it may be obtained. (e) Admission requirements.

IV. Financial aspects of the vocation. 

A. Present financial condition. 

1. To cover support during the period of training. 

2. To cover the costs of special training. 

B. Wages paid in the vocation. 

1. Beginning wage. 

2. Average wage after five years' experience.

Ch. 3] 

3. Maximum wage. (a) Amount. (b) When reached.

C. Working period during year. 

1. Steady. 

2. Seasonal. (Determine if wages are sufficient for the year's needs.) 

D. Opportunities for advancement. 

1. Prepare a "vocational ladder." 

2. Indicate the steps in advancement. 

E. Danger of technological changes. 

1. Probability of displacement by machinery. (Refer to

II B). 

2. General trend of the industry. 

F. Nature of the product. 

1. Staple product. 

2. Fad or novelty. 

G. Unemployed employed ratio. 

1. Statistical data. 

2. Conclusion as to overcrowdedness of vocation. 

H. Opportunities for employment in New York City or elsewhere. 

1. Compare the number employed in New York City with the numbers employed in other cities in the country. 

2. Proportion of the sexes in this occupation. 

I. Probable length of working life. 

1. Hazardous — nonhazardous. 

2. Special short-lived skills. 

J. Economic independence after retirement. 

1. Indicate what provisions, such as pensions, are made in this occupation.

V. General Conclusions. 

A.

Your opinion concerning the advisability of entering the vocation. 

B. Your parents' views on this choice of vocation.

UNIT III THE WORLD OF WORK INTRODUCTION 

"Shopping" for a Vocation. Before an intelligent person purchases a suit of clothing, he may "shop" around, that is, he may visit several stores and examine the clothing on the market. If he is in the market for an automobile, he is even more cautious in his selection because he realizes that he may not be able to purchase another for a long time. 

The choice of a vocation is even more serious because it means a life-time occupation. Wouldn't it be wise to apply the same method, that of shopping around, in our choice of a vocation? We must be careful shoppers if the vocations we choose are to be the right ones.

Knowledge of the World of Work. If you were asked to list the number of vocations in existence today, how many could you mention? You would probably list those vocations in which your parents, relatives, and friends are engaged. 

Could you list 100 different vocations? Actually there are about 20,000 vocations listed in the United States census of employment.

A well-known authority on vocations relates the following incident: "An investigation of the occupations chosen by several thousand ninth-grade girls showed that 40 per cent expected to be secretaries and stenographers; 20 per cent, teachers; and 10 per cent, nurses. Thus, 70 per cent expected to crowd into these three occupations, although the census shows that of the ten million women actually at work, only 

18 per cent are in these three fields." 88

Unit 3] 89 

THE WORLD OF WORK 

One of the reasons that we tend to choose vocations such as those just mentioned, is that we know more about these vocations than we do of others. If we are to be intelligent shoppers, we should become acquainted with the other vocations about which we know little or nothing at all. In this way we can broaden our view and thus have a wider choice from which to make our selection. 

Studying the World of Work. Our purpose in this unit is to explore the world of work and to observe the great number of opportunities for employment that exist. If we were to make individual studies of the 20,000 vocations listed in the census reports, we could not do justice to more than a few within the limited time we have. Yet we cannot afford to overlook all the others. We may in some instances suggest only a name. Yours is the task of further investigation. 

Of one thing, however, we may be certain. Vocations arise out of wants and out of the economic structure that has been erected to satisfy these wants. As we examine the economic systems of the past and present, we shall mention some of the vocations that satisfy these wants. Some of them may attract you more than others. Investigate them carefully.

CHAPTER IV  

If we could turn back the pages of history to the beginning of man's economic life, our task of selecting a vocation would be quite simple. As man progressed through the ages to the present time, the number of vocations increased many fold. Today a prospective worker has many vocations from which to make his selection.

As we turn quickly through the pages of history, we shall notice, however, a common bond existing between man of the past and man of the present. The basic wants for food, clothing, and shelter, and the necessity of working to satisfy those wants, are common to both periods. 

The history of man's economic life has been a continuing story of change. It is difficult to state exactly when each stage in this change took place. For convenience, however, we may divide man's economic history into five stages: 

1. The Hunting and Fishing Stage. 

2. The Pastoral Stage. 

3. The Agricultural Stage. 

4. The Handicraft Stage. 

5. The Modern Industrial Stage. 

Individual Self-Sufficiency Hunting and Fishing Stage. The earliest recorded history of man pictures him as little better than the beasts of the forest. Man worked only to satisfy his immediate elementary wants and those of his family. He roamed the forests, a nomad, seeking to wrest from nature the goods he needed to satisfy his simple wants. For food he hunted wild animals, caught fish in nearby brooks, and picked the fruits that 90

Ch. 4] 

Nature provided. For clothing he wore the skins of animals he killed. For shelter he sought natural caves or other protected places. When food became scarce, he wandered to another section.

Man, however, soon began to show his superiority over the beasts about him. He discovered that if he spent part of his time and energy in preparing crude fishhooks and bows and arrows, he could more easily obtain the food he desired. 

Thus the human race first learned the use of capital. 

Man later discovered another way of satisfying his evergrowing wants. When, for example, he had a large quantity of game but no fish, he might find that another person who had more fish than he needed would trade some of that fish for part of his game. Thus began the important system of exchange by which man today obtains a great variety of goods. When no money is used in a transaction involving exchange of goods, the exchange is known as barter. Barter marks the beginning of one of man's most important eco nomic activities of today-merchandising (See Chapter V). 

Pastoral Stage (Man Learns Thrift). It was not long before man learned another lesson. As we have noted, he had been working only to satisfy his wants of the moment. Because game and fish were not always available, he sometimes roamed for days without finding much food. 

Finally man realized that if he captured an animal or two and kept them alive, he would have a reserve supply of food that he could use as he needed it. As a result, man began to catch animals alive, to tame them, and thus to provide himself with a potential food supply all year round. 

Because man decided that, instead of killing the animals in order to satisfy immediate wants, it would be better to save the food represented by these animals until a time when food would be scarce, the idea of thrift became part of the heritage of the race.

206 [Ch. 4 

The capturing and domestication of animals was not a one-man job. It became necessary for men to live in groups (tribes and clans) in order to take care of the herds of cattle and sheep they acquired and to protect them from less thrifty people who became thieves. This banding together gave an impetus to the development of groups that eventually grew into nations.

Sheep nibble at grass so deeply that grazing land is soon exhausted. In order to provide pasturage for his sheep, man therefore had to continue his nomadic ways of life, traveling from place to place where pasture land was available. 

Man was

Agricultural Stage (Man's Wants Increase). 

not content with only meat for food. He soon discovered that he could help Nature to provide him with food from the soil through the use of plant products such as wheat, corn, and barley.

He therefore decided that wandering in search of pastoral lands was no longer desirable. Instead, he settled in a region and learned to till the soil, to sow seed, and to reap the harvest of plant products. The individual men in the tribes or 

LUI 

000 

Courtesy New York Public Library AGRICULTURE IN ANCIENT TIMES 

Egyptians plowing, as shown in a bas relief of the oldest dynasty, Memphis.

93 

PROGRESS IN AGRICULTURAL METHODS 

The time required in preparing an acre of land for planting is estimated to be: for the primitive man without manufactured equipment, forty days; for the man with a spade, ten days; for the man with a walking plow, one day; for the man with a riding plow, onefourth of a day; and for the man with a tractor, onetwelfth of a day. 

Courtesy International Harvester Co.

[Ch. 4 

clans marked off plots of land for their own use. In this way, the concept of private property was born. 

At about this time another change took place in man's economic life. In earlier years man had killed his vanquished enemy. He now realized, however, that an enemy, after capture, could serve a useful purpose if left alive. Therefore, instead of killing his captive enemies, man set them to work as slaves, tilling the fields and performing other services for him. The institution of slavery, undesirable as we now know it to be, was an improvement over the practice of killing captive enemies.

Man's Self-Sufficiency. It might be well to take stock of man's growth up to this point. Man had learned valuable lessons; his wants had increased; a greater variety of goods were now available to him. By this time, for food he had plant products of his farm and animals from his flocks or herds. His sheep provided him with wool, which his wife and daughters spun and wove into cloth for clothing. His home was made from the lumber of the forests about him. One family was sufficient to provide itself with the goods it needed to satisfy its own elementary wants. 

Community Self-Sufficiency 

Handicraft Stage. This stage in man's economic life differed greatly from the preceding stages in several ways. 

Division of Occupations. Man continued to progress. 

Though within his own family estate he could find all the things he needed, he sought better products and easier means of getting them.

People who were proficient in one kind of work began to specialize in that work and perform it for themselves and for others in the community. This brought about a division, or specialization, of occupations so that it was not long before

Ch. 4] 

separate tasks, such as shoeing horses, weaving cloth, baking bread, and others, were taken from the farm and became separate occupations in the town nearby. This phase marks the beginning of the industrial development of man. 

Community Self-Sufficiency. This new method of producing goods was an improvement over the old. A farmer could now obtain a better suit than the one formerly made on the farm, and a person skilled in another line of work could get his food more easily than if he himself had to provide it. 

At the same time, however, each family ceased to be a self-sufficient unit. The farmer learned to depend on the town tailor for his clothing. The latter, in turn, depended on the farmer for food and for wool with which cloth was made. 

Each worker now depended on the other members of his community to supply him with the goods he needed to satisfy his wants. Man's dependence on his fellow men was increasing.

Working Methods. This period in man's development precedes the most important turning point in man's economic existence. People sometimes say that man would be better off if he had gone no further. It might be well, then, to pause a moment to consider the conditions of that period.

Let us venture into the shop of the local tailor of that time to discover how goods were made. We notice first that his workshop is part of his own home. We see no racks of clothing already made up. A needle, a thimble, and a pair of scissors are the only tools one sees about. The shop is dimly lit by candle, for this is the only means available to supply illumination. A few yards of coarse cloth may be found lying on a table in the shop. This cloth has just been brought in by the weaver next door. A young boy is sweeping the shop.

A customer enters. He, too, is the tailor's neighbor. The tailor greets him and proceeds to measure him for a suit.

[Ch. 4 

Courtesy John White & Co. 

EARLY SPINNING WHEEL 

Carefully he records the individual measurements of his customer. The customer then examines the cloth that the shop owner has available. The selection is pitifully small. 

The customer finally approves the cloth and leaves. 

The tailor then proceeds to cut the cloth and to sew the garment. He makes the entire suit himself as the young boy watches. The work is done almost entirely by hand with the aid of the few simple tools we noticed lying around. After several days the customer calls for a fitting or "try-on." A

Ch. 4] 

97

long period of waiting follows until the garment is finally 

We question the young boy about his connection with the tailor to discover whether he is the tailor's son or an employee. He informs us that he is not related to the tailor but has been apprenticed by his family to this tailor in order to learn the trade.

He speaks with awe of his master. His eyes glitter with expectation as he informs us that some day he, too, shall make a whole suit by himself. He, too, will be a "master." 

Many long years of training lie between him and his goal. His apprenticeship will continue until he is twenty-one years of age. He will then travel for several years, learning new methods from tailors in other towns. He calls this the

INDENTURE OF APPRENTICESHIP 

This indenture of apprenticeship is typical of those of colonial times. Under the terms of this contract, James Parker, who later became a famous printer and a partner of Benjamin Franklin, became the apprentice of William 

Bradford, the first printer in New York City.

Ch. 99 

journeyman stage of his training. Then, some day, he will return to his own town and make one suit, which he will present to the association of tailors (the guild) in his town. 

This will be his "masterpiece." If it is acceptable to the guild members, he will be allowed to open his own tailor-shop and become a master.

This is a picture of how man lived and worked in those days. Would you prefer to have lived then?

On the one hand, we noticed that the worker was interested in his work. He made the entire product himself and was therefore more interested in the work he was doing. Also, he was his own employer.

On the other hand, the worker made only one product at a time. It took him many days to make the one product, working day and night. He spent many years training for his vocation. Although he charged a high price, he in turn paid high prices for the products that he purchased. He had but little to choose from when he made his purchases. standard of living was quite low.

His 

Domestic System (Getting Ready for the Change). Even during the period that we have just described, man's increasing wants kept urging him onward. The lords and nobles of the time were not satisfied with the meagre choice of clothing and foods available. They desired silk and spices. 

To satisfy these new wants, a new group of workers came into being. Merchants sent ships and men to distant climes to obtain these new goods. Thus arose the trade between western Europe and the Orient, and with it came the discovery of the New World. This, in turn, created new sources of raw materials and new markets for finished products. 

Merchants grew rich. Soon they began to hire the masters to work for them. They supplied the craftsmen with raw materials, paid them for their labor, and sold the product themselves. The masters were no longer their own employers

[Ch. 4 

but were reduced to the position of employees. This system came to be known as the domestic system because the articles continued to be made at home under the conditions we noted before. Only the position of the master was changed to that of employee.

Industrial Revolution (The Change). The stage was set for a change in the methods of production. New sources of raw material had been discovered. New markets had been found. Man's wants continued to increase. Only the slow manufacturing methods of the handicraft stage stood in the way of man's further progress in the satisfaction of his increasing wants.

Introduction of Machinery. In order to cope with the need for more rapid manufacturing, man proceeded to turn his attention to devices that would hasten the manufacturing process. Man's efforts were fruitful. In rapid succession came a series of inventions destined to change man's whole economic life.

In cloth-making, for example, we find the flyshuttle invented by Kay in 1736, the spinning jenny by Hargreave in 

1767, the water frame by Arkwright in 1771, the spinning mule by Crompton in 1779, the power loom by Cartwright in 1787, and the cotton gin by Eli Whitney in 1792. In addition to these, various other machines were invented in other lines of work.

Man was not completely satisfied. He now sought a way by which machinery could be more speedily driven than by human power. In 1790 Watt perfected the steam engine.

This further speeded up the process of manufacturing, for steam power could now be harnessed to drive the machinery. 

Throughout the period between 1769 and 1830 direct steam power was applied to manufacturing. Since 1900 electric power developed from coal has been taking the place of steam.

Ch. 4] 101 

At present hydroelectric power obtained from the force of running waters is becoming increasingly important. 

Courtesy Chevrolet Motor Company HYDRAULIC PRESS 

This hydraulic press is a good example of the great progress man has made in the development of machinery to perform necessary operations. 

Transportation and The World is Brought Together. 

communication facilities had to be geared up to the speed of manufacturing. In 1807 Fulton's steamboat sailed up the 

Hudson River. In 1814 Stephenson's locomotive took to the rails. Between 1830 and 1870 the whole field of transportation was steam powered. Morse's telegraph in 1832 and 

Bell's telephone in 1876 made distance unimportant when man desired to communicate speedily with others.

Thus was born our present economic set-up.

[Ch. 4 

ROBERT FULTON'S CLERMONT, THE FIRST STEAMBOAT The Modern Industrial System-A Preview 

The change in man's methods of procuring goods is of important significance to us. As future workers under these methods, we must become aware of these conditions so that we shall be prepared for them when we enter upon the field of work.

Just as we visited a master tailor to learn how man worked in the days before the Industrial Revolution, let us visit a modern clothing factory to learn how man works today. 

As we enter the factory early one morning, we notice several things that contrast strongly with the shop of the master tailor of the handicraft stage. Instead of a small room, part of the tailor's home, we see a huge room running the entire length and width of the building. A table, fifty feet long, lines the side of the loft. Sewing machines arranged in orderly fashion cover much of the floor area. Various types of pressing machines are found. Bolts of cloth are packed at one end of this huge room. At the other end are many racks of suits.

Soon a staff of about 100 workers arrive for the day's work. None of them lives in the place of work as did the worker of the past. Each takes a prearranged seat at one of the machines.

Ch. 4] 103 

Each worker has a special task to do. One sews a certain part of a coat; another sews another part. The garments are passed from one worker to the next. Each does his own small job. When a garment reaches the last worker, it is finished. 

If we were to summarize the outstanding characteristics of this new method of producing goods, we would include: 

Courtesy Ford Motor Company PART OF THE ASSEMBLY LINE IN THE FORD PLANT 

Large-Scale Production. The most prominent characteristic of modern production is the fact that large quantities of products are manufactured in a short time. This characteristic is known as large-scale or mass production. Instead of waiting for a customer to order one suit, our manufacturer has about a thousand suits on his rack ready for shipment.

[Ch. 4 

In order to bring this about, the manufacturer makes use of scientific methods. Among them are: 

Highly Effective Factory Organization. The shop has been planned so that the article to be manufactured starts as raw material, passes through the hands of several workers, and emerges a finished product. This results in the production of the maximum number of articles in the minimum time. 

Highly Efficient Labor-Saving Machinery. Whereas for merly the task of sewing garments by hand was tedious and long, now machinery has been developed to a point where but little handwork is needed. Even the sewing on of buttons and the stitching of buttonholes are done by machine. 

Division of Labor. When we observed the merchant tailor at work, we saw that he made the entire garment himself. 

In our observation of the factory method, we observe that each worker does only a small part of the entire task. This latter method of producing goods is known as division of labor. 

Has Man Really Progressed? Critics of our modern industrial system have stated that the Industrial Revolution marks the beginning of man's economic ills, that machinery has been a curse rather than a blessing. are: 

Among the charges directed at this change in industry 

1. Class Struggle. The industrial system has separated people into two groups: one group, the capitalists, who own the machinery and factories, and another group, labor, who do not own the machinery but are employed by the first group to operate the machinery. This second group must depend on the first for a living. The groups are frequently in

Ch. 4] 105 

disagreement. In the "good old days," workers and employers were one. So serious has the problem become that we shall reserve a whole unit (Unit IV) for this problem. 

2. Debasement of the Worker. Division of labor is thought in some instances to have debased the worker. A worker no longer regards the product as his own because he has made only a small part. He has therefore lost interest in the product, unlike our master craftsman of the past. The modern factory worker's job is monotonous and dull. 

3. Technological Displacement. In Chapter III we dis cussed the danger to workers of being displaced by a machine invented to do their work. The loss of a job for this reason would not be present if machinery were unheard of. 

4. Effects on the Product. Even the product has become inferior, according to the critics of the machine age. Articles made by machinery, they say, are inferior to those made by hand. A hand-made suit may be better than one made by machinery.

The product has become standardized, that is, each product is an exact duplicate of the next. This and other criticisms we will discuss in Chapter XII. 

Progress Achieved. After examining this list of charges against the modern system, we, too, might yearn for the good old days. Being critical-minded, we must delay our judgment until we hear the other side of the case: 

1. Making Work Easier. Steam shovels ease the back breaking toil of the open-pit miner. Agricultural machinery has made farming a less grueling task. All about us we see evidences of the fact that machinery has made man's work easier. Machinery has lightened the physical burden of work. 

2. Shorter Working Day. As the use of machinery increased our production, so it successively lessened the day's

[Ch. 4 

work. The farmer who toiled all day long to till his field can perform his duties in fewer hours. In the city, the hours of factory workers have been reduced to such an extent that a considerable part of the day is left for leisure time activities. 

3. Quantity Output. In spite of shorter working days, more products are being manufactured. The use of machinery has made possible manufacturing on a large scale. Whereas our tailor of the handicraft stage labored for weeks over one garment, our modern system of production permits a great number of suits to be made at once. 

4. Higher Standard of Living. Man is much better off than he was before the Industrial Revolution. Large-scale manufacturing has lowered the cost of goods. We may now buy the goods we need at a fraction of their former cost. We also have a greater variety of goods from which to make our selection. Only a few years ago radios were considered 

10 MILLION NEW HOMES BUILT 22 MILLION HOMES WIRED FOR ELECTRICITY 19 MILLION TELEPHONES INSTALLED 29 MILLION RADIOS IN HOMES INCREASING STANDARD OF LIVING Courtesy National Association of Manufacturers

Ch. 4] 107 

luxuries. Today hardly a home lacks a radio set. In the past, only the wealthy could afford the luxury of a book or of a carriage. Today the cost of books and newspapers is so low that they are within the purchasing power of all but the very poor. Automobiles are within reach of many workers. 

5. Work Created by Machinery. Machinery has had a double effect on man. It has displaced many workers. The medieval scrivener lost his vocation with the invention of the 

FROM THE HORSE AND BUGGY TO THE AUTOMOBILE THIS INDUSTRY MADE JOBS FOR 1,000,000 IN 1900 THIS INDUSTRY MADE JOBS FOR 6,100,000 IN 1937 Courtesy National Association of Manufacturers AN EXAMPLE OF HOW MACHINES INCREASE JOBS 

printing press. On the other hand, the number of people employed in publishing and printing industries today runs into millions, while new occupations were opened to writers and distributors of newspapers and periodicals. Thousands of men are employed by paper mills and pulp-wood processors.

[Ch. 4 

In like manner, the invention of the automobile displaced the cartwright, the wheelwright, and the blacksmith. In their place are the thousands of people employed in automobile factories, in steel mills, in gas stations, in oil refining. 

In the long run, the invention of machinery has been labor creating as well as labor saving. 

The World of Vocations Today. So great have the num ber of occupations become as the result of changes in man's methods of working that to study all in detail would be a task of years. If we were to attempt to deal fully with all the possible vocations from which future workers might make their choice, we could not possibly do so within the confines of this book. The most recent United States Census lists over twenty thousand different occupations. We could not do justice even to the thousands of occupations within our city alone.

Fortunately, however, in spite of the large number of occupations, many of them bear similarities in the purpose they serve to society. Let us group these occupations according to the functions that they perform. 

1. Production of the Raw Material. As the name indi cates, this huge army of workers supplies society with raw materials, such as lumber, wool, and coal. 

2. Manufacture of the Product. This group of workers serves society by making the finished product from the raw materials supplied by the first group. For example, within this group are those who convert wool into the suits that the members of society want. Others in the same group make the machinery that is used in making this change more rapid and in greater quantity. 

3. Buying and Selling of the Product. Because of the method of manufacturing in large quantities, and because we

want goods that are not produced near at hand, it has become necessary for goods to be bought and sold many times before reaching us. Those engaged in the buying and selling of goods render this service. The activity is known as merchandising or trade. 

4. Financing of the Transactions. Another group of oc cupations has the task of providing funds to aid the tradespeople in the purchase and sale of the product until it reaches the user. One economist has compared the work carried on by this group to that of the bloodstream, which carries nourishment to all parts of our bodies. He calls it the "vasomotor system" of our economic life. 

5. Delivering of the Product. The large number of occu pations in the transportation industries do their part in satisfying man's wants by delivering the raw materials to the places where they may be manufactured, and the finished products from their original locations to a point where the user may obtain them without difficulty. The communications industries provide the means by which the necessary negotiations that have to be conducted in connection with the shipment may be made. 

6. Personal Service. Workers in this group perform certain services for those engaged in business, which the latter might otherwise have to do themselves. By relieving those engaged in business from these tasks, the personal service group permits businessmen to devote more of their time and energy to their business. 

7. The Professions. This group of workers performs certain highly skilled and important tasks for workers in all groups. Because of the very special training they must undergo before being permitted to perform these tasks, they are grouped for separate study.

Ch. 4] 111 

8. Public Service. This group consists of employees of the Federal, state, and city governments who provide the many services about which you have read in your course in civics.

Interdependence of Workers. All the groups working together have contributed something to each and every article you are using. Many of these workers perform services directly for you and your family. In close co-operation with 

FARM AND FACTORY WORKING TOGETHER TO PRODUCE NEW WONDERS Courtesy National Association of Manufacturers each other, these groups supply man with the goods to satisfy his wants. Together they give us a concise picture of all the worth-while jobs from which each of us will select the vocation in which he will most likely succeed, if his choice is wise. 

It might be well for us to give each group careful study in making our selection.
