3. Man's Progress in the Search for Food. 

4. Clothing Through the Years. 

B. To illustrate one of these topics, prepare a series of pictures of objects seen in your visit.

C. Construct models of interesting objects seen. 

D. Prepare a series of clippings showing different styles of clothing or architecture, or furniture used by men in the past and present.

CHAPTER V OPPORTUNITIES IN THE MATERIAL GOODS INDUSTRIES 

Man's wants include two important kinds of goods. One kind is made up of tangible or material goods, such as clothing and food. The other kind is composed of services, such as entertainment, medical aid, house cleaning help, and protection from thieves. 

Let us study first those vocations that are involved in supplying man with tangible goods. 

The steps involved are: 

1. Providing the Raw Materials. 

2. Finishing the Product. 

3. Selling the Product to the User. 

Vocations That Provide Raw Materials 

Importance of Raw Materials. Would it be possible for us to have bread without wheat or rye, automobiles without metal, this very book without trees? Of course, the answer is quite definitely "No."

Raw materials are the very foundation of a country's well being. Without them a country is hard put to supply her inhabitants with the goods they want. Behind the newspaper headlines such as "Japan Invades China," "Germany Seeks Colonies," is the story of a lack of raw materials. 

Fortunately the United States has many natural resources from which raw materials can be obtained. Unlike 

Russia, which also has vast resources, the United States has developed industries that have been long engaged in wresting these precious raw materials from Nature. Many of her inhabitants are engaged in this work. Almost one-third of our country's population is engaged in agriculture alone. 114

Ch. 5] 115 

 Industries Within the Group. Since the United States is well supplied with natural resources, many raw-material industries offer opportunities to prospective workers. Within this group the following industries are found: 

1. Agriculture. This vast industry supplies us with many of our food products as well as raw materials for goods to satisfy other wants, such as cotton and flax for our clothing. 

This industry supplies us 

2. Grazing and Dairying. 

with meat and dairy food products. In addition, the hide of cattle is the basic raw material for shoes. Other by-products provide us with tallow for candles, special extracts for medicines, wool, and other goods. 

Courtesy U. S. Department of Agriculture SHEARING SHEEP BY MACHINERY 

3. Mining. This branch of the raw-materials industries supplies us with iron for the manufacture of machinery. It also supplies coal and petroleum, which are converted into power to run machinery or are changed to heat to make our

[Ch. 5 

homes comfortable during the winter months. This branch, too, supplies us with the various metals, such as copper, silver, and gold, that are used in industry. 

4. Lumber Industry. This industry supplies us with the raw materials for products to be made of wood, such as furniture and building materials. Even your pencil and book also have their origin in the raw material supplied by this industry. 

5. Fishing. All along the coastal waters and rivers of the 

North American continent, many varieties of fish are caught in great quantities by workers. They include haddock, cod, salmon, oysters, and many others. While fish is primarily used for food, it has other uses also. For example, the menhaden, a small fish found along the Atlantic Coast is used as fertilizer by farmers.

Basic Vocational Opportunities. The raw-materials industries, like others we shall discuss, offer opportunities to workers with differing abilities. 

Professional Opportunities. Several professions may be given as examples of how professional people serve this portion of our modern industrial world. A veterinary is a doctor who directs his study to the ailments common to animals. 

A mining engineer devotes his attention to the engineering problems of mines. Those of us who have visited a coal mine or seen pictures of one realize the many engineering problems that face the mining engineer. Some research scientists devote their entire attention to such problems as finding less expensive ways of extracting minerals from ores. 

Others turn their attention to ways and means of destroying insect pests, such as the boll-weevil, which ruin the farmer's crops. Still others seek ways of breeding animals, flowers, and fruits to provide us with greater varieties and better prod-

Ch. 5] 117 

ucts. The horticulturist and tree surgeon are outstanding examples of this group.

Skilled Labor Opportunities. The raw-materials industries provide employment for skilled workers as well. Farmers are required to know certain "tricks of the trade" if they are to succeed in their work. At one time farmers needed to know but little to operate their farms. Today, many farmers are university graduates.

Even our city schools have recognized the importance of this work and have organized courses in agriculture for those who desire to pursue this study. Agriculture is now a science.

In lumber camps there is need for specially trained men who climb trees to remove parts of the upper reaches. In mines, highly skilled workers place dynamite charges so that sections of rock underground will be blown away without injuring people or destroying the rest of the mine structure.

Unskilled Labor Opportunities. Most of the workers in the raw-materials industries are of the unskilled type. In mining, for example, after the careful planning of the mining engineer and the careful execution of important parts by the dynamite charge-setter, the pick and shovel work is largely in the hands of men who require but little training. For this last group, physical strength is most essential. The life is a hardy and strenuous one. 

It is generally true that most of the work in the rawmaterials industries is done by the unskilled workers. Farmers' helpers, lumber camp workers, all form part of this great group. This may help to explain why industrial unionism (page 303) has as one of its strongest proponents the United 

Mine Workers of America.

[Ch. 5 

Modern Trends. At one time any person who entered a vocation in the raw-materials industries was compelled to cut all ties between him and civilization. Farmers, miners, and others had no connection with city life. Today, however, the radio and other speedy means of communication and transportation are bringing raw-materials workers and others in closer social relationship. The "hick farmer" is the farmer of yesterday.

One of the most important trends in these industries that a future worker must bear in mind is the increased use of machinery. Farming, to a great extent, has become mechanized. The introduction of machinery into this process has resulted in the displacement of workers. Any plan to enter this field of industry must be carefully considered in the light of this trend in industry. 

Another change of great importance to prospective workers in this field is one that hardly would seem possible. It seems surprising that the industries which are the backbone of a nation's economic success should ever find themselves in dire straits. Yet this condition is present. Ever since the end of the World War farmers have found it very difficult to make a living. In Great Britain the government has been compelled to buy the coal mines from the private owners. 

In this country our Government has set up a commission to regulate the activities of the bituminous coal mines. 

On the other hand, scientific advances may change the entire future of the raw-materials industries. The agricultural scientists have made much headway in the work of destroying insect pests. New industrial uses have been found for agricultural products. The soy bean, originally a food product among the Chinese, now provides raw materials for paints, varnishes, and other important products.

Small cotton fibers (linters), as well as cornstalks, which were formerly waste products, are being used to make cellu-

Ch. 5] 119 

lose, which, in turn, provides us with cellophane and other valuable products.

In some countries in which raw materials are scarce, products such as gasoline from coal, and cloth from wood, are being made. This is typical of the ersatz industries of 

Germany. 

Most of these scientific advances have come about because the supply of the original raw materials is rapidly becoming depleted. It is for this reason that the Americans, who have been blessed with so many of these raw materials, are preaching and practicing conservation, that is, the careful use and saving of these materials. 

Opportunities in New York City. While many vocations are found in this group of industries, these industries unfortunately offer but little opportunity for employment in New York City. Although agriculture alone gives employment to about one-third of our country's population, yet within our own city, opportunity for employment in this field is limited to the few small truck farms in the outlying sections of the city. The maintenance of parks, home gardens, and tree "nurseries" offer opportunities to a few. 

Most of us who prefer to remain in this city must forego the opportunity of engaging in work in this group of industries. 

Vocations That Provide Finished Products 

Importance of Manufacturing. Our study of the rawmaterials group of industries may have left us somewhat discouraged concerning opportunities for employment in New York City. However, when we come to a study of the manufacturing industries, our country cousins may well be envious of the occupational opportunities that we, in the leading manufacturing center of the world, have close by. 

As we shall see from our study of the geography of the city, New York is well suited to manufacturing. Producers

[Ch. 5 

have been quick to realize the advantages of being situated here. As a result, there are approximately 36,000 factories in our city, employing 918,000 wage earners.

In most instances the standard of living of a whole country is determined by the extent of her manufacturing activities. This is true even if the nation involved is wealthy in natural resources. Russia, for example, has been making every effort in recent years to build up her manufacturing industries. The general high standard of living in the United 

States is a tribute to her greatness as a manufacturing country. New York City plays no small part in our country's manufacturing activities. 

Industries Within This Group. Manufacturing and me chanical industries are so alike in most respects that we may study them as if they were identical. However, one important distinction should be noted. 

We found that early in man's economic life he realized that he could get more fish, for example, if he made a fishhook and used it instead of trying to catch fish by hand. 

The same thought is present in modern industry. Man has found that if he wants more shoes or clothing, he must first devote his attention to making the machine necessary to manufacture them. This is known as the "roundabout" method of production.

The mechanical industries are those whose task involves the making and repairing of machinery. The manufacturing industries are those which actually make the end product, that is, the finished article which man really wants, such as shoes and clothing.

With this preliminary distinction brought out, we may now proceed to consider the number of industries that make up this field.

This task is relatively simple. All one need do is to list the many articles he uses in his daily life. Each product

Ch. 5] 121 

represents a separate manufacturing or mechanical industry.

For example, this book is the product of the printing and publishing industry. The shoes you are wearing are the product of the boot and shoe industry, and the bed you sleep in is a product of the furniture industry. 

In early handicraft days each product represented the work of one man. Today it is the work of a whole corps of workers in one industry. Each industry includes many different vocations. 

Manufacturing Industries in New York City. As we have noted before, New York City is a leading manufacturing center. Within our reach are vocations in many manufacturing industries. New York City leads all others in the manufacture of men's and women's clothing. At the same time, this industry is the leading manufacturing industry in New 

York City.

While New York City does not lead in the manufacture of all products, it nevertheless offers opportunities in nearly all lines of work. Troy, in upper New York State, leads in the manufacture of men's collars and shirts. Nevertheless, many shirt and collar factories may be found within New 

York City. A list of manufacturing and mechanical industries in this city includes:

22,192 8,256 2,076 142 Divisions of These Industries. In studying this group of industries, we must bear in mind that the picture of modern

[Ch. 5 

industry, which we presented in Chapter IV, has not entirely replaced the older methods of manufacturing. In some instances handmade goods are still in demand. In the clothing industry, for example, merchant or custom tailors still ply their trade. In the furniture business, cabinetmakers still make articles of furniture to order. Though in most instances these craftsmen use modern machinery, there is no division of labor, and most of the work is done by hand. 

Still another division of this industry is engaged in repair work. For such items as clothing, automobiles, and other durable goods, occasions may arise when these goods must be repaired. For example, the engine of an automobile may stop functioning properly after a little use. Your suit may tear or require cleaning and pressing. With a little adjustment and repair, these articles could be restored to use once more.

In years gone by it would have been customary, under such circumstances, to repair the suit or the wagon (automobiles did not exist at the time) at home. If the situation were serious, the broken article might have been taken back to the master craftsman from whom it was bought. The master craftsman was both manufacturer and repairer. 

Nowadays, however, an entirely separate group is employed in making repairs on durable goods. Within this category are the local tailor, garage attendant, mechanic, shoemaker, and others.

The members of this group generally get their training in the factory in which the product is made. In most instances they are workers who wish to be their own employers but do not have the funds needed to set up their own manufacturing plant. Opening a repair shop does not require a great outlay of money. In most instances, the owner of the shop is the only worker. Only rarely does he employ many workers. Large-scale manufacturing is the most important of these divisions and gives employment to most workers

Ch. 5] 125 

today. We should, therefore, give more attention to vocations in this branch of manufacturing. 

Basic Vocations in Modern Manufacturing. At first glance the task of analyzing the thousands of occupations in this group of industries is a staggering one indeed. Each occupation requires a separate skill. Each has its own peculiar requirements. Yet there is a pattern of similarity in many of these vocations that may help us in our survey. 

If we examine the steps in the manufacture of one product, we shall find the basic vocations in all. Let us return to the clothing factory, which we visited in Chapter IV, and observe the men at work. We notice that the manufacture of the suit involves the following steps: 

1. The cloth is usually manufactured outside of New 

York. It is a well-established fact that the textile industry in New York City is devoted chiefly to the manufacture of silks and rayons, and not woolens and worsteds. The New 

England States have long been the center of cloth-weaving. 

Present tendencies show that the industry, particularly that part of it manufacturing the cheaper grade of textiles, is moving toward the South, to get the benefit of cheaper labor. 

2. A salesman calls on the clothing manufacturer with samples of cloth representing the various weaves, qualities, and colors. In the clothing industry these samples of cloth are known as swatches. 

3. The manufacturer, or his buyer, selects the various cloths in which he is interested and orders them by the bolt or piece. A bolt is a roll of cloth about sixty yards long and about seventy-two inches wide, folded lengthwise. Approximately twenty suits can be manufactured from a bolt of cloth. 

4. When the cloth is received from the mill, it undergoes another process known as sponging. This means that steam is passed through the cloth to shrink it before it is cut. The suit can then be worn in all kinds of weather. 

At the same time, the bolt of cloth is examined for possible defects in weaving. In order to warn the manufacturer

[Ch. 5 

of any irregularities, a string is attached. At the same time the cloth is also remeasured. This work is done by workers known as spongers and examiners. 

5. Meanwhile, designers have been preparing patterns, which represent the very latest styles. These patterns are made of a very heavy cardboard and are later used as described below. 

6. The cloth is then unrolled on a long table in the manufacturer's plant. Approximately three yards of cloth are needed for each suit. Therefore, fifteen yards are required for the cutting of five different sizes. The patterns of these five sizes are then placed on the cloth by a skilled worker known as a marker.

This work, although it appears to be simple, requires a great deal of training and pays the highest wages in the industry. The pattern must be very carefully handled. It is important to avoid injury to the material. The stripes and weave must run in regular order in a finished suit. The minimum length of cloth used in cutting a single garment is immaterial. In cutting 100,000 suits, however, a considerable saving can be effected by a careful placement of the patterns on the cloth so that there is only a minimum of waste. A good marker can save his employer several hundreds of dollars a year.

The patterns are outlined with wax chalk directly on the cloth. This piece of cloth is known as the mark. On account of the color and texture of the material used in women's wear, in the manufacture of ladies' dresses the patterns are marked on a piece of paper equal in size to the cloth. This paper is then called the mark. 

7. The mark is then removed. Layers of cloth of the same length are placed one on top of the other, according to the number of suits of a particular size required. If the manufacturer desires twenty suits of each size, nineteen layers are so placed. 

8. The mark is placed on top of these layers, and either the marker or his assistant cuts along the line of the mark with a machine especially designed for this purpose. This machine is electrically operated and has a motor that causes a blade to move and cut the cloth as directed by the person controlling the machine.

Ch. 5] 127 

Courtesy Associated Wool Industries FINISHED WOOL FABRIC COMING OFF PRESSING MACHINE 

9. The cuts are then sorted; and the necessary trimmings, such as lining, buttons, and canvas, are added by another worker known as the trimmer, who is usually an apprentice learning the work of the clothing cutter. 

10. In many cases the garments are not sewed together in the manufacturer's plant, but are shipped to contractors who sew the parts or pieces together, charging the manu-

[Ch. 5 

facturer an agreed amount per unit. In the factory used in our illustration, however, the work is all done in the same place. It is here that we find division of labor to a very high degree. 

11. The pieces are taken to workers known as tailors or basters. These workers sew the garment together temporarily. 

This process is known as basting. 

12. After the pieces have been assembled in the basting process, sections such as the sleeves, the back, and the collar are sewed together by machine. The workers who sew the garments on the machines are known as operators. 

While one operator may be sewing together the upper and under parts of the sleeve of a coat, another operator may be sewing the sleeve lining of the same coat, and still another may be sewing the back of the coat. Each part requires a slightly different skill. As a result, one worker sews the sleeves; another, the collar; and so on. Sometimes certain parts are sewed by hand, e. g., the collar. 

13. The garment is then turned over to buttonhole makers and button sewers. In many instances this work is done by hand. Often machinery is employed for this operation as well. 

14. The garment is then pressed and worked into shape by various pressing devices. The work is done on machines by skilled pressers. 

15. The finisher receives the garment, inspects it for defects, cuts off threads, and otherwise prepares it for shipment.

From this study of the process of manufacturing a suit, we can discover basic vocations that are similar in many respects to those in other manufacturing industries. 

Designing. Before a product is manufactured on a large scale, the manufacturer wishes to be sure that the product will be satisfactory. The first step, therefore, is to design or plan the product that is to be made, whether it is a suit, a pair of shoes, a building, or living room furniture. 

This need has created a number of vocations in the general field of designing. Architects who plan new buildings,

Ch. 5] 129 

engineers who design new automobiles, stylists who create new designs for clothing and other apparel-all are included. 

In the clothing industry alone, cloth designing, dress designing, and suit designing represent three of the vocations in that field.

This type of work offers lucrative returns for those who have ability. Among the requirements of the work are an ability to draw, a great deal of creative ability, and consider- 

A SKILLED WORKER IN THE LITHOGRAPHING FIELD 

able mathematical and engineering ability in some instances. 

Special training can be acquired through courses at some

[Ch. 5 

of the vocational high schools in this city, as well as in a number of private schools.

It is interesting to note that there is no limitation as to sex. Although one might get the impression that only a man can design clothes for men, and only women can design clothing for women, this is not the case. As a matter of fact, such leading dress designers as the late Jean Patou, of France, and Orry Kelly, who designs dresses for Hollywood motion-pictures actresses, are men. Molyneux, an Englishman of French descent, who originally wanted to be a painter, is a designer whose creations are sought after by many women. He is a former British Army Captain, who was wounded three times during the World War and who received the British Military Cross. On the other hand, women are entering designing vocations in which men are considered supreme. For example, many women are entering the field of architecture. 

Carrying Out the Plan. The artisan or skilled worker enters this part of the manufacturing process. In the clothing factory illustration, we noted such expert workers as the sponger, the examiner, the clothing cutter, and the hand tailor. Correspondingly, in other lines of work we find skilled workers. For example, the carpenter, the electrician, and the plumber play a part in the building trades; the linotyper and other highly skilled workers are found in the printing trades. Each is highly skilled and performs the more intricate parts of the task of making the product. 

On the other hand, the operators and less skilled workers also find employment in manufacturing. The operation of some of the machines in the clothing factory is a relatively simple task. The hod carriers in the building trades and the great army of workers on the assembly line of an automobile plant are all examples of semiskilled and unskilled workers. 

The counting of buttons in a clothing factory requires but little skill.

Ch. 5] 131 

AN UNSKILLED WORKER IN WOOL-MANUFACTURING 

Modern Trends. Future workers who intend to enter the field of manufacturing must be prepared for many changes in this group of industries. 

Technological changes are most pronounced in this field of work. New inventions are constantly being introduced into manufacturing processes. As a result, old vocations may cease to exist, while new ones are constantly being introduced.

[Ch. 5 

Certain products are not in constant demand. The taste of the public changes in regard to some products. Such items as parasols, novelty watch fobs, and similar luxuries are not always in demand. As a result, workers who train themselves for this kind of work may find themselves without employment as styles change.

On the other hand, certain staple articles are always in demand and are not likely to be discarded. Clothing, shoes, other articles of apparel, buildings, bread, and other basic food products are likely to be desired as long as human beings live on this earth. The future worker should, if possible, avoid a manufacturing vocation in novelties and frills. 

The industries that make staple products are the ones that endure.

Along with the invention of new machinery comes further specialization. Work in manufacturing is constantly being broken up into smaller parts. 

The manufacturing industries are, in general, highly unionized, particularly in New York City. The Amalgamated 

Clothing Workers of America and the Big Six are examples of important unions in the clothing and printing industries, respectively.

The Field of Trade 

Consumption is the ultimate goal of production. This means that goods are produced so that they may be purchased by those who will put them to use. For example, the suit of clothes whose manufacture we described in our study of manufacturing must finally reach some person who wants to wear that suit and who is willing to pay for it, that is, the ultimate user or consumer. This important work lies in the field of trade or merchandising.

Early History. In earlier days the task of bringing the manufactured article to the ultimate user was relatively

Ch. 5] 133 

simple. In our study of the handicraft system, we noticed that the consumer purchased his suit directly from the man who made it for him.

Merchandising at the Present Time. Today, however, conditions are entirely different. As consumers, we enter a store whose owner does not make products himself. We find suits made in advance of our coming. We may select a suit from a stock to which several manufacturers have contributed. More than that, we need not travel far to purchase any one of a thousand different products, many of which, like coffee, are not produced even in our own country, let alone our own city. The vast field of trade offers additional opportunities to workers. Workers in this field perform work as important as that of raw-materials providers and those who make the finished product. Without trade we could not enjoy the great quantity and large variety of products now within our reach.

The Usual Merchandising Channel. Under ordinary circumstances, the article manufactured does not pass immediately from producer to consumer, as it did in handicraft days. Instead, it is sold and resold until it finally reaches the consumer. The business people engaged in buying and selling are known as merchants. The sale of goods is the foundation of trade. 

Goods leaving the manufacturer's factory are usually sold from one type of merchant to another. Each, although not adding to the quality of the product, performs certain necessary services for which he receives a profit. 

Wholesalers. As we have seen, the manufacturer, under modern methods of manufacturing, produces large quantities of goods. If he were to attempt to sell directly to the consumer, he would become involved in many problems besides

[Ch. 5 

 MANUFACTURERS WHOLESALER RETAILERS THE FUNCTION OF THE WHOLESALER 

the titanic problem of manufacturing. He would have to invest a great deal of money in buildings, in hiring salespeople, and in many other things to accommodate many individual customers in order to dispose of the stock. Only in a few cases do manufacturers tackle the problem by opening retail chain stores in order to sell the great quantity of stock that they have produced.

The producer usually prefers to shift the burden and to sell in large quantities to merchants so that he may dispose of his entire product more conveniently and speedily. The merchant who purchases in large quantities from the producer is known as a wholesaler or jobber. There are nearly 

13,000 wholesale dealers in this city.

Ch. 5] 135 

Retailers. The wholesaler purchases from many manufacturers and, in turn, distributes or sells in smaller quantities to other merchants known as retailers. The retail merchant deals directly with the consumer. This step in the flow of goods helps the consumer in that he may have a wider variety of products from different manufacturers. 

In view of the large population in our city that serves as a consumers' market, it is little wonder that we find that there are more than 150,000 retail stores in existence in New York.

Types of Retail Stores. Although all retail stores deal directly with consumers, some differ from others in certain respects. These differences may suggest special vocational opportunities to those interested in this field of economic activity.

Independent Retailers. The simplest type of retail business is that of the independent retailer, consisting of but one store, selling usually but one type of article, and usually operated by the owner himself. The number of salespeople and buyers whom he employs varies with the amount of business done. 

Chain-Store Retailers. As the name implies, a chain store consists of a series of stores, selling one or more types of articles, under one ownership. Each store is managed by a special employee, the store manager, who takes charge of all activities in that particular store, making regular reports to the main office of the firm. Illustrations of chain stores are the Atlantic & Pacific Tea Company and the F. W. 

Woolworth Company.

Many manufacturers who do not sell their products to wholesalers open retail chain stores and thus dispose of the large quantities they produce. Illustrations are Thom McAn and Crawford Clothes.

[Ch. 5 

Mail-Order Houses. The mail-order house supplies consumers who do not reside in the city, or who, for other reasons, cannot personally visit a retail store. Mail-order houses send by mail catalogues or lists of articles in their stock directly to consumers who, in turn, order by mail what they wish from these catalogues. Illustrations of this type are Sears, Roebuck & Co. and Montgomery Ward.

Department Stores. The unique feature of a department store is that it sells a wide variety of articles. A trip to any of our larger department stores will show us what a complete line of commodities is offered for sale. It includes men's, ladies', and children's wear, food, books, household needs, furniture, cosmetics, notions, and many other types of merchandise. One of our larger department stores offers about 

250,000 different kinds of commodities. This type of store has become quite popular because customers who wish to purchase articles of different kinds can do so conveniently in one store.

Because department stores are generally large business enterprises, the store is divided into departments or units, each of which sells a special type of article. 

Sales Specialists. We have just described the usual channel by which goods are supplied to satisfy the wants of consumers. This channel, however, is not always suitable for all goods under all circumstances. When conditions arise that prevent the flow of goods in this channel, specialists are called in to keep the goods moving. 

Auctioneers. Emergencies may arise in which a merchant with a stock of goods may need cash at once. The usual method of selling goods takes too much time. A typical case of this kind is the condition of a manufacturer who has a large stock on hand and is faced with the necessity of paying certain bills at once. He cannot realize sufficient

Ch. 5] 137 

money on his stock to pay his bills immediately. He may therefore call on a special salesman, known as an auctioneer. 

The auctioneer advertises the fact that a sale of certain property will be held at a specified place and that the goods will be sold to the highest bidders. Customers who are interested in purchasing bargains will then bid for the various articles offered for sale. The auctioneer, who is licensed by the state to conduct sales of this nature, sells the merchandise to the highest bidders, taking a certain percentage of the sales prices for his services.

Although the manufacturer may lose money in a sale of this kind, he is willing to take the loss in order to obtain ready cash. He is said to liquidate his stock.

Sales Agents. Certain costly products, such as automobiles, cannot be sold in the regular wholesaler-retailer chain because of their bulk and the cost per item. At the same time, the manufacturer does not wish to burden himself with the task of selling his cars to individual purchasers. An automobile manufacturer, for example, instead of opening stores throughout the country himself, enters into arrangements with enterprising businessmen in various communities, who open their own salesrooms and sell the automobiles for the manufacturer directly to the consumer. They arrange all details of the sale, including payments on the installment plan taken over by finance companies, and they take a certain percentage of the sales price for their services. 

Brokers. Let us assume that you are the owner of a building and that you wish to sell it. Inquiry among your friends leads to no customer. Since real estate is not an article that may readily be disposed of in the ordinary manner, it will probably be necessary for you to hire some one who is in business to buy and sell real estate for others. A person so employed is called a broker. Because his clients are people who are interested in property, he is in a position

[Ch. 5 

to know some one who wishes to purchase real estate. For bringing the buyer and the seller together, he gets a fee, which is usually a percentage of the selling price of the real estate involved.

People engaged in this activity, like auctioneers, must also be licensed by the state. Brokers frequently handle other commodities, like stocks, bonds, and insurance. 

Advertising Agencies. Because merchants are always desirous of increasing sales, they may, if they do not have an advertising department of their own, hire an agency to take charge of advertising their products for them. This agency makes known to the public the products sold by the merchants.

Many interesting vocations peculiar to advertising may be carried on in this field of work. Commercial artists are responsible for the pictures and "layouts" of the advertisements used in buses, subways, billboards, and newspapers. 

Research workers and field workers determine where and how advertisements should be placed. Window dressers set up the beautiful displays in the windows of local stores. These and other highly trained workers may be found on the pay roll lists of advertising agencies.

Storehouses. We have just seen how merchants keep goods flowing from manufacturer to consumer. Other circumstances may prevent this flow at its usual speed. Ordinarily a manufacturer produces his merchandise so that it will be ready when it is wanted. Nature determines when some goods, like farm products, are ready for sale. They, too, must be held until there is a market for them. 

It becomes necessary, therefore, to provide a means of holding these goods for a time. As a result, stockyards, warehouses, and cold-storage plants have been erected in this city. These storehouses provide employment for many of our workers.

Ch. 5] 139 

Vocations in Trade. This bird's-eye view of the field of trade may have suggested a number of interesting vocations in this field. However, this field of economic activity is so vast and the vocations are so numerous, that not a few of these opportunities may have escaped us. Let us see what other vocations are available to us while surveying this field from another view.

Vocations in Business

We have traced the life history of commodities from their birth as raw materials to the time when they are made available to consumers as finished products. In our study of occupations in the raw-materials and manufacturing industries we limited ourselves to a study of those workers directly engaged in the task of providing raw materials or of making finished products. In our examination of trade we discussed only the structural setup of that field. 

But there are many other vocations in industry and trade, which we have not yet discovered. To discover them requires a somewhat different analysis. 

The various productive activities that we have studied so far are carried on as business units, which are called concerns, companies, or firms. The owners of these firms are primarily interested in selling their products at a profit. 

Each firm is a commercial enterprise or establishment. 

As a result, each firm requires the services of workers other than those directly concerned with wresting raw materials from nature or with manufacturing the finished product. Whether the firm is a mining company, a clothing company, a wholesaler, a retailer, or an advertising agency,

[Ch. 5 

each has a business "end" or business office that serves as the foundation of the concern. Without it, the mine or the factory would not be operated.

Let us study the organization of the business end of a firm to discover a new field of vocations. Here we shall find the so-called "white-collar workers." Though they may not soil their clothing in a factory or a mine, nevertheless they play an important part in the firm's success. 

Activities and Vocational Opportunities in a Business 

Office. A business office usually offers a variety of occupations. Work of numerous kinds is necessary in order to bring about the main function or purpose of the business. 

The positions available can be classified into several main 

Buying. Everyone is interested in getting the most for his money. Businessmen, in order to make profits, must be able to buy their goods at a low price and then dispose of them at a higher price. It therefore behooves the merchant, whether he is a manufacturer in search of raw materials or a retailer in search of the finished product, to be expert in making a "good buy." The responsibility of buying is usually delegated to a person who is fully acquainted with the nature of the articles to be purchased as well as with market conditions at the time.

The number of buyers employed by a particular merchant will depend on the volume of his business and the variety of articles that he sells. A department store, for example, ordinarily employs one or more buyers for each department.

Buyers who remain on the premises and order merchandise from salesmen coming into the store from other companies are known as resident buyers. The business section of newspapers furnishes a list of buyers who arrive in this city daily.

Ch. 5] 143 

Selling. Merchants offer opportunities in the selling field to those who wish to sell commodities for them. It is a commonly accepted truth that a merchant's business existence is measured by the competency of his sales force. 

1. Travelling Salesmen. Manufacturers who produce articles for sale outside of the city employ salesmen who travel to other cities and states to contact customers. Larger companies that have a nation-wide market divide the country into sections or territories, and organize their sales force with sales managers in charge of the various territories. 

2. House Salesmen. Merchants, particularly retailers, frequently employ salespeople to sell their products in their own establishment. The number of house salesmen that a merchant employs depends on the volume of business done. 

3. Canvassers. Certain firms, such as those dealing in brushes, vacuum cleaners, and other household necessities, employ salespeople to visit from house to house and attempt to sell their products directly to housekeepers. These salespeople are commonly called canvassers, and are most frequently employed on a commission basis. Their earnings depend entirely on the number of articles they sell. 

Communicating with Others. Both the purchase and the sale of goods involve considerable negotiation. Messages must be transmitted from one firm to another. So important is this aspect of business transactions that, we shall find later, a whole group of industries have come into being to cater to this need only. 

In many business offices special workers are employed to take care of the delivery of messages. 

1. The Stenographer. One of the main duties of a stenog rapher is to write in a specialized type of writing, known as shorthand, whatever message or other material the employer wishes to dictate. Hand in hand with this work is that of a

[Ch. 5 

Courtesy Eastern Airlines CLERICAL POSITIONS IN A MODERN OFFICE 

typist, usually the same person, who transcribes the shorthand notes on the typewriter.

In some offices the dictator talks into a special machine known as the Dictaphone, which records his voice. Then the typist listens to the record made and transcribes the recorded statement on the typewriter. In some offices, too, the stenographer takes her shorthand notes on a special machine, called a Stenotype, the keys of which differ from those of an ordinary typewriter. The Stenotype machine prints special letter combinations that must, like shorthand, be transcribed on the typewriter. 

2. The Switchboard Operator. In larger establishments in which there are many telephone trunk lines and connecting telephones, a special employee takes care of all incoming and outgoing calls.

Ch. 5] 145 

Switchboards are of two types, the Monitor and the Plug. 

The method of operating either may be readily learned without much training. The New York Telephone Company maintains a school where training courses in this work are 

Salaries paid are correspondingly lower than those paid to stenographers. In many instances, the work of the stenographer is combined with that of switchboard operator, so that a thoroughly trained stenographer will also have a knowledge of switchboard operating. 

Errand boys and messengers might also be grouped under this branch of business. Their task involves assisting other clerks in the office, delivering interoffice communications, and performing relatively unimportant odd jobs in an office. 

This type of employment should never be considered as a permanent career but as a steppingstone to higher positions in the business office.

Transportation of Goods. The purchase or the sale of goods culminates in the delivery of the goods from the seller to the buyer. Here, again, the task of transporting goods is of such vast import and magnitude that a large number of industries, which we have reserved for later study, cater to this need of business.

Certain members of the business office staff are in charge of this work for their firm. 

1. Shipping Clerk. The shipping clerk's work consists of gathering together the various articles ordered by a customer, wrapping them securely, affixing the address of the customer to the package, and seeing to it that the package is delivered to the express company or post office for shipment.

Since the work involved is not of a highly technical nature and requires but little training, the job does not command a very high wage. It is usually looked upon by

[Ch. 5 

Courtesy R. H. Macy & Co., Inc. 

SHIPPING ROOM OF A LARGE DEPARTMENT STORE 

those engaged in this occupation as a steppingstone to a position higher up in the organization. 

In some firms the work is done by a staff of workers who comprise the shipping department. When more than one person handles this task, it may be broken up into smaller units of work similar to the division of labor employed in manufacturing. The department would then have sorters, packers, addressers, and others. 

2. Receiving Clerk. This employee receives all shipments of goods made to his employer, checks their contents, and compares them with the bill or invoice that accompanies each shipment. He does this to determine whether his firm has actually received the goods charged on the bill. As in

Ch. 5] 147 

the position of shipping clerk, little training is required for the position of receiving clerk, and the salary paid is correspondingly low. In like manner, this work may be handled by a department, in which case the workers would include unpackers, sorters, checkers, and others.

Financial Aspects of Business. Money transactions are the crux of any business structure. Whether an article is manufactured and sold, or merely bought and sold without changing its form, whether an article is food or clothing or stocks or bonds, the relation of the money paid out and the money received will determine whether the business will continue to exist. It is therefore necessary that every business have a competent staff to handle money transactions. 

It might be well to recall at this point that business transactions may be consummated on a cash or a credit basis. If a cash basis is used, payment is made on or before delivery of the article to the purchaser. If credit is allowed, payment is made some time after the goods are delivered to the purchaser. The period before payment may be as short as ten days or as long as three months or more. About 90 per cent of the world's business is transacted on a credit basis. 

The installment plan is a special method of handling credit transactions. Payments are not made in full at the time of the purchase; but they extend over a long period of time, with small payments made at frequent intervals until the entire amount is paid. As a general rule, the article is delivered to the buyer, but the ownership remains with the seller. This is a popular method used in the sale of expensive goods to consumers.

The work in the finance division involves the following basic activities: 

1. Receiving Funds. Some members of the financial staff of a business concern devote themselves to handling payments for the goods sold by their firm. Among these workers

[Ch. 5 

are the cashiers employed almost entirely in retail stores and collectors employed, particularly in installment houses, to make collections from various customers who are required to make weekly, biweekly, or monthly payments. 

2. Disbursing Funds. Each business has certain debts that it must pay, such as wages, bills for raw materials, rent, and other items of expense. This work is generally handled by a comptroller, who may delegate individual jobs, such as wage payments, to clerks. 

Courtesy International Business Machine Corporation INTERNATIONAL ALPHABETICAL ACCOUNTING MACHINE 

3. Keeping Records. All transactions must be faithfully and accurately recorded if the firm is to know how well it stands financially. This work is handled by bookkeepers, who record all purchases, sales, and expenses of the firm. In many

Ch. 5] 149 

instances, those with only high-school training in bookkeeping are qualified for this position. Their work is carefully planned and checked by the accountants and auditors. 

The accountant prepares or opens the books of the firm, and the auditor examines them for errors made by the bookkeeper. In many instances, neither is a regular employee of the firm but is hired at intervals to examine the books of the firm. The profession of certified public accountant will be discussed later.

Filing clerks may be employed to keep various records, letters, and other data of a business in such a manner that each item may be readily found. 

4. Controlling Credit. The credit manager determines by investigation whether a prospective customer who desires credit is sound enough financially to warrant the extension of credit. He may request information from other creditors of the customer, or he may consult credit-rating books, which indicate the amount of credit various businesses can safely be given. An example of a firm that supplies such information and publishes such a book is Dun & Bradstreet. 

5. Stock Control. As we mentioned in Chapter II, mer chants are interested in finding out the condition of their merchandise at any given time. They wish to replenish their stock, if necessary, or to dispose of excess merchandise. For this reason, the positions of stock-control clerk or inventory clerk are available to future workers.

Modern Trends 

The field of trade is daily becoming more and more important. Over 200,000 men and women are employed in this work in New York City alone.

Merchants are demanding better workers in this field. 

Their buyers must be scientifically trained in analyzing products to determine their quality. They must be aware of market conditions so that the merchant will not be over-

[Ch. 5 

charged. They must be able to prophesy the wants of the buying public so that the merchant's store will have in stock those goods which consumers are anxious to buy. They must have good taste and good judgment in purchasing so that the merchant will not have deadwood in his store.

Salespeople must now have additional qualifications. 

They must not only know intimately the products they sell, but they must also possess a high degree of the social graces. 

The merchant sells not only products but also service. His salespeople must give that service. They must be helpful in suggestions to customers, show good taste in dealing with customers, and must be polite at all times. "The customer is always right" is the slogan of modern retail stores. 

Competition for jobs is very keen. Merchants select only applicants with high qualifications. Nearly the entire sales staff of some stores is made up of college graduates.

Ch. 5] 151 

Competition is keen also in jobs of a clerical nature in business offices. Within the last twenty years there has been a tendency for workers to select "white-collar" vocations instead of jobs involving manual labor. This tendency has resulted in an oversupply of clerical workers as compared with manual workers. This has had the effect of making jobs scarce in clerical occupations. 

Another trend worth watching is the increased use of office machines, such as the Stenotype, Comptometer, billing machines, Dictaphone, and others. Those who are headed for general clerical careers might do well to learn to handle these machines as part of their training. 

It is interesting to note that in some branches of clerical work the number of women employed far exceeds the number of men. This is particularly true in the case of stenographers. 

Courtesy International Business Machine Corporation INTERNATIONAL ELECTRIC WRITING MACHINE

[Ch. 5 

Labor unions have never made much headway in the white-collar vocations. This is partly due to the attitude of most white-collar workers who feel that unions are associated with manual workers only. Another reason for the absence of unions in this field arises from the fact that this type of work is general and widespread among thousands of business concerns. The recent depression and the resulting greater activities of unions have made many in this field "union conscious." Only the future can tell how strong unions of these workers will become.
