CHAPTER VI OPPORTUNITIES IN THE SERVICE INDUSTRIES 

We have just completed a study of how material things are produced and made available to us. We observed the many vocations that offer opportunities in performing this task for us.

We shall now turn our attention to other groups of workers who produce services. These services are called intangible goods.

Early economic thinkers refused to recognize these workers as being productive, like workers in the field of trade, because their work does not in most cases produce a material product. Yet without these service workers, our lot would be a sorry one indeed. Not only would our share of material things be considerably reduced, but, in addition, many other wants we possess, such as the want for medical aid, for music, or the human desire to keep in close contact with our loved ones in some distant land, would go unsatisfied. Modern economists all agree that those workers whose products are services are productive as well as those who produce material things. 

Vocations in the Field of Finance

Importance of Financial Institutions. We observed in our study of business that many transactions result in the transfer of money. Sometimes a deal results in the payment of cash. Often a deal is carried on by means of credit or a promise to pay money in the future. Even your purchase of candy or a notebook involves a payment of money. Since the use of money and credit is an important element of our economic system, it is not surprising that separate institu- 154

Ch. 6] 

155 

tions dealing only with money and credit have come into existence. They play an important role in keeping the wheels of industry moving.

History of Financial Institutions. During the early handicraft days banks such as we know them today did not exist. 

When the local craftsmen received a payment of gold, they tried to hide it in a strong box away from prying eyes and hands. Bandits and thieves soon found their hiding place.

The local goldsmith was the only one who provided himself with proper safeguards because of the precious metal with which he worked. Soon other craftsmen brought their gold to him for safekeeping. They paid him well for protecting their gold.

As time went on, the goldsmith discovered that his neighbors generally permitted most of their gold to remain with him for a long time, adding to their store at some times and removing a part of it at other times. 

He soon realized that he might make additional profit if he loaned part of these funds to others. After awhile it became so profitable to lend money that he paid local craftsmen to deposit their funds with him. Out of these simple beginnings arose the modern institution of credit and banking.

Banks. Banks serve the same basic function that was performed by our medieval goldsmith. They provide a place where people may deposit their surplus funds. They serve as a source of loans for people who need money. 

Many types of banks have developed, depending on the type of deposits accepted and the uses to which they put their funds.

The commercial bank is generally regarded as a businessman's bank. It accepts his deposits and permits him to make withdrawals by check immediately afterwards. Because of the fact that its deposits are returnable on demand, this bank

[Ch. 6 

makes loans for short periods. However, it pays no interest on deposits. 

If a businessman needs funds to finance the purchase of goods, he may discount his promissory note, that is, borrow money to pay for these goods. When the goods are sold, he repays the loan and receives back the note he discounted.

In this way commercial banks finance many transactions, from the purchase of raw materials to the sale of finished goods to consumers. They finance transactions whether they are local in nature or even international in scope. The latter type involves imports and exports of goods to and from this country.

The investment bank serves as a source of long-term loans to business firms. When a manufacturing company wishes to enlarge its plant, it realizes that such a debt cannot be repaid in a short time. It issues bonds (long-term promissory notes with the enlarged plant as security). The investment banker underwrites the issue, that is, guarantees a fixed sum to the company. He sells the bonds at a profit. 

The savings bank also serves as a source of long-term loans. These loans, however, are of a limited nature. The funds used for loans are made up of small deposits from men, women, and children who put their money into the bank for future needs (See Chapter XIV). Therefore the State Banking Department limits the bank's loans to safe investments such as government bonds, railroad bonds, electric company bonds, and other public utility bonds. Deposits made in this bank are known as time deposits. This means that the deposits are not withdrawable on demand. Interest is paid on such deposits.

Trust company funds are also a source of long-term loans similar to those of savings banks. These funds are received from people who leave money in their wills to heirs, with the provision that these heirs shall receive the money when they come of age or at some other specified time.

Ch. 6]  

157 

Courtesy Dry Dock Savings Institution DRY DOCK SAVINGS INSTITUTION ABOUT NINETY YEARS AGO AND NOW 

Other institutions that deal with money are insurance companies, which receive premiums on life insurance, accident insurance, etc., and then invest these funds until they must pay the policyholder or his beneficiary; personal finance companies, which loan money to needy individuals at high rates of interest; brokerage concerns, which handle the purchase and sale of stocks and bonds that represent money invested in the business that issued these stocks and bonds. 

Basic Vocational Opportunities. Essentially, financial institutions are special forms of business units. The chief difference between them and other business units is the fact that they deal primarily with money or documents similar to money. As a result, if we attempt to make a study of basic vocational opportunities in the field of financial institutions, we shall find in many instances vocations that are, in the main, similar to those in business. However, we shall

[Ch. 6 

note other vocations that are followed only in the field of banking and finance.

Receiving Funds. The first function of financial institutions is that of receiving funds. These funds may be your deposit in a savings bank, premiums paid to an insurance company, or the deposit of some businessman in a commercial bank.

In some instances an important phase of collecting funds has to be determined in advance. For example, in insurance companies premium rates must first be prepared before insurance policies are issued.

The actuary is an important official and, as such, receives a high salary for his services. His job involves the computation of premium rates that the company charges for its insurance. These rates, however, are subject to approval by the State Insurance Department. 

To illustrate the task of the actuary: the rates that a company will charge for insuring the life of a man 25 years of age and the life of a man of 50, differ; the charges for fire insurance of two or more buildings may vary also. It is up to the actuary to know the risks that the insurance company takes and to determine what charge should be made so that the insurance company will not lose by the transaction. 

To become an actuary, one must pass a series of difficult examinations. If you are not an expert in higher mathematics, you can never become an actuary. 

In general, however, the actual work is done by clerks assigned to this task. In this field most clerks are called tellers. Therefore, the receiving teller, as the name indicates, receives deposits from depositors and records such deposits in the depositors' bank books and in his own records. He also records accruals of interest on bank deposits and any withdrawals made by the depositors in savings banks. In stock

Ch. 6] 159 

brokerage houses the person performing these duties may be called cashier.

Since checks are an important part of the incoming funds, another avenue of employment is open to young people who wish to enter the banking field. The rack or debit boy assorts checks received from depositors according to the various banks on which the checks are drawn. These checks are then sent to the bank's main office, where checks of all the branches are assorted and sent to the clearing house. This work is relatively simple and does not command a high salary. It serves as a steppingstone for those who intend to find their vocation in the field of banking.

Administering the Funds. The most important function of a financial institution lies in the policy and manner in which it uses the funds that it receives. The determination of loans, investments, and premium rates is the very foundation of a financial structure. 

This task is turned over to officials who are highly skilled in handling such matters. In banks, the vice-president, an elected official, is at the head of the investment department, which takes charge of investing the bank funds so that they may be increased in order to be able to make a profit. These investments are made in accordance with various laws of the state or of the National Government. This department staff also passes on business loans to depositors.

After a loan has been approved, the actual work is carried on by clerks. For example, the note teller in a bank receives promissory notes from depositors. These notes may be those of other parties owing money to the depositors and have been placed with the bank for collection. Other notes are made by depositors who wish to borrow money and are left with this teller for discounting.

If the work involves financing a deal with a firm in a foreign country, the foreign exchange teller issues letters of

[Ch. 6 

credit to foreign countries and handles all currency exchanges of the various countries. 

When payments are to be made by an insurance company, other special workers are called upon. For example, if an automobile accident has taken place and the owner is insured, his insurance company will be required to pay damages if the person insured is responsible. Upon report of such accident, the insurance company sends investigators to find out from witnesses and parties to the accident the exact nature of the accident and the damages resulting from it. 

If the insurance company determines that it is responsible for the damages resulting from the accident, it becomes the duty of the adjuster to negotiate with the injured parties and to attempt to settle their claims. Very often lawsuits are avoided by the arrangements made by adjusters. 

Safeguarding Funds. Many banks retain the age-old function of the master goldsmith of handicraft days. They provide a safe place where people may keep valuable papers, jewelry, and other important items without fear of robbery.

All financial institutions have as their "merchandise" a considerable amount of cash, stocks, bonds, and other kinds of valuable documents that may readily be exchanged for cash. They therefore employ a large group of workers to protect these articles from robbery. This task offers employment to private policemen, detectives, armored car operators, and others, whose duty it is to safeguard this peculiar merchandise The Financial Institution as a Business. We have noted that a financial institution performs special functions in our economic system. Although it provides these special services, it is, nevertheless, basically a business unit. As such, it requires a staff of workers similar to that of any other business unit. A review of occupations in a business office would be wise at this point.

Ch. 6] 161 

Courtesy American District Telegraph Co.

SAFEGUARDING PROPERTY 

A watchman, shown in the illustration at the right, registers at intervals, while he tours the plant, by means of an electric signaling device. His signals are reproduced in a central office, where they are checked by clerks. If a signal is not received at the time it is due, a report is given to the police employed by the protection company, who make an investigation.

Financial institutions sell services. They must employ workers who will take care of advertising and selling these services. For example, in brokerage houses, special workers known as customers' men try to persuade investors to maintain active accounts with the brokerage firm they represent. 

As business units, financial houses, too, offer jobs in communicating with others. Stenographers, switchboard operators, errand boys, and others are part of their personnel list. 

Banks employ messengers who deliver checks, cash, and other valuables from one bank to another. Runners are em-

[Ch. 6 

ployed in stock brokerage houses to deliver securities from one firm to another or to the Stock Exchange.

Another instance of a vocation in communication is that of the telephone man employed by stock brokers. His job is to transmit orders from the brokerage house, where customers congregate, to the Stock Exchange, where a representative of the firm places the orders. A telephone man must be extremely rapid and accurate. A mistake of one-eighth of a point in the purchase or sale of stocks may mean a loss of hundreds of dollars.

Vocations in recording business transactions are an important aspect of financial institutions as business concerns. 

Many bookkeepers, accountants, and filing clerks are employed in such businesses.

Modern Trends. The field of finance and banking offers lucrative possibilities to people with intelligence and a willingness to work. The field is rapidly growing larger as trade increases. However, certain changes are taking place, which may prove interesting to future workers in this branch of economic activity.

The Government is exerting more and more control over banking and its related activities. In recent years, banking laws have tended to increase government supervision and care over banks. The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation has been created to protect and insure deposits. The Securities Exchange Commission carefully supervises the activities of brokers and stock exchanges.

As a result, more employees are required in government and financial institutions to prepare and maintain the various records that are required by these government organizations. 

Still another tendency, particularly in banking, is the fact that small banks in suburban communities are rapidly ceasing to exist. Instead, large banks are opening branches in small communities to take the place of individual banks.

Ch. 6] 

163 

This presents interesting possibilities to future workers. 

Formerly, if one had a sizable fortune, he might open a bank of his own. Nowadays the money required to open and operate a bank is staggeringly high. Very few individuals, if any, have the huge amount necessary. 

On the other hand, the advent of branch banking offers opportunities in the upper brackets to workers who might otherwise never have the chance to reach those heights without a large amount of money to back them. Promotion to the position of branch manager is a star to which willing workers may well hitch their wagons. 

As to the clerical jobs in banking, the modern trends that we discussed under general business vocations apply equally here in addition to one other requirement. Because of the peculiar nature of the "merchandise" handled by financial institutions, the quality of honesty is the one most highly regarded by employers in this branch of man's work. Nearly all employees are bonded; that is, they are covered by a form of insurance taken out by the employers against theft or embezzlement by the workers. A bonding company conducts a careful investigation of the worker's life before it grants insurance of this kind. It would be well for workers in this field to avoid even the slightest hint of dishonesty. 

The Communication Industries. 

Man differs from other animals in that he can think and in that he is able to transmit those thoughts to others. 

Nature has gifted man with brains and the ability to speak and to write. The field of communication is that branch of economic activity by which man can transmit these messages.

History. From prehistoric times we find evidence of man's desire to transmit his thoughts. Writings on the walls of caves, the development of an alphabet, the development of

[Ch. 6 

a language, the use of smoke signals and tom toms, all serve to prove that man desired to transmit his thoughts to others close by as well as in more distant reaches. 

Up to about 100 years ago, attempts to communicate with others at a distance by the means available were entirely unsatisfactory. Any attempt at long-distance communications was a slow procedure. In the War of 1812, the Battle of New 

Orleans was fought after peace had been declared because the armies stationed there were unaware that the war had ended. There was a definite need for rapid communication over long distances. 

"Attention, Universe! By Kingdoms, Right Wheel." 

This message, the first to be sent by magnetic telegraph over a long stretch of wire, was tapped out by Samuel F. Morse, the inventor of the telegraph and the code that bears his name.

This peculiar message heralded the approach of a new era in rapid long-distance communication. It symbolizes the extent to which man has progressed in his efforts to communicate with his fellow men. Time and space have been annihilated. Messages from one part of the world can be transmitted almost instantaneously to the rest of the world. 

The far-flung sections of the world are united. 

Importance of Communication Today. We noted in our study of business activity that communication is an important phase in the sale and delivery of goods. If the manufacturer were unable to communicate with the wholesaler, or the wholesaler with the retailer, trade would come to a standstill. If buyers and sellers in different countries were unable to communicate with each other, international trade would cease. Our standard of living would be materially lowered. 

But modern means of communication serve not only the needs of business but also the needs of all of society. Modern communication facilities tend to lessen the distance between

Ch. 6] 

165 

friends and relatives who go to far-off places to live and to work. If you go to a summer camp or to some far-off school to study, it is comforting to know that you can reach the members of your family at a moment's notice. 

Modern Methods of Long-Distance Communication. If we should list the various ways at our disposal for sending messages, we would find that those used most frequently are mail, telephone, telegraph, and radio. So vital is this phase of human activity that separate industrial enterprises have been built up whose only business is to construct, maintain, and operate these connecting links.

Communication by mail is handled directly by our Federal Government. All of the other devices are in the care of private industries, although they are strictly regulated by government authorities.

Basic Vocations in Communication Industries Construction and Maintenance. The modern methods of communication require the use of special equipment and machinery. Telephone and telegraph require miles of wire over which messages are to be sent. Radio requires powerful broadcasting stations, receiving sets, and other carefully constructed apparatus and instruments. 

This need is answered by many workers with varying degrees of mechanical ability. The radio engineer designs and perfects new types of instruments for the construction and improvement of radio broadcasting. This is one of a group of professional workers employed in the field of communication.

Among the examples of skilled workers who handle construction and maintenance work in the telephone company are the lineman and station repairman and the cable splicer. 

Particularly in those parts of our city and state where tele-

[Ch. 6 

phone wires are still above ground, the former has the job of keeping these lines in order and repairing whatever damage has been done to prevent these lines from functioning properly. Since lines are frequently broken during storms, the repairman may be called upon to work out of doors in the most inclement weather. The cable splicer is another outdoor worker who works in the manholes in the streets of the city. 

His job consists of connecting wires underground to central offices and from one office to another. 

Among other skilled employees who work in the telephone exchange are the switchman, who takes charge of many wires that come to the central building, and the power man, who handles the electrical part of telephone communication. 

Semiskilled and unskilled workers are employed in factories that make radio sets, telephone receivers, and other instruments on a large scale. For a more complete discussion of this portion of the communications industry, a review of vocations in large-scale manufacturing will suggest the vocations in this portion of the communications field. 

Operation. The apparatus designed, constructed, and repaired by the group we have just studied would be useless pieces of steel, wood, and wire, were it not for that group of trained workers who operate these devices. 

The operating division of the telephone company is composed entirely of women. This group is charged with the duty of connecting the person at one end of a telephone to the person he wishes to call. About 10,000 women are engaged in this work in New York.

This large staff of workers is organized in much the same fashion as an army, each worker responsible to a superior officer.

In the lower ranks are the operators who make the connection between one telephone receiver and another. Operating work at first appears to be highly involved, but once

Ch. 6] 167 

learned it becomes mechanical. Applicants for this work are paid a small sum while learning. The Telephone Company in this city has its own training school for applicants. 

Operators who show an aptitude for locating numbers are assigned to handle all calls for information. Special classes in this branch of operating are also conducted in the training school of the company. Other operators are trained in the use of the manual and dial systems of telephoning. 

Operators are employed on an eight-hour day. Since the telephone is used every hour of the day and night, operators must be on the job twenty-four hours a day. The day is therefore divided into "tricks" or "tours" with various concessions given to those girls who are employed during the less popular hours. For example, those girls who work evenings work for three hours in the morning and four hours in the evening, making a total of only seven hours a day. 

Girls who, after several years of service, have shown special executive ability as well as proficiency in operation, are promoted to the job of supervisor. This job involves control of about twelve operators. When a difficulty arises between the telephone user and the operator, which the latter is unable to handle, she refers the matter to the supervisor. 

Some supervisors are assigned to the job of instructing in the school, and others "plug in" on certain operators' lines in order to determine whether a particular operator is doing her job properly.

When the nature of a dispute between a telephone user and an operator becomes such that even the supervisor cannot help matters, the user is then referred to the chief operator. The chief operator is in complete charge of all operators in her particular exchange. One such person is assigned to each telephone exchange. There are approximately two hundred in the entire city.

This position is obtained only through promotion from the ranks of operators and supervisors. It requires executive

[Ch. 6 

Courtesy New York Telephone Company SUPERVISORS AND OPERATORS AT WORK IN A TYPICAL CENTRAL OFFICE 

ability of a high degree. The chief operator is correspondingly more highly paid than her subordinates. 

The telegraph company also employs a large staff of workers to operate telegraph instruments. These workers, who are mostly men, work under the same conditions generally so far as hours and "tricks" are concerned. 

Some of these workers are Morse code operators, although newer methods of telegraphic transmission are rapidly displacing the old-fashioned dot and dash method. The teleprinter and the Multiplex or Mux are now used in many telegraph offices. These new transmitting devices are similar to typewriters. For this work proficient typists are used. 

Radio has opened many vocations in the operating branch of communication. Among them are the control room workers in radio broadcasting stations. These workers are highly skilled workers who have had considerable training in en-

Ch. 6] 169 

gineering. In addition to those employed in broadcasting stations are radio operators employed on ships, on airplanes, and elsewhere. These operators must be trained not only to handle radio apparatus, but also to repair it if necessary. 

Other requirements, such as ability to typewrite, are demanded of these workers. All such operators must pass an examination and obtain a license from the Federal Communications Commission. 

Courtesy National Broadcasting Company

RADIO ANNOUNCER WITH CONTROL ROOM IN BACKGROUND 

Those whose work is actually heard on radio programs form a group for special study. These include musical artists, announcers, sound effects experts, writers, and other talent. 

Semiskilled and unskilled workers also are employed in this branch of communications. Messenger boys in the tele-

[Ch. 6 

graph company and page boys and ushers in radio broadcasting stations are representatives of this group. These jobs should not be considered as ultimate goals but merely as steppingstones to more important work in those fields. 

For example, many telegraph operators are former messengers. 

The Business "End" of Communications. We must constantly keep in mind that telephone companies, telegraph companies, and radio companies are business units. As such, they require a complete staff of workers similar to those in other business units. Here, again, opportunities as stenographers, bookkeepers, accountants, and others are present. 

Modern Trends. It is significant for future workers to note that in most instances opportunities for employment in this field of economic activity are limited to a few large companies. Because of the great expense involved in the construction of plants and transmission lines, large amounts of money must be centralized. To duplicate this investment would be wasteful since the same transmission lines can handle all the communication needs of the community. 

They are known, therefore, as natural monopolies. 

The Government therefore restricts the number of such companies in the same section, although it regulates carefully those already in existence. This work is done by the 

Federal Communications Commission. 

As a result, workers cannot find jobs in the same line of work in different companies. A future telephone operator can find employment only in one telephone company in a city. 

It therefore behooves workers to keep in mind that the loss of a job in that company because the worker is unsatisfactory means that the worker has little opportunity for finding em-

Ch. 6] 171 

ployment in that type of work elsewhere, unless it is with a commercial house.

Another fact to remember is that in large organizations opportunities for promotion in charge of others are more readily available than in small business units. We noted before that the chief operator in the telephone company was formerly an ordinary operator. One of the vice-presidents of the New York Telephone Company started as a splicer's helper. David Sarnoff, head of the Radio Corporation of

America, started work as an office boy with the Marconi 

Telegraph Company of New York.

Another trend of large companies in all branches of work, and more particularly in this field, is a changing attitude toward workers. Recreation rooms, profit sharing, and other methods of making workers' lives happier are provided by large organizations. These are more fully described in 

Chapter VIII. 

These large companies maintain laboratories, such as the Bell laboratories of the telephone company, where scientists are employed to find cheaper, more rapid, and more accurate methods of communication. These laboratories are constantly increasing in number and in size. Those with a scientific bent would do well to investigate opportunities in this branch of work.

These laboratories have produced results that affect future workers in the industry. They have perfected laborsaving devices, such as the dial telephone, which has reduced and may ultimately displace the large number of telephone operators needed with the "manual" phone. On the other hand, new jobs are made available. The teletype machine in telegraphy may displace Morse operators entirely but give employment to teletypists. Television is becoming an important field of employment in communications.

[Ch. 6 

 The Field of Transportation 

Importance of Transportation. Hand in hand with the growth of communication has come the rapid increase in the means and speed of transportation. The field of transportation consists of the means of delivering persons and goods from one place to another.

Without speedy means of transportation our lives would be sorry ones indeed. Even in the early stages of man's development, we found men traveling from one place to another in search of food and other goods. Today we may remain at home and receive these goods. Oranges from groves thousands of miles away are served fresh on our breakfast tables. 

Milk from cows a hundred miles away reaches us fresh every hour of the day. Without our rapid means of transportation, the wide variety of foods we enjoy, let alone many other goods, could not be had. 

Should we desire to travel to other places, these same means provide rapid and convenient transportation to our destination.

History of Transportation. The history of transportation is the history of speed, ease, and safety. In ancient times, the marathons of the Greeks and the chariot races of the Romans testified to man's desire for speedy transportation. Today we regard the trip of a few hundred miles as a matter of a few hours' travel. Yet when our country was founded, it was agreed to hold Election Day for president in early November, but that the inauguration would be postponed until the following March. This delay was provided to give the newly elected president time to reach the capital. 

The turning point in man's search is intimately tied up with the Industrial Revolution. We noted in our study of the history of man's work that during medieval times a merchant group arose to provide goods from distant climes. This, in

Ch. 6] 

222111 

ENGLISH STAGECOACH USED ABOUT 1750 

turn, created a demand for more goods. The changes wrought by the Industrial Revolution satisfied this demand. 

Yet there was little use in producing large quantities of goods unless they could be quickly distributed to purchasers. 

Workers of the time found the answer when they applied the same power that turned machinery in manufacturing to provide power in transportation as well. The use of steam and electricity and finally the gasoline engine gave the world modern, speedy, and efficient transportation. Only recently has the field of aviation become an important part of our transportation system.

Methods in Use Today. In addition to methods of loco motion used in the past, including "shanks' mare," beasts of burden, sailing ships, and other similar methods, a list of modern methods would include the large number of different conveyances propelled by some form of modern power. 

Transportation takes place on land, on water, in the air, and underground. The specific transportation industries may thus be classified. On land we use steam and electric railroads, gasoline-driven automobiles, trucks, buses, and electrically driven trolley cars; on water, modern steamships;

[Ch. 6 

underground, subways, pipe lines (transporting oil); in the air, airplanes, dirigibles, and zeppelins. 

Each of these means of transportation requires the services of a large number of workers. We shall attempt to classify the vocations in transportation under a few basic groups. 

Basic Vocations in Transportation Industries 

Construction. The task of making airplanes, automobiles, railroad cars, and other vehicles of transportation, has opened a vast field of employment to many. The automobile industry alone employs thousands of workers. 

The manufacture of these vehicles is generally a largescale enterprise. To discuss this in detail would be to repeat what we learned about vocations in manufacturing. The same principles and the same groupings of workers are found in the manufacture of these products as well. In fact, automobile-manufacturing is one of the finest examples of modern large-scale manufacturing.

Maintenance. The great part of maintaining this equipment is in the hands of skilled mechanics. There are opportunities available as airplane mechanics and such specialized vocations as engine mechanic and instrument mechanic. In shipbuilding and maintenance, as well as in railroad maintenance, similar vocations are found. 

Operation. The operation of vehicles of transportation is a task that requires skill and care. Since the lives of passengers are in the hands of the operator, considerable care and training are necessary. In all cases of vehicle operation, our city, state, or Federal government provides for testing and licensing of operators.

Automobile drivers are licensed by the state government. 

Taxicabs and their operation are carefully supervised by the

Ch. 6] 175 

959 

Courtesy Eastern Air Lines INSTRUMENT BOARD USED BY AN AIRPLANE PILOT 

city police department. Captains and pilots of boats are licensed officers who have passed examinations given by the 

U. S. Steamboat Inspection Service of the Department of 

Commerce. Quartermasters, bow-watchmen, mates, and able seamen are also licensed. Engineers of steamboats receive a careful examination by the same government authority.

[Ch. 6 

In air transportation, too, the pilot, whether he is a regular transport pilot who supervises the operation of a plane in flight, the copilot who assists the transport pilot, a sales pilot who demonstrates and sells airplanes, or a test pilot who tests new and rebuilt planes, requires a transport pilot's license. Such licenses are issued by the Bureau of 

Air Commerce of the United States Department of Commerce after various tests have been passed by the applicant. 

Special Services. Special workers are assigned to the task of caring for the needs of passengers. This includes information men, Pullman porters, conductors, and those similarly employed.

A relatively new and unique type of employment for women is that of air stewardess. The requirements for this occupation are strict, in accordance with the responsibilities placed on her shoulders. She must be an unmarried, registered nurse capable of comforting ailing, frightened passengers en route. A man who does this type of work is known as a flight steward.

Other personal services such as the operation of diners and sleeping quarters for passengers on boat, train, or airplane, will be more carefully discussed in a later section. 

Dispatching. Many of the important vocations in transportation are those dealing with dispatching of traffic. The secret of a successful railroad company aviation line or bus company is to provide trains, planes, or busses when people want them. Schedules must be prepared and strictly adhered to. Busses, trains, and other vehicles must leave on time and arrive at their destination on time. Failure to keep to schedule is disastrous.

The Business "End." As in the case of finance and communications, here, too, we must remember that transportation activities are carried on by business units. As such,

Ch. 6] 177 

these concerns require workers similar to those of any other business concern. In aviation, transportation by air must be sold to a still skeptical but rapidly growing public. In steamboat transportation pursers are employed to take care of the sale of tickets and staterooms and to keep the necessary records; ticket collectors to collect tickets from passengers; freight clerks to bill and keep records of freight shipments. Wireless operators are employed to communicate with other ships and with stations ashore. 

Modern Trends. All of the modern trends referred to in vocations in communication are equally applicable here. In addition, other tendencies of interest to future workers should be noted. 

Courtesy Eastern Air Lines LOADING AIR MAIL AND AIR EXPRESS

[Ch. 6 

In our desire for greater speed we are casting off older methods of transportation for newer and speedier means. 

Railroads are on the decline. Air transportation is on the upswing. Another industry that is fast dying out is that which uses horses as means of locomotion and includes such occupations as the operation of livery stables and the vocations of blacksmith and harness maker.

On the other hand, the use of trucks and buses continues to grow. Opportunities as drivers of buses and trucks are open to those equipped for the task. The necessity for good roads for this type of traffic has given rise to vocations, generally under government supervision, in construction and maintenance of roads, streets, and bridges. The United States can boast of having about 3,065,000 miles of road, or about one-third of the total highway mileage of the world. Also, more than two-thirds of the world's automobiles, equivalent to about 28,520,500 cars, are registered in the United States. 

Recently President Roosevelt expressed his approval of a plan to construct a series of super-highways to connect all 

BURLINGTON 

Hect

Courtesy Burlington Lines MODERN STREAMLINED PASSENGER TRAIN DRIVEN BY A DIESEL ENGINE

Ch. 6] 179 

parts of our country. This project would take many years to complete and employ many workers trained for this work. 

To keep pace with newer methods, railroads are developing new types of trains. Streamlined, lightweight trains equipped with Diesel motors are taking the place of the steam locomotive trains. The Burlington Zephyr typifies this new trend in railroad transportation.

Another interesting development not so pronounced in the communications industries is the growth of unions in the transportation lines. Railroad employees are in the main unionized. Even subway conductors and motormen are being unionized under the Committee for Industrial Organization or the American Federation of Labor. (See Chapter X.) Vocations in Personal Service 

We now come to a study of a group of vocations so unique and so varied that they almost defy classification. They are similar only in that they pertain to certain services for individuals which these individuals do not have the time and, in some cases, the ability to do themselves. The care of the home, the preparation of food, and the keeping of one's appearance in good order are among the tasks assigned to those who specialize in this work. 

History of Vocations in Personal Service. In earlier days. most of these personal services were done by the individual himself. Mothers took care of their homes and the preparation of food. Whatever pains that an individual took with his appearance, he generally performed himself. 

Only in exceptional circumstances did workers perform those tasks for others. The ladies of the court took pride in performing such tasks as combing the queen's hair or acting as her lady-in-waiting. This task was considered to be an honor.

[Ch. 6 

On the other hand, those people who were wealthy hired servants to perform the tasks of cleaning and cooking. These servants were generally poorly paid and, in some instances, were slaves.

Importance of Personal Service Today. Nowadays peo ple in all stations of life require the services of people engaged in personal service. Chief among the reasons to account for this are: 

1. The American woman is rapidly becoming a woman of business. Her time is so taken up with that activity that she has but little time for home duties. As a result, she must resort to living in a hotel in order to obtain the necessary home services such as cleaning, cooking, and laundering. If she has her own home, she must employ outside help to assist her. 

2. America is a nation of travelers. Few of us remain at home all our lives. New York entertains daily many thousands of visitors from different parts of this country and from other countries and must supply visitors with the comforts of home. 

3. Since we no longer work at home, our midday meal at least must be obtained outside the home. Our day at school means a visit to the school lunchroom. 

4. Some personal services require a high degree of skill. 

This includes such work as haircutting. Learning our particular life's work is so taxing that we have little time to learn the skills needed for such personal services. 

5. We are becoming more and more appearance-conscious. 

In early days care of appearance was reserved to those who had time on their hands or who were particularly wealthy. 

Good grooming was associated with wealth or idleness. Today good grooming is as important to a clerk as it is to the owner of a store.

Ch. 6] 

181 

Basic Vocations. Any attempt to classify vocations in personal service could not possibly include all of them. However, we shall attempt to classify these workers on the basis of our needs.

Our Need for Prepared Food. A whole group of business houses cater to our need for prepared foods. Restaurants and hotels are among those which supply us with these goods. In some instances the preparation of menus and the purchase of food are directed by a dietitian who has had wide experience and training in determining the quality of foods and their proper arrangement in a well-balanced meal. 

If we were to enter the kitchen of a restaurant or a hotel, we would find among those employed there the chef, whose chief task is to prepare special dishes. Many chefs have become widely known to the public because of the skill with which they prepare these special dishes. Oscar of the Waldorf is known the world over.

Assisting the chef are cooks who actually prepare the variety of dishes on a menu. Bakers prepare the bread and the rolls. 

Waiters and waitresses serve the patrons. Bus boys clear the tables of soiled dishes, serve water, and generally assist the waiter or waitress in the service.

Keeping House. In some homes a whole staff of servants may be employed. The maid assists the lady of the house with house cleaning. The governess takes care of the children of the family. The butler answers the doorbell, announces callers, and assists the maid in the heavy house cleaning. 

In hotels the task of keeping rooms clean is turned over to the chambermaids, who change the bedding and keep rooms clean. Bellhops carry baggage to rooms and run errands for the guests.

[Ch. 6 

Our Appearance. Several groups of workers perform services to keep us well groomed. The barber shop includes skilled barbers, bootblacks, manicurists. Closely akin to the barber shop is the beauty parlor where milady goes in search of beauty. We find here the hairdresser, the masseuse, the "facial expert," and the manicurist. Various schools in this city offer courses for men and women who are interested in work of this kind.

Other important industries designed to lighten the housewife's burden include power launderies, which wash and iron bed linens, shirts, and other washables. Dry cleaning establishments clean clothing, rugs, ties. 

Modern Trends. The field of personal service is a growing field. More and more are personal tasks turned over to specialists. New ideas to satisfy human wants are constantly being evolved. Recently a novel shopping service was inaugurated. Under this arrangement a person may turn over his shopping list to a special shopper who makes the purchases for him at a slight charge. Other unique services that lighten our work are eagerly awaited. 

In addition, this field of work is rising in its educational level. Schools are conducting courses in this work so that those engaged in personal service vocations are becoming more and more skillful in their work. Nowadays the barber's work is a work of art. New hair designs for women are becoming increasingly important.

Probably the most important feature of this type of work, particularly in this city, is "tipping." A tip is a payment by a customer, in addition to the regular charge for what he has purchased, given to the particular employee who served him. 

The size of the tip varies according to the type of service rendered, the wealth of the customer, and many other factors too numerous to mention.

Ch. 6] 183 

Courtesy Eastern Air Lines FLIGHT STEWARD ON A TRANSCONTINENTAL PLANE PERFORMING SERVICES FOR PASSENGERS 

Much may be said in favor of this practice. Yet there is a growing spirit of rebellion in opposition to this practice. 

Mrs. F. D. Roosevelt recently recommended that a flat service charge of ten per cent be provided on bills instead of promiscuous tipping. Some restaurants forbid tipping. 

Whatever the future may hold, at least at present tipping forms a large part of the particular employee's weekly wages, so much so that wage scales issued by some waiters' unions provide for a wide differential in wages for those restaurants that permit tipping and those that do not. 

One of the factors involved in tipping is the type of service rendered to the customer. It is very important for the person depending on tips for the greater part of his salary to perform

[Ch. 6 

such service with an extremely high degree of courtesy. 

Those pupils who may desire to enter any of the vocations in this group should train themselves in this one elementcourtesy-more than in any other. 

The Professions 

Elsewhere in our analysis of vocations we referred to professions as being vocations on a higher level of educational achievement than all others. We have already made reference to the work of professional people in industry. We group them here for special study because they represent a kind of vocation that differs from all others and therefore requires special consideration. A profession is a vocation that generally has certain definite educational requirements and that requires a license issued by the state in order to permit one to engage in it. This license is granted on the basis of a special examination conducted by the State Department of 

Education or other government agency.

Importance of Professions. Merely to name the profes sions would be sufficient to prove the importance of this group of workers. If you or a loved one is ill, the doctor's coming is awaited feverishly. The nurse carefully observes the doctor's instructions in his absence. Your father calls a lawyer when he receives a summons to appear in court. 

Your mother's foot ills are cured by the ministration of a professional podiatrist. Without the help of these highly trained specialists, death, pain, and mental anguish would often hover about us.

History. The early civilizations considered professions in high repute. Hippocrates, a Greek physician living about 

400 B.C., gave the world a pledge of service to humanity, which is repeated today by graduates of medical schools. 

Roman law is the foundation of law today.

Ch. 6] 185 

The early middle ages are known as the "Dark Ages" because of the scant attention paid to learning and the professions. Surgery was performed by barbers; hence, the symbolic barber pole in use today.

It was not until the Renaissance or rebirth of learning that human minds were once more turned to professional work. It was after the work of Paré in the fifteenth century and of William Harvey in the first half of the seventeenth century that medicine came into its own. 

The Professions Today. All professional workers are constantly at work trying to improve in their work. Today professional people must go through years of arduous study before they are allowed to practice their professions. A world of opportunity in the professional field is open to willing and able future workers. Today the professional fields are held in high regard.

General Considerations. Professions are treated here as a separate group because they involve certain common considerations, which future workers should keep in mind. 

Training. Professional workers require a long and arduous training period after high-school graduation. 

1. College. After graduation from high school with very high marks, pupils must attend college. For most professions a baccalaureate degree is required. A baccalaureate degree is the reward given to students who have satisfactorily completed certain units of work, involving, in most cases, four years at college. Among the degrees commonly given are 

Bachelor of Science, Bachelor of Arts, Bachelor of Business 

Administration, and others. 

2. Professional School. As a general rule, most professions require additional specialized schooling after college graduation. This may be at law school, medical school, or a school

[Ch. 6 

of architecture, for example. After completing such additional work, the student receives another degree, such as 

Doctor of Medicine (M. D.), Bachelor of Laws (LL. B.), or Doctor of Jurisprudence (J. D.). 

3. Apprenticeship. Just as the guilds of handicraft days required a period of apprenticeship as part of the training of craftsmen, so professions today in some cases require a period of training "on the job." 

4. Licensing. Most professional workers are required to pass severe examinations before they are allowed to enter the professions of their choice. This includes the bar examination for lawyers and the so-called "state boards" for medicine and pharmacy.

The state requirements for various professions are subject to change. Be sure to get the most recent requirements from the New York State Department of Education. 

Cost of Training. Besides the arduous training required of professional workers, the cost of this training may compel many to forsake the opportunity of entering one of these vocations. During the preparatory period, not only is the student earning no money, but he is also put to considerable expense to pay for his education. The only profession that one may enter in this city without cost is that of teaching. 

The student may attend one of the free colleges sponsored by the City of New York. The four such colleges are The College of the City of New York, for men, Hunter College, for women, Brooklyn College, for men and women, and the newly organized Queens College.

The "Lean Years." Even after a person has received his license to practice the profession in which he has decided to engage, there is a period of time during which the lawyer is attempting to secure clients, the doctor is trying to convince patients of his ability, and the teacher is seeking appointment, during which time obviously the young professional

Ch. 6] 187 

Courtesy Federal Art Project. Dept. of Health. New York City MODERN OFFICE OF A DENTIST 

man or woman is hard put to earn a living. It is therefore necessary that the aspirant to a profession have the means to carry him through this "lean" period before he begins to earn a living.

General Trends in the Professions. The professions have become overcrowded in recent years. In view of the prestige and the former high monetary returns that were earned by one engaged in a profession, too many people have entered the professions. As a result, we find that monetary returns

[Ch. 6 

in the professions today are not usually the "gold mines" that so many of our less educated believe they are. 

The professions now cover such a wide field of learning that specialization has set in the professions as it has in industry. After securing a background in the general field of a profession, some members turn to specialized fields. In law, lawyers specialize in criminal law, corporation law, or some other phase of law. In medicine we find dermatologists, pediatricians, and others.

New professions are becoming important in modern life. 

Social work, or the investigation and care of less fortunate members of society, is a growing field. The field of vocational guidance continues to increase its membership. Radio, aviation, and television are creating new professions in engineering.

There is a general tendency toward stricter requirements for entrance into the professions. In recent years the educational requirements for entrance to law school have been steadily increased. As late as 1927 no college education was required. In succeeding years pre-law training increased from one to two years. Soon, a college degree may be required. 

Special Trends in the Professions. Certain modern ten dencies in the field of professions apply to specific professions and should be carefully studied by future professional workers.

Medicine. There are several points that a student desirous of entering this vocation must remember. At the present time it is extremely difficult to enter a local medical school. Many boys and girls must therefore travel to other states and even foreign countries in order to get an education in medicine. This necessarily involves a great deal of expense as well as separation from your family. Only those pupils with exceptionally high marks are admitted.

Ch. 6] 

189 

Law. Although it is comparatively easy to enter a law school, as contrasted with a medical school, it has become correspondingly more difficult to pass the examination given by the State Bar Examiners. 

Dentistry. In connection with the practice of dentistry, special attention is called to a special license granted to women. This license is that of dental hygienist. Its requirements are less stringent than those of dentistry itself. This may serve as an opportunity for women who would otherwise be unable to enter this field. 

Pharmacy. Aside from the legal requirements that have been set forth here, the pharmacist of today must be a person of varied abilities. The modern drugstore is no longer a little corner drugstore dispensing patent medicines and compounding prescriptions. It is now a miniature department store selling such items as toilet articles, books, cosmetics, toys, and, in many cases, even food. The pharmacist in such an establishment is not only required to prepare and sell medicines and drugs, but may be called upon to advise Miss 

America in the selection of her perfume and cosmetics.

The hours of employment vary according to the locality of the place of employment. In the business section of the city, pharmacies are open only during the hours of the business day; but in residential sections and amusement centers, they are open longer. As compared with the amount of study required for entrance into this practice, remuneration is relatively small.

Teaching. The teacher is the professional worker with whom you come into daily contact. You are in a position to realize some of the problems related to teaching. As a profession, teaching is overcrowded in New York City. There are many elementary-school teachers waiting for a permanent position. The Teachers' Training Schools for elementary

[Ch. 6 

school teachers in New York City have been abandoned. The high schools offer very little relief to those trained to teach. 

Teachers' examinations are highly competitive. In one recent examination for a high-school teacher's license, over 600 applications were received, of which only 23 candidates received licenses.

Vocations in Civil Service 

In all of our previous studies of vocations, we have discussed them from the point of view of employment in private industry. We shall now consider them from the point of view of employment by the Government. 

Basic Vocations in Government Service. To list the vari ous and many jobs available in public service would be to repeat an almost complete list of jobs available in nearly all industrial activities and professional pursuits and then to add many more. To list the specific requirements and the nature of the work of each would necessitate many volumes. We may, however, group many of these vocations about the basic functions of government. 

Protective Functions. The first and primary purpose of the Government is to protect our citizens and our property. 

This has created the need for policemen, firemen, health inspectors, forest rangers, soldiers, sailors, and others.

Regulative Functions. Our Government regulates the activity of many businesses. For example, it requires the payment of taxes and investigates rates charged by public utilities. This involves work for tax collectors, accountants, engineers, and others.

Operative Functions. The Government is in business itself. Our Federal Government runs the post office system. 

Our city government owns and operates the Independent

Ch. 6] 191 

Subway System. As a business, it requires the many workers we mentioned in our study of the business unit. 

Government Occupations Similar to Private Industry 

Vocations. If we were to discuss opportunities in government service in the way we have classified private industrial activity, we would find, among others: 

Manufacturing: Tennessee Valley Authority (TVA); 

Works Progress Administration (WPA) temporary projects, which include clothing manufacturing, etc. 

Merchandising: Water supply system; publicity bureaus, e.g., Bureau of Milk Publicity. 

Finance and Banking: Treasury department staff; New 

York City Comptroller's office. 

Courtesy Federal Art Project, Dept. of Health, New York City A DEPARTMENT OF HEALTH DOCTOR 

This is one of the many Civil Service positions.

[Ch. 6 

 Transportation and Communication: Independent Sub way; stenographers; typists. 

Professional: Teachers; lawyers; doctors; nurses; etc. 

Special Public Service: Policemen; firemen; food inspectors. 

Election. The important positions in government, such as that of President, Governor, Mayor, Congressman, were and still are filled by popular choice. We shall not discuss the intricate details connected with obtaining one of these higher positions because few of us ever attain those positions. This matter has already been more fully discussed in our study of civics.

Direct Appointment. Formerly, all other jobs, from that of Secretary of State to the lowliest position in our government service, were filled by people appointed by our elected officials. At a certain period of time in our history, it mattered not how well a particular job-seeker could do the work required for the job. The important quality that he had to possess was that he was a member of the particular political party of the appointing elected official. His remaining on the job depended on the whim of the person who appointed him, and then only for as long as that official remained in office. 

It was the belief at that time that all jobs in government other than those filled by election were the prizes of the victorious political party. "To the victor belong the spoils" was the attitude of elected officials. The system of appointment became aptly known as the spoils system. 

Merit System. The spoils system could not long endure. 

Can you imagine the quality of milk that would be fed to the people of our city if our milk inspectors were selected not on the basis of their ability, but according to the political party with which they were affiliated? Can you imagine the attitude toward his job that a worker will take when, no matter

Ch. 6] 193 

how hard he works, he knows that he will undoubtedly lose his job if his sponsor is not re-elected? Government functioning of this kind could only bring about a disastrous effect. The need for improvement became apparent. 

Finally, in 1883, our first Federal Civil Service Law (the Pendleton Act) was passed. Our state and city soon passed similar laws. Additional laws supplementing those first passed have constantly improved the method by which most governmental positions are now filled. 

Most of the jobs in government today are filled by competitive examinations. Once appointed to the job after qualifying in these examinations, jobholders need not depend on the whim or fancy of their supervisors to retain their jobs. Such appointees may be removed only under the most unusual circumstances, such as misconduct in office. 

Our Civil Service Law does not entirely remove the direct appointment method of filling jobs. The higher positions in government, such as those of Secretary of State, Police Commissioner, and others, are still filled by direct appointment. 

Others known as "exempt" positions are also filled in this fashion. 

Finding Out About Jobs in Civil Service. We must remember that we live not under one government, but under three-the Federal, the state, and the city governments. 

Each requires its own staff of workers, many of which are duplicated in the three governments; each requires workers in this city.

Each of our three governments has special agencies that prepare requirements and examinations and promulgate the list of successful candidates from which the various positions are filled. The Federal Civil Service Commission is in charge of this activity for the Federal Government; the New York 

State Civil Service Commission, for the state government; the Municipal Civil Service Commission, for the city govern-

[Ch. 6 

ment. These agencies will willingly supply information of forthcoming tests.

In addition, various newspapers are devoted solely to the publication of news to civil service workers and those who aspire to positions in civil service. Such a newspaper is The Chief.

Civil Service vs. Private Industry. Because so many po sitions in Civil Service are similar to those in private industry, the question may arise as to the relative advantages of employment in either. Among the advantages of civil service employment are: 

1. Fixing of Salaries by Lau. Salaries cannot be lowered unless the legislative branch of government votes on them. 

Some jobs have "mandatory" increases. 

2. Tenure of Office. No civil service worker may be discharged after he has satisfactorily completed his probationary period except for reasonable causes and after he has been tried and found guilty. 

3. Pension. Part of the worker's salary is retained by the government and a fund is created to provide for him after retirement.

Among the disadvantages of civil service employment are: 

1. Inelasticity of Salaries. Salaries do not rise with the cost of living, so that during "good times" when prices are high, the civil service worker is not paid in proportion. 

2. Lack of Working Incentive. Payment is not made for extra effort by those who wish to work harder. The minimum requirements set for the job tend to become the maximum. 

3. Influence of Politics. Political connections still play an important part in getting into the "higher brackets" of government employment.

Modern Trends. There has been a great increase in the scope of government activity within the past few years.

Ch. 6] 195 

Such laws as the Social Security Act and others have increased the number of workers needed in government. In our Federal Government alone, 850,000 workers are employed. 

As a result of the last depression, unemployed workers have sought civil service employment because of tenure of office and the payment of a living wage. Many thousands of applicants apply for each examination offered. Requirements and examinations are becoming more and more severe. 

Another interesting tendency is that of the "career man" in municipal civil service. Mayor LaGuardia has stated that he wishes civil service workers to become more interested in their work and promises to promote them to higher positions. 

To date, only a few workers have been so advanced. Commissioner Valentine of the Police Department, Commissioner 

McElligott of the Fire Department, Commissioner Goodman of the Department of Water Supply, Gas & Electricity, are all examples of men who have risen from the ranks in their own departments.

CHAPTER VII OPPORTUNITIES FOR  Why Study Opportunities for Working in Our Own City. 

Opportunities for working are present everywhere. All over the world, men and women toil constantly in order to provide themselves and others with the goods they want. In France, workers may be observed pressing grapes for wine. In 

Switzerland, skillful watchmakers put together the minute parts of watches and clocks. Even in different parts of our own country many thousands of specialized occupations may 

Although we must not forget the many opportunities available elsewhere, we must realize that most of us will probably seek work near our family and friends. About 95 per cent of us will find employment in the City of New York or in the neighboring cities of New Jersey. We must therefore pay particular attention to vocational opportunities which may be found in our own locality. 

In many towns or cities there is only one industry of importance. For example, in certain New England towns the manufacture of cloth is the only industry. In certain communities in Pennsylvania almost all workers are employed in mines. Work in such towns is limited to employment in the one industry of the town. 

Ours is no mill town or mining town, however. As dwellers in this great city of ours, we are fortunate indeed in having a host of varied opportunities right next door to our homes. 

True, there is but little opportunity for those who seek their fortunes in agriculture or mining. Ours is a city of manufacturing and commerce. Within its boundaries lie oppor- 199

[Ch. 7 

tunities for the many thousands of us who prefer to remain at home instead of going elsewhere in search of employment. 

Exploring Our City. Surprisingly enough, very few of us really know the city in which we live. Pupils living in one part of the city rarely visit other parts. New York City, about 309 square miles in size, comprising 6,000 miles of streets, is a very large community. 

Even among those of us who travel about the city, there are many who have failed to observe the real New York City, an economic world, a place where thousands work at thousands of different occupations. It is in this economic world that we shall find our vocations. Let us examine this world of work as it exists about us. 

Physical Features. Before going off into this new strange land, the economic world, let us, like true explorers or soldiers before advancing, reconnoitre to get the lay of the land. Better still, let us send up a scouting plane to get a bird's-eye view of this strange land to which our parents and older brothers and sisters travel daily. 

Our scout plane returns with an aerial photograph of this great commercial and industrial center. Stripped of its great buildings, which stick up like quills on the back of a porcupine, how is this area really shaped? Any map of this city will disclose certain outstanding physical features. 

We are struck first by the fact that the region is broken up by rivers. Manhattan and Richmond are islands. Brooklyn and Queens are partially separated from each other by the waters of Newtown Creek. The Bronx is separated from 

Manhattan and Queens. Hand in hand with this peculiar characteristic goes a long coastline. Measuring its length along piers and shorelines we find that there are 991 miles of waterfront. We note also that this same coastline is extremely irregular, with little inlets and bays in larger bodies of water.

Ch. 7] 

201 

We notice, also, that this same area is located on the Atlantic Coast. The water in and about this section is relatively deep. 

This area has a mild climate because it is located in the 

North Temperate Zone.

Political Subdivisions. Our map discloses that the city is divided into four geographic regions, but that there are five political divisions. Peculiarly enough, these five divisions are dual in character. We know them as the five boroughs of 

New York City, but at the same time these boroughs serve also as units of the government of the State of New York. 

These units are known as counties. 

Several cities and towns usually comprise one county. 

Our northern neighbor, Westchester County, includes important towns such as New Rochelle, Mt. Vernon, and Pelham Manor. New York City, however, is itself made up of five counties identical with its boroughs. In three cases, the names of the boroughs coincide with the county names; in the remaining two cases, the names are not the same. They are as follows: Bronx. Queens.

New York as a Seaport

Territorial Division of Labor. Not all human beings are equally gifted in being able to perform certain acts; some can do certain things better than others. Industry has found it profitable to take advantage of each individual's talents and to assign a special task for each worker. In the same way, various sections of the country as well as of the whole world are found to be naturally suitable for carrying on particular activities. Oranges, lemons, and other citrus fruits are grown
