REGIONAL PLAN OF NEW YORK AND ITS ENVIRONS ENGINEERING DIVISION

[Ch. 7 

in Florida and California. Detroit and the nearby Michigan cities manufacture most of the automobiles we see in the streets of our city.

Even among nations this rule is true. Brazil is the great coffee-producing region. The Malay States furnish a great deal of the tin for the rest of the world. China and Ceylon specialize in tea-growing. Our own United States leads in the production and refining of oil. 

When human beings are assigned special tasks to perform, we call this arrangement specialization or division of labor. Applied to geographic areas, this same principle is known as territorial or regional division of labor. 

Natural Advantages of New York as a Seaport. New York 

City has been gifted with many natural features, which make its use as a seaport very desirable. On the other hand, these features present problems that cannot be overlooked. 

The fact that the region is broken up by bodies of water serves as an advantage and as a disadvantage. This physical feature permits ships laden with raw materials to come almost to the back doors of our factories and expedites the loading and shipping of our finished products. In addition, it provides us with a long coastline permitting hundreds of ships to dock without difficulty. We have so much waterfront that some parts are but little used. This can be seen from the almost bare appearance of southern Richmond. Even this section, however, may soon be teeming with activity for reasons that we shall discuss later. 

One important disadvantage must be noted. This same peculiar characteristic of the region has made intracity transportation difficult. Expenditures of many millions of dollars have been made to provide connecting links between these parts for the convenience of transportation. Many bridges and tunnels have been and are being built to overcome this obstacle.

Ch. 7] 205 

The irregularity of our coastline makes our port a haven of refuge for ships. Gowanus Bay, which is a small inlet of 

Upper New York Bay, provides ample protection from storms. 

Another feature that fits in admirably with our indented waterways is the depth of the water. Instead of large ships being compelled to unload several miles from shore and transfer their cargoes to smaller boats, which can operate in shallow waters, our deep waters can accommodate the very large transatlantic liners. Docks have been reconstructed to accommodate the Queen Mary and the Normandie, the latest "floating palaces." This, of course, permits speedier and more economical transportation of goods from ship to shore. 

CETTON 

Courtesy Fairchild Aerial Surveys, Inc.

S. S. QUEEN MARY DOCKING IN NEW YORK WITH THE AID OF MANY TUG BOATS 

Location. We are admirably located for commerce. Being almost opposite the great industrial and commercial centers

[Ch. 7 

of Western Europe, our city is the logical beneficiary of the great flow of goods passing from Europe to the United States. 

Likewise, because there are no barriers that prevent the flow of goods to and from the rest of the United States, the land to our west (the hinterland) serves as a source of goods for shipment and as a market for goods shipped. The New York 

State Barge Canal serves as our water link to the west. 

Climate. Ordinary maps indicate that we are almost on a direct line with Europe and that our port seems to be the shortest distance from Europe. This is not entirely true. 

Since the earth is round, the principles of plane geometry do not apply. Actually, the shortest distance would be a great circle. As a result, ships and planes from Europe travel in the direction of Halifax in Canada before steering toward 

New York. 

Why, then, should not Canada be the location of a great port? Here, too, New York has an advantage because its waters are virtually free from ice during the whole year, in contrast to some ports of Canada, which are icebound four months of the year.

New York suffers no extremes of temperature and it is practically free from fog. There is no need to stop work during the midday hours because of the heat of the day, as is the case in many Asiatic ports. In all, New York, because of its moderate climate, offers uninterrupted facilities to importers and exporters and gives service all year round. 

Man-Made Improvements. Man has improved upon 

Nature. Blessed with the natural advantages we have just discussed, New York City has continued to improve the port. For example, it was found that Lower New York Bay was too shallow to permit heavy boats safe passage into the heart of the city. Engineers, divers, and laborers were given the job of remedying this slight defect. As a result, a section

Ch. 7] 207 

of the bay was dredged and deepened. Ambrose Channel was the result.

Under the skillful hands of specially trained harbor pilots, who relieve the captain, and guided by lights and bell buoys, which outline the course of the Channel, even the heaviest of ships can be safely navigated to their docks high on the Hudson (North River) or East Rivers. Various parts of our port waters are being deepened. A survey is in progress at the present time under the direction of the War Department to determine the advisability of deepening the East River from Brooklyn Navy Yard to Long Island Sound and the 

Hudson River to 59th Street.

Another series of improvements has been made in the erection of wharfs and storehouses along the waterfront. 

Electrically operated cranes and derricks load and unload the ships' cargo in record time. Stevedores and other dock hands contribute to the speed and efficiency of this task. Bush 

Terminal is a striking example of modern improvements in handling cargo.

The Free Port. In order to give impetus to shipping, Congress in 1934 enacted the Free Zone Act, or Foreign 

Trade Zones Act. This Act, sponsored by Representative 

Celler of New York, provides that certain designated ports in the United States would be allowed to receive goods from other countries free of tariff duties. This Act, however, does not permit goods to pass into our country's trade free of taxation since such a law would violate the Constitutional provision that tariffs shall be uniform in all parts of the country. Since New York has been designated as a free port, let us see how this law is applied here so that we shall better understand its provisions.

As has been pointed out before, parts of Staten Island are but little used as ports. Ships have preferred to continue deeper into the heart of the City. Stapleton on Staten Island

[Ch. 7 

has recently been designated a "free zone." Around this section a great wall has been built. This wall is a wire fence about 12 feet high, with one guarded opening. Ships may unload their wares within the area that the wall surrounds. 

Adequate facilities for loading, unloading, storing, and manipulating are provided. Owners of cargo may display, repack, assemble, sort, clean, and perform other operations with this merchandise except that they may not manufacture or engage in retail trade except by special permission. If a customer is found for the merchandise, the goods are sent from this point. Before the goods pass the great wall to enter the channels of trade, the tariff is collected. If the goods are not sold to purchasers beyond the wall, they may be sent away from these shores without payment of the tax. Thus, the "cumbersome restrictions necessarily imposed upon goods under customs supervision" are eliminated.

Many advantages have been advanced for this novel device. Among them are: 

1. It will develop Richmond and increase the capacity of our port. 

2. It will encourage foreign shippers to send their goods to New York instead of other ports that do not have this privilege. 

3. It will increase employment for local workers both in commerce and industry. 

4. It will relieve the strain on the concentrated shipping facilities now in use in this city. 

Supervision of the Port of New York. Since this excellent seaport is not entirely within our state, but is also partly included in New Jersey, both states have buried their business jealousies in order to establish the Port of New York Authority. This body exercises supervision over present and future plans for improvements of the Port of New York. Six members from New York and six from New Jersey comprise

Ch. 

209 

COMERT KNE Courtesy Port of New York Authority THE HOME OF THE PORT OF NEW YORK AUTHORITY 

the governing body of the Port Authority. We shall see further manifestation of their great work later as we examine how, in spite of the disadvantage of separated regions caused by our waterways, they have, nevertheless, by bridges and tunnels made our city and its environs one integrated whole. 

All improvements within the boundaries of the city itself are supervised by city agencies, e.g., Triboro Bridge Authority.

[Ch. 7 

Since our port is an important element in our national defense, our Federal Government, through its War Department, also exercises jurisdiction over this vital spot and must approve all improvements recommended for our port.

New York as a City of Industry 

One might think, from reading the foregoing pages, that New York is principally a shipping center and that vocational opportunities lie only in this field. A trip through the city will soon dispel this thought. In fact, in point of number of workers, the shipping industry is dwarfed by the many other industrial activities carried on in this city. 

A Trip Through the City. Let us take an imaginary trip through the city. In this way we can observe the many important industrial activities that offer vocational opportunities to us. Time permits but a hurried trip through the heart of the city, the borough of Manhattan. 

Starting at Battery Park, at the southernmost tip of Manhattan, with the beautiful panorama of New York Harbor behind us, and with Manhattan's beautiful skyline before us, let us wend our way northward along Manhattan's most important thoroughfare, Broadway.

First, we notice the offices of the important steamship lines whose boats dock along the eastern and western sides of Manhattan. Nearly all of the important companies are represented, each inviting potential travelers to avail themselves of the accommodations offered by their transatlantic liners. 

After passing this outer fringe, we find ourselves in the canyon of lower Broadway. Tall buildings on both sides of the street shut off most of the sunlight. We are now in the heart of the city's financial district. Nearly all banks have branches in this area on Broadway, and east and west of Broadway. Insurance companies, stock brokers, lawyers, ac-

Ch. 7] 211 

countants-all maintain offices in this section. One block west behind the famous Trinity Church we find the Curb 

Exchange where stocks and bonds are sold. One block east of Broadway on Wall Street is the more important Stock Exchange, where millions of dollars worth of securities pass from one person to another. Nearby are the Cotton, Coffee, and Produce Exchanges. 

As we veer from east to west along the crowded, narrow streets, we are inclined to stay close together for fear of being lost. We see clearly the result of the lack of city-planning from which this section of the city suffers. 

We travel to the western fringe of this huge labyrinth, and, as we approach the North River, we see sidewalks piled high with fruits and vegetables. Store after store offers these commodities at wholesale and retail. Trucks loaded high with produce come and go. United Fruit liners bring their cargo of bananas and other tropical fruit. Here and there may be seen retail shoppers, men and women, who stop on their way from their offices to pick these choice bargains. 

This is the famous Washington Market. 

We now travel east to the other side of southern Manhattan Island, a comparatively short walk. Not far from our destination our noses can already detect what we shall soon see. Here we find store after store selling all varieties of fish from all parts of the world. Old and much the worse for wear, this dilapidated section will not remain much longer in this condition. Already a huge section fronting the water has collapsed, and we shall shortly see a more sanitary and certainly a more sturdy market take its place. If the day is warm, our curiosity is soon appeased, and we take our leave of the Fulton Fish Market.

These two markets in Manhattan, together with the 

Bronx Terminal Market and the Wallabout Market in Brook lyn, supply most of New York City's perishable food.

[Ch. 7 

Courtesy Federal Art Project, New York City Dept. of Health FULTON FISH MARKET 

Before leaving the Fulton Fish Market, we notice nearby the small landing field for the amphibian plane, which is in service carrying bankers and others from their homes in the suburbs to their offices downtown. 

Returning once more to Broadway, we continue northward through a maze of heavy traffic. This slow progress compels us to make a mental note of this condition for discussion later. As we pass Maiden Lane, we discover many firms dealing in jewelry. Other sections of the city have their own jewelry centers, such as the Jewelry Exchange located at the Bowery and Canal Street, and watchmakers and repairers located along Canal Street at the Manhattan Bridge Plaza. On our way uptown, important retail jewelry stores at Fifth Avenue that we might visit are Tiffany's and Cartier's. Maiden Lane, however, is the city's chief wholesale jewelry center.

Ch. 7] 213 

As we continue up Broadway to Park Place, we pass the Woolworth Building and find ourselves in City Hall Park, the setting for the city's civic center. A cluster of imposing buildings contains the offices of all important governmental departments. Here is the City Hall, in which we find the 

Mayor's offices. Nearby is the City Court; the Hall of 

Records; the Surrogate's Court; and the Municipal Building, which houses nearly all city departments; the new Federal 

Court Building, whose officials formerly had quarters in the former General Post Office; the new State Supreme Court 

Building; the State Building, which contains branches of all of our State departments; and finally, the recently constructed Health Building, which is occupied by the City of 

New York's Departments of Health, Sanitation, and Hos pitals. 

Courtesy Federal Art Project, New York City Dept. of Health HEALTH BUILDING

[Ch. 7 

A somewhat similar grouping of buildings is found at Borough Hall, the civic center of Brooklyn. 

A stone's throw away from the new Health Building and the State Building, along Centre Street, is the forbiddinglooking Tombs Prison, its Bridge of Sighs connecting it with the Criminal Court Building. Not far removed are the offices of the District Attorney of New York, who has the job of prosecuting criminals in the court nearby. 

Let us pause for a moment in our northward trek and turn to the lower East Side of Manhattan. Here, again, are narrow streets crowded with children. On each side are dilapidated tenements with the morning wash flapping from fire escapes. Elevated train structures overhang some of the streets, completely shutting off the precious sunlight. 

A slight improvement has been made in recent years. 

Tenements have been razed, and in their place a few playgrounds offer safety to children. Allen Street has been widened so that the elevated structure above it only partially shuts out the rays of sunshine on the street below. A complete row of homes from Forsyth to Chrystie Streets, and extending from Manhattan Bridge Plaza to Houston Street, has been completely wiped out, giving way to the beautiful Roosevelt Parkway, with wider streets for bridge traffic and tree-fringed walks for the boys and girls and their parents to enjoy. Here and there other tenements have been replaced with new model-housing developments, such as Knickerbocker Village, a huge building covering one square block and housing over 1,500 families.

In addition to retail stores lining the streets of the East Side, we see row upon row of pushcarts along the curb. All kinds of merchandise are sold by the pushcart dealers. 

Here and there may be seen a woman carrying on her head a bundle of partially completed clothing. In many cases old baby carriages have been commandeered to carry the load. Their rusty wheels offer squeaking though sufficient

Ch. 7] 

CATHARINE ST Courtesy Fred F. French Cos. 

NARROW, CROWDED STREET IN EAST SIDE NEW YORK 

testimony to prove that the clothing industry is not rid of the evils of industrial homework, i.e., finishing goods at home instead of in the factories. Too, this scene is evidence of the fact that the clothing industry has for many years localized in this area. Only recently have many of the men's clothing factories moved to Broadway between 4th and 14th 

Streets. 

Leaving the narrow congested streets of the East Side with the prayer that the city will do even more to improve

[Ch. 7 

conditions there, we turn once more to Broadway, to the new center of clothing manufacture. Tall buildings again mark our course. This time, however, the buildings are not office buildings, but the shops and lofts of clothing manufacturers similar to the one we visited in an earlier chapter. 

On our way uptown, we passed an important department store at Broadway and Ninth Street (Astor Place). This store, founded by A. T. Stewart but now bearing the name of Wanamaker, is located at what might be called the southern fringe of the retail shopping area located in the neighborhood of 14th Street and Broadway (Union Square).

Here we find a number of important retail stores specializing in ladies' wear. A little to the west of this section, on Fifth 

Avenue at Fourteenth Street, we find Hearns, another large department store. When this store was first built, this section was considered to be quite far up town and, for many, much too far to travel. Now, however, this locality may be reached in a few minutes from the southern tip of Manhattan. 

Continuing northward to 34th Street, we arrive at the 

Pennsylvania Railroad Terminal. At 42nd Street and Park 

Avenue, several blocks to the northeast is our other important railroad terminal, Grand Central Station. Together these constitute our railroad transportation center from which trains depart for all parts of the country. Together, they provide 

New York with an advantage that has greatly benefited manufacturers. 

In our short trip from Pennsylvania Station to Grand

Central Station, we traversed the heart of New York's retail shopping center, extending from 34th Street to 57th Street, east and west of Fifth Avenue. Most of the important department stores are located within this section. Like the 

Fulton Street and Borough Hall section of Brooklyn, all kinds of goods are offered for sale here at retail. On our journey through Manhattan's shopping center we passed

Ch. 7] 217 

the two tallest buildings in the world, the Empire State Building at 34th Street and Fifth Avenue and the Chrysler 

Building at 42nd Street and Lexington Avenue.

But what lies in the regions east and west of this central area? South of 42nd Street and west of Broadway we see little hand-trucks containing suits and dresses on hangers being rushed from factory to wholesaler. This section is the heart of the clothing section, more particularly women's apparel. Nearby on Seventh Avenue north of 26th Street is the important fur finishing and dyeing center. East of this central shopping area are the important wholesale and retail furniture stores. The Furniture Exchange, which houses the exhibits of many furniture manufacturers, is located at Lexington 

Avenue and 32nd Street.

We must not wander too far from our course. If we could, we would find ourselves at the East and Hudson River waterfronts, where, at 42nd Street, are located the slaughter houses, or abattoirs, which supply our city with meat. 

Stretching from the Battery to 50th Street is dock after dock, where loading and unloading of cargo and passenger ships from all parts of the world greet the eyes. 

If time permitted, we could also visit the important sugar refineries at the waterfronts on the Brooklyn side of the East River, at Edgewater, on the New Jersey side of the Hudson 

River, or at Yonkers in Westchester County.

Included in our waterfront journey would be a visit to the immense oil refineries at Bayonne, New Jersey, and to those located in the Greenpoint section of Brooklyn, on Newtown Creek.

By this time night has fallen. We return to Broadway at 

42nd Street, where we are welcomed with multicolored lights and music. We see thousands of people hurrying on foot and by car to their theatre appointments. Glittering marquees announce the current motion picture or stage shows. We have reached the theatrical center, Times Square. Yielding to the

[Ch. 7 

earnest appeals of our companions and to the raucous shouts of the announcer outside the theatre, we succumb and leave the world of reality we have just explored to enter the realm of imagination.

Advantages of New York City as an Industrial Center. 

After this hurried trip through industrial New York, many questions present themselves. Why have so many businessmen decided to operate in New York City? Will they remain, or are disadvantages driving them away? If there are shortcomings, what remedies can be applied to overcome them?

We must seek the answers to these questions, for within the boundaries of this region lie our own opportunities for employment. If industries move away, fewer jobs are available to residents of this city. 

Let us try to find out what manufacturers want of the locality in which they establish their factories, and then determine whether or not New York satisfies their requirements.

Availability of Capital Funds. The first need of the producer that must be satisfied is that he obtain money to build and operate his factory. The businessman calls these funds capital. Since approximately 90 per cent of the world's business is done on credit, or borrowed money, our producer must have a source from which these funds may be borrowed. 

New York is quite able to take care of this need. Wall 

Street and the rest of the financial district surrounding it house the main offices of the important banking houses of our country. Banks, brokerage houses, insurance companies, and mortgage companies are located throughout the entire metropolitan area. Even railroads whose lines do not enter 

New York maintain offices here, drawn by the magnet of funds available for investments. New York is indeed the meeting place of the financiers of the country.

Ch. 219 

Price of Land. The producer seeks to reduce his expenses to a minimum. He must do so in order to be able to compete with others. The lower his costs, the lower his prices can be. 

New York cannot offer cheap land to a prospective factory builder. Although we read in our history books that the 

Dutch bought the whole of Manhattan Island for about twenty-four dollars' worth of beads and other trinkets, this sum today would not purchase a square foot of land in many parts of the city. This high price of land is due to the fact that so many people wish to operate in New York City. 

Because of its popularity and importance, New York is faced with the serious inconvenience of high land costs. 

This, plus other costs, has caused many producers to move their plants to less populated sections of the country and to forego the other advantages of New York in order to have the comparative advantage of cheaper lands. Condé Nast, publisher of magazines such as Vanity Fair, Vogue, and others, which are sold here in New York, has removed his plant to Greenwich, Connecticut.

Available Labor Force. There are, however, other important advantages that outweigh the high cost of land. 

Our great local population, the cause of high land prices, offers to the producer several important benefits. 

Assuming that our producer has decided to build here and already has the necessary land and capital to build his factory, his next requirement is an adequate number of skilled workers. Our large population offers the producer a great labor market from which he may select the workers he wishes. 

He need offer no special inducements to bring workers to his factory. Contrast the long line of applicants for jobs in this city with the special offers of outside producers who are compelled to erect homes in the smaller towns in order to induce workers to leave the large city.

[Ch. 7 

MAYOR'S ON CITY COMMITTEE PLANNING THE CITY OF NEW YORK SOUTH RICHMOND NON RESIDENTIAL RESIDENTIAL VACANT BLOCK PARKS &CEMETERIES NOIL FOR SOUTH RICHMOND SEE INSERI ABOVE

WPA PROJECT N 65-47-319x Courtesy Regional Plan Association, Inc. 

RESIDENTIAL AREA WHERE NEW YORK'S LABOR FORCE RESIDES 

It should be remembered, however, that there is a "fly in the ointment" regarding this so-called advantage. The cost of living in the city is higher than it is in other sections of the country; therefore the laborer must demand a higher salary. 

As a result of this higher cost of production, many producers have hesitated to open plants in New York City. In fact, the textile industry, which flourished in New England for many years, is moving slowly to the southern states where it hopes to reap the benefit of lower labor costs.

Ch. 7] 221 

Available Market. At the same time, this large population provides a huge market, which purchases the finished products of the manufacturer. Many of the suits manufactured in this city are purchased and worn by our own citizens. 

Probably the only reason that the slaughterhouse industry still occupies an important position in our city, in spite of the fact that the meat-packing center is located at Chicago, is that there is a large, immediate market for meat here. More particularly, because the Hebrew dietary provisions require freshly killed and especially prepared "kosher" meats, our local slaughterhouses find it profitable to continue their stay in New York City.

Not all producers, however, find this a ready market for their goods. For example, agricultural implement manufacturers have found it advisable to move their plants westward in order to be nearer their particular market. Aside from a small group of truck farmers and amateur gardeners, our citizens do not engage in agriculture. 

Transportation Facilities. A producer prefers to be located near his source of raw materials. Meat-packing and slaughtering are centered in Chicago so that the great herds of western cattle need not be transported too far from their grazing land. The boot and shoe industry has been moving farther west because of this reason. St. Louis has gained a considerable portion of the shoe-manufacturing business that formerly was carried on in Haverhill and Brockton, in 

Massachusetts, and Binghamton and Endicott, in New York.

The cheaper textile industry is moving from New England to the South to be nearer to its source of raw material, the raw cotton. 

New York City, unfortunately, does not have many of the raw materials of industry near at hand. 

In addition to the fact that New York City does not have raw materials close by, it cannot consume the quantities of

[Ch. 7 

products that pour out of our factories for countrywide and world-wide consumption. 

Fortunately, modern transportation has offset these shortcomings in some respects. If New York City cannot provide raw materials or a large enough market, at least it makes both easily accessible to producers of finished products. 

Our excellent seaport provides us with excellent transportation facilities. Sugar from Cuba comes to us in a semirefined state; upon leaving our refineries, it is ready for the consumer. 

Many other semifinished products come from all parts of the world to New York City to be prepared for the final consumer. In addition, our seaport is an outlet for finished products to be sent abroad.

Nor has our connection with the hinterland been neglected. New York is exceeded only by Chicago as a transportation center. Building materials such as steel, lumber, brick, and cement are produced outside of the metropolitan area to be used in New York. Machinery is shipped in parts by rail and water to be assembled in New York City. 

Waterways within the city play an important part in transportation. For example, in New York City all of the plants of the Consolidated Edison Company are located near the water. Thus, they are able to receive coal by boat. Both boats and pipelines from Texas bring oil from its source directly to our oil refineries here in the city. Freight trains on boats called lighters convey their cargo from the west to the east bank of the Hudson.

Sources of Power. Since modern production uses power to turn its machinery, our producer naturally seeks a source of such power. At an earlier time in our industrial history this factor generally determined the location of the plant. 

Direct application of falling water or the burning of coal for steam were the only two sources of power. As a result, plants were established near a waterfall or a coal field.

Ch. 7] 223 

Now, however, this reason is fast losing its importance. 

With the use of electrical power, factories are no longer "harnessed" to a waterfall or a coal field.

Electrical energy may be transmitted by means of wires for many miles without serious loss. In fact, man is creating his own waterfalls in order to create electricity. The TVA development is an example of a man-made source of energy. 

However, it is still cheaper for the producer to be close to his source of power. As we noted before, in New York 

City the Consolidated Edison Company has located its plant near water so as to bring in by boat the coal used to make electricity at lower cost. Water transportation, which is cheap, tends to offset the lack of coal or water power around New York City.

Localization of Industry 

We have observed that businesses dealing with the same commodity tended to operate near each other. This we call localization of industry. Instead of fearing the competition of their rivals, businessmen in the same field are usually near one another, for they all wish to enjoy the benefits of a particular locality.

Dependence on Transportation Facilities. Let us exam ine first those industries near the water and near the railroad freight lines. We find that many of those industries deal with bulky raw materials and bulky finished products. Oil-refining, sugar-refining, and meat-packing take advantage of the cheap transportation by water. Furniture manufacturers and wholesalers are located near the Grand Central Railroad Station.

Wholesale food markets remain near terminals for speedy service because they deal with perishable foods. Meatpacking, in addition, is compelled by city ordinance to remain at the edge of our residential areas since from it emanates foul odors and the possibility of disease.

[Ch. 7 

 Importance of an Early Start. Jewelry, a commodity of small size and great value, does not require easy access to cheap transportation. Its localization on Maiden Lane was due entirely to the advantage of an early start, since this area was formerly the center of the city. Also, the localization of the financial center is more or less due to the same reason. We find that the main offices of banking and other financial institutions are located in Manhattan, while branch offices have sprung up all over the city for the convenience of their clients living in other sections of the city. In view of the fact that jewelry dealers are handling a very expensive commodity, their nearness to the financial center provides them with the necessary funds.

Nearness to Labor Supply. We noted that the manufacture of clothing was established formerly on the lower East Side of New York City. This was due to the fact that there the producer found an available supply of the particular kind of labor he needed. Immigrants from Europe usually settled in this area, and rarely ventured into the interior of the country. These people were particularly gifted in the manufacture of clothing, and, as a result, clothing manufacturers established their plants nearby. This is the same reason for the establishment of silk mills at Paterson and Passaic, in 

New Jersey, only a thirty-minute ride from New York.

The clothing producers, of course, realized that in view of the low standard of living to which these immigrants had been accustomed abroad, and which they continued here, they would be satisfied with low wages. This advantage kept the clothing industry in this section for many years, in spite of growing disadvantages to the producer. 

The better skilled clothing worker soon became accustomed to the higher American standard of living and moved to better living quarters, leaving the slums to the poorer groups. The industry was compelled to move farther uptown to be near its source of labor.

Ch. 7] 225 

Nearness to the Market. Another reason for the shift of the clothing industry away from the slum areas was that wholesale buyers from other cities found it distasteful to visit clothing establishments located on the lower East Side. 

The new area to which the industry moved is located near the important means of railroad transportation, bringing buyers to the newer and cleaner showrooms. At the same time these factories are also accessible to workers who have changed their living quarters to better sections of the city. 

The localization of the fur industry near the clothing center is another illustration of the tendency of industry to move close to the market. Manufacturers of women's clothing generally use large quantities of fur for trimming. This, therefore, affords an excellent opportunity for fur dealers, dyers, and others to supply the clothing manufacturers with their product, and is the reason why the fur industry is close to the clothing center.

Retail stores, too, must cater to the convenience of customers. As a result, shopping centers have developed at points most accessible to various types of passenger transportation. Pennsylvania Station, the terminal of the Pennsylvania Railroad, bringing customers from Long Island and from other cities and states, has helped to develop the midtown Manhattan shopping district. All subways have stations at or near this point. Elevated railroad lines, buses, and other means of public transportation convey thousands of potential customers into this area.

In each of the five boroughs, shopping centers have developed where transportation is centralized. Brooklyn's shopping center is located in the Fulton Street-Borough Hall section, in the neighborhood where many subway lines meet. 

It is little wonder that retail merchants are anxious to open stores in these sections, in spite of competition, in order to be near the market.

[Ch. 7 

The theatrical center is admirably located to accommodate the tired shopper who seeks relief in the comparative ease of the soft chairs of a nearby theatre, just as we, after our trip through town, succumbed to the glittering welcome of Times Square.

The Future of New York City 

New York City Is Not Perfect. Considering the many advantages that New York City affords producers, one might think that this city is an ideal place for industry. Yet in 1900, the Port of New York handled 63 per cent of the import trade and 37 per cent of the export trade, whereas twentyfive years later these figures dropped to 47 per cent and 36 per cent respectively. At the same time the removal of industry from New York City has become so serious that some time ago the Comptroller of New York City inaugurated a study to determine the causes for the exodus and to suggest suitable remedies. In various local business papers appear advertisements from other cities inviting New York producers to leave this city. 

Importance of the Problem. Industries are an important source of the funds used to operate our city government. 

These funds are used to pay for our schooling and for other benefits that we enjoy. If industries leave the city, the various government activities will have to be curtailed. 

In Chapter III we referred to the danger to workers of a regional shift of industry. If industries continue to leave New 

York City, many of us will find ourselves unable to find employment in our chosen vocation. 

As future workers and voters, we must become acquainted with the causes of industrial removals. As workers, it becomes a matter of how we shall earn a living. As voters, perhaps we can help find remedies to overcome the shortcomings of New York City as an industrial center.

Ch. 7] 

227 

 Disadvantages of New York City. The many advantages that attract so many people to our city act as a boomerang in creating a problem from which many disadvantages flow. 

These disadvantages are both industrial and social in nature. 

Industrial Inconveniences. The inconveniences to industry are:

High Cost of Land. Although we have seen that economically this drawback has little ill effect, it nevertheless frightens producers away.

Inadequate Transportation Facilities. Transportation fa cilities leading to and from the city, as well as within the city, are still inadequate. Producers still rely, in a large measure, upon antiquated, slow-moving ferries and boats previously described as lighters to convey much of our goods. The excessive overcrowding of our subways is eloquent testimony of the lack of adequate passenger transportation. The loud honking of horns on trucks and other motor vehicles add their bit to prove that our crowded streets do not offer the producer's goods swift passage to their destination. 

Frequent traffic tie-ups on the various bridges about the city are further evidence of the inadequacy of our means of transportation.

This loss of time in the handling of goods is most expensive. For instance, a grapefruit costs one cent in California, and one cent to transport it to the Jersey market, but the New York City housewife is fortunate if she can buy one from the local grocer at the price of eight cents.

According to one traffic expert, we find that national traffic congestion has been estimated by the National Conference on Street Highway Safety to cost the people of the United States more than $2,000,000,000 annually. This is more than $15 a person. Contrast our local traffic and the accommodations provided for it with the open roads of many

[Ch. 7 

communities, and we may safely add a considerable amount to the $15 per person as our local annual share of the loss. 

Multiplying that amount by our huge population, we discover the result to be tremendous-certainly a heavy tribute to pay for congestion.

Labor Problems. Many factories have moved away from this city because of difficulty with labor unions. Mayor La- 

Guardia has recently expressed the desire to make New York 

City a closed shop.

New York State is most progressive in labor legislation. 

As a result, many employers have moved to other states to avoid the strict labor laws that are enforced here. 

This subject deserves more careful treatment. We shall reserve a discussion of the subject for Unit IV. 

Social Inconveniences. From the social rather than the industrial point of view, overcrowding has brought much hardship to many inhabitants of New York City. It is difficult to obtain satisfactory living quarters at reasonable rents near our industrial centers. With overcrowding go insanitary living conditions, which take their toll of life and health among the people of the city. 

The more prosperous workers who leave their squalid surroundings for better homes farther away from their places of work find that transportation facilities are inadequate. After having been compelled to stand to and from work in crowded subway trains and buses, many workers regret having left their less pleasant homes nearer to their places of work. As one stenographer remarked, "I find the trip to and from my office more tiring than the day's work." 

Remedies. Several remedies may be suggested to relieve these unfortunate conditions.

An Improved City Plan. The congestion in various parts of our city is due to improper planning, or more truthfully, to

Ch. 7] 229 

lack of planning. When the city was young, those people who did look to its future development believed that the city would grow along the river fronts instead of through the midtown section. City Hall, almost at the southern tip of Manhattan, was at one time the northernmost section of the city. 

In fact, its north wall was built with a less costly and less decorative brick because "no one will see it anyway." The streets of lower Manhattan have no plan at all. You will remember this from our trip through the financial center. 

Fortunately, the city fathers saw this error of omission in time to correct this difficulty in the newer parts of the city, where the checkerboard or gridiron plan was used in laying out avenues and streets. While this plan is less decorative than the radial plan employed by Major L'Enfant in planning the city of Washington, it nevertheless has an advantage in affording convenience and ease in finding one's way about the city.

Tax-Exemption Plan. Many proposals have been offered and tried to relieve the congestion in specific areas within the city. In order to induce city dwellers to move their residences to less crowded sections, the city offered to exempt property owners in the suburbs from payment of the city's real property tax for a period of ten years. However, the desire to be near the place of work, and also, in many cases, the lack of funds with which to purchase tax-exempt property, kept many of the workers from taking advantage of this opportunity. 

Stagger Plan. Another device used to relieve congested areas is the "stagger plan" recommended by former Police Commissioner Whalen, and put into effect in the theatrical district. Before this plan was put into effect, the curtain rose at 8:30 P. M. in all theaters in the Times Square district of Manhattan, bringing thousands of people out in subway trains, buses, in taxicabs and on foot, all practically at the

[Ch. 7 

same time, thus seriously overtaxing our present means of transportation. When the stagger plan was put into effect, certain shows began at 8:30, some at 8:40, and others at 

9 o'clock. This has relieved congestion in the section, for as the 8:30 audience leaves the street to find seats in the theatre, more room is left in the streets for the 8:40 and 9 o'clock audiences. Congestion is relieved also upon leaving the theatres, for some audiences leave at 11:15, some at 11:30, and others at 11:40.

Industry has adopted this principle to a lesser extent. 

Certain offices in the downtown district have arranged to have their employees arrive and leave at different times. 

Even lunch hours are staggered to prevent the crowding of streets and restaurants. 

Traffic Regulation. Our city officials have tried to improve conditions. For instance, our Police Department endeavors to make the most of existing traffic facilities in speeding up traffic, not, however, at the expense of the safety factor. It has developed various schemes, such as one-way streets, play streets, traffic lights, "no parking" regulations, and "jay-walking" regulations in an effort to help the shipper.

Some drivers look upon these traffic laws as causes for delay and inconvenience, but, in the long run, industry does benefit from them. The rule against parking on certain streets is a good example. You, no doubt, have expressed annoyance when you have driven downtown to the office with your father and he finds it impossible to park his car because someone else was there first or because the police stanchion reads "No Parking Allowed." You, perhaps, felt that you should have the right, as a citizen, to park wherever and whenever you choose.

Legally you have not the right to do so, for our courts, in interpreting our law which is based upon English common

Ch. 7] 231 

Courtesy Dept. of Parks, New York City THE NEW WEST SIDE HIGHWAY IMPROVEMENT SHOWING THE TRAFFIC CIRCLE AT 72ND STREET 

law, have held that a highway is for the movement of traffic and not for the storage of vehicles, that "the King's highway is not to be used as a stable yard." Parking is a privilege and not a right.

That parking slows up traffic is obvious. If a street is wide enough for four lanes of traffic and the two nearest the curb are used for parking, only two lanes, or one-half the capacity, are being used for traffic. 

The use of devices such as one-hour parking limits, or the parking meter as used in Oklahoma City, does not to any extent correct this difficulty. A writer on this subject feels that the only way to correct this abuse is to get the cars off the streets. To accomplish this, he recommends the use of garages or vacant areas for parking space. New York City is planning to adopt the last method to overcome the problem of street parking.

Additional Traffic Facilities. Much has been done to increase the number and capacity of the main city thoroughfares through which flows the bloodstream of the city's industries. Many streets have been widened. New express highways have been built along the west side and the east

[Ch. 7 

side of Manhattan. Modern buses have taken the place of trolleys, thus speeding up traffic across town. 

For years industry has sought better means of connection with the various parts of the city. In addition to the Brooklyn, Manhattan, and Williamsburgh bridges connecting Manhattan with Brooklyn, the Queensborough bridge connecting

Manhattan with Queens, and the few smaller bridges such as the Washington Heights Bridge and MacCombs Dam

Bridge connecting Manhattan with the Bronx, we now have the new Triborough Bridge that links together the three boroughs of Manhattan, Queens, and the Bronx. A vehicular tunnel between Manhattan and Queens is no longer merely a plan on paper, while another huge tunnel under the bay from the Battery to Richmond will soon be a reality. The municipal subway connecting Manhattan, the Bronx, Brooklyn, Courtesy Dept. of Parks, New York City

TRIBOROUGH BRIDGE

and Queens is each day increasing its mileage deeper into the hearts of these boroughs. As a result of improved transportation facilities, Queens, which was once the least populated, has become the most rapidly growing borough of the city. 

Work of the Port Authority. The Port of New York Au thority has been doing its part in the development of the City through the construction of four beautiful new bridges and two tunnels connecting New York with New Jersey. All of this has been done within the last decade. The majestic 

Goethals Bridge and Outerbridge Crossing connect Richmond with Elizabeth and Perth Amboy, New Jersey, respectively, by spanning Arthur Kill. Across Kill Van Kull the impressive Kill Van Kull Bridge connects Richmond and Bayonne, New Jersey, by an arch, which is the largest of its kind in the world. A huge suspension bridge high above the waters of the Hudson River connects upper Manhattan with 

Fort Lee, New Jersey. This is the George Washington Memorial Bridge. 

In addition to these bridges, the Port Authority also controls and operates the Holland Tunnel, built for vehicular traffic only and connecting lower Manhattan with New Jer- 

Courtesy Port of New York Authority OUTERBRIDGE CROSSING

[Ch. 7 

Courtesy The Port of New York Authority INTERIOR VIEW OF LINCOLN TUNNEL 

sey. It accommodates two lanes of traffic in either direction at a speed that permits the passing of over 1,900 vehicles through each tube every hour. It has recently opened the 

Lincoln Vehicular Tunnel, also connecting mid-Manhattan with New Jersey.

The Port Authority has not given all its attention to bridge-building. In order to facilitate the movement of freight through the Port District, the Authority has developed a plan for the establishment of a series of inland freight stations located away from the waterfront and so arranged as to be most convenient to shippers and receivers of freight living farther inland. The first of these stations has already been put into operation. It is located in the Port 

Authority Building at 8th Avenue and 15th Street, Man-

Ch. 7] 

235 

hattan, thus serving the largest package freight section in the world. The Pennsylvania Railroad, the New York Central Railroad, the Lehigh Valley Railroad, the Erie Railroad, the Baltimore and Ohio Railroad, the Delaware, Lackawanna and Western Railroad, the New York, New Haven and Hart ford Railroad, and the Central Railroad of New Jersey have all agreed to use this terminal as a freight station. Merchants shipping or receiving "small package" merchandise have opened shipping and delivery departments in the Port Authority Building. In this way local merchants can save trucking expenses.

These and many other projects are attempting heroically to keep pace with the growing needs of our city's industries. 

Slum Clearance. In trying to improve industrial conditions, the city has not entirely ignored its social problem arising from overcrowding in many sections. With better subway transportation has come a gradual exodus of workers from the congested areas to those less settled. The State 

Housing Board, with the financial assistance of the Federal Government, is engaged in the clearance of many slum areas and the building of modern apartment houses. Most of these new apartment houses are rented to families in the lowincome brackets. A housing development in the thinly settled 

Hillside section of the Bronx has attracted many tenants. 

For those workers who find it necessary to remain downtown, Knickerbocker Village offers up-to-date housing instead of the insanitary and unsafe conditions that had to be endured in the buildings that formerly occupied the site. 

The conditions in the slum area of Williamsburgh in Brooklyn and of the Harlem section in Manhattan have been vastly improved. New state and city parks and playground areas have been placed at the disposal of all citizens, thus affording more opportunities for recreation and improved health. 

The zoning laws have helped to make the city a better place in which to live and thus a better place for business.

[Ch. 7 

Courtesy Fred F. French Cos. 

KNICKERBOCKER VILLAGE, A RECENT SLUM CLEARANCE PROJECT 

Finally, in line with these efforts to improve our city, is a provision in New York City's new charter, which has led to the creation of the Mayor's Committee on City Planning. 

Planning for the Future. An important project is being carried on for the future citizens of our city. It includes in its area of operation all territory within a fifty-mile radius from City Hall, the center. This work was begun in 1921 by a group of well-known public citizens who formed the Committee on Regional Planning of New York and its Environs. 

After a careful study, this committee published its findings and made recommendations in a series of eight volumes in 1929. This material was turned over to the Regional Plan 

Association, another association that is working for the adoption of this plan. About 50 per cent of its proposals have already been put into effect. 

While it would be almost impossible in this discussion to describe minutely the various parts of the plan, about 500 in

Ch. 7] 237 

number, a summary of its principal proposals will help us to understand its work. It includes: 

1. A regional highway system to relieve present congestion and to provide adequate facilities for the future. 

2. A regional railroad and suburban rapid transit system co-ordinated with the existing rail and harbor facilities. This will open large areas for new development. 

3. A regional airport system of 46 airports, about half of which are already in existence. 

4. A park and parkway system for recreational purposes easily accessible from all parts of the city. 

5. A regional system of land uses. This plan provides for certain areas to be used for specific purposes, e. g., manufacturing, residential, etc. It will bring the worker closer to his work and at the same time provide him with far more recreational comforts. 

A closer study of this plan will prove highly profitable to those of us who wish to know what the future holds for our city.

Conclusion. An attempt has been made in the foregoing pages to describe briefly the plan or apparent lack of plan of the little world in which most of us shall find employment when our period of formal schooling is over. To those of us who seek guidance in their choice of vocation has been presented a picture of industrial New York. 

As citizens of the City of New York, we should be proud of the industrial greatness of our city. We should recognize, however, the importance of continuing the good work on the part of those who are charged not only with making our city a better place in which to work, but also the best place in the world in which to live.

UNIT IV THE WORKER'S SEARCH FOR ECONOMIC SECURITY INTRODUCTION 

One might think that our economic citizen, having selected wisely from the desirable vocations which make up our economic system, would need but to train for his chosen vocation and express his willingness to work hard. Economic society should then stand ready to welcome him with open arms and to provide him with the goods he needs to live in return for his services. This situation would be ideal. 

Unfortunately, however, this is not always the case. If today we were to examine one hundred people aged 25, we know from statistics that in thirty years out of the one hundred examined, twenty would be dead and eighty alive. 

Only fifty of the eighty living would be financially independent, while the other thirty would be partly or wholly dependent on others for economic aid of some sort. 

Thousands of men and women are now unemployed even though they are both able and willing to work. Thousands of others are working for a wage far below that which is necessary to provide them with the goods they need. Others are enfeebled and old after years of hard work and find themselves without funds, vainly trying to support themselves although they can work no longer. Some workers and employers are in bitter disagreement as to wages, hours of work, and union membership.

As future workers, we must have information concerning these difficult conditions before we go into the economic world. As American citizens, we must assume an even 243

[Ch. 7 

graver task. In this capacity, we are charged with the duty of trying to help remedy the situation and to help plan means by which such calamities can be avoided in the future. 

Critics have arisen who scoff at our present economic system. Some of them seek to arouse the people in order to achieve certain personal gain. Others honestly recommend drastic changes that would upset all of the benefits which human beings have gained under our present democratic system.

Citizens of a number of other countries have sought a remedy for economic ills by overthrowing their governments and setting up new ones. In some cases they have given up all of the advantages enjoyed under their former type of government, particularly that of individual freedom, in order to overcome certain disadvantages. 

The American way is different. Though we, too, desire to destroy the disadvantages of our present economic system, we are unwilling to give up its advantages and the freedom that our government gives to us. Our way is to analyze the difficulties that arise and systematically to seek remedies within the framework of our own political and economic system. We must critically consider recommendations made for improvement. Like good doctors, we do not wish to effect a cure by killing the patient.

Let us consider the problems that have arisen in our economic system and then examine the remedies attempted both by workers and by our government.

CHAPTER VIII  Meaning of Economic Security. When we discussed the various motives for working (Chapter I), high on our list was the motive desire for gain. Nature provides us with an abundance of a few things, such as the air we breathe and the water in our streams and lakes, without work on our part. These we call free goods. However, other goods that we need in order to live, for example, our food, clothing, and shelter, have required considerable human effort to produce. 

They are relatively scarce and are called economic goods. 

We must work to earn enough money to purchase these economic goods. Most of us recognize this rule and try to live according to it.

Unfortunately, the ability to work and the desire to work do not guarantee that a person possessing these qualities will always be able to obtain the goods that he needs in order to live. Many obstacles stand in the way of the willing worker. These obstacles are the key to the problem of economic security. Economic security is the condition in which human beings are free from worry over opportunities to earn a living.

History of the Problem of Economic Security. The search for economic security dates back to time immemorial. 

Primitive man was seeking this goal as he gradually changed from hunting and fishing to domesticating animals. 

The problem, however, has taken on greater significance since the time of the Industrial Revolution. Until the period of the Industrial Revolution most men were employers as well as employees. Agriculture was the predominant industry and "every rood of ground maintained its man." The 245

[Ch. 8 

worker owned his tools, his sheep, his wagon, and his home with its accompanying farm. Cloth for clothing was made at home, hence, the name homespun. The man sheared the sheep, his wife carded the wool, and his unmarried daughters (spinsters) did the spinning. The weaving was done on crude hand looms. Unemployment existed, but it was not characteristic of this earlier system. The farmer, when weather conditions did not permit working in the fields, was kept busy building and repairing wagons and barns. He also did his own tanning of leather to make saddles and bridles.

The advent of the modern industrial system changed all this. The worker came to live in cities. No longer did he own his own little farm from which he could obtain at least the agricultural products to provide him with food and clothing. One group, known as capitalists, owned the means of production (tools, implements, and raw materials), while the other group furnished only their labor. Thus began the separation of the industrial worker from food and materials of his own production, and the distinction between employer and employee.

To achieve economic security, the worker now must seek and find employment in factories or businesses owned by other people. Then, having obtained a job, he must make a wage sufficient to permit him to buy from others the goods that he needs. He is no longer self-sufficient. Unemployment

One of the chief obstacles to economic security is the inability of the worker to find employment. Many thousands of willing and able men and women walk the streets daily seeking jobs. "No Help Wanted" signs appear on many factory doors and shop windows. This first obstacle must be surmounted before economic security can be achieved.

Ch. 8] 

247 

History of the Problem. Before a doctor attempts to treat a patient, he usually prepares a "case history," that is, he lists all the available information in regard to his patients' health. In a similar scientific spirit we, too, should become acquainted with the "case history" of unemployment. 

Unemployment is not a very recent problem, although it has become extremely severe within the past few years. 

In 1873, for example, there were about 3,000,000 unemployed out of a population of 30,000,000. During the so-called prosperous years, 1922-1927, Dr. Leo Wolman estimated that 1,500,000 were constantly unemployed, this figure rising to not less than 2,315,000 in 1927. The peak of industrial and agricultural unemployment listed by the American Federation of Labor was 13,698,000 in March, 1933. In 1937 

Congress authorized a census to be taken of the unemployed. 

MARCH 

1929 

MARCH 

1931 

MARCH

Estimates have varied between 8,000,000 and 10,000,000 as the unemployed portion of our total population. 

Causes of Unemployment and Their Remedies 

The next step taken by a doctor in his effort to advise his patient is to seek the cause or causes of the ailment he hopes to cure. It is important for him to determine the exact cause, because he cannot recommend an appropriate remedy if he fails to find the source of the trouble that he must treat.

Should we examine carefully the case history of unemployment, we would find that a number of different causes present themselves. For each cause, certain remedies can be suggested that need not result in an overthrow of our government or of our present economic system. 

Personal Causes. In our case history we have noticed that even during prosperous times a large number of workers are unemployed. Many of these workers are without jobs because of personal reasons. Some are old, crippled, sick, or otherwise unable to work. Others are temporarily out of work because they cannot find a job, although jobs are available. Still others, like the hobo, the drifter, or the loafer, are unemployed because they do not want to work. 

Suggested Remedies. The number of persons out of work because of personal reasons is growing smaller. In the first place, the state and the individual himself have cooperated to improve the health of the worker. Epidemics in the United States have been virtually wiped out. Sanitary conditions and safety devices exist in most factories today, and education has taught the laborer to take better care of his mind and body.

When a man has been permanently injured so that he cannot perform his accustomed work, means are now pro-

Ch. 8] 249 

vided whereby he may learn a new type of work that he can perform, even though he is no longer completely able-bodied. 

Learning a new type of work is known as vocational rehabilitation.

To help the unemployed worker find a job, an organization known as the International Labor Organization, with branches in many important cities of the world, has been working on plans that will lessen unemployment due to these causes. As part of the New York State Unemployment 

Insurance Plan (see Chapter IX), unemployment offices have been set up in various parts of this city to help workers find jobs. For those who can but do not wish to work, economic society has nothing but contempt. 

Seasonal Unemployment. We may notice also in our case history that within any one year the number of the unemployed rises and falls at certain times within that period.

Many workers engaged in certain vocations are unable to find employment during certain times of the year and therefore swell unemployment lists temporarily. Bricklayers, textile workers, stevedores, sailors, and many farm laborers seldom, if ever, have work all year round.

In the women's clothing industry the peak of employment occurs during the months of January, February, March, April, September, and October. During the other six months of the year many of the workers engaged in this line of work are unemployed.

Disgruntled workers blame the employer for these layoffs. But this evil often results from conditions beyond the employer's power to control.

A great deal of seasonal unemployment is directly caused by the irregular purchases and buying habits of the consuming public. For example, many consumers buy more things

[Ch. 8 

than usual around Christmas time. Fashion changes sometimes prevent purchasers from buying goods until the last minute. The ladies' millinery industry awaits Dame Fashion's word before it offers its new line for the season. White shoes are purchased only in the late spring and early summer. 

Overcoats are bought in October and November. 

Many of these purchases are made at about the same time because of weather changes. In turn, weather conditions may be a primary cause of seasonal unemployment in other lines. Employment in agriculture is based to a very great extent on weather conditions. This, in turn, affects the production and sale of agricultural implements. Outside 

SEASONAL EMPLOYMENT 

Cutting rubber bathing suits is a type of employment that is highly seasonal in nature.

Ch. 8] 251 

workers in the building industry are unable to work during cold or rainy weather. The demand for ice cream decreases in winter, and the demand for stoves and coal decreases in the summer. As a result, workers in the corresponding industries may be employed only part of the year. 

Suggested Remedies. Some seasonal unemployment cannot be avoided. Stevedores cannot load ships when bad weather conditions prevent navigation. Farmers cannot change the weather. They cannot employ as many workers during the winter months as they can in harvest time. However, some adjustments can be made. 

1. Spreading out the Work. In such industries as the making of boots and shoes, clothing, straw hats, and the like, there is no inherent reason why the work should not be evenly distributed through the year. Many business firms have worked out a production plan that gives employees year-round employment. Meat-packing, for example, is a seasonal industry that generally gives employment chiefly at those times when the farmer sells his cattle and hogs. 

Yet at least one company has worked out an arrangement with its employees that assures them of fifty-two equal salary checks. 

2. Dovetailing Industries. In meat-packing, year-round employment may be provided in subsidiary industries manufacturing by-products. For example, a worker employed in the slaughter house during the winter season may be given employment in the candle-making department of the same enterprise during another season. This same plan can be applied in other industries too.

Technological Unemployment. Among the unemployed are many who have found themselves jobless because of the invention of a machine that was able to do their work more speedily and, in some cases, more expertly. Employers in

[Ch. 8 

search of cheaper methods of producing their goods have, in many cases, substituted machines for the men whom they formerly employed. Steam shovels have reduced the number of common laborers needed, while the talking picture has thrown many musicians on the unemployment list. Because of the improved methods of manufacturing glass, skilled glassblowers now belong to an obsolete vocation. 

Most workers look with fear to the invention of a machine that is likely to displace them. A life-time of training for a vocation may become worthless under such circumstances. Are their fears for the future groundless?

Technological unemployment is not new. The invention of the printing press deprived the medieval scrivener of his occupation, that of transcribing in longhand copy after copy of the few books of the time. 

Nor has technological change reached an end. In 1886 

Carrol Wright, Chief of the Bureau of Standards, thought advances in technical fields had reached the point of saturation. But invention keeps right on. Charles F. Kettering, Director of Research for General Motors, prophesies that the future holds many new inventions in store for us. Technological unemployment has become semipermanent. 

Suggested Remedies. In earlier times when workers found themselves deprived of their jobs because of the invention of machinery, they became enraged. In France, workers threw newly invented machinery into the Seine. 

Even today many still proclaim that method as the only way to "give work back to the workers." 

We who are about to enter as workers in the economic system might well pause to consider whether the destruction of machinery is the most desirable method of solving the problem of technological unemployment. In view of the benefits that have been derived from the introduction of

Ch. 8] 253 

machinery (see Chapter IV), it would be sheer folly to follow the example of the eighteenth-century worker. 

Yet the problem of technological unemployment cannot be so easily laid aside. No matter how public spirited a worker may be, no matter how appreciative he may be of the benefits of a machine age, a displaced worker's lot is not a happy one. A new occupation, no matter how simple, cannot be learned in a day. What can be done to provide that worker with economic security until he finds another job? 

1. Careful Observation of Changes. In their report to the 

President of the United States in July, 1937, the National Resources Committee stated that it was possible to forecast future technological displacements. Armed with such information, future workers can be on the lookout for such changes and avoid the vocations that are threatened. 

2. Controlled Introduction of Machinery. The introduc tion of new labor-saving machinery may be controlled by the manufacturers or government agencies so that only a small number of workers will be displaced at any one time. 

This small group will have a better opportunity of obtaining employment elsewhere than if a large number of workers were displaced at the same time. For example, the Rust brothers, inventors of a mechanical cotton-picker, proposed a plan to avoid the difficulty that would result if cottonpicking were mechanized too rapidly. In this way it was hoped that those who were accustomed to cotton-picking could, in small groups, make satisfactory adjustments in other employments. 

3. Retraining. Most important of all remedies for technological unemployment is to train and provide the displaced worker with other employment. In the example of the mechanical cotton picker, some workers might be trained in the operation of the machine, others might be employed

[Ch. 8 

in the manufacture of the machine, and still others might be given instruction in other lines of work where their abilities can be used.

Cyclical Unemployment. The most serious cause of unemployment comes as the result of changes that occur at certain intervals in business conditions. Every few years business conditions change for the better or the worse. 

Starting at any point in business conditions, we can trace a movement that runs up and down until it once more reaches the original level at which we started our study. This rhythmic rise and fall is called the business cycle. 

For example, in recent years, beginning with 1926 and lasting until about 1930, business enjoyed good health and many people were employed. This period was a period of prosperity. A rapid decline in business then followed, with an accompanying reduction in the number of people employed. This period was described as the crisis. From that time to about 1933, business was in a period of depression. 

During this period, business was almost at a standstill. Many millions of people were without jobs and many employers lost money. After 1933 business began to improve. This phase is called a period of recovery. Should recovery continue, we shall once more reach a point in the cycle known as prosperity.

The conditions of this last decade are descriptive of similar movements of business in the past. The business depressions of 1819, 1837, 1857, 1873, and 1893 were also accompanied by severe unemployment. This type of unemployment is known as cyclical unemployment. 

Suggested Remedies. Since this type of unemployment is the result of business changes, business itself must be examined and cured if the evil of cyclical unemployment is to be removed. Many economists have, therefore, been
