[Ch. 8 

working diligently to discover the causes of business cycles. 

If business itself can be prevented from passing through critical periods and can thus be stabilized, the problem of cyclical unemployment will be solved. 

On the other hand, some economists maintain that business cycles are inevitable. They argue that business cycles are the price we must pay for our efforts to raise our standard of living. 

If this is true, we must seek means that will lessen the undesirable effects of a depression on the working population. 

1. Public Works and Prosperity Reserves. It has been recommended that the Government put aside a certain proportion of its tax receipts during good years, thus creating prosperity reserves to be spent during lean years. The construction of public buildings, roads, dams for irrigation projects, and similar government works might be postponed in periods of prosperity and undertaken in periods of depression. The prosperity reserves could then be used to provide work of permanent value at a time when workers would otherwise be out of work. 

Our Government has in the past several years spent millions of dollars in the construction of public works as well as in various other projects. The Civilian Conservation 

Corps (CCC), the Works Progress Administration (WPA), and others are illustrations of the methods by which Government funds have been used during the period of business depression.

The plan of accumulating prosperity reserves to be spent in periods of unemployment is theoretically desirable but is rather difficult to administer. Many people, including both employers and employees, will wish a public building program started whenever there is the slightest danger of a business recession, and will urge the indefinite continuance

Ch. 8] 257 

1900 

GOVERNMENT EXPENDITURES 

1937 $1 

INCREASED ELEVEN TIMES 

$$$$$$$$$$$ 

Courtesy National Association of Manufacturers. 

A large portion of these expenditures has been used for work relief. of the building of public works long after a period of depression has disappeared. The so-called prosperity reserves may then be used up and huge debts accumulated. The rising debt of the Federal Government during the past years has been looked upon with fear by many citizens. Borrowed money must be paid back sometime, and only a relatively few projects, like the Holland Tunnel, pay for themselves. 

2. Spreading Out the Work. Another recommendation offered to lessen unemployment is to spread out among many workers the work that remains to be done. This, in part, means raising the minimum age for working and shortening the hours of work for adults. The late National Industrial 

Recovery Act (NIRA) was an attempt in this direction. 

There is no doubt that such adjustments will improve the lot of the adult as well as that of the child. It is probably better to have two men working at $15 a week than one man at $30 a week. It would be best, however, to employ both men at $30 a week. If a reduction from a 54-hour to a 

40-hour week is accompanied by a corresponding cut in wages, the worker is very little better off. 

3. Unemployment Insurance. A modern businessman may, if he wishes, shift the risk of destruction of his property by fire or other calamity. He does this by paying annually a certain sum, known as a premium, to an insurance company,

[Ch. 8 

which agrees to reimburse him under certain conditions if his property is destroyed or damaged. Likewise, a man may insure his life so that in the event of his death his dependents will receive a sum of money. This same idea has, within the last few years, been transferred to the situation of a man and his job. In other words, if a man should lose his job through no fault of his own, and if he has insured himself against this loss, he likewise will be reimbursed. 

The first question that arises in this connection is, Who will pay the premium-the employers, workers, or the Government? If the Government provides the funds with which unemployed workers are paid, there is no insurance but merely a dole or charity. Employers maintain that since the workers alone benefit by such payments, the workers should pay the premium. On the other hand, many workers insist that losing their jobs is the fault of the employer and that the latter should pay the bill. They argue further that if employers are compelled to pay workers even if they are discharged, employers are less likely to discharge their help. 

This and other questions have been finally decided upon by our own government in the Unemployment Insurance Law, which became effective on January 1, 1938. We shall discuss its provisions in greater detail in Chapter IX. 

It should be noted, however, that this is not the only unemployment insurance plan in existence today. England, Germany, France, Italy, Austria, Sweden, and other countries have established some form of unemployment insurance. In some countries the plan is voluntary on the part of certain trades; in others, the law is mandatory. 

Factors in Economic Security While Working 

Let us assume that the problem of unemployment has been satisfactorily solved by one or more of the methods we have indicated. Has economic security been attained? Unfortunately, there remains much more to be done before

Ch. 8] 259 

workers can achieve that goal. Other obstacles remain in the way.

Health and Safety. One obstacle to economic security is the danger of being compelled to leave one's work because of illness or injury arising out of the work. As a result, it has become necessary to provide various health and safety devices to prevent sickness or injury among workers. 

These improvements have been helpful both to workers and employers. To workers the various devices have proved beneficial in that the number of cases of injury and disease has diminished considerably, The employer benefits in that the efficiency of the worker has been increased so that maximum production can be carried on. 

For those unintelligent employers who feel that costs of maintaining health and safety equipment are a needless expense, our Government has passed legislation to compel the installation of these devices.

Responsibility for avoiding accidents does not rest solely with the employers. Workers can aid in protecting themselves and their fellow workers. Strange as it may seem, many workers refuse to avail themselves of modern safeguards and conveniences that are provided by employers. 

Some workers, for example, refuse to wear safety goggles although their work requires this precaution. Others violate "no smoking" rules that are set up for the protection of themselves and their fellow workers. Many industrial accidents could be avoided if workers were careful and obeyed the rules that have been established for their protection. 

Hours of Labor. We noticed in an earlier discussion that the use of labor-saving machinery has shortened the day's work. Within the last 100 years, hours of work have constantly been decreased. The normal working day in manufacturing establishments has shrunk from 11.4 hours in 1840 to 8 hours in 1930.

[Ch. 8 

At the present time there is a movement afoot to reduce still further the daily working hours to 6 hours. The chief arguments presented for this additional reduction are: 

1. Labor, with the aid of machinery, has become so productive that it is entitled to this further reduction. 

2. Shorter hours would increase employment in general and would thus increase the purchasing power of the workers and the prosperity of business. 

On the other hand, some employers argue: 

1. Shorter hours would increase pay rolls, and this would result in an increase in the cost of the product. This, in turn, would reduce the demand for the product and lessen employment. 

2. Industry has not yet reached a point where further reduction in hours would be granted without resulting in a lessening of production. This would mean a lowering of our standard of living.

For reasons of public welfare, our Government has carefully regulated the hours of work in special instances only. 

In Chapter IX we shall deal with these special cases. 

Wages. A much more important factor in economic security while working is the problem of the wages that the worker should receive for his work. Being employed even at shorter hours is no guarantee of economic security if the worker cannot purchase the goods he needs because his wage is inadequate.

If we are to reach a sound conclusion concerning the problem of wages, various features of the wage system must be clearly understood.

Real Wages vs. Nominal Wages. John Kelly worked as a laborer in Philadelphia during the Centennial Exposition in 1876. He worked ten hours a day and his pay was three

Ch. 8] 261 

200 

FACTORY WORKER'S WAGES AVERAGE ANNUAL WAGES 

100 1899 1909 1929 

COST OF LIVING INCREASE FASTER THAN THE COST OF LIVING 

200 100 

Courtesy National Association of Manufacturers 

According to these figures, how have real wages been affected? dollars for that time. Today an office boy receives fifteen dollars a week for a forty-four hour week. But let us look at another side of the picture. Kelly could buy an evening meal consisting of beef stew, plenty of bread and butter, a piece of pie, and a large cup of coffee for ten cents. The same meal today would cost three or four times as much as it did sixty years ago. During the Civil War men in the slaughter house were receiving sixty dollars a week. But tea was selling for $1.40 a pound, and the costs of other products were also high. In 1929 ordinary laborers were receiving a dollar an hour for doing odd jobs around the house and garden. The housewife, however, felt lucky if she could purchase a steak for the evening meal for less than two dollars. Steak was selling at sixty-five to seventy cents a pound. Under the circumstances, in which period were the workers better off?

[Ch. 8 

We are interested in the number of dollars in our pay envelope. This actual amount of money is known as our nominal wages. Much more important, however, is the extent to which goods can be purchased with these dollars. 

The amount of goods and services that can be bought with the amount of money received represent our real wages. This means that our nominal wages represent dollars, but that our real wages represent the purchasing power of those dollars. If an increase in the worker's pay envelope is followed by an increase in prices of merchandise, his real wages have not increased although his nominal wages have. It is necessary to understand this difference at the outset if one wishes to realize the problem of the worker's wages. 

Methods of Wage Payments. Still another feature of the wage system must be analyzed if we are to understand the wage problem. This feature involves the methods by which workers are paid for their work. Let us examine a few actual cases.

Time and Piece- Work Wages. A special policeman at 

Ebbets Field or the Polo Grounds receives three dollars a day; and the President of the Baltimore and Ohio Railroad, $50,000 a year. A teller in a bank earns from $25 to $35 a week. When you go to the doctor, you generally pay him $2 for the visit. A cigar maker is paid one dollar for every hundred cigars he rolls; and a lather, a fixed sum for every lathe. The United Parcel Corporation has paid its drivers $40 a week plus one cent a parcel for every parcel delivered after the first one hundred and eighty. Lawyers frequently demand $100 for a day in court or a definite fee for trying a case.

Thus we can see that the worker may be paid for the time he spends, time wages, or for the quantity of work done, piece wages. In the United Parcel Corporation case we have a combination of time and piece wages.

Ch. 8]  

263 

Sliding Scale. Jimmy Foxx, the leading home run hitter in 1933, was offered a salary for 1934 to be based on the 1934 paid attendance at Shibe Park, Philadelphia. Foxx refused on the ground that his salary would not reach $25,000 unless a million fans went through the turnstile that year. In coalmining the wages paid are based on the retail price of a ton of coal. This method of payment is called the sliding scale method and depends in most part on the volume of sales of the employer. In many of the chain stores the manager receives a weekly salary of from $25 to $40, supplemented by 1 per cent or 2 per cent of the volume of business exceeding $500 or $1,000 a week.

Profit Sharing. Akin to the sliding scale method of wage payment is the system known as profit sharing. 

The American Telephone and Telegraph Company has allowed its employees to purchase stock in the company at a price well below that on the stock exchange. Thus the worker adds to his regular wage by sharing in the profits. 

As a result, the worker is stimulated to work harder. In 

1934 the baseball club of one of our large cities devised a novel plan for allowing the players an opportunity to add to their yearly income. After all expenses had been paid, the profits over a fixed amount were to be divided among the players. 

Task and Bonus System. Frederick C. Taylor, the father of the Taylor Society, tried to help the lot of the workman in a scientific way. He made careful studies of many jobs and carefully analyzed the results. These studies are known as time and motion studies.

After the task is reduced to the shortest time according to these studies, the worker is paid a fixed amount for the standard job. If he is able to complete the specified job in less than standard time, he is given a bonus. The task and

[Ch. 8 

bonus system was publicized recently as the Bedaux Hour 

Although we consider this as another method of wage payment, the Taylor idea is, strictly speaking, a system of business management rather than of wage payment. 

Welfare Work. Some corporations provide free medical service, recreation rooms, excursions, and other conveniences for the benefit of their workers. The Metropolitan Life 

Insurance Company maintains a sanitorium for its employees who have become sick or run down in health. Many firms maintain restaurants where the worker may get his meals free. This, of course, increases the worker's real wage because he may use the money that he ordinarily would spend for his lunch to purchase other goods. 

Courtesy New York Telephone Company EMPLOYEES' REST ROOM 

Criticism of These Methods. Welfare work, as well as profit sharing, does not appeal to many men and women on the job. They look upon these innovations as an excuse for paying less than the customary wage. An hour for lunch is

Ch. 8] 265 

often preferred to a free meal with only a half-hour's time to consume it.

Another objection to some of these methods of payment (particularly the profit sharing and the sliding scale) is the uncertainty of the return. In only rare instances can the worker help himself. The amount he receives depends on the ability of the employer in selling the goods. 

The worker can help himself when he is hired on a piecewage basis. The better worker can earn more money. Unfortunately the employer has in some instances counteracted the ability of the better workers by "nibbling at the piece" or by employing a pace-setter. In the latter situation the owner uses the efficient worker as a means of overdriving or underpaying the ordinary worker. This is the objection leveled against the task and bonus system too. Workers also object to this method on the ground that it is too machine-like and impersonal. Increased production is discouraged by lowering the rate per piece. This practice is known as nibbling at the piece. Piece wages are not possible where processes and jobs cannot be definitely separated and assigned to one individual.

Because of these objections, most labor unions are opposed to piece-work payments particularly and prefer timework payments to all others. 

Minimum Wages. Whatever the method of payment, the worker's chief concern is to obtain enough funds to provide himself with economic security. During periods when work is scare and workers are plentiful, employers pay low wages to the workers they do employ. Women and children particularly receive low wages. Many workers feel that the 

Government should take a hand and compel employers to pay at least enough so that workers may be self-supporting. 

A minimum-wage law would prescribe the lowest rate of wages an employer might pay.

[Ch. 8 

Even if the Government should be willing to pass such legislation, its task would be a difficult one. 

In establishing this rate, the aim is to pay a living wage. 

However, it is very difficult to define a living wage. Does a living wage include ownership of a home or an automobile? 

Does it imply money in the bank? 

Another more difficult problem virtually links the minimum wage to the unemployment problem. A factory owner may be compelled to pay a minimum wage, but he cannot be compelled to employ more workers than he needs. It seems impracticable to compel the payment of a minimum wage to every Tom, Dick, and Harry who applies, irrespective of their numbers and their varying abilities. Labor unions object to minimum-wage laws, for they say the "minimum tends to become the maximum." We shall see in Chapter IX how our Government has tried to solve this problem.

Special Care for Special Groups 

So far we have discussed mainly the problems of ablebodied, willing workers in search of economic security. Other groups, however, such as women, children, and the aged, need special consideration. They, too, require food, clothing, and shelter. However, in their search for economic security, they find even greater obstacles. In some cases they interfere with able-bodied workers. In other cases they are imposed upon by unscrupulous employers.

History of the Problem. The problem of women, children, and the aged is not a new one. For example, people today speak of the number of women working as if this were a recent situation. Women have always worked, in many instances harder than the men. Even in earlier days, they did all the cooking, baking, and spinning; helped with the milking and the feeding of poultry; and performed

Ch. 8] 267 

numerous other odd jobs as well. In truth, "man worked from sun to sun, but woman's work was never done."

In like manner, children, too, worked hard. They aided their parents by performing daily chores about the home and in the fields. If orphaned, they were turned over to orphanages that forced them to do strenuous work in return for their keep. 

DECLINE IN CHILD LABOR 

1900 

18.2% OF AGE GROUP 

1930 4.7%

AGE TEN TO FIFTEEN YEARS Courtesy National Association of Manufacturers 

As to the very old, their treatment depended in most instances on the civilization in which they lived. In one country, it is said, it was customary to kill aged people to avoid supporting them. On the other hand, in China, even today, age has always been venerated. As a result, considerable care is given to the aged.

It should be noted, however, that before the Industrial Revolution workers continued to perform their duties until

[Ch. 8 

they were quite old or died. The old-age problem was quite different from that of today. 70%

AGRICULTURE 

炊​烛​烛 ​郏 ​TION 10%

MANUFACTURING COMMUNICATION, TRANSPORTATION, ETC. 

DOMESTIC SERVICE PROFESSIONAL JOBS DIS CHILD LABOR Courtesy National Association of Manufacturers Women and Children in Industry. The Industrial Rev olution did not create but rather intensified the unfortunate circumstances of these groups.

In many instances it was discovered that machinery could be equally well tended by women and young children as by adult and able-bodied male workers. As a result, many men were discharged and their jobs given to women and children.

This was done because women and children could be paid lower wages than those demanded by adult male workers. Even today, many women will work for lower wages because they feel that their working days are limited. They will, therefore, accept almost any wages that are offered.

Ch. 8] 269 

In addition to the fact that women and young children deprived able-bodied adult male workers of their employment, other serious problems presented themselves. While it is true that women had always worked, the new type of work in which they became engaged meant backbreaking toil in a factory instead of the work they had performed in their own kitchens with no one to give them orders. Children, likewise, were compelled to work many hours of the day in sunless factories, away from parental care. Whatever education they might have received at home was denied to them. 

They were withdrawn from school and sent to work to provide funds for the family. Long hours and working conditions of the time were dangerous to their health. They could not even enjoy the benefits of an elementary school education. 

Soon it was seen that women and children comprised a group that needed special provision made for them if they were to escape exploitation by unscrupulous employers. 

Our New York State Labor Law makes special provision for these two groups, as we shall see in Chapter IX.

Problem of the Aged. Modern methods of living and working have made a number of important changes in the problem of the care of the aged. Medical progress has given us a longer life span. In earlier days the group of aged no longer able to work was quite small because death took a great number in the group. Today, although a man has outlived his usefulness as a worker, he may live for many more years. The average age of the population is higher. 

Another reason for the increase in the number of the aged has been a change in our point of view of how old a man must be to be considered useless for work. Because industrial processes have been speeded up, man's life span as a worker, that is, his economic life span, has been cut short. Many of our pioneers were seventy years old or more when they accomplished their great feats of strength and

[Ch. 8 

endurance as they struggled through our western wilderness. 

Today, however, there is a tendency to discharge older workers and hire younger men.

Studies show the existence of unofficial age limits for hiring new men in many industries, especially in large firms. 

In unskilled work, rarely will a worker be employed if he is over 40, almost never if he is past 45. In the skilled lines, a person 50 years of age would find it a difficult task to obtain new employment if he was discharged from his present job. 

What shall be done for this ever-increasing group of citizens who have apparently outlived their usefulness in industry? Should they be accorded the treatment of ancient times and be destroyed?

Organized society today has taken upon itself the burden of caring for these dependents. The workers of the community have come to realize that they, too, may soon be members of this group. They realize that "There, but for the Grace of God, go I."

Many employers, too, have long realized the problem of providing for this group. Private pension systems have been devised in many firms. Government employees have long been under a pension system. To take care of the rest of the group not so fortunately situated, our Goverment has set up a plan of caring for them also. We shall discover the working of this system in Chapter IX. 

Summary 

We have examined some of the problems that beset us in our search for economic security. Opportunities for employment, a living wage, the protection of children and women workers, health and safety protection for all workers, old-age security and pension, all go hand in hand to give

Ch. 8] 271 

our citizens economic security. Should these factors be disturbed, economic security would be lost. Let us see how our Government has attempted to solve the obstacles that stand in the way of economic security.

CHAPTER IX HOW THE  

Early Attitude of Governments. In the year 1776, Adam 

Smith, the father of economics, wrote his book The Wealth of Nations. In it he advocated a policy of laissez faire, which, freely translated, means "hands off"; that is, the government should have as little as possible to do with business affairs and should not interfere in any way with economic activity. 

This, of course, included any governmental attempt to make laws concerning labor.

Lest Adam Smith be classed as an enemy of labor, let us hasten to see why he adopted this attitude. 

1. He felt that "every individual is continually exerting himself to find out the most advantageous employment . . . 

But his own advantage leads him to prefer that employment which is most advantageous to society . . . Every individual can in his own local situation judge much better than any statesman or lawyer can for him."

According to this theory of self-interest and laissez faire, the worker who did his own job well would benefit not only himself but the whole community as well. 

2. This was the spirit of the time in which Smith wrote. 

Americans were seeking their liberty from Great Britain. 

Indeed, a few years later when our Constitution was created, no reference was made to labor regulation. 

3. At the time that this theory was advanced, as you will recall from your study of the history of man's work, there was no serious labor problem. The Industrial Revolution had not yet had an opportunity to bring about the evils that later befell labor. 273

[Ch. 9 

 Changing Attitude. As the Industrial Revolution pro gressed, its evil consequences became the concern of our lawmakers. As time went on, the Government began to regulate conditions in business. Within the past few years, more and more has the Government attempted to give workers their just due. Organized labor has applied considerable pressure, with the result that a series of laws have been passed to help workers in their search for economic security. In fact, the Government not only has regulated business conditions but also has gone into business itself. 

Our Dual Government. Before attempting to make a study of laws passed for the benefit of workers, it might be well for us to review the nature of our government. In the 

United States each citizen comes under the jurisdiction of two distinct and separate governments, i. e., the Federal 

Government at Washington and the state government (in our case, the New York State government, whose capital is located at Albany). We must first understand the relationship of the Federal Government to the government of the individual states before we can analyze any laws passed by either.

If you wish to mail a letter, you must place a United States stamp on the envelope. When you travel by train from New York to Chicago, the rate per mile that you pay is determined by the Interstate Commerce Commission, a 

Federal agency. The nickel, dime, or quarter that you spend at the corner store was coined at a United States mint. On the other hand, the amount of your gas and electric bill, the age at which you may leave school, and your fare on the trolley car or bus are determined by the laws of the state in which you reside. These differences exist because of the federal form of our government. 

State governments existed before the present United States Constitution was adopted. When the Constitution

Ch. 9] 

275 

was adopted, the various states surrendered some of their powers to the Federal Government. These powers, including the power to coin money, establish post offices, and regulate commerce with foreign nations and among our own states, are called granted or delegated powers. State governments did not yield all of their powers to the Federal Government, however. Some powers they denied even to themselves. The powers that were not granted to the Federal Government or denied to the states were retained by the states. From this, we say that the powers of the Federal Government are granted or delegated, while the powers of the individual states are inherent or retained. All delegated and denied powers are listed in our United States Constitution. 

Before Congress or the State Legislature may pass laws on any subject, the power to do so must be found in a Constitutional provision. If either the State or the Federal 

Government encroaches upon the powers of the other, the guardians of the Constitution, the United States Supreme Court, will declare such a law unconstitutional or null and void. The only way by which these powers may be shifted is by an amendment to the Constitution.

If we examine the Constitution, we find that the power to regulate labor is neither delegated to Congress nor denied to the states. As a result, we must conclude that the power to regulate labor has been retained by the individual states. 

Congress therefore has no power to regulate labor directly. It is for this reason that attempts have been made to amend the Constitution so as to give this power to our Federal Government. The states, on the other hand, desire to keep as much power for themselves as they can. A recently proposed amendment to the Constitution to permit Congress to regulate child labor has failed to receive the ratification of the necessary thirty-six states. The NIRA 

was declared unconstitutional because it sought to regulate intrastate labor.

[Ch. 9 

 FEDERAL POWERS STATE POWERS 

The shaded area is called the "twilight zone.'

THE DUAL POWERS OF OUR GOVERNMENT 

The Federal Government has taken advantage of other powers that it does possess to pass laws indirectly affecting labor. 

1. By its power to regulate commerce among the several states, it passed the Adamson Act. This act regulated the hours and wages of railway workers. The working conditions of sailors were regulated under the La Follette Seaman's Act. Other laws the constitutionality of which is doubtful have been passed under this power. 

2. Under the Sherman Anti-Trust Act, a combination in restraint of trade in commerce between the states was illegal. 

In Danbury, Connecticut, the union hat workers went on strike. The Supreme Court decided that, as a result of certain of its activities in connection with the strike, the union was acting as a combination in restraint of trade. The 

Federal Government passed the Clayton Act to remedy this situation. A clause in this law states that labor could not be considered a commodity, and did not come within the provisions of the Sherman Act.

Ch. 9] 277 

3. By its power to control immigration, Congress excludes Chinese laborers or contract labor of any kind. Alien labor agitators may be deported for good and sufficient reasons. 

4. The Federal Government can exercise a great influence on the nation's labor force by its attitude toward civil servants. The number of Federal employees has been constantly increasing, the Home Loan Mortgage Corporation alone adding eighty thousand to the pay roll. 

Although Congress has attempted in many ways to regulate labor, it must be remembered that the power to regulate labor within the state is a power retained by the state. We must turn, in most part, to our state laws to see how our government has helped the worker in his search for economic security.

Among the earlier state laws still in effect today are the 

New York State Labor Law and the New York State Workmen's Compensation Law. The first regulates conditions under which we work. The second provides for payment to workers injured while working. Let us examine the first to see what industrial evils that law attempts to overcome. 

New York State Labor Law Children in Industry 

Child Labor. Under this law no child under 16 years of age may be gainfully employed except in rare instances. In order properly to understand this provision, you must also understand that our Compulsory Education Law provides that you must attend day school until you reach the age of 

18 years unless you have been graduated from high school.

To permit children between the ages of 16 and 18 to work and at the same time obey the Compulsory Education Law, it is provided that children between those ages may work and attend a part-time (continuation) school (not night school) for at least four hours a week.

[Ch. 9 

Before this may be done, the pupil must, however, obtain a Standard Employment Certificate. This certificate will be issued only after the boy or girl (1) has a written promise of a job, (2) can supply adequate proof of age, (3) has passed a physical examination conducted by doctors from the Department of Health, (4) can obtain the written consent of a parent or guardian who must in person make the request for this certificate and for the release of school records, and (5) can pass a literacy test showing that he can read and write English. 

Exceptions. Certain exceptions are made. If you are a high school graduate, you may work even at the age of 14 years. However, even a high school graduate under 18 years should have an employment certificate. 

Any child over the age of 14 years may be employed when school is not in session, that is, after school hours or during vacations. This worker, however, must have a vacation work permit, which is issued in the same manner as

was outlined for the Standard Employment Certificate, except that no literacy test or schooling record is required. 

However, this type of certificate does not permit employment in factories. 

Another type of certificate that may be issued is known as a Special Employment Certificate. This is issued to a child over 15 years who is found to be "incapable of profiting by further instruction available." Again the same requirements must be met as in the case of the Standard Employment Certificate except that a schooling record is not required. But here, too, the child may not work in a factory. 

Restrictions on Employment. In addition to the restrictions placed on employment under the Vacation Work Permit and the Special Employment Certificate, children between the ages of 14 and 16 years are limited in the type of work in which they may engage, even though they have an employment certificate. Children between those ages may not work with dangerous materials. This, of course, excludes them from working in chemical factories or match factories. 

One may readily see the danger to other workers, as well as to children, if chemicals are carelessly handled. Children between those ages may not operate dangerous machinery, such as circular saws or elevators. Children between those ages may not work at night.

The Labor Law provides definite hours between which a child may be employed. These hours are as follows:

[Ch. 9 

A 48-hour week is prescribed as a maximum for boys and girls between 16 and 18 years of age. New York State has passed a law forbidding the sale of goods made by child labor. 

This has been done to overcome the competition from other states that do not have child labor laws and thus permit employers to hire children, pay low wages, and reduce the selling price of their merchandise. 

Women in Industry 

Another group of workers whose health, safety, and morals are safeguarded are young girls and women. In addition to the age requirements that affect boys and girls, our lawmakers have seen fit to make additional age requirements for girls in industry. No girl under the age of 16 years may work at a job that requires constant standing. No girl under 

18 years may work as an elevator operator; no girl who has such a job may work for more than 48 hours a week. No girl under the age of 21 years may work as a messenger; no girl who is a messenger may work for more than 48 hours a week. No girl under the age of 18 years may be employed selling newspapers or shining shoes. No girl or woman, with but few exceptions, may be employed after 10 P. M.

Health and Safety of All Workers 

The Labor Law provides that the condition of the place of work shall not be injurious to the worker's health or safety. The place of work must be kept clean at all times, and the floors must be swept at regular intervals. The law provides for proper ventilation, lighting, and heating in all factories. A sufficient number of washrooms must be available for the use of workers.

Fire-prevention devices must be installed. Buildings must be of fireproof construction. Fire extinguishers must be available, and workers must be trained in fire drills in order to avoid injury or loss of life in case of fire. In order

Ch. 9] 281 

to prevent the collapse of a factory building, the building must be properly constructed and approved by the State Department of Labor. The number of workers who may occupy a certain floor space is also provided for in the law. 

All dangerous machinery must be safeguarded and well lighted. First-aid equipment must be placed so that it will 

Courtesy New York Department of Labor FACTORY HOSPITAL ROOM 

This room contains first-aid equipment, as required by law. be easily found in case of emergency. A recent amendment provides that safety equipment is required for window cleaners in all buildings. When work is to be done at home, that is, industrial homework, a license must first be obtained. 

Although there is no law covering the maximum hours of work for adult males, provisions are made for various cases. 

Workers performing contract work for the Government may

[Ch. 9 

work a maximum of 40 hours a week. Employees of street and elevated railroad lines may not work longer than 10 hours a day. Bus and truck drivers may not work more than 

10 hours out of 24 and must have at least 8 consecutive hours of rest. All workers must have 1 day of rest in 7. Workers in factories receive an hour for lunch; in other establishments at least 45 minutes must be allowed.

Federal Government attempts in this connection have been the Walsh Healey Law, providing for a 40-hour week for employees working on Federal contracts, and the pending Wages and Hours Bill, which provides for a 30-hour week for all. Wages

Until very recently all attempts on the part of the states to pass minimum-wage laws were declared unconstitutional by the United States Supreme Court. The Court felt that employers should have the right to obtain the services of employees at whatever wage they were willing to give and the employee was willing to accept. Any law that prevented these rights from being exercised was in violation of the constitutional provision that prohibits a state from passing a law impairing the freedom of contract.

In 1933 the Wald Law, a minimum-wage law passed by this state, was declared unconstitutional. More recently, however, the United States Supreme Court declared a minimum-wage law passed by the state of Washington to be constitutional on the grounds that the state had, under its police power, the right to give protection to certain groups. 

In 1937, therefore, New York State passed a new minimumwage law known as the Fischel Minimum Wage Law. It has as its purpose to give women and minors a wage sufficient to provide adequate maintenance and to protect their health. 

It provides:

Ch. 9] 283 

1. That only women and minor workers are under the protection of the law. It further excludes domestic servants and farm labor of any age or sex. 

2. That when unfair wages are being paid in a particular industry, a petition bearing the signatures of at least fifty residents should be sent to the State Labor Department. 

The Labor Commissioner will then appoint a wage board composed of not more than three employees, three employers, and three disinterested individuals. This wage board investigates conditions in the particular industry and decides what a fair minimum wage should be. 

3. If the Commissioner is satisfied, he will notify all employers of that industry that the particular amount agreed upon is to be the minimum wage for the workers in that industry. At this point, this notice is called a directory order. The punishment for violating this directory order is that such employers who refuse to pay the amount stated have their names published in the various newspapers in the state for their customers to see. This punishment is not meted out until after several hearings are held. Employers are allowed to voice their grievances before the Commissioner. 

4. The Commissioner may then issue this same order in a mandatory form. If the violation continues after the mandatory order has been issued, the employer may then be punished by a fine or imprisonment. Minimum wage rates for women and minors in the laundry industry have already been ordered. For New York City, the wage rate is $14 for a 40-hour week. For work done in excess of 40 hours, the rates are 35 cents an hour for each hour up to 45 hours, and time and a half for every hour after that. These new rates affect 22,000 workers in this industry. According to Elmer 

F. Andrews, State Industrial Commissioner, this is the first time in any state or any industry in the United States that a weekly minimum wage has been ordered.

[Ch. 9 

 Workmen's Compensation Law 

We have just studied the measures that our government has taken to prevent injury to the worker and to protect his health. Yet, in spite of all precautions, accidents may happen.

Situation Before 1914. The unfortunate position of injured workers before 1914 may be illustrated by the case of Mr. S. Mr. S had been employed as a counter man in a cafeteria. While he was cutting bread, the knife blade broke and permanently injured his hand. Since he could no longer render efficient service at his old occupation, he was discharged.

Mr. S could bring suit against his former employer because of the employer's negligence in providing him with a defective knife. Because of his poverty, it was difficult for him to obtain the aid of a good lawyer to handle his suit. 

Fortunately, he finally found one who was willing to take his case. Even with this lawyer's assistance, however, it was difficult to prove that the employer was at fault in bringing about the injury, that Mr. S had not through his own carelessness aided in causing the injury, and that no fellow worker was responsible. All of these things had to be proved before 

Mr. S could establish a claim against the employer. 

Several years elapsed before the case actually came up for trial. During this time Mr. S was not working and was reduced to extreme poverty. Finally, when the case came to trial, it was proved that the employer was responsible and the jury decided that Mr. S should receive $600. Mr. S was then given a judgment, a court order directing the employer to turn over the money to his former employee. When the sheriff tried to collect for Mr. S, it was learned that the employer was bankrupt and that nothing could be collected. 

Although not every injured employee was so unfortunate as Mr. S in his relations with his employer, nevertheless it

Ch. 9] 

285 

INDUSTRIAL ACCIDENTS 

1927 1937 

61% LOWER THAN 10 YEARS AGO Courtesy National Association of Manufacturers 

In increasing safety on the job and in providing better medical service in its plants, American industry has contributed much to our enviable health record. Applications for workman's compensation have thus been materially reduced. is easy to imagine the deplorable conditions in which many injured workers were placed. The law, as it existed, had to be changed so that the workers would be better provided for if injured while at work.

Situation Under the Workman's Compensation Law. 

Our lawmakers had made laws to prevent injury. They now set about making a law that would help the injured worker. As a result of their efforts, we now have a law that has put an end to the disadvantages workers suffered when they attempted to obtain compensation for injuries. Let us examine the law that has justly been described as a truly "forward step in the progress and development of a civilized state."

The New York State Workmen's Compensation Law provides for and insures payment by employers to workers for injuries sustained arising out of and in the course of their

[Ch. 9 

employment. Let us analyze each of the parts of this statement.

I. Insurance of Payment. Employees who are entitled to payment for injuries they have received need no longer fear that they will not be paid. Employers, upon putting men to work, must become insured either with some insurance company issuing workmen's compensation policies or with the state.

If the employee is injured, the insurance company, or the state, as the case may be, pays the injured worker. When the employer thus insures himself, the injured worker no longer needs to sue as Mr. S did. 

A recent amendment requires insurance companies to turn over to the state the funds they would have to pay to the worker over a long period of time. In addition, all insurance companies must contribute to a central fund from which money will be used for any insurance company that is unable to pay. This, of course, is to protect the worker against the possible failure of insurance companies.

II. Nature of Payment. The law provides for a specific amount to be paid to the worker if he is injured. This sum is generally two-thirds of the employee's weekly wage, but never less than $8 or more than $25. The number of weekly payments depends upon the seriousness of the injury. In addition, if the injury results in permanent total disability, the minimum is not less than $15. 

For example, for the loss of a thumb, the worker is entitled to 75 weeks' compensation; but for the loss of an eye, he is entitled to 160 weeks' compensation. The law contains a complete list of injuries and the corresponding compensations to be paid. In case of death, the worker's widow and children are provided for.

When a child under 18 years of age is employed at work that is prohibited for him by the New York State Labor

Ch. 9] 287 

Law, he is, in the case of injury, entitled to double the amount he otherwise would get. Employers, therefore, generally require from young applicants age certificates showing that they are over 18 years of age.

III. Time of Payment. The injured worker is entitled to immediate medical treatment at the expense of his employer or the insurance company of the employer. Compensation begins to be paid within a month after the injured worker has filed his claim for compensation with his em ployer and with the State Industrial Commissioner. Al though compensation is not allowed for the first seven days, this claim should be made as soon after the accident as possible and must be made not later than thirty days after the accident, if the employee wants to be paid at all. 

It is interesting to note that by a recent amendment to the law, the worker is entitled to select his own doctor, whereas formerly the employer selected one. This was done in order to prevent the doctor from minimizing the seriousness of the injury. The injured worker must, however, select a doctor from a list prepared by the Labor Department.

IV. Nature of Injuries Compensated. Not all injuries that a worker suffers entitles him to receive payment under this law. It most certainly would be unfair to require an employer or his insurance company to pay an employee who becomes ill at work because of certain food he has eaten at home, or when the worker was run over by an automobile while he was taking an evening stroll unrelated to his work. 

Only when the injuries are the result of industrial accidents or occupational diseases does the law provide that the worker should be paid.

Occupational Diseases. The Workmen's Compensation 

Law lists certain diseases that workers may contract as the result of their handling materials in various industries. For example, those engaged in the handling of wool, hair, bristles,

hides, or skins are subject to a disease known as anthrax. 

Glanders, a disease that grooms and other stablehands may contract because of their handling of horses, is also listed as an occupational disease. It should be remembered that not all diseases are occupational, and, unless the Workmen's Compensation Law so provides, the worker will not be paid. 

Here, again, an interesting change should be noted. 

Whereas the law originally listed a limited number (27) of occupational diseases, it now provides for "any and all occupational diseases in any and all employments listed as hazardous in the Workmen's Compensation Law." 

V. "Sustained in the Course of." Unless the injury came as a result of, and in the course of, the work for which the worker was employed, he cannot collect compensation. 

It is difficult at times to determine whether a particular injury arose under these conditions. As a result, this phrase has been the subject of many and various interpretations by the courts.

The courts have held that when a night watchman, guarding a factory, is set upon by burglars and receives various injuries as a result of the encounter, he is entitled to compensation for these injuries. However, in another case, when a night watchman "dozed off" while on the job, fell from his chair and was injured, he was not entitled to compensation.

Another interesting pair of cases that help us to understand this provision are these: Mr. Smith burned his finger while lighting a cigarette. Mr. Jones burnt his foot in an effort to keep warm while on the job. The court held that 

Mr. Jones was entitled to compensation but that Mr. Smith was not.

It is not necessary under this law to show that the injury was the fault of the employer, or that the worker was not partly to blame. The question of fault is entirely eliminated.

[Ch. 9 

Of course, when an employee is intoxicated, or when he purposely injures himself, he will not be allowed to recover compensation.

VI. Employment. The Workmen's Compensation Law does not place its mantle of protection over all workers.

It 

1. That the employer must be insured when the workers are engaged in certain hazardous employments and in all other cases in which there are four or more workmen or operatives employed. 

Courtesy Port of New York Authority A HAZARDOUS EMPLOYMENT 

Employers of workers engaged in work such as this must be insured under the Workman's Compensation Law. 

2. That when employers require their workers to engage in certain hazardous work, such as furniture moving, brick

Ch. 9] 

291 

laying, and others listed in the law, the employers must be insured no matter how few workers are employed. A complete list of these hazardous employments appears in the law. 

3. That clergymen, domestic servants, farm hands, and nearly all government employees are excluded and may not derive the benefits of this law.

Case of Mr. S Under the Workmen's Compensation Law. 

Let us now reconsider the case of Mr. S. The employer is now insured under a workmen's compensation policy. Immediately after his injury, Mr. S is treated by a doctor selected by him. The doctor's fees are paid by the employer or his insurance company. Mr. S immediately afterwards presents his claim. Since all of the technicalities of a regular courtroom are set aside, the services of a lawyer are not needed. The Workmen's Compensation Board, which meets in the State Building at 80 Centre Street, New York City, soon makes the award and Mr. S continues to receive nearly all of his salary until he is well enough to find work or to return to his old job.

Advantages. How different all this is from the suffering that the Mr. S of long ago had to endure. If injured, a worker may now be certain of payment, instead of winning a possible worthless judgment. His claim is settled speedily. 

The technicalities of a law suit have disappeared. 

The Right to Organize 

In an effort to give workers the chance to form unions without hindrance from employers, the Federal Government passed the Wagner National Labor Relations Act in July, 1935. Since the Federal Government jurisdiction covers only interstate affairs, New York passed a similar law to govern workers within this state. The latter law, known as

[Ch. 9 

the "Little Wagner Act," applies the principles of the Na tional Labor Relations Act. 

The state law, like the federal law 

1. Created a Labor Relations Board empowered to pro tect the right of workers to organize or bargain collectively through representatives of their own choosing; 

2. Forbids certain unfair labor practices by employers. 

Among them are the employment of spies or "finks"; the use of the black list; interfering with or dominating a workers' organization; the refusal to bargain collectively with workers or to discuss their grievances; the discharge of workers for union activities; or the use of a yellow-dog contract. (These terms are explained in Chapter X.) Workers may complain to the Labor Relations Board if employers are guilty of any of these practices. 

3. Permits workers to vote for the union they desire when more than one union, that is, craft or industrial, has been organized. A majority of workers determine the union, under voting conducted by the Board. 

Among the criticisms leveled at this law by those who believe that the act should be modified are the four following: 

1. It does not compel agreements between employers and workers. 

2. It does not list any unfair practices by workers or unions. 

3. It does not interfere with the right to strike. In fact, it specifically provides that nothing in the law should be interpreted to prevent workers from striking. 

4. It does not prevent a union from forcing nonmembers to join.

- 

 Social Security 

Thus far we have discussed various governmental attempts to aid workers while working. We saw, however, that there were other groups of citizens who, though wishing to work, were unable to do so. Until recently little attention was paid to them.

In recent years our government has spread its mantle of protection over those who unfortunately are unable to work. 

This protection is embodied in the Social Security Act. 

The act attempts to protect three groups of nonworkers. 

1. Mothers and children in need of funds; crippled children in need of medical, surgical, and corrective services; homeless and neglected children; the needy blind; and those physically disabled who can be trained for other vocations. 

2. Those people who are over the age of 65 years. 

3. That large group of persons who, though willing and able to work, cannot find jobs.

For all these cases the Federal Government has set up governmental machinery and has appropriated funds offering the states financial assistance if they will pass legislation that conforms to the standards set forth in the Federal act. 

Fortunately for us, New York State has accepted the offer so that we may, therefore, enjoy all of the benefits. 

We, as future workers, are concerned primarily with the last two groups listed above since we may some day find ourselves in the ranks of either. Let us analyze each of these more carefully in the light of our own state law. 

New York State Unemployment Insurance Act Unemployment Insurance Fund. This act, patterned after the Federal law, requires certain employers to pay a tax to a special state fund. From this fund, which is in-

[Ch. 9 

creased by Federal and state appropriations, certain unemployed workers will receive certain weekly payments for a certain period. Workers do not contribute to this fund. 

Employers Covered by the Law. The law provides that any employer who employs four or more persons within the state in any employment, except those listed below, within each of thirteen or more calendar weeks, must pay a tax of 3 per cent of his pay roll based on the number of those eligible for unemployment insurance whom he has in his employ. 

It should be noted here that the Federal Government also levies a tax of 3 per cent on pay rolls of employers who employ eight or more workers. New York employers may deduct their state contribution from the Federal tax up to 

90 per cent of the latter.

Let us assume that employer A employs 10 eligible workers. He thus comes within both the state and the federal law. If his total annual pay roll is $10,000, he must pay $300 to the state. However, he need pay only $30 to the 

Federal Government. 

Employers Exempted. In addition to every employer who employs less than four workers (exclusive of his wife or minor child), the following employers are also excused: 

1. The Government. 

2. Farmers. 

3. Charitable, religious, scientific, or educational organizations.

Employees Covered Under the Law. Employees covered under this law include citizens and aliens, as well as residents and nonresidents. The test to determine eligibility rests on the fact as to whether the work was performed within New 

York State.

- 

Workers employed in the special employments listed above do not come within the law even if they meet the requirements mentioned in the preceding paragraph. 

In addition, to be eligible, a qualified employee must have been employed in work with an employer (other than those exceptions) for a period of at least 90 days within the preceding year or 130 days during the preceding 2 years.

In other words, Mr. X, employed by a charitable organization, is not eligible, no matter how long he has worked for that employer. Mr. Y, working in a factory for a month within the last year or the last two years, is not eligible.

Mr. Z, who also has worked in a factory for 6 months before he claims unemployment insurance, is eligible.

Procedure. Mr. Z must immediately, when unemployed, register as such at a local state employment office. He must then wait three weeks before he may receive payments.

Once registered, he must report for work regularly at the designated office.

If a job is available, Mr. Z must accept it if he is "reasonably fitted by training and experience" for the job. If he refuses to accept such employment, he cannot claim unemployment insurance payments.

On the other hand, he need not accept even such a job under certain circumstances. If the job is an unreasonable distance from his home, he may refuse it. If the employer objects to his union affiliations, or if the hours, wages, and conditions are substantially less favorable than those prevailing for similar work or are such as to tend to depress wages, again the unemployed worker may refuse. 

Placement Service. Our state now provides its own employment agencies where unemployed workers may register to obtain jobs. This provision has met with considerable criticism from privately owned employment agencies that

[Ch. 9 

have suffered a loss of business. In 1936, 177,000 jobs were filled through the State Employment Service. 

Incidentally, these private agencies must now obtain a license to carry on their work. This law became necessary when it was learned that many young applicants were cheated by unscrupulous employment agencies to which they had applied for jobs.

Benefits. If, under the above circumstances, the employee is still unemployed three weeks after he has registered, he then becomes entitled to payment. (a) Amount. The amount he receives per week will depend on his former wage. He is entitled to 50 per cent of his wage, but not more than $15 or less than $7. Mr. A, whose wage was $20 a week, will now receive $10. Mr. B, whose former wage was $50 a week, will now receive $15. 

Mr. C, whose earnings were $8 a week, now receives $7. 

Benefits to those who have earned more than $3,000 annually are based on the first $3,000 of their salary. (b) Period. An employee receives these weekly payments based on the number of days he was employed during the preceding year. He is entitled to one week's payment for every 15 days so employed. In no case, however, does he receive more than 16 consecutive payments in any one year. 

Assuming that Messrs. A, B, and C had been employed for all of the preceding year, they would then receive $160, $240, and $112, respectively, if unemployed all of the following year. 

Care of the Aged

As part of the Social Security Act, provision was made for persons over the age of 65. This group has been divided into two classes: those who are unable to support themselves,

Ch. 9] 297 

and all others over the age of 65. A pension is paid to the first, while compulsory insurance is provided for the second. 

Old-Age Pension. The New York State Old-Age Pension 

Law was passed in 1930 and was then amended to conform with the Federal Social Security Act. It makes provision for any resident of this state, who is over 65 years, who is "unable to support himself, either in whole or in part; has no children or other persons able to support him; and who is without other means or sources of income by which he can maintain himself."

The funds to pay these unfortunates have been provided by the state and the city. Now, in addition, the Federal 

Government also contributes under the Social Security Act. 

In no case does the Federal Government pay more than $15 a month for each of these needy persons.

Old-Age Insurance. This plan differs from the other in that it provides for those over 65 who are not necessarily in need. Secondly, the fund to pay this group comes in most part from the workers themselves and their employers. 

Under this law employers must deduct a certain percentage from each worker's pay, add a similar amount themselves, and forward this sum to the Federal Government. 

This percentage gradually rises from 1 per cent in 1937 until it reaches 3 per cent in 1949, and then continues at 3 per cent yearly thereafter.

The plan for this group is similar to the ordinary annuity insurance policy except that it is compulsory. This deduction from a worker's pay is similar to a premium one pays on the usual insurance policy. Upon reaching the age of 65, the worker then receives a yearly payment or annuity. The amount the worker receives depends on his yearly salary and the number of years the worker has received this salary.

[Ch. 9

This plan provides a form of life insurance as well, for if a worker dies before 65, his estate will receive a lump sum of money.

A Last Word. We have noted in some detail the various ways by which our government has attempted to provide

- 

the deserving with economic security. Before leaving this field of study, however, let us remember two most important 

1. The reader should be cautioned that what has been set down as the law is not entirely obeyed by some of our employers. Sweatshops, factories where insanitary conditions prevail, may still be found in New York State, although, thanks to the painstaking efforts of our Labor Department, they are few and far between. It is for this reason that the provisions of each law have been carefully discussed in this chapter. The worker, equipped with full knowledge of his rights, should make every effort to assist the Labor Department in its work so that the rights granted to him by a benevolent state may be more fully protected. 

2. Although our Government has painstakingly attempted to provide some measure of security to its workers, many exceptional cases appear in the law. All of these workers, except those employed by the Government, have no security. 

More serious than that, however, our Government has as yet made no provision to provide for cases of sickness outside the scope of one's employment. We may yet see health insurance as a law of the land.
