CHAPTER X  Early History. The Industrial Revolution brought about many desirable and many undesirable changes. It increased greatly the amount of goods that a group of workers could produce, and made available for the average person many things that could not otherwise be obtained at a reasonable price.

But not all its effects were good. It brought about the grouping of many workers in factories and mines where they were forced to work according to the terms laid down by the employer. The hours were often long, the conditions of work were bad, and the wages were low. 

As more and more workers began to feel the evil effects of the Industrial Revolution, resistance developed. In 

France, for example, workers rioted in the streets of Paris, destroyed factories, threw machinery into the Seine, and, in general, ran amuck. Some contrived the idea of throwing their wooden shoes (sabots) into the whirring machines, causing the machinery to break down. Others, less violent, suffered in silence. Many workers in this country adopted the second method in the beginning. 

A typical case of the less violent group was that of Jacob 

Hoffman. Jacob Hoffman was an efficient cigar maker employed by the Puffrite Tobacco Company. He received $1 for rolling and filling 100 cigars. By working 50 hours a week, he was able to earn $22.50. One morning he found a notice on his bench that the rate of his wages was to be reduced to 75 cents a hundred. Jacob was a mild-mannered individual, however, and was not unduly alarmed. After a week, his pay had dwindled to $18 and he had put in 54 301

[Ch. 10 

hours instead of 50. Jacob decided to approach his employer.

He received no encouragement; but, on the contrary, he was told to accept the reduction or look for work elsewhere. 

Jacob had no alternative. He had to accept the cut or quit. 

Beginning of Unions. Other employees were equally handicapped in bargaining with their employers. Any attempt of an individual worker to voice a grievance or to bargain with his employer concerning the terms of his employment met with a curt "Take it or quit." This often left the worker with no choice but to accept the terms offered him, for no general statement can portray a true picture of the fear that haunted a worker lest he be without work. 

Soon workers began to realize the truth of "In union there is strength" and began to band together into organizations of various types. All the workers of a factory presented their complaints through their representative, instead of as individual workers. The employer, under these circumstances, listened more carefully, since a refusal on his part might leave his plant entirely unmanned. This method of negotiating with an employer is called collective bargaining. 

During the period 1789 to 1827 these unions of workers were generally local in character. As time went on, the movement spread until now unions of workers are nationwide in scope.

Labor Unions. Workers have adoped various forms of organization. Some of these forms have continued to the present; others lived for a while and then ceased to exist. 

Among the latter types was a form known as the labor union. An outstanding example of this type was the Noble 

Order of the Knights of Labor organized by U. S. Stevens at Philadelphia in 1869. All laborers including carpenters, teamsters, butchers, steel workers, cigar makers, and even professional men (except lawyers) were admitted. This

Ch. 10] 303 

organization sought "to gather into one association all branches of honorable toil, without regard to nationality, sex, creed, or color."

This type of union did not have a long life. Although it had a membership of over 700,000 in 1886, a few years later it was a thing of the past. It had failed because the workers had problems peculiar to their own trades and were interested primarily in the betterment of conditions within their own trades. 

Trade Unions. Many skilled workers have found it desirable to organize themselves with other skilled workers of the same trade. The Brotherhood of Locomotive Engineers, the Brotherhood of Conductors, the Brotherhood of Firemen, the Brotherhood of Trainmen are all separate and distinct unions although all are connected with the railroad industry. 

The printing trades have unions of compositors, pressmen, linotypists, and stereotypers-distinct organizations in themselves. A worker in these groups belongs to the local branch of conductors or pressmen, as the case may be, and not to the railroad or printing workers' unions. 

Industrial Unions. In some industries the industry itself, and not a specific craft, is the basis of organization. The 

United Mine Workers of America, the United Brewery

Workers, and the Amalgamated Clothing Workers of America are the outstanding examples of this type. In view of the recently organized Committee for Industrial Organization (C. I. O.), about which more will be said later, many more industrial unions have come into existence. As far back as 

1850 the Industrial Workers of the World (I. W. W.) tried to spread the gospel of industrial unionism in the United States. The struggle between trade and industrial unionism will also be dealt with later. 

Company Unions. The type of union known as the company union had a relatively short life, although within its

[Ch. 10 

 ALL WORKERS IN COMPANY ELECT 

REPRESENTATIVES SELECT COMEMBERS OF COMMITTEES ON COMPLAINT RULES SAFETY PENSION HOURS WAGES APPEALS SELECTS CO-MEMBERS OF COMMITTEES ON PRESIDENT OF THE COMPANY- MANAGEMENT ARBITRATION UNDER AUSPICES OF LABOR DEDT. 

STRUCTURE OF A COMPANY UNION 

The broken line indicates the path of complaint from one of the workers. short span this form was extremely popular. Before 1933 no law had given workers the express right of organizing unions to bargain collectively. Section 7a of the NIRA gave the workers this right. Many employers began to organize their own unions by forming organizations of all the workers in their plants, regardless of the type of work each employee performed. The Wagner Labor Relations Law, de signed to "put teeth" into this section, outlawed company

Ch. 10] 305 

unions if they were controlled by the employers. This had the effect of destroying many company unions. An example of a company union is indicated in the diagram on page 304. 

At least one taxicab war in New York City was a struggle not between the workers and the employer, but between a company union and another group that was attempting to form all taxi drivers into one trade union. 

Union of Unions

American Federation of Labor. In 1881 a successful attempt was made to unite a group of national unions of skilled labor. This organization came later to be known as the American Federation of Labor. The American Federation of Labor is a federation of trade and industrial unions, though most of the members are trade unions. The basic unit in the organization is the local union. For example, a worker may join a local motion-picture operators' union. 

This union in turn is affiliated with or belongs to the central organization, known as the A. F. of L.

The American Federation of Labor has done a great deal for labor. Although it has not formed a political party in America, it has given active support to congressmen, governors, and state legislators who advocate progressive legislation. At Washington it maintains a powerful lobby to prevent the passing of laws unfavorable to the worker as well as to advocate legislation that will improve his working conditions.

Committee for Industrial Organization. Within the past few years intense feeling has grown within the ranks of the American Federation of Labor between industrial-union members and trade-union members. In November, 1935, John L. Lewis, President of the United Mine Workers of

America, an industrial union, severed his connections with the main body.

[Ch. 10 

Taking with him his own union and several others, all industrial, he formed the Committee for Industrial Organization (C. I. O.) to further the formation of industrial unions instead of trade unions. In 1936 these unions were suspended from membership in the American Federation of Labor. At the present time the Committee for Industrial 

Organization and the American Federation of Labor, the first composed only of industrial unions, the second mainly of trade unions, are engaged in battling each other in addition to protecting the rights of workers against employers. 

The struggle is now three-sided. 

Trade Unionism vs. Industrial Unionism. Among the grievances that the industrial unions, as represented by the Committee for Industrial Organization, are said to have against the trade unions, as represented by the American 

Federation of Labor, are the following: 

1. Structure. The trade-union structure is considered to be out of date by those in favor of industrial unions. Because of technological improvements and the high degree of division of labor in industry, the ranks of skilled labor have been depleted and now represent but a small part of the working staff. Most of the workers are unskilled, and no protection is afforded them. Therefore, the only way of protecting workers is by organizing all of the workers within the industry. The various trades that make up one industry have problems in common and should be organized in industrial unions. A conductor on a train, for example, comes into contact with the engineer, fireman, and trainmen of the same train much more frequently than he does with conductors of other trains. 

2. Number of Members. In 1930 the American Federation of Labor had approximately three million members including virtually all trade unions. A number of powerful unions were not members. These included such important

Ch. 10] 307 

unions as the Big Four, that is, the railroad brotherhoods, as well as the Amalgamated Clothing Workers of America. "At no time, however, has trade-union membership in America exceeded ten per cent of all those gainfully employed," says Stuart Chase, a well-known American writer. 

The Committee for Industrial Organization maintains that it will be able to increase union membership tremendously by industrial organization. 

3. Method. The Committee for Industrial Organization maintains that the American Federation of Labor has been ineffective in dealing with employers. 

Purposes and Policies of Unions 

Although unions may differ as to type, as a rule certain policies and purposes are common to all unions. Among these common policies are: 

Collective Bargaining. If each laborer had to make a separate agreement with his employer, he would have to take the wage that the employer offered or look elsewhere for work. Today union workers elect a committee to bargain with the employers or with the representatives of employers' associations. This is known as collective bargaining and applies to hours and working conditions as well as to wages. 

Collective bargaining has brought about a higher wage than many workers could possibly have obtained through individual effort.

Virtually all progressive large-scale employers recognize the benefits of collective rather than individual bargaining. 

An agreement entered into with a responsible union should lessen labor difficulties. There will always be some differences of opinion, but some satisfactory solution should be obtained. However, some employers feel otherwise about this matter. Henry Ford, for example, though paying high

[Ch. 10 

wages, has not agreed with the principle of collective bargaining through representatives not in his employ. 

The Wagner Labor Relations Act (see Chapter IX) guar antees to workers the right of collective bargaining. 

The Closed Shop. Unions demand a closed shop, that is, one in which only union men may be employed. Employers have insisted on an open shop. In an open shop both union and nonunion men may be employed, as the employer sees fit. At this point, it is important to note the difference between a closed shop with an open union and a closed shop with a closed union. If a man wishes a job on the Pennsylvania Railroad, he applies to the personnel director or the employment manager. If hired, he must join the union. In the clothing trades, however, the employer of a closed shop may hire only those who present union cards. The first case is an example of a closed shop with an open union, while the second case is an example of a closed shop with a closed union.

Restriction of Membership. In some trade unions, workers find it difficult to become members. High membership dues are required. Some unions compel applicants to take tests. This policy is designed to prevent an oversupply of that kind of labor when jobs are few so that members, if limited in number, will be taken care of. This could not be done so easily if the membership were unrestricted. 

Restriction of Output. Many workers believe that only a certain amount of work is available. The longer it takes to finish the job, the longer they will be employed. This idea is known as the lump of labor fallacy. It is a fallacy because human wants for goods are endless. The chief difficulty is in providing greater purchasing power to people in general.

Ch. 10] 309 

Efficient and rapid production that decreases costs will, in the long run, increase demand and increase the amount of work available. This increase may not come at once, however. The employee may, then, be temporarily out of a job when he has finished a certain amount of available work. If workers are not to favor restriction of output in their particular industries, steps should be taken to assure the worker of regular and profitable employment. 

At times, restriction of output is favored to combat a device used by employers who try to cut down too greatly on labor costs. This device is called the stretch out system. 

The employer may require one worker to tend several machines at once. If the employee rebels, the employer will be compelled to hire more help. 

Accomplishments of Unions The unions have accomplished some worth-while objectives that should be noted. 

Higher Wages. "The more perfected the organization of the union, the higher the wages" may be cited as a general conclusion. It must be remembered, though, that the skilled workers make up the bulk of those belonging to unions. It is interesting to note that unions object to minimum-wage laws because they say the minimum tends to become the maximum; that is, if all workers must receive at least a certain wage, the employer will reduce wages of highly paid workers to make up for the increase he must give to the poorly paid workers. 

Shorter Hours. That the eight-hour day and the fiveday week would have come eventually cannot be doubted. 

Organized labor, more than any other force, hastened these welcome changes. The pending Wages and Hours Bill, providing for a thirty-hour week, is being fostered by labor

[Ch. 10 

1860 

WAGE RATE INCREASED FIVE TIMES SINCE 1340 

TODAY 

Courtesy National Association of Manufacturers 

A part of this increase is due to the activities of organized labor. organizations. This bill is a federal attempt to regulate labor and has been awaiting enactment for several years. 

Stricter Labor Laws in the States. Safety devices, proper sanitation, and better working conditions in general have been given impetus by workers' organizations. 

Fraternal Aid. Monetary aid has been given those unemployed, and benefits have been paid to the sick and to the families of deceased members. 

Reading rooms, trade publications, and classes for apprentices have been established at the expense of the unions. 

The question of higher wages, however, is the most important of these objectives. Other purposes have not been neglected, but it is mainly to bring about an increase in the pay envelope that labor insists on collective bargaining, the right to strike, boycotts, picketing, and in many instances a share in the management of the business.

- 

 Methods Used by Workers 

This brings us to a study of some of the methods used by labor organizations to make their demands felt by employers. 

Strikes. A strike is sometimes described as a collective refusal to enter on a contract of labor. Professor Taussig, a noted economist, insists that a strike is more than this. He says, "It is a concerted withdrawal from work with the design of securing return to the same employment under better conditions than are offered at the time by employers." 

Better conditions may refer to an increased wage, the prevention of a decrease in wages, shorter hours, or improved working conditions. "The intention of a strike is to secure satisfactory terms, while retaining the positions, not to leave the positions and go elsewhere." In other words, the striker aims to retain the same position he had. 

When strikers are reinstated on their own terms, the strike has been successful. If strikers come back on the old terms or if they have to seek other employment, even though they may find better jobs elsewhere, the strike has been unsuccessful. The strike is the most effective weapon that the worker possesses. Weapon is the correct term, for a strike is a movement of a military nature and is used to offset the right of discharge or lockout-the main weapons of the employer. 

Strikes cause loss of wealth to workers, employers, and the public alike. Nevertheless, no adequate substitute for the strike has been worked out up to the present time. 

A relatively new idea in labor warfare is the sit-down strike. In addition to stopping work, the workers remain in the plant to prevent others from taking their place. 

There is a serious doubt about the legality of such a method, inasmuch as the workers on a sit-down strike control the property belonging to the employer. The Constitution

[Ch. 10 

of the United States provides for the safeguarding of private property. A sit-down strike appears to violate the law. 

Courtesy Ewing Galloway

STRIKE SCENE 

This factory is closed and the workers are temporarily unemployed, because the owners and the workers did not agree on working conditions.

Ch. 10] 313 

On the other hand, workers maintain that their right, the human right to their jobs, is higher than the right of private property.

Picketing and Boycotting. Unions seek to carry on a strike successfully by placing guards or patrols in front of the buildings or plants affected by strikes. These men march up and down, calling attention to the strike and seeking to persuade strikebreakers not to enter the factory, mine, store, or restaurant. This is called picketing. Pickets keep in constant motion to avoid the possibility of arrest for loitering. 

To combine against an employer by refusing to deal with him, is called boycotting. Customers are urged by word of mouth or through printed circulars not to patronize the business or enterprise against which a strike has been called. 

In New York City "sandwich men" may be seen walking up and down in front of factories and stores. The sign suspended in the front and rear of the picket not only calls attention to the shortcomings of the employer, but urges customers not to patronize him. This is a combination of picketing and boycotting. 

If you have ever visited a magistrate's court, you may have seen groups of workers brought in by the policeman on charges made by the employer. The highest court of our state has, however, declared "peaceful" picketing to be legal.

There is, however, one form of boycott that, under certain circumstances, may be illegal. If a manufacturer's workers are on strike, they may picket a store in which his goods are sold. If they urge customers not to buy the manufacturer's product, this form of boycott is legal. If they urge customers not to buy anything in that store and the store sells other manufacturers' products, such a form of boycott, known as a secondary boycott, is illegal.

Sabotage. As we mentioned earlier in our discussion, in 

France a worker on strike used to throw his wooden shoe (sabot) into the machinery. Today any wilful destruction of machinery by workers constitutes sabotage. If the destruction is caused by others, it is called vandalism. At the beginning of the World War the railroad workers in France tied up the entire transportation industry by deliberately rerouting packages to the wrong destination. In recent strikes in the 

United States factory windows and other property were destroyed by workers. 

Sabotage is illegal. Of course, employers have on occasion resorted to violence. Frequently employers and employees accuse each other of injecting gangsters and racketeers into the dispute. Both employers and workers blame these undesirable persons for any illegal activities. 

Unfair List. Unions have drawn up lists of employers said to be unfair to labor. When jobs are plentiful, members of the unions will refuse to work for an employer whose name appears on the list. This leaves his factory undermanned. 

This method has been declared illegal. In order to overcome this difficulty, unions now draw up a fair list, a list of employers who are fair to labor. The result is the same. 

Methods Used by Employers 

Employers' Associations. In order to combat labor organizations, in many industries associations of employers have attempted to overcome the weapons of unions by weapons of their own. The Steel Institute, an association of steel manufacturers, and the National Manufacturers Association are typical of employers' associations. 

The Lockout. This method involves a wholesale temporary discharge of all workers until they will listen to the employer's orders. As has been indicated before, this method is similar to the strike in that work stops. This method is

- 

particularly effective when jobs are scarce, since the workers must now look elsewhere for work and will find difficulty in getting jobs.

Black List. While the union prepares its unfair list, the employer draws up a black list that consists of a list of men who have become objectionable. This list is passed on from one employer to another. Though illegal, the black list is effective because its existence is difficult to prove. 

An employer may sometimes have private detectives to ferret out the "troublemakers" in his plant. These detectives, as well as workers who spy on their fellow workers, are called finks. 

Strikebreakers. If a strike is declared, employers may hire nonunion workers to take the strikers' places. Often he will pay them more in wages than the strikers are demanding. These newly hired men are called strikebreakers, or scabs. The sit-down strike is a device used to prevent strikebreakers from entering the plant. 

Injunction. An injunction is a court order requiring a laborer or labor union to refrain from some specific action that may cause irreparable damage to the employer's property. If disobeyed, this injunction is enforceable in criminal "contempt proceedings." Injunctions have been used effectively to break strikes and are vigorously opposed by labor unions. 

Yellow-Dog Contracts. In order to prevent unions from entering their plants, employers at one time required new employees to agree not to join a union. This type of contract was frowned upon by unions, which maintained that such an agreement should not be offered even to a "yellow dog," much less to a man. Both the Wagner Labor Relations Act

[Ch. 10 

of the Federal Government and the State Labor Law specifically prohibit employers from offering such a contract to workers.

Promotion of Industrial Peace 

From the discussion of labor difficulties one may readily get the idea that the worker and the employer are constantly at war; that each has his army and weapons; that both are battling constantly with each other for supremacy. Unfortunately, this is so in many instances. 

Inasmuch as we have seen that no one really gains from this constant warfare, it might be well for us to seek ways and means of preventing recurrences of this difficulty. 

Among the methods that have been devised to promote industrial peace are:

Conciliation. Conciliation refers to the settling of disputes between the employer and the employee without any help from the outside. Conciliation may be used to prevent a dispute or to settle a situation that has ended in some kind of open warfare. The distinguishing characteristic of conciliation is the absence of a third party. When a third party steps in and attempts by persuasion to have the contestants (workers and employers) settle their differences peaceably, conciliation has given way to another method. 

Mediation. When an uninvited third party steps in to try to settle the dispute between the employer and the workers, the method of settling the dispute is called mediation. This third party may be a public-spirited citizen or some government agency.

In New York State we have a State Board of Mediation consisting of five persons appointed by the Governor and approved by the State Senate. They are empowered to take steps effectively to carry out the policy of the law that created

Ch. 10] 317 

the board, that is, to attempt to settle an industrial dispute to the best interests of the people of the state in order to avoid economic waste and to consider the interests of the consumer in their attempts to clear up the difficulty. 

It should be noted, however, that the law prescribes only voluntary mediation. The disputants need not present their cases to this board.

Arbitration. The hearing and determining of a controversy by a person or persons mutually agreed upon by the parties to the dispute is known as arbitration. If both parties agree to submit their differences to a third party, the arbitration is voluntary. In New South Wales the law requires that both parties must submit their disputes to arbitration. 

That is called compulsory arbitration.

In America many workers and employers have objected to compulsory arbitration, the worker contending that it interferes with the right to strike and the employer that it prevents his use of the lockout or discharge of obnoxious workmen.

Although the National Labor Relations Law, as well as the state law, provides for a Labor Relations Board to which workers may come to present their grievances, the law does not compel acceptance by the workers since it specifically provides that labor shall have the right to strike. 

Arbitration in the United States may be accomplished by means of voluntary arbitration only. To illustrate, let us take a dispute in a clothing establishment. The clothing workers had asked for a change in the method of wage payment used. They contended that the employers had been using pace-setters, thus violating a general wage agreement. 

The employers' association, on the other hand, maintained that they had lived up to the contract. In submitting the matter to arbitration, the union selected one member, and the employers another. A third member was selected by

[Ch. 10 

the first two. In that case the decision was virtually left to the third man, and the opportunities for real contributions by the other two were lost.

Public boards, such as the Labor Relations Boards of the 

State and Federal governments, especially when appointed in advance of a dispute, are more likely to furnish opportunities for each side to contribute fully. The fact that in most controversies, however, the parties prefer to have one of their own members among the judges, and therefore prefer private to public boards, shows the inherent difficulties of the situation.

Arbitration is not a cure-all for labor disputes, but it is helpful. In no instance of voluntary arbitration is the decision of the arbitrators binding. Arbitrators in England and France have used the device of publishing their reports, thus bringing public opinion to bear in aid of settlement. 

A Last Word 

With labor conditions changing as they are, it is difficult to advise future workers concerning what they should do regarding these labor difficulties.

Present tendencies indicate that membership in a union may be a prerequisite to entering any employment. Mayor 

La Guardia, in a recent statement, stressed the hope of making New York City a closed shop.

As to the type of union that our future worker should join, again it is impossible to venture an opinion. One point, however, should be noted. Care should be taken to become acquainted with the responsible heads of the union selected. In a recent exposé in New York City, Special 

Prosecutor Thomas E. Dewey proved that some labor unions are organized by racketeers and cheats. The dues paid by members are kept by these thieves, and no help is given to the members. Organizations of this kind are a menace to labor and to society.

Ch. 10]  

319 It might be well also for a good economic citizen to remember that whether he is an employer or an employee industrial warfare does not pay. Everyone loses. The employer loses because he cannot fill orders. The consuming public suffers because it cannot get the product. If the product can be had, the price is exhorbitant. 

Most of all, the worker loses even if he wins a labor dispute. Labor is a perishable commodity. If a worker loses a week's work while on strike, he can never make up the time lost.

If disputes arise, they should be settled speedily and justly. Let us hope that some day, under the leadership of good economic citizens, this result will be achieved.

UNIT V ECONOMIC INDEPENDENCE - . 

THE ULTIMATE GOAL INTRODUCTION 

Desirability of Economic Independence. We have just completed a study of the workers' struggle for economic security. Aided by a progressive government, workers have succeeded in obtaining legislation designed to give them a certain degree of security. Fortunately, however, most people are not satisfied with this limited security. Their goal is a higher one, often called economic independence. 

Economic independence is a term used to describe the situation in which a person can provide himself and his dependents with a comfortable standard of living and is able to continue to do so even if he should stop working. This independence is desirable for several reasons. The chief of these reasons are: 

1. The provisions of our present laws do not provide economic security to everyone under all conditions. Those who, during a certain period of time, do not work the required amount of time are not covered by unemployment insurance. 

Furthermore, various groups of workers are not covered by this insurance. Also, there is no provision in the governmental plans for economic security that insures payment if a person's earning power decreases because of an injury from an accident of a nonindustrial nature. There is also no provision for payment for loss of work due to any serious illness that is not classified as an occupational disease. 322

Unit 5] 323 

ECONOMIC INDEPENDENCE 

2. The sums provided for by law are very small. To a person who is unemployed or has passed the age of sixtyfive, a small income is very much better than no income at all. It is not expected, however, that the compensation received from the government as a result of an accident or the funds received from unemployment or old-age insurance will equal a person's normal earning power. The amount paid provides no more than is required for a bare subsistence and not enough for a reasonable degree of comfort. 

Advantages of Economic Independence. Economic inde pendence, that is, the ability of a person to take care of his own financial needs, is therefore important for three reasons: 

1. It provides funds that can be used in any emergency. 

These funds are available in the case of sickness, accident, unemployment, or old age. They can be used by the owner according to his needs and not according to the provisions of a law. 

2. The funds set aside for economic independence depend on individual initiative and individual success. They may be considerably larger than those provided by law. Even if they are not large, they can be used to supplement any funds received under the Workmen's Compensation Law, the 

Unemployment Insurance Law, or the State Old-Age Pension

Law. For example, if an elderly person has funds of his own to supplement old-age insurance, he can maintain a higher standard of living and can be in a position to enjoy life more than he otherwise could. 

3. These funds are constant in nature and do not depend on the whim or fancy of lawmakers. The owner is therefore able to make definite plans for using them and to have reasonable assurance that he can carry out those plans. 

Factors in Economic Independence. Let us examine this highly desirable goal and learn how we may approach it.

[Unit 5 

As we noted before, economic independence may be defined as the financial condition of one who is earning enough to provide himself and his dependents with a comfortable standard of living and who has enough set aside in the form of insurance or savings to provide a satisfactory income in the event of voluntary or involuntary retirement. 

The story is told of a farmer who decided to build a reservoir on his farm so that his cattle would have water all the year round. He searched his property and found a few rivulets and streams. Knowing that dry weather would soon arrive, he directed all his efforts to piping these streams or changing their course in order to fill the reservoir. So intent was he on filling the reservoir that he thought of nothing else. He worked diligently until finally his work was completed. That night a torrent of rain increased the flow of water, and the farmer was sure that his reservoir was full. 

When he reached it, however, he discovered that the reservoir was dry and little wonder; he had left the sluice gates open.

This story is descriptive of many of us. We have centered our attention so long on earning money that we pay scant attention to what happens to it once it is earned. We put forth a great deal of effort to earn dollars, and yet we give them up without a struggle. 

The ladder of economic independence contains three rungs. They are: 

1. Earning. 

2. Spending Wisely. 

3. Saving Intelligently.

We have spent most of our efforts preparing to climb the first rung. Let us spend a little of our time learning to climb these last two rungs, which we have so far neglected. 

A good economic citizen must know more than how to earn money. He must know how much of his income to

Unit 5] 325 

ECONOMIC INDEPENDENCE 

spend. He must know how to spend it wisely so that he may enjoy a higher standard of living in return for the money he spends. Finally, he must know how to save intelligently so that when the "rainy day" comes and he stops work temporarily or permanently, either of his own free will, or because of illness, injury, old age, or dismissal, he will have a "nest egg" set aside for use during the nonearning period.

His sluice gate must be in good working order and his reservoir walls must be firm and strong. 

Let us try first to determine an answer to the question, How much may I spend?

CHAPTER XI  

Income vs. Expenditure. "Mr. M, could you lend me a nickel for fare home?" This question was addressed to a teacher in a high school in your neighborhood about dismissal time.

Mr. M's first impulse was to dig into his pocket and give the nickel to his pupil, and, with a slap on the back or a friendly remark, put the pupil at his ease. But Mr. M stopped short. "I'd like to talk to you for a minute after class is dismissed" he said instead.

With the class dismissed, Mr. M returned to his room, sat, and chatted with the boy, Tom, who had addressed the question to him. He learned that the boy, who lived some distance away, had been given his regular allowance that morning. Part of this sum he had used for fare in the morning, part for lunch, and then he stopped. "Did you spend all the rest of your money at the lunch counter?" asked Mr. M. "Well," said the boy hesitantly, suddenly becoming uncomfortable, "I went to the candy counter." Realizing that he was confessing weakness of will power, he blurted out his fatal misstep-a particularly tempting piece of candy had been his undoing.

To the surprise of the boy, Mr. M smiled and patted him on the back. "Has this happened before?". "Yes," Tom admitted. "Sometimes I have enough, sometimes I don't. I generally borrow fare from one of the fellows but today they were all short."

The next few minutes were a revelation to the boy. Mr. 

M produced pencil and paper. Together they went over 

Tom's allowance and discussed how it should be spent. Each item of expense (including fare home) was listed and ac- 326

counted for to avoid dictating to the boy, the teacher permitted him to determine the amount-and wonder of wonders, there was enough left in each day's allowance for a piece of that same tempting candy that had cost him his fare home.

Tom left that room with more than the nickel which the teacher had given to him. He had learned how to plan and to plan in advance of spending rather than to regret purchases afterward.

Need for Planned Spending. Imagine how inefficient and unattractive our school and office buildings would be if an architect had not prepared plans for their construction in advance. Imagine the financial condition of a businessman who does not keep records of his accounts or plan for future purchases and sales. Imagine the condition of a government that does not bother to determine the costs of administration and to prepare for them, but permits debt after debt to pile up without considering payment. Recall, if you will, the dilemma of Tom. All are in the same group. Spending, like building, requires careful planning in advance. 

A plan for spending is known as a budget. Made in advance for a period of time (a year, let us say), it consists of two parts, probable income and probable expenditures for that period. Under the first part is listed an account of all probable sources of income, and under the second part is listed an account of all probable expenses. 

Factors in Planning the Budget 

Income vs. Outgo. How much we are to spend, outgo, must be determined primarily by how much we earn, income. 

A person who spends more than he earns becomes indebted either to merchants or to loan companies or banks. 

If he becomes indebted to merchants, he must at some future time pay his debts. He may therefore be unable to

This is one form of record record of income buy things that he needs very much. If he does not pay his debts, he may be embarrassed and inconvenienced by the efforts of the merchants to make collection. Not so many years ago, debtors were imprisoned for debt. Today, although imprisonment for debt is no longer permitted by civilized society, the debtor's lot is nevertheless an unhappy one.

If a person seeks to avoid being indebted to merchants by borrowing from a loan company, he is no better off. In fact, he is faced with the necessity of paying a high rate of interest-often as much as 36 per cent. Much of his future income must be used not only for the paying of debts but also for the paying of interest. 

If our planning is to aid us to avoid the unhappy lot of the debtor and to help us to have something left over for the nonearning period, we must keep our spending within our present earnings.

Those of us who have read David Copperfield may recall Mr. Micawber's advice to the young man: "Annual income twenty pounds, annual expenditure nineteen, nineteen, six: result, happiness. Annual income twenty pounds, annual expenditure twenty pounds, ought, six: result, misery. The blossom is blighted, the leaf withered, and the God of Day goes down upon the dreary scene forever. Sources of Income. Since income is the foundation of our budget, let us spend a few moments in analyzing it. 

Income may be derived from various sources-wages, profits, and the returns to capital commonly called interest or rent.

When a person works for another and is paid for his services, he is said to receive wages. If you work in a neighborhood store on Saturdays, the amount you receive is known as wages. Other members of your family may earn wages as employees in a factory or in a store.

The owner of a business does not receive wages. If he is efficient, he will find that, after paying all his expenses, a surplus remains. This is his payment for having taken the risk of possible loss in business as well as for his services in managing the business. He is known as an entrepreneur, and his income is called profit. If your father is the owner of a neighborhood store, he is an entrepreneur and his income is received from profits.

The money and property that people own are commonly called capital. Quite commonly, people pay others for the privilege of using their capital. For example, you may have a sum of money that you deposit in a savings bank. The

[Ch. 11 

bank pays you interest for the use of the money. You are then a capitalist, and the amount that you receive is interest. 

You may also own a bicycle that you rent to a friend at a fixed rate per hour. Again you are a capitalist obtaining income from the use of your property. If your father does not own the house in which you live, he pays rent to a landlord. In this case the landlord is the capitalist. When a capitalist receives his income because he permits others to use his money, he is said to receive interest. When he permits others to use his property, he is said to receive rent. 

The Government also takes its share from the producer. 

Since it provides protection to the producer and performs other services, it, too, deserves an income. This income is known as taxes. 

Types of Income. Returning once more to your own case, whatever the source of your income may be, we must consider this income from still another point of view. Can you, without difficulty, determine even approximately how much your income for the next year will be? The following examples illustrate various types of income and the problems of determination connected with each. 

The simplest case is that of a worker in government service. If you will review our discussion of employment in civil service, you will recall that for this group of workers 1. salaries are fixed on an annual basis, payable weekly, biweekly, or monthly. 

2. the jobholder is reasonably certain of his pay and does not fear discharge unless he is guilty of misconduct. 

3. a pension fund provides for his needs after retirement. 

Keeping this in mind, you can readily see that the civil service worker has little difficulty in determining how much he will earn the following year. He can therefore plan his purchases on the basis of a regular, fixed income. Rent, gas,

Courtesy Federal Art Project, Dept. of Health, New York City CIVIL SERVICE WORKER 

A worker in a civil service occupation has a steady income. electricity are paid every month. The pension arrangement protects him in the future.

One might say that such a worker needs no budget at all since his income is replenished at short regular intervals, and he need not fear a "rainy day." However, as we shall see later, certain expensive purchases must be provided for over a long period. Such articles as a piano, furniture, or even a suit cannot readily be paid for from the proceeds of one monthly check. Such expenses must be spread over a longer period of time. In addition the civil service worker's pension is not a large sum and may compel a reduction in his standard of living. Also, the income is generally small and

[Ch. 11 

unless carefully spent may result in placing the worker in debt.

In passing, it is well to remind one that in New York state and city service being in debt is a particularly annoying burden to him. Judgment creditors may readily obtain payment of their debts by garnisheeing 10 per cent or more of the debtor's salary. While this last statement is true of workers in all fields of endeavor, in civil service more than in other occupations the judgment creditor has little difficulty in collecting. Not only do such legal proceedings reduce the worker's income, but in addition result in a fall in his prestige.

Our next case is somewhat more difficult but is applicable to a greater number of people the great mass of workers in private industry. Let us find out how John Brown, a mechanic, might determine his future income. 

Courtesy Fordson Coal Company A SEASONAL WORKER IN PRIVATE INDUSTRY 

A worker engaged in this type of occupation has an uncertain income.

He cannot depend on a fixed salary determined by law. 

Although he may be paid weekly, he cannot be sure that his job is permanent. He may be laid off, his salary may be reduced, he may lose his job. His income is uncertain. 

Nevertheless, John Brown must budget his income. His need for planning is even greater than that of the civil service worker. He may reach a probable income in the following 

1. He can determine how much his average annual wage has been for the last few years. 

2. He can set a lower amount for his probable future income. It is wiser to be pessimistic about the future where spending is involved. Setting too high an amount may result in debt, while setting too low an amount may permit an additional sum for saving. 

3. If his work is seasonal, he can determine during what months or weeks he earns most or least and can make provision for necessary expenditures during the "lean period."

Now we arrive at the type of income that is most difficult to foretell. While such persons as businessmen, doctors, and other professional men come within this group, probably the best example to illustrate this type is a lawyer. His is a fluctuating income. He may sit in his office day after day without a client to visit him. A few unimportant cases with small fees may or may not come to his office. One day an important matter may arise that will net him a large sum. 

This may or may not re-occur the next day, the next month, or possibly the next year. One day his expenses for sheer necessities may be greater than his income; the next day he may make a very substantial sum of money. It is a most difficult problem for him to foretell his income, but by the same token it is most necessary for him to plan his expen

He must follow the same rules set for John Brown, but he must be even more pessimistic. He might try, in addi-

[Ch. 11 

tion, to obtain clients on an annual retainer. This means that in payment of a yearly sum by a client, the lawyer will agree to represent the client in whatever legal matters the client may have within that period. He must also build an ample reserve from previous years in law or from other sources. Most important of all, he must remember that large fees do not come at regular intervals. He must not get into debt with the expectation that the large fees he received one month will repeat themselves in the next. 

With the hope that the amount we have stated to be our probable future income will materialize into actual dollars and cents, let us turn our attention to the other half of our plan the spending plan. Here is further difficulty. 

Expenditures. The first step is the preparation of the items of expenditure. This, of course, will vary with the person or family preparing the plan. Your own personal budget might include fare to and from school, lunches, school supplies, candy, movies, dues for clubs, etc. As you grow older, you may be faced with other expenses, such as your own clothing. Your father has other expenses for which he must plan. These include rent, food, and clothing for the family.

A convenient listing of family expenses generally includes food, clothing, shelter (rent, gas, electricity, etc.), medical aid, recreation, education, and miscellaneous items. Since this itemization may not account for all expenses, expenses may be incurred that were not included in the budget. To avoid this, I. E. Lord, in her book Budgeting Your Income, provides a complete list containing about thirty-five headings in alphabetical order. Whatever the choice of arrangement may be, our budget-maker must remember to include all probable items of expense.

Our first step completed, we now must face an even more difficult job. How much should be allowed for each item?

The allotment to each of these items depends on many factors. Illness in the family may cause a cautious budgetmaker to apply a greater sum to probable future medical expenses than he might ordinarily need. Generally, however, the allotment for expenses depends on the following factors: 

1. Total Income: This, of course, limits the total expenditure. 

2. Standard of Living: This dictates the wants of the person or family. 

3. Getting Your Money's Worth: This reduces the cost of living and also provides additional sums for spending and saving.

While each applies its own pressure on the budget-maker, these three factors are nevertheless interrelated. The standard of living of a person is generally determined by that person's income, since fewer goods may be purchased with a reduced income. If a man earns $100 a week, he is more likely to buy a greater number of goods than a person earning $25 a week. On the other hand, however, a miser may have a higher income but may refrain from buying goods; thus his standard of living may be low. Then again, a careful spender with a low income may have a higher standard of living than a person with a higher income who squanders his money by buying goods that he does not need or use. 

As we study each of these factors, we shall see this interrelationship more clearly.

Many rules have been advanced to aid the budget-maker in his allotments. For example, one may often hear the rule that one-quarter of the monthly income should be the maximum for the item of shelter. Otherwise expressed, monthly rent should be no greater than a week's salary. 

While this rule may be a good "yardstick" for some, it may not do for all. The city dweller may have problems different from those troubling the farmer, or even of the

suburbanite, when he seeks a dwelling. Rentals in a city are generally higher than those in the country. 

Even within a city, one worker may find it more to his advantage to live in one locality than in another even if rents in the first are higher. One worker may decide to live close to his place of work and thus save the cost of fares and restaurant lunches. Another may decide to live some distance away and incur these expenses.

Probably the best way to determine these costs is to compute the average costs of the items in the past. Here, as in estimating income, care must be taken. An accurate record of previous years should be kept to serve as a guide for future budgets.

For additional guidance in planning our allotments to expenditure items, we might benefit from the experience of others in the past.

In about 1850 Dr. E. Engel, a German statistician, published the results of his studies of family budgets in various parts of Germany. About seventy years later our own 

United States Bureau of Labor Statistics compiled similar figures. An examination of the figures of both studies yields the following results.

As the family income increases, the percentage of income spent on the various expense items reacts in the following

Before proceeding further with this interesting contribution to our study of income and expenditures, we should

Ch. 11] 337 

remember here that these changes are in percentage and not amount. When a person's income increases, he does not eat less or spend less for food as this table indicates at first glance. The truth of the matter is that he spends more. If a man earning $1,000 spends forty per cent for food, he is spending $400. A man earning $5,000 may spend only twenty per cent for food, but in money he spends $1,000. 

This study, which is known as Engel's Law, should help us to understand that with a lower income greater care must be used in making the allotments to the elementary wants of food, clothing, and shelter, and that the amounts must be reduced to a minimum. Unless we do so, we must forego the advantages that flow from the satisfaction of the cultural wants of education and recreation. 

Summarizing the important aspects of budgeting, we may conclude: 

1. That total outgo must never exceed total income. 

2. That greater care is necessary when the income is of an uncertain or fluctuating nature. 

3. That greater care is necessary for those in the lower income brackets in order to make provision for the cultural needs. 

Standards of Living 

These studies also indicate the close relationship of income and our standards of living. A standard of living may be defined as the relative measure of the quantity and quality of the necessities, comforts, and luxuries to which a person or group is accustomed. Obviously the greater the income, the more goods can be purchased.

The terms used in our definition need further definition. 

What is meant by the phrase relative measure? We can measure length by inches or miles, and weight by ounces or tons, but we cannot assign a unit of measure for the way in

[Ch. 11 

which a person lives. We can measure this only by comparing one with another. For example, we say that a workingman in China has a lower standard than a workingman in America, or that one person has a higher standard than another.

Likewise, it is difficult to determine whether one thing is a necessity, comfort, or luxury. What may be a necessity to one person may be a luxury to another. An automobile may be a necessity to a traveling salesman who must make the rounds of customers in "out of the way" places. On the other hand, to a person who uses an automobile for traveling to and from work instead of using the subway, a car may be considered a comfort. Here, again, the measure must be relative.

Through the combined efforts of science and industrial management, the automobile is no longer considered a luxury. Yet under exceptional circumstances a car may be above the comfort class. When, for example, an average worker selects a high-priced car instead of a low-priced one, he is indulging in a luxury that he may not be able to afford. 

The terms quantity and quality should now be examined. 

While both the Chinese and the American must provide themselves with food and lodging, they may differ in the quantity and quality of these necessities. For example, workingmen in China are accustomed to a simple diet consisting mainly of rice and millet. In America workingmen are accustomed to a greater variety in their menus. Similarly, in China a workingman's family is content to dwell in one room with few conveniences. In America a workingman's family is accustomed to a larger number of rooms, equipped with such conveniences as steam heat, ice or mechanical refrigeration, and electric lights.

Lest we leave with a false impression concerning the American standard of living, we must note that there is no one standard of living in the United States, although it is

Ch. 11] 

339 

difficult to draw the line between one standard and another. 

For convenience, our standards of living in America have been classified under the following five headings with approximately twenty per cent of the population in each group.

Pauper Standard. When this standard is mentioned, we may think first of those who will not work, the hoboes who depend on handouts or free lunch kitchens for their meals, castoffs for their clothing, and a park bench, a hallway, or an abandoned hut for their shelter. But this group makes up only a small portion of those living on the pauper standard. A much larger group is made up of those who are dependent upon charity because they are aged, very young, sick, insane, or blind. For them, society makes some provision. Asylums, hospitals, and homes are built and maintained by government or private agencies. Also, the Social 

Security Act, discussed in a previous chapter, provides aid for the needy blind and aged, and dependent children.

A still larger group of people who at least occasionally are on the pauper standard is made up of able-bodied workers who cannot find employment. To aid this group in a period of widespread unemployment, public works are undertaken, such as the WPA and PWA projects of recent years. 

Also, many unemployed will in the future receive aid from the unemployment insurance provisions of the Social Security Act. In some instances direct aid or gifts are, as a temporary measure, needed for this group. 

Bare Subsistence Standard. The people within this group are but slightly better off than those classified as paupers. 

Probably the best example of this group is the self-supporting workingman in China. Content with one room for his entire family, a menu consisting of one inexpensive staple food like

[Ch. 11 

Courtesy Fred F. French Cos. 

A FAMILY LIVING ON THE BARE SUBSISTENCE STANDARD 

rice or millet, and inexpensive cotton clothing, he goes through life never rising above this subsistence standard. 

The American example of this standard lives in a tenement in the slum area. Gas or candlelight dimly illuminates one or possibly two rooms in which an entire family eats and sleeps. A window in one room may be the sole source of air. 

Plumbing is poor and insanitary. Toilets are in a room off the hall and are shared by all the families on the floor. Sometimes the toilet is in the yard. A bath is a rarity. Bread and potatoes constitute the chief items on the menu. Clothing is secondhand or, if new, consists of cheap garments, which barely protect from the frosty winds. 

This may be the picture of a person on the upper fringe of the pauper standard, a person on relief, let us say; or it may

Ch. 11] 341 

be the picture of one who is earning his own living but whose wage is so small that he can afford little better. 

It is for this group that minimum-wage laws are urged. A wage that will provide more than bare subsistence, that will give this group a wage that will provide at least a minimum for health and decency, is one of the pressing needs of our country.

Health and Decency Standard. This brings us to the third level-a standard that provides for health and decency. 

This includes better housing conditions; private toilet and bathroom for each family; sanitary plumbing in the kitchen and the bathroom; hot and cold running water; possibly steam heat; an icebox for food; adequate room for sleeping; a greater variety of good food, not necessarily expensive but more wholesome and nutritious; and warm clothing to protect the body. This is the picture that may be drawn of those living under this third standard of living-a minimum for health and decency.

Comfort Standard. The health and decency standard with a few additions becomes the comfort standard. The addition may, for example, be a home free from mortgage; mechanical refrigeration for the kitchen; a substantial fund set aside for that "rainy day"; less stinting in the matter of food; a fund for the boy or girl of the family who wants to attend college; a better car or perhaps a second car for some other member of the family who must get around. 

If we add all of these additional items to the family's goods, particularly the additional car, we are coming very close to the fifth and highest level. 

Well-To-Do or Luxury Level. As we approach the study of this level, which so few of us approach in real life, we can give our imagination full sway. It may include not one beautiful home, but several, among which might be a

[Ch. 11 

home in the city for the winter, a home near a southern beach for visits during the winter, and a home in the cooler parts of Maine for the warm summer months. 

Food of all varieties imported from foreign lands grace the table, even if some of this food may not be so nutritious as its cost would indicate. A wide variety of good clothing for every occasion is included within this standard of living. 

Yet, in spite of the huge sums of money spent for these items, Engel's Law teaches us that those whose incomes permit such expenditures nevertheless have a great deal more for recreation, education, and advancement than do those with smaller incomes. Cars, the best and most expensive schools, servants at home to cater to the whim and fancy of the members of the family and to relieve the mistress of the burdensome details of housekeeping are common to families in this group.

Relation of Standard of Living to Expenditures 

Now that we are acquainted with the various standards of living, we may well ask, "How will our standard of living determine our expenditures?"

It would seem that income, and income alone, is the chief factor in determining our standard of living. "Give me the money and I'll show you how to live" is a statement often heard. Since our income limits our purchases, our income and the amount of goods we possess go hand in hand. 

Yet, let us consider the "typical successful workingman."

He may be willing to sacrifice part of the allotment for food to the item of clothing. This is due to the fact that what he wears is more likely to be seen by his neighbors than is the food on his table. Thus we may see that, in order to maintain his standard of living, he is willing to make sacrifices in necessities for the sake of things that "will show."

Ch. 11] 343 

In his expense items beyond the necessities, his “entertainment" item may receive a larger share of his income than that of savings. This again reflects his desire to maintain a higher standard of living at the expense of future economic independence. 

Probably the best example of how one's standard of living affects one's allotment of income to expense is that of Dr. K. In order to put up a "front" and impress his patients, he lives in a fashionable neighborhood in New York. He sends his child to an exclusive private finishing school. He takes frequent trips to Europe. Yet his income is barely sufficient to provide all of these luxuries. He, too, is permitting his standard of living to dictate his allotments of income.

Many of us are led to purchase goods by the dictates of fashion or by another's standard of living, whether or not we have the funds to pay for these goods immediately. It is for this reason that the installment plan of payment has grown by leaps and bounds in the past decade.

It is not the purpose of this study to make misers of us. 

Nor is its purpose to avoid expenditures for items that will raise our standard of living. We must always seek to raise our standard and to permit this standard to assist us in making our allotments. But we must be careful not to set up a false standard. We should let our standard guide us in our allotments, and not dictate them. At the same time, we must not permit this false standard to force us into extravagant, useless, and unbusinesslike spending.

Wisdom in making the proper allotments of income, and intelligence in making the actual purchases will provide greater satisfaction in the long run. Under a program of this kind we can not only maintain our standard and reach economic independence sooner, but also raise our standard of living.

[Ch. 11 

We shall postpone a discussion of actual purchases until we have completed our budget and found a guide to economical, useful, and businesslike spending.

Extravagance vs. Economy in Spending. Our first con sideration is to determine what to us are necessities, comforts, and luxuries. Silk stockings, at the expense of warm clothing or wholesome food, are a luxury that must be dispensed with in a limited budget. The purchase of rich pastries and cake instead of more wholesome food indicates a lack of wisdom. 

The doctor to whom we referred, who sent his child to an exclusive and expensive private school instead of a public school provided by our city, is extravagant because he cannot really afford to maintain the standard of living he desires. 

We shall see later in our discussion of leisure-time activities that extravagance is shown to an even greater extent in the matter of recreation. We shall reserve this, however, for later study.

Before leaving this factor in budget-making, let us try to clear up a possible misunderstanding. You may say, and rightly so, "What's the fun of living if I can't indulge in a few extravagances occasionally? As long as I keep within my income, there's nothing wrong with that." It would seem at first glance that the complaint is just. But where will the money come from for these extravagances? 

If, for example, a stenographer is willing to do without a new winter coat and to wear her old one for a period of time until she has saved enough money for a fur coat, this is not extravagance. But if her old coat is threadbare or shabby, and does not protect her from cold, she is being extravagant, for she is willing to sacrifice her health and is doing without a necessity in order to provide herself with a garment which, though useful in itself, is more important for social prestige than for warmth.

Ch. 11] 

345 

BUYING A COAT 

The girl should determine whether she can afford a coat of this kind. 

When a young man falls victim to the slogan "Clothes make the man" and proceeds to enlarge his wardrobe, there is no great evil in his act. Even when he buys more ties than he needs and discards the old ones, which with a cleaning and pressing would look just as smart and fresh, he is committing no wrong. But when he takes from his budget part of the sum that he allotted for savings or for his college education, he is being extravagant. 

In our penal law, we find a crime called embezzlement. 

When a person is entrusted with certain funds for another person and he misappropriates these funds to himself, he is guilty of this crime. When the budget-maker steals funds

[Ch. 11 

from an item that he really needs and applies those funds to items that are unimportant, he should likewise be held guilty of a crime to himself, for which his punishment is a lower standard of living and an insecure economic independence. No good economic citizen should be guilty of such a crime.

Wastefulness and Usefulness. All of the little extravagances that we analyzed in the examples just mentioned were, nevertheless, in the purchase of some article that had a use. More serious than such purchases is the purchase of things that serve no use. These articles, to whose manufacture we referred in a previous chapter, are called illth, as contrasted with wealth, which constitutes the really useful things that are produced. No sane person would admit that he purposely bought a useless article. Unfortunately, the uselessness of an article is generally hidden in a cloud of airy advertising. Generally, too, these purchases are made on the spur of the moment, on impulse. Careful planning in advance will lessen the likelihood of impulsive spending. Our next chapter will assist us to go beyond the high-pressure salesmanship of the sponsors of these useless articles so that we may really get our money's worth. But for present purposes, if budget-making becomes a tedious task, let us keep always in mind the statement of Stuart Chase, a well-known writer, that "unbalanced spending leads to the consumption not of wealth, but of illth."

Financing of Purchases 

Businesslike Spending. Next we come to a consideration of how to finance the purchases we have decided to make. 

In this we have the advantage of studying certain sound business financing principles and applying those principles to our own purchases.

Ch. 11] 347 

When we studied financing operations in a retail business, we analyzed them from the point of view of the occupations that opened themselves to us because of the nature of these operations. Now, however, we shall study them from another point of view.

You will recall that these retail stores made sales on either the cash or the credit plan. The latter form permitted two methods, either straight credit with full payment at the end of, let us say, thirty days, or installment credit with partial payments every week or some other period of time. Now that we are purchasers, not applicants for jobs, we must decide which method to accept. 

Buying on the Installment Plan. Were it not for the great dangers to which installment buyers expose themselves, we might pass this topic by with but a few words of caution. 

But installment buying, or conditional selling as lawyers would say, has grown to tremendous importance within the last twenty years. The attraction of having a radio, a fur coat, or furniture, for example, at once for "a dollar down and a dollar a week" is great. The tempting advertisements and our own desire to "exercise our credit" have helped to make the installment plan a popular American finance arrangement. It has grown to such tremendous proportions that a special set of laws has had to be drawn to cover transactions under this plan.

Let us glance through the law books to see the important points covering purchasing on the installment plan. 

1. Although possession of the article is given to the purchaser, ownership remains with the seller until the last installment is paid. 

2. The buyer may not remove, sell, or mortgage the property without notifying the seller. Some contracts require

the consent of the seller to do so or even forbid the buyer from doing those things at all. 

3. If the buyer disposes of the property, even with the approval of the seller, he is still responsible for the price. 

4. If the goods are stolen, or lost by fire or otherwise, the buyer must pay for them. 

5. If the buyer misses a payment, the seller has the right to take back the goods even if part payment has been made. 

If the buyer has paid less than fifty per cent of the purchase price and he does not pay the balance or demand a resale by the seller to another person, the seller does not have to refund any payments made by the buyer. 

6. If the buyer has paid fifty per cent or more of the purchase price, or, having paid less, he insists on a resale, the seller must try to resell the article taken back. If the article brings in more than the balance due plus the expenses incurred in retaking and reselling the article, the amount that is left goes back to the original buyer. If the article sells for less than the balance due and the expenses, the original buyer must pay the difference. To illustrate this last case, let us assume that a fur coat was bought on the installment plan. 

The price of the coat was $1,000. Mr. B, the buyer, has paid $600 and can pay no more. The seller, Mr. S, takes the coat and resells it to Mr. C for $300. The seller has spent $50 for retaking and reselling the coat.
