CONDITIONAL BILL OF SALE OF PERSONAL PROPERTY ON INSTALLMENTS

7. Some contracts have additional and stricter provisions. 

We have learned before that when one owes a debt the creditor may, after winning a judgment, garnishee the debtor's wages. Some installment contracts provide, however, that, as security for the debt incurred in buying on the installment plan, the buyer assigns his entire wage. 

Though this provision in a contract is illegal, it occurs frequently. 

8. Although this point does not appear in law books, common sense will indicate that the price of an article on the installment plan is greater than the price of an article for cash. The seller takes the additional sum for lending you the goods before you pay for them; you must pay the charge for having the enjoyment of the article before you have paid for it in full.

As we proceed through this startling array of facts concerning installment-plan buying, it is little wonder that we approach an installment purchase warily.

Rules for Buying on the Installment Plan. Should we buy on the installment plan at all? Let us see whether sound business principles can guide us and help us to formulate rules in installment buying. 

1. Buy for cash whenever possible. Every businessman knows that cash payments for merchandise result in savings. 

Although he may be given liberal credit terms, he is also given discounts or reductions in price if he pays promptly. 

So, too, in your purchases, cash payment means reduced costs. 

2. Limit the use of installment buying strictly to the purchase of durable goods. As we saw in our study of finance and banking, two sources of funds are available to the businessman. If he wishes to borrow money for a long period, he will go to an investment bank. For shorter periods he can go to a commercial bank. If he needs funds to buy new

- 

machinery he will borrow from the first source because he knows that the machinery will last a long time and will slowly but surely pay for itself in increased earnings. Because it has lasting qualities, he is willing to accept a longterm loan and the investment banker is willing to grant it. 

On the other hand, when the businessman needs funds for raw materials or for coal to run machinery, he borrows for a short period, because after manufacture he will dispose of the finished product.

We should apply the same standards to our purchases. 

In the purchase of a home, a car, a typewriter, or other articles that may be enjoyed over a long period of time, the installment plan may be desirable. Such goods are known as durable goods. But to buy clothing, food, gifts, or other articles of comparatively short life on the installment plan shows poor financial policy. To do that would be as unwise as the action of a businessman who applies short-term loans to his building and machinery that cannot pay for themselves in a short time.

To illustrate this important point: A fearful customer dressed rather shabbily approached a salesman in a clothing store to purchase new clothing on the installment plan. The salesman recognized the man as one who had been outfitted by him only recently. Before selling this customer a new suit, he checked the man's past account and discovered that the man had paid only half of the price of his old suit. Here was a man who had purchased a relatively "perishable" good that became almost useless before the owner had completely paid for it. Now he was adding to that debt in order to get a new outfit to replace the old suit as yet unpaid for. Would you like to be in his place? 

3. Never undertake any considerable use of installment buying unless you first prepare a budget that will include future. payments. No wise businessman will borrow money unless

[Ch. 11 

he feels reasonably sure that he can repay the sum on the date due, nor will he extend credit to another unless he is certain of being repaid.

Many of us are so attracted by the small down-payment on articles offered for sale on the installment plan, that we wish to make the purchase whether we are sure that we can continue such payments or not. Installment dealers, on the other hand, do not worry too much about their purchasers' ability to make further payments since the law extends so many rights to them.

Before making a purchase on this plan, let us be sure that our future income will be sufficient to take care of the payments as they become due. We must be certain of this if we are to avoid the loss of the article, the money we paid, and the possibility of incurring further debt. 

Budget planning and installment buying must travel together. Mr. Jones was a hard-working barber, the father of five children. His one greatest desire, like that of all of us, was to make life pleasant for his family. The cost of outfitting the entire family with clothes seemed to be greater than his income. The installment plan settled his problemfor a short period. Succumbing to the blandishments of high-pressure advertisements and salesmen, he purchased new household furnishings, a complete wardrobe for his family, a radio, and even a wrist watch for his wife's birthday. 

Before he was through with this wave of buying, he found himself saddled with installment payments totaling $80 a month. His total income was only a $100 a month. Were it not for the kindly intervention of a friend who succeeded in reducing payments and who paid off some of the debt, Mr. 

Jones's family would have had their clothing and furniture, it is true, but they would have been unable to pay the rent to house the furniture and to buy food to nourish the bodies that wore the clothing.

Ch. 11] 

353 

4. Make some provisions to take care of future happenings that cannot be foreseen. We have noted before that, according to law, property bought under the installment plan belongs to the seller but that the buyer is responsible if the property is destroyed while in his possession. To avoid this danger, it would be wise to insure valuable articles against fire, theft, or other dangers that might threaten them.

In addition, to save them from being taken from you by the seller, set aside a savings account for them in case of a reduction in your income or loss of your job, and you will then have enough to tide you over. 

5. Finally, when making the purchase, read the contract carefully. Unscrupulous dealers generally protect themselves in every possible way against loss. Sometimes the contract is written in highly technical terms, in which case it would be wise to employ legal aid. Sometimes the contract is written in small print. Take the trouble to read your installment contract carefully and to be sure that you understand its terms.

Advantages of Buying on the Installment Plan. One might imagine from the strictness of the comments above that the installment plan should be avoided if at all possible. 

Although buying for cash is preferable, we must see both sides of the picture. Among others, the installment plan offers the following advantages, which must be weighed in the light of the evils attending it. 

1. It aids those who have steady incomes and who keep a budget to raise their standard of living. If Mr. X wishes to buy a car, he need not wait until he has saved the required amount of money. Nor is it necessary for him to charge the whole amount to one month's income, if he wishes to buy the car immediately. Instead, he may spread the cost over many months and thus avoid the necessity of further inconvenience.

[Ch. 11 

2. It permits us to enjoy commodities now instead of in the future. Despite the axiom that "anticipation is greater than realization," human beings place a lower value on goods that they are to receive in the future than on goods that they can enjoy immediately. The installment plan, by delivering goods for present enjoyment in return for a promise to pay in the future, gives us the opportunity to enjoy the greater satisfaction that we get from present goods. 

Testing the Budget 

Now our work of preparing the budget is almost done. 

But before we start to market to make our purchases, let us give the budget a few more tests. 

Living Today or Today and Tomorrow. Have you al lotted all your income to immediate needs, or have you set aside a surplus for deposit in the bank? Remember that your income won't go on forever. Is it fair to yourself to allow so much for movies and entertainment this month when it means a smaller deposit in the bank or no deposit at all? 

If your income should stop, you might be happy in the remembrances of the plays you saw or the parties you gave, but there is greater satisfaction in having a roof over your head and food and clothing for your body without depending on private charity or a benevolent government. 

It might be a good idea to deduct $5 from the item marked entertainment and increase the savings item by that amount. 

Purchasing for Today-or for Today and Tomorrow.

When we discussed the rules of installment buying, we distinguished between durable and perishable goods. An important point of difference between these two types of goods is that the former generally cost a greater amount of money. 

As a result, cheap imitations are offered to a gullible public.

Have you allotted enough money to buy a good carpet sweeper, or do you think it wiser to buy a cheap one? Mathematically, the latter choice is unsound. If the life of a good carpet sweeper is five years, an inferior carpet sweeper at even half the price is an unprofitable investment if its life is appreciably shorter than two and a half years. 

If you are planning to buy a wrist watch out of this month's budget, will you buy a good one or a cheap one?

Repair costs may soon eat away the difference in price. In addition, the cheaper watch will not serve the purpose intended, for you cannot trust it to tell the correct time. 

Do not get the impression that higher price always indicates better quality. We shall see later that this is not entirely true. But be sure that the durable good is of high quality. Make provision for it in your budget. 

Must You Really Make the Change? Durable goods are, as the name implies, long-lasting. What about the wrist watch or carpet sweeper you bought last year? Must you get a new one?

Perhaps the local watch repairer can make your old watch carry on. Perhaps the carpet sweeper needs only a new brush. It might be wise to investigate before discarding your old durable goods.

This suggestion is equally true of the less durable goods. like clothing and shoes. Each of these, carefully kept, can last a long time. Even after a long period of use they may still continue to be of service. Have that rip in your suit weaved together. Have your shoes soled and give them a shine. Your local tailor, shoe repairer, and carpenter devote all their time to prolonging the life of goods in your neighbor's home. Why don't you avail yourself of their services? 

Conspicuous Consumption. Now that your neighbor has been brought into this discussion, what items have you in-

cluded, not because you need them, but because your neighbor has them? What items have you included in order to "show-off" your wealth to your neighbor?

Some human beings never grow up. Little Brother John wants a G-Man gun because his friend has one. You may want a tie or a scarf similar to the ones being worn by your classmates. Your older sister wants a new hat because her friend just bought one. Mother wants a Frigidaire like Mrs. 

Smith's. And Father, good old settled Dad, is trading in his old car whose motor is still purring smoothly, because he says, "It's last year's model." 

Let us examine our expenditure list once more. Let us "prune" those items which are intended to imitate others or to show off. Is that large investment in jewelry really worth the sacrifice you must make in the necessities of life, or in your education and health?

Here again one must be careful. Mother may want that 

Frigidaire, not because of her desire to imitate her neighbor, but in order to keep foods fresher, in a more sanitary condition, and to make them last longer. The wise economic citizen will weigh the reasons for his want before reaching a decision. 

Finally, recheck your addition. Do your columns balance?

If your expenditures are higher than your income, keep pruning until you reach a balance. And now the wise planner is ready to "go to market" and execute the plan that he has so carefully prepared.

CHAPTER XII  

Problem of the Consumer. Armed with a good plan for spending our income, such as was developed in our last chapter, we go to market to purchase our goods--and here our troubles begin anew. 

If you have ever visited the grocer on an errand for your mother, you have but a slight inkling of the problems that will confront you when you go out into the market to purchase goods with your own hard-earned money. 

Of course, your mother has made your task very simple. 

If she wants a can of salmon, she will tell you what grocer she prefers, exactly what size and brand she wants, and the price you will pay. 

Suppose you were left on your own? Could you buy a can of salmon, other articles of food, a suit, a dress, or a pair of shoes, feeling certain that you got "your money's worth"? Going even further, is your mother sure that she is getting her money's worth for the salmon that she directed you to purchase? Was your father's suit, or his car, worth what he paid for it? 

What the Consumer Wants. 

The intelligent consumer has but three simple demands: (1) He wants to know the nature and the quality of the article he is purchasing in order to determine if it will satisfy his requirements; (2) he wants to know the exact quantity he is receiving in return for his money; (3) he wants to be charged a fair price for the quality and the quantity he is paying for. Only if these demands are satisfied can he be assured of getting his money's worth. 359

[Ch. 12 

Years ago, people expressed the belief that the consumer had little cause for worry because the economic forces of supply and demand would protect him. Adam Smith's theory of laissez-faire (see Chapter IX) applied also to consumers. 

Competition among sellers to dispose of their wares would compel them to give the consumer the right price, the right quality, and the right quantity.

Unfortunately, this has not worked out. Simple as are these demands of the consumer, they are not always easy of fulfillment in today's complex economic world. As we review the economic structure we analyzed from the vocational point of view, we shall see more and more stumbling blocks placed in the path of the consumer in search of his goal.

Production from the Point of View of the Consumer 

When we studied manufacturing, we learned that with changes in the process of manufacturing came changes in the products. We learned that new methods of production brought with them larger quantities of goods. We saw new products. We saw that products are standardized. We saw that products are made, not at home under mother's careful scrutiny, but in far-off places. How does each feature affect us, not as workers now, but as consumers?

Large-Scale Production. Producing goods in great quan tities has, on the whole, proved beneficial to the consumer. 

Prices of goods have been considerably reduced. Whereas in earlier days a man could afford but one suit over a long period of time, he may now purchase several suits at a cost equal to that of the one. The prices of radios, automobiles, and many other articles dropped considerably when the large-scale manufacturers came into those fields. 

While it is true that the savings in cost of production under this new method have been translated into cheaper

Ch. 12] 361 

150 135 120 105 76 

A FOURFOLD INCREASE IN STANDARD OF LIVING IN THE UNITED STATES 

1790 1800 1810 1820 1830 1840 1850 1860 1890 1900 1110 

REAL WAGES 

-1913 100 

Courtesy National Association of Manufacturers 

Modern production methods have made this possible. prices for the consumer, many people maintain that prices have not been sufficiently reduced. Professor Moulton, one eminent economist, points out that prices can be still further reduced. In addition, the high cost of marketing these goods, as we shall see later, has also taken its toll, with the result that the consumer is not getting the full benefit of these savings. In spite of all this, however, large-scale production has increased our real income and has enabled us to raise our standard of living.

Variety of Products. The fact that a greater variety of products is now manufactured serves as a decided advantage to the consumer. In the past, for lighting the consumer had only candles, gas, or oil. Today, in addition, he has the more efficient electricity. For recreation, in addition to the piano,

he may now select a victrola, a radio, talking pictures, and automobiles. To the conveniences at home he may now add the comfort of mechanical refrigeration and of air conditioning. These newer products, coupled with a cheaper price as the result of large-scale production, have made it possible for many people to enjoy a higher standard of living. At the same time our consumer, in search of a particular article to satisfy a particular want, can find it in a market that has a great variety of goods.

But this characteristic of modern production is not without its drawbacks. To the consumer a vast array of goods is offered for sale. New materials, such as rayon, are offered on the market. Even within the product itself variations are made. If you purchase a bathing suit, you will find any number of styles, varieties, and colors. Can you tell which will give you your money's worth?

Mechanical refrigeration has become a boon to mankind. 

Yet if you were to purchase a refrigerator, which make would you buy? What tests would you apply to determine if you are getting your money's worth? Our consumer becomes so confused that frequently he chooses wildly. If he makes a good choice from this wide selection, his is a greater fortune than that of his grandfather, who had but a limited choice. Woe to him, however, if he chooses poorly. Luck plays too important a role in purchasing needed goods. 

Standardization. In spite of the great variety of products available, there is another element in modern production that somewhat counteracts that feature. A machine cannot change the shape or form of each product it helps to produce. For example, each 1938 automobile or electric refrigerator is like the next of its particular type and model. 

Products of some types should be standardized. If a consumer buys a lamp for an electrical fixture at home, he should feel sure that it will fit the socket of that fixture. If

Ch. 12] 363 

badnad 

Courtesy Briggs Body Company STANDARDIZATION OF AUTOMOBILES 

Hundreds of bodies exactly like this one are turned out weekly. he buys leads to refill his mechanical pencil, those leads should fit his pencil. If he needs a new part for his automobile, he should be able to buy it without difficulty. 

Not only would the lack of standardization be inconvenient to the consumer, but it also would result in waste to him and to society. The local stationer or electrician would otherwise be required to keep a larger stock. Manufacturers would need more machinery to make the various sizes in use. The consumer would pay the resulting expenses in the form of higher prices. 

On the other hand, standardization can be carried too far. Certain personal items like clothing should not be too standardized. People generally prefer to dress as individuals.

[Ch. 12 

Your sister may have discarded a dress that is still serviceable, because she saw someone else wearing a dress like it. 

Your mother has probably shopped far and wide to get that "different" hat.

In passing, it might be said that women consumers have been more steadfast in their fight against standardization than men have. Standardization has made greater inroads in men's clothing than it has in women's. It is generally recognized that standardization in articles like sewer pipes and farm machinery is more desirable than in articles like women's hats or evening dresses.

Factory vs. Home. No longer do we know what ingredients went into the soup that we are having for dinner tonight. No longer do we know how carefully the wool was spun and weaved to make the clothing that we wear. The shift of manufacture from home to factory has created a multiplicity of problems for the consumer. 

The product frequently may be better. Perhaps the chef who prepared the soup knows more about cooking than does your mother. Probably the soup was made and canned under better sanitary conditions than your own kitchen affords. The factory worker, aided by his machine, was probably more efficient than your great grandmother, who spun the wool for the suit that your great grandfather wore.

On the other hand, sometimes the product may not be so good. Sometimes ingredients of inferior quality are put into canned products. The long period between cooking and eating may have destroyed the quality of the food and made it unwholesome. Perhaps the suit that you are wearing will not last as long as did your great grandfather's.

These and other problems beset the consumer who must buy the article on reputation alone, because the manufacturing is not done before his eyes. What is the quality of the product? Is the consumer getting his money's worth?

Ch. 12] 

365 

Waste. Before we leave the field of production as seen by the consumer, we must touch upon one very important part of this field. When we studied the field in search of vocations, we purposely overlooked those which serve no useful purpose to society as we did those which were harmful to society.

Yet if we are to be intelligent consumers, we must be fully aware of their existence and take steps to crush them if we are to get our money's worth. 

When we last discussed the subject of waste, we stated that wasteful purchases are due in great part to lack of planning. Buying on impulse, we said, could be curbed by budgeting. Although no sane person would admit that he planned the purchase of useless articles, nevertheless the sums so spent are staggeringly high. Even with planning, unless the consumer is cautious, he may purchase illth instead of wealth.

Producers are in business because they manufacture goods that satisfy human wants. If they can convince consumers that these wants will be satisfied by the use of their product, they will continue in business. 

A few of the reasons why consumers purchase waste, in spite of careful planning, are: 

1. The purchase may be caused by lack of knowledge or by misinformation concerning the articles to be purchased. 

Among the commodities purchased for this reason are patent medicines that usually do no good and, in fact, often prove harmful; the services of quack doctors; adulterated foods; such books as How to Become Wealthy by authors who themselves are penniless; hair rejuvenators that do not rejuvenate hair; reducing machines that often reduce your purse more than your weight; shoe whiteners that do not whiten shoes; and scores of others that have been exposed in various books dealing with these useless and sometimes harmful goods.

[Ch. 12 

The wise planner may have set aside a sum for expenses under the item of "Health." The money will avail him naught if he uses the services of quacks or if he purchases certain drugs and patent medicines, which are offered to him on the market. Read a book like 100,000,000 Guinea

Pigs by Kallet & Schlink and discover how useless and harmful these purchases are. 

2. The purchase may be caused by bad habits of the consumer. Purchases of drugs or of excessive amounts of intoxicating beverages simply confirm such habits. 

Unfortunately in a study of this kind we can do little for the purchasers of this last group of commodities. We can, however, point out that, aside from moral grounds, these commodities generally represent waste and as such are a drain on one's funds that could be put to a more beneficial purpose. 

Stuart Chase, in his highly entertaining book entitled

Tragedy of Waste, lists these and many more examples of waste or illth.

He treats this subject from a broader or social point of view, instead of the personal point of view we adopt here. 

He points out not only the waste of human effort in the purchase of these articles, but also the waste of human effort and capital in the manufacture and merchandising of these articles. With the last activity he includes the great quantities of necessarily false and misleading advertising that must be undertaken to sell these useless, harmful commodities to a gullible public.

Care in the purchase of commodities with respect to their usefulness will aid greatly in releasing a substantial part of your income and permit you to enjoy a higher standard of living on your present income. In your expenditure for health, for example, you should place yourself under the care of a good physician instead of visiting a quack doctor or attempting to cure yourself with the useless "cure-alls"

Ch. 12] 367 

offered for sale. In the long run, even if the good doctor charges a higher fee, you will find the latter method the less expensive way.

Merchandising From the Point of View of the Consumer One might imagine that the survey we have just made of the effects of modern production offered enough problems to Mr. Consumer, but his troubles are not yet over. Our complex system of merchandising the product provides many more problems for Mr. Consumer in search of "his money's worth."

Wide Choice of Products. Under production we spoke of the great varieties of products from which the consumer may make his choice. To make this problem even more complicated, our merchandisers add to our troubles. They may sell one producer's product in a variety of ways. They may sell it in bulk form or in packages. They may vary the size, shape, or color of the package. They may adopt a series of different trade names and thus market the same product at varying prices-all to the confusion of the consumer. 

Loose Form vs. Package Form. If you go to the grocer's for sugar, you may select between purchasing it loose or in package form. Coffee, prunes, beans, and other commodities may be similarly purchased. Which type will you choose?

The advantages of buying food in bulk or loose form are: 

1. Generally the price of the "loose" product is lower.

Since the producer or merchandiser saves the expense of boxing and of higher transportation costs, he can afford to sell the loose product cheaper. 

2. Since the product is not hidden under wrappings (cardboard, paper, or metal), you can actually see the

[Ch. 12 

product instead of guessing as to the contents of the package. 

Some packers now make use of cellophane so as to give the consumer the opportunity to see, if not feel and smell, the product. Even this new method of packing is not without its defects. Colored cellophane is used by some packers to mislead the consumer. Packed in yellow-tinted cellophane, noodles will give the appearance of rich, egg-laden food.

Removed from their wrapper, these noodles take on a much lighter hue. In like manner, the greenness of peas may be simulated by the use of green-tinted cellophane.

The disadvantages of buying food in bulk or loose form are: 

1. There is danger of contamination. Products thus exposed may be handled by customers. Floor sweepings and germ-laden dust may enter the product and in some instances may cause illness to the consumer. 

2. The Pure Food and Drug Act does not aid the purchaser of bulk products. Prunes, for example, come to the retail grocer in wooden boxes of 25 or 50 pounds each. While these containers may state the additional ingredients used in the preservation of prunes, the consumer who purchases one pound does not know what appears on the box. However, while packaged goods do have the advantages of avoiding contamination and of coming under the provisions of the Pure Food and Drug Act, it does not follow that these products are always free from danger, as we shall see shortly. 

Preserved and Fresh Foods. Closely akin to the problem of choosing between bulk and packaged goods, our merchandisers have given us still another problem with which to contend. There has been a growing tendency in industries dealing with perishable products to provide the consumer with products that, by means of preservation, can be kept over long periods of time. Heinz' 57 brands of food, Campbell's soups, and other products too numerous to mention here, enjoy wide use.

Ch. 12] 369 

Preservation of food products is accomplished in a variety of ways. Bottling or canning processes provide the chief methods. The food is prepared and cooked in advance, and sometimes a preservative is added. The food is then placed in airtight containers.

The advantages of having food prepared in this fashion are many: 99 

1. It permits consumers to obtain products "out of season." Preserved peaches, strawberries, and scores of other seasonable merchandise are available all year round. 

2. A greater variety of products is made available. 

Smyrna figs, Russian caviar, and other delicacies of foreign climes are available because preservation prevents their decay before reaching our shores. 

3. Food may be stored for time of need. Although a drought may destroy our corn crop, an ample stock of canned corn will prevent a scarcity of that product for a while at least. 

4. This method is very convenient for the modern housewife. She avoids the necessity for spending long hours in the kitchen to prepare foodstuffs. Vegetables, soups, meats, and other foods have already been cooked and are ready to serve. Merely heating the contents of the can and then opening the can are the only kitchen chores that the housewife need perform in preparing preserved foods. 

The disadvantages of processed and preserved foods are as follows: 

1. We do not always know the quality of the product when it is preserved. Usually the best quality of food is canned, although it is still possible for an unscrupulous packer to use food of poor quality for canning. Our Food and Drugs Administration has at times been compelled to seize numerous cans containing food that was tainted or spoiled. The condition of the can may be some guide to the

[Ch. 12 

condition of the contents; viz., if a can is "swelled," the product within is probably in an unhealthful state. 

2. The use of preservatives has at times proven harmful to consumers. Thanks to the pure food laws, many harmful preservatives have been outlawed. Other preservatives, such as benzoate of soda in small quantities, are still permitted, although there is divided opinion among scientists as to whether even this preservative is entirely harmless. The label must indicate the kind of preservative and the amount that has been used. If you will examine the syrup jar on the counter of your local candy store, you will find that its label will probably read: "1/10 of 1% benzoate of soda added." 

3. Preserved food may lack certain desirable food qualities found in fresh foods. 

Medical opinion is divided as to whether canned foods retain the vitamins and other desirable food elements found in fresh foods. Some say that the long delay between processing and eating destroys or dissipates the essential food constituents. Others say that they remain intact. Still others maintain that the food contained in cans may be of better quality than food purchased from the "green goods" man. In a large city like ours, fresh foods are subject to delay in transportation. Canned goods are generally processed near the place of growth.

Sometimes arsenic or some other deadly poison is sprayed on fresh fruit to destroy insects. Fresh fruit should therefore be carefully scoured before being eaten. Unless the housewife is as careful as the canner who cleans the fruits carefully, these poisons may affect the members of her family. 

Variations in Size. With the coming of packaged and processed goods have come additional problems that the consumer must handle. Exactly how much bread is a loaf of bread? How much fish is there in a can of salmon? How many ounces of juice are in a can of pineapple juice?

Ch. 12] 371

CO. DOE 

BRAND VANILLA EXTRACT I FL. OZ.

RELATION OF SIZE OF BOTTLE TO CONTENTS 

The larger bottle has the smaller volume because it has a smaller space inside for the extract.

Although the contents by weight or measure are stated on the can, some unscrupulous packers will put up their product in packages, cans, or bottles almost the size that the average consumer recognizes; e. g., a thirteen-ounce jar looks about the size of a pint jar. At the present time, there is a movement to standardize various products. A "loaf" of bread will actually mean a certain definite weight. 

The use of

Use of Brand Names and Trade-Marks. 

brand names and trade-marks by manufacturers is not of recent origin. The use of symbols on articles of manufacture

[Ch. 12 

goes back to ancient Rome. It was and still is a means of knowing who manufactures or sells the product that you purchase. Even the western ranch owners use a brand to distinguish their cattle from others. Our law provides that brand names and symbols may, with certain limitations, be registered at the United States Patent Office and be protected from infringement by others. There are about 500,000 trade-marks in use today, and about 200,000 of those are registered with the Patent Office.

This method of selling goods under a brand name helps the consumer a great deal. It enables him to use the symbol as some standard of value. If, for example, a consumer has found the X brand of salmon to be the best he has tasted, he can repeat his purchase of the same brand. On the other hand, the consumer may be adversely affected. A few of the criticisms leveled at the use of brand names are as follows: 

1. Many people maintain that the trade name is not a good standard of value because the product may have variations in quality even under the same brand name. Only the manufacturer's conscience and reputation compel him to retain the same standard of quality. He may change the quality after establishing his reputation and thus fool the consumer. Fortunately, most merchants try to improve the quality of their goods rather than to lower it. 

2. The same product may be sold under different brand names at differing prices. It has been found that some merchants, knowing that the consumer relies on brand names, have offered products for sale under various assumed names. Such simple and inexpensive products as cold cream or lye may be offered to the consumer under other, more attractive names. The gullible consumer thinks he is getting an entirely new product and is willing to pay a higher price.

Many so-called "medicines" with fancy names have been analyzed and found to be elementary in nature, some useless,

Ch. 12] 373 

others harmful. Were these products offered for sale under their true names, few people would buy them. Dressed up in unusual names suggesting cure, they tempt the consumer. 

3. Brand names may be imitated. In spite of the protection offered by our laws, many brand names have been simulated by unscrupulous manufacturers. As a result, careless consumers sometimes purchase inferior imitations of the real thing. 

4. Brand names may lead to monopoly. So thoroughly do manufacturers or merchandisers pound their particular brand name into the minds of consumers that consumers fear to purchase goods of like or better quality that do not bear that particular manufacturer's brand. Each manufacturer insists that his product is different from all others. The "Accept No Substitute" slogan used by many manufacturers results in removing from the market products of other manufacturers even though those products may be better in quality and cheaper in price. 

5. Choice of brand may be determined by a strong advertising campaign rather than by actual quality. The purchaser of the X brand of salmon may have bought it because it was advertised as best, not because he had found it to his liking. Many consumers were converted to the use of a certain brand of coffee because the merchandiser of this coffee was clever enough to engage a well-known entertainer to advertise the product. Since all producers offer their products as the "Best That Money Can Buy," the consumer is at a loss to determine which truly will give him his money's worth. 

6. A brand name adds an expense that the consumer must pay. The particular name that a product enjoys does not enhance the satisfaction which may be derived from it. 

Yet the consumer must pay for it. In his search for a standard of quality, the consumer must buy "by the label," since no other standard of value is available. He must therefore stand the expense involved in bringing this label to his attention.

7. Brand names are an example of economic waste. The retailer must carry many brands of the same product in order to satisfy his customers. When we realize the great number of brands of salmon, canned peas, fruits, and others that a local grocer must stock; the huge sums spent for advertising each; and the armies of workmen whose efforts were utilized in the manufacture, merchandising, and advertising of this great number of brands, we cannot help feel that some other, more economical method might be used to judge the quality of a particular product. 

Under the recently passed Feld-Crawford Law in our state, the manufacturer of goods sold under a brand name may dictate the price at which his article is to be sold by the retailer. Under this law the local grocer may not sell the product below the established price in order to induce customers to enter his store. The consumer is therefore required to pay a price fixed by the manufacturer, and is given no choice to "shop around" for bargains. The rise in price of a certain tooth paste from 21 cents to 33 cents a tube is an example of the result of price-fixing under this law. 

Advertising. As if the merchandiser had not made the consumer's problem sufficiently complicated, he calls upon his henchman, the advertiser, who further befogs the consumer's path to getting his money's worth.

When we analyzed the field of advertising in search of vocational opportunities, we saw the function advertising performed. We learned that the purpose of advertising is to convey information to the consumer to induce him to buy the advertiser's product. We did not at that time examine some of the methods used by copy-writers and their fellow workers to induce consumers to purchase that product. 

These inducements are known in the jargon of advertising as appeals. The appeals may be directed to a reader's emotions or to his intelligence. For example, appeals may be

Ch. 12] 375 

made to fear, to vanity, or to mother love. Only occasionally does an advertisement appeal to the intelligence. Banks generally print their bank statement of assets and liabilities to attract depositors. Such advertisements are directed to the saver's intelligence. Unfortunately, the average consumer does not fully understand the items appearing in a bank statement and therefore gains nothing from this informative advertisement.

Advertisements should be read with a great deal of caution. Even those which deign to appeal to the consumer's intelligence rather than to his emotions may contain information that is misleading. Even in the more closely watched commodity groups, such as foods and drugs, false and misleading information may enter the reader's mind through advertisements. Since the Pure Food and Drugs Act forbids false data on the label of the product, an unscrupulous advertiser will insert false statements in the advertisement hoping that the consumer will transfer this information, in his mind's eye, to the label itself and thus defeat the purpose of the Pure Food and Drugs Act. 

The New York State Penal Law makes it a crime to ad vertise false and misleading statements. The Federal Trade 

Commission has some control over advertising. But, as we shall see later, these means are not so effective as we would like them to be.

It should be understood that the advertisement serves a very useful purpose. It increases sales, thus giving the manufacturer an opportunity to use large-scale production methods. This results in lower prices. 

As we return once more to the economic structure of merchandising, we encounter further difficulties.

Middlemen. When we studied the field of merchandising, you were doubtless amazed at the great number of steps that each article takes on its trip from the maker to the

[Ch. 12 

consumer. We tried to justify the necessity for each of these steps and for the existence of the wholesaler, the retailer, and others who do not add to the quality of the article but nevertheless receive a profit for the services they perform. 

As consumers, however, we become interested in them for the profits each receives, for these profits are added to the retail sales price of the article. When we discover that, of the 5 cents we pay for an orange, only 34 cent goes to the fruit-grower and the balance of 44 cents goes to middlemen, we begin to wonder whether, in this instance at least, we are not paying too much for services furnished by middlemen. 

Retailers. Closely allied with this problem of merchandising expense that we must meet comes another interesting problem: In what type of retail store are we most likely to get our money's worth?

We learned that various types of retailers offer their wares for sale to consumers. Chief among them are the manufacturing retailer, the independent retailer, the chainstore retailer, the department store, and the mail-order house. 

Since the last is a special type of retailer catering primarily to those outside the city who cannot conveniently visit the store to make purchases, we shall limit ourselves to a discussion of the first four, which abound in the city. 

Manufacturing Retailers. The chief advantage offered to the consumer who purchases from the manufacturing retailer is the apparent savings in merchandising costs. The slogan "From Maker to Wearer" is intended to convey to the consumer the idea that by purchasing from such a retailer he will save the costs going to the middlemen. Adopting our orange example: instead of the 5 cents we pay to the local fruiterer, we can purchase the same orange from the grower at 34 cent, thus apparently saving 44 cents.

This sales point is not true. As we have seen before, if the producer is to take advantage of the lower cost of pro-

Ch. 12] 377 

duction brought about by a large-scale production, he will have a large quantity of goods of which to dispose. He will therefore be compelled to open many retail stores in order to dispose of his product. He will have to assume all of the duties imposed upon the middleman: of opening stores; of transporting the merchandise (which in the orange illustration is an important cost); of holding the goods till wanted; of providing a shipping staff, a sales staff, and an accounting staff. He must therefore demand a higher price from the consumer than from the wholesaler who relieves him of those burdens. He will demand a higher profit for the trouble he has been compelled to face. Whether or not this profit is less than the combined profits of the middlemen is a serious question that the consumer must decide. The consumer must not forget that services must be paid for. Middlemen are necessary and are entitled to a payment for service. The payment must not be exorbitant, however. 

Chain Stores vs. Independent Retailers. The department store and the chain store, particularly the latter, maintain that they can serve the public better than the independent retailer. The rapid growth of the chain-store system has made deep inroads in the financial success of the local storekeeper.

This matter has become so serious that independent retailers have organized to combat the "chain-store evil."

Legislation of all kinds has been proposed to curb the rapid growth of chain stores. The Federal Wright-Patman Act, although not specifically called an anti-chain store act, is nevertheless designed, according to many, to be such a law in fact. We shall see how it operates later in our discussion. 

Advantages of Buying from Chain Stores. Some of the advantages claimed for the chain-store system of retailing are: 

1. Chain stores make possible cheaper prices to consumers. Chain stores can achieve cheaper prices because (1)

[Ch. 12 

A central purchasing unit can get lower prices from manufacturers because of volume purchases to supply many stores. (2) There is a more rapid turnover in stock. Instead of being compelled to keep goods on hand for a long time, chain stores can dispose of goods rapidly and thus lower their expenses. This, in turn, is translated into lower prices for consumers. (3) Efficient scientific management makes it possible to cut administrative expenses. 

SCALE 4 HAMMER 

Las 

Courtesy R. H. Macy & Co., Inc.

TEST TO DETERMINE RESISTANCE OF CHINA TO BREAK- ABILITY AS PRACTICED BY A LARGE DEPARTMENT STORE

Ch. 12] 379 

2. Chain stores have a chance of providing a better quality of merchandise because (1) A chain store can demand a higher standard of quality from manufacturers since it has great bargaining power. (2) A chain store or department store can afford to maintain testing laboratories to determine the quality of the merchandise they offer for sale. R. H. 

Macy & Co., a department store in this city, maintains such a laboratory. (See the illustration on page 378.) 

3. Chain stores are able to render better service because (1) A chain store can train its employees scientifically and provide consumers with an efficient and well-trained staff of salesmen. These employees can be of greater service to the consumer and can provide intelligent help in determining the consumers' wants and how they can be satisfied. Trained as they are, they will keep foods clean and fresh and maintain this cleanliness in the store itself. (2) The savings in merchandising can be used to provide additional conveniences to consumers.

Contentions of Independent Merchants. The arguments of local independent retailers throw a different light on the situation. They claim: 

1. The matter of price is affected by various conditions: (1) Although volume purchases have resulted in lower costs to chain-store buyers, these savings are not translated into lower prices for consumers. Actually these savings are pocketed by the so-called "efficient" managers. (2) The lower prices that chain stores offer to the public come, not as the result of bulk purchases, bulk sales, and efficient management, but as the result of unfair pressure on the manufacturers. Since the chain store does wield the weapon of bulk purchases, it has an unfair bargaining power. In order to get the order, the manufacturer will sell at a lower price to one purchaser and a higher price to another. The 

Patman Act was designed to do away with this price dis-

[Ch. 12 

crimination. Its purpose was to compel the manufacturer to charge the same price to all his customers, with certain exceptions. (3) Cutthroat competition is often present. A chain store will sell merchandise below cost in a particular store in order to drive out a local rival retailer. Since chain stores have huge funds at their disposal, they can afford to do this until the local retailer is driven out of business. Having achieved their goal, they then increase their prices considerably since they then have a monopoly. (4) The lower prices of chain stores are the result of "sweated labor." The manufacturer pays low wages to his workers so that he can sell to chain stores at lower prices. In addition, the chain stores pay low wages to their employees and require them to work long hours. The local retailer's hour-wage-level is better than that of the chain store. The Feld-Crawford Act is justified on the ground that it prevents the larger stores from cutting prices at the expense of labor. (5) The so-called "efficient" managers are motivated by personal interests rather than those of the stockholder and the consumer. 

Sometimes "feathering one's nest" is more important than efficient organization and administration of sound methods of merchandising. 

2. The quality of merchandise is not better in chain stores. 

The pressure of bulk purchases by chain stores is directed, not for better quality, but for cheaper prices. The so-called laboratories maintained by these stores are merely "advertising stunts" to fool the public. The scientists in these laboratories may find information to benefit their employers, but they are instructed in advance as to what they will make public to the consumer. On the other hand, the local retailer tells us that he does not entrust the testing of quality to others. He himself supervises the purchase of articles that he offers for sale. In effect, he is giving a personal guarantee of the quality of the merchandise he selects for his store.

Ch. 12] 381 

3. The cost of the services provided by chain stores is passed on to the consumer. Any service rendered by a chain store costs money, which the consumer must pay for in the form of higher prices. The independent retailer is willing to provide similar services if the consumer is willing to pay for them.

On the other hand, the local retailer offers a unique service that the others, because of their size, cannot perform. The employees of these large companies handle each customer impersonally. The employee is merely a cog in a large wheel. 

He is not interested in the product he sells or in the customer. The local retailer lends a personal touch to each transaction. He is interested in each of his customers. He is friendly. He is personally acquainted with the product he sells and the customer who purchases it. He knows the demands of his particular type of trade and knows how best to satisfy these demands.

A Last Word. We may now see the herculean task of determining what to buy, confronted as we are by this vast array of goods on the market. We must not throw up our hands in disgust, although that may seem the easiest way. We must face the problem. We must seek improvement. We must take the hard way. Above all we must not expect miracles. 

We can do something, but we cannot expect a cure-all.

CHAPTER XIII  

Attitude of the Economic Citizen. We learned in the preceding chapter that buying in the modern market is a difficult task. Many buyers think that the problem is so difficult that they make no special effort to buy intelligently. They surrender to this problem just as a lazy student may surrender to an intricate algebra problem. 

The economic citizen who has worked hard to earn his money refuses to dissipate it in the payment of high prices for inferior or useless goods. He wants his money's worth. 

He knows that when he purchases a useless article, he is furthering the progress of a "waste" industry; that every dollar he spends in this fashion is a vote cast in favor of continuing this waste. 

He knows that each dollar spent unwisely means a lower standard of living for him; that every dollar he spends in this way lowers the standard of living of the entire country. 

If the great amount of human effort employed in providing us with illth were released and put to work to provide us with wealth, such as low-cost housing and the manufacture of other necessities, the standard of living of all would be materially increased. 

Urged on by these motives of good citizenship and personal savings, our economic citizen examines the problem of getting his money's worth and seeks a scientific solution to his problem.

Inadequacy of Methods in Use. If we should take stock of the methods in use today for trying to get our money's worth, we would soon realize that the average consumer 383

[Ch. 13 

goes to market with but little knowledge of the nature and the quality of the product he intends to buy. Some of the guides he uses are unscientific; some are economically unwise; and, in many cases, the guides he uses may even be ridiculous. Let us examine a few to determine their soundness.

Guides Used in Buying Dependence on Neighborhood Standards. 

Many consumers depend for guidance in buying on the customs and habits of their neighbors and on neighborhood standards. 

In many instances these customs and standards are not founded on good common sense. Thus he is prevented from getting his money's worth.

For example, Mr. Consumer may buy a special type of suit, not because it fits his needs, but because his friends and neighbors have set the style, which he now follows blindly. 

If checked suits are "the rage," he buys a checked suit, whether he is fat or lean, short or tall. He disregards the fact that choice of proper design of cloth and suit may help to overcome any possible defects in physique. Striped cloth 

AN OPTICAL ILLUSION IN CLOTH DESIGNS TO OVERCOME DEFECTS IN PHYSIQUE 

Both lines are the same length. adds to one's height; checks tend to broaden one. Instead of following simple rules of design, he follows the dictates of style.

Ch. 13] 

385 

Nor is Mrs. Consumer without blame. Even in her hats, she, too, follows the dictates of fashion. If small hats are in style, she buys a small hat, whether it is becoming to her face or not. If it is customary to wear one's best clothes to visit the grocer or the butcher, she will wear hers, unmindful of whether or not her clothing may become soiled. 

She pays in comfort and sometimes even in health to follow the dictates of style. Many women wear low-cut shoes with high heels although more sturdy shoes with low heels would be more suitable. Because false neighborhood standards demand sheer stockings at all times, Mrs. Consumer may wear those stockings even when performing household tasks when heavier hose would give better wear. 

Dependence on Advertising. The average consumer may accept the advertiser's blurb. If an advertisement suggests that the X Brand of bed sheet is best, the consumer accepts the suggestion as the truth. If the advertiser includes a testimonial of some housewife or scientist, the consumer is thoroughly convinced. He does not bother to check on the skill or honesty of the testimonial-giver, or whether the testimonial is given for pay or because of an earnest desire to acquaint the public with the quality of the merchandise. 

One instance of the unworthiness of some testimonials may be sufficient to prove this point. In a nationally advertised cigarette advertisement a well-known football coach was quoted as saying he did not find a certain cigarette to be harmful. This statement was true because this football coach did not smoke cigarettes.

Psuedo-Scientific Methods. Some consumers rely on un scientific and often meaningless tests. For example, in buying cloth they "feel the material." This test may not indicate the true quality of the product because certain producers "weight" or adulterate their products with inferior materials to give the appearance of good quality or greater quantity.

[Ch. 13 

Reliance on Price. Some consumers rely on price as an indication of quality. A better article generally costs more. 

Yet it has been found that price, too, is a poor indicator of quality. Producers may take advantage of this reliance and increase price without increasing quality. G. H. Johnson, in his book Textile Fabrics, tells us, "Tests of the warp and filling strength of five Turkish towels indicated that a 25 cent towel was clearly a better buy than a 50 cent towel and that a $1 towel was clearly a better buy than a $1.50 one." S. 

Schlicter, in his Modern Economic Society, states: "A store found that summer underwear was going slowly at 59 cents a suit. On ‘dollar day,' it was marked up to two for $1.30, at which price it sold rapidly." A recent report of Consumers' 

Union indicates that a bathing suit selling for $2.95 was a better buy than one more widely advertised, selling for $4.95. 

On the other hand, some consumers are lured by bargain sales. To them, any kind of sale represents a saving. Many sales, however, are fraudulent. "Going out of business" sales have been known to last for long periods of time. Some bankruptcy or fire sales are positively false. The merchandise offered in these sales is usually inferior to regular-priced goods. 

This type of sale has become so popular with merchants who are not going out of business, who have not had a fire, and who have not gone into bankruptcy, that a New York City ordinance was passed to prevent fraudulent sales of this kind. This law provides that a merchant must get a permit before running a sale of this kind. 

Similar to this lure is the use of loss leaders in otherwise legitimately operated grocery or other retail stores. The merchant selects one well-known product and offers it for sale at a price far below the cost of the product to the retailer himself. He does this in order to attract customers to his store. Then, when the customers go to the store, they generally buy other items. Imagine their consternation when

Ch. 13] 

387 

they discover that their bill is the same as or higher than that which they would have paid in another store that had no "loss leader." The merchant accomplishes this feat by increasing prices on his other products. 

Even should the prices of all his merchandise be low, another matter should be considered. Often merchants can charge less because they pay manufacturers a low price. The manufacturer in turn reduces wages. The result is "sweated labor," as we saw in the previous chapter. Does the economic citizen want low prices at the expense of "sweated labor"?

In summary, then, we see that all of the methods used by the average consumer are inadequate. Probably the consumer's best protection is the reputation of the retail merchant and the manufacturer. If he buys only from retail merchants who have a high reputation for handling worthwhile goods, the chances are that goods of a suitable quality at a price that will be reasonably fair will be offered to him. 

A good economic citizen in search of his money's worth will go even farther, however, in trying to make certain his buying is done with the utmost skill. He will continually seek better methods for use in judging the quality and prices of goods that are offered to him. 

Aid Given by the Federal Government 

According to a report prepared by the National League of Women Voters, it has been found that there are at least twenty-five government agencies in the Federal Government that try to help the consumer. We shall examine the activities of a few of these agencies in order to discover how effective government aids really are. 

Department of Agriculture. Chief among our Federal 

Government's contributions to consumer protection is the work done by the Department of Agriculture.

[Ch. 13 

Bureau of Animal Industry. This bureau of the Department of Agriculture is charged with the inspection of meats for interstate shipment. An inspector of this bureau carefully examines the carcass of an animal and, if he is satisfied that it is free from disease, he places a stamp of approval on the carcass. This blue stamp of approval is an indication that, when examined, the meat was fit for human consumption.

Food and Drug Administration. This division of the De partment of Agriculture was organized in 1927 for the purpose of handling a group of acts that are designed "primarily to promote purity and truthful labeling in certain commodities essential to the public health and economic welfare of the nation."

The six acts administered by the Food and Drug Admin istration are: 

1. The Insecticide Act, which protects farmers, fruitgrowers, market gardeners, stock and poultry raisers, householders, and others from buying and using insecticides and other similar products that fall below the strength claimed for them, that will not accomplish the results promised, or that are injurious to plants. 

2. The Import Milk Act, which prohibits the entry into the United States of milk and cream that have not been produced under prescribed sanitary conditions, that do not come from healthy herds, or that do not meet certain specified standards at the time of entry. 

3. The Tea Act, which provides for the examination of all tea offered for entry into the United States and the admission of only such tea as meets the standards of quality, purity, and fitness for consumption set by the United States Government. 

4. The Caustic Poison Act, which requires special labeling on certain products in order to safeguard the household

Ch. 13] 389 

 POISON POISON POISON LABEL RECOMMENDED BY THE CAUSTIC POISON ACT 

against accidental injury from ammonia, lye, carbolic acid, and other dangerous substances commonly used in the home. 

5. The Naval Stores Act, which is designed to prevent deception in transactions involving such commodities as resin and turpentines, by authorizing the department, to examine, analyze, and grade the products. 

6. The Pure Food and Drug Act, which is the most important of all the laws this agency enforces. It was passed by Congress in 1906 after it was learned that many lives had been lost during the Spanish-American War, not in battle, but because soldiers had been fed contaminated foods. 

This act forbids the sale of adulterated or misbranded foods, beverages, drugs, and medicines. However, this law was passed when public anger was at its height, and at a time when industry had not reached its present complexity. 

As a result, this act, though amended as recently as 1935, has been found to contain various defects and has been subject to a great deal of criticism. Some of the points criticized are: (a) Limited number of commodities that can be regulated. The act does not regulate the sale of certain products that consumers expect to be under regulation. Such com-

[Ch. 13 

modities as cosmetics or other beauty preparations, unless bearing medicinal claims, do not come within the law. Reducing preparations, devices such as aids for hearing, and machines for curing deformities are beyond this law. (b) Lack of standards. It is difficult to determine what is adulterated food. How much water should be allowed in oysters? How much artificial matter should be permitted in strawberry jam, for example, before it may be considered adulterated? How much poison is poison? As we saw before in our study, preservatives may be added even under present law. Standards adopted by the administration do not have the force of law, and, as a result, it is difficult to prosecute offenders. An amendment to the act passed in 1930 gives the Secretary of Agriculture the authority to set up a legal standard of quality for each class of canned food except meat and milk. (c) Lack of control over advertising. While the producer may limit his label to truth and keep it above reproach, he may go as far as he likes in advertising the product without violating the act. Fortunately, advertising is controlled to some extent by another government agency, which we shall discuss shortly. (d) Inadequate punishment for violation of the act. 

Generally, the Government will seize the shipment and destroy it after court proceedings. It is for this reason that one reads of such a case as "U. S. v. 166 Gallons of Cream."

Sometimes the manufacturer is prosecuted, but he gets off with a light fine.

Although various attempts to remedy these defects have been suggested to Congress, the necessary changes have not been made. Many citizens believe that the Copeland Bill was defeated in the Congressional Session of 1937 because lobbies sent by powerful business interests applied sufficient pressure on our representatives in Congress to prevent the passage of this law.

Ch. 13] 391 

 Bureau of Home Economics. This bureau has prepared a series of valuable pamphlets dealing with the economical use of various products. Many of these pamphlets describe methods to guide consumers in making their purchases. Economic citizens should avail themselves of this service. 

Federal Trade Commission. This commission was created primarily to protect honest manufacturers from unfair trade practices by their unscrupulous competitors. It has the power to issue "cease and desist" orders for such practices as misbranding merchandise, that is, labeling secondhand goods as new; using misleading names that are likely to confuse consumers; and making false statements in advertising. As a result of pressure brought to bear upon him by the Federal Trade Commission, one manufacturer of automobiles agreed to remove from his advertisements that his car could be bought "on an easy 6 per cent credit plan." 

Actually, the interest charges were considerably greater. In another instance, the manufacturer of a reducing preparation was compelled to remove certain exaggerated claims he had made in his advertisements of the product. 

Though designed to protect the manufacturer, the Federal Trade Commission offers some service to consumers. 

First, it extends its power over all commodities and is not limited to foods and drugs. Second, it checks false advertising to some extent.

In addition, it performs another service. It encourages competitors to draw up codes of fair practice among themselves. Recently such a code was signed by a group of merchants who market rayon. Since this product can be made by different chemical processes, or can be mixed with other materials such as silk, cotton, or wool, an understanding has been reached by them as to what constitutes "rayon" when this material is advertised.

[Ch. 13 

On the other hand, the commission has no initiative and must wait until a manufacturer makes a complaint before it may act. It decides only a limited number of cases. Finally, it interests itself only in damage to the competing manufacturer, and not to the consumer. Unless a merchant complains about the unfair trade practices of his competitor, the commission will not act. 

Department of Commerce. The work of this department is to foster commerce and is primarily concerned with aiding the commercial consumer, that is, the one who buys for the purpose of resale. But since the purchases of the commercial consumer affect us as ultimate consumers who purchase the products, it might be well to examine the activities of this department to see how they benefit us. 

Standard Weights and Measures. Probably the most outstanding unit in this department is the Bureau of Standards. This bureau has in its possession the standard weights and measures that we use in making our purchases. So carefully are these instruments kept that not even the slightest change is permitted to take place. For example, the metal rod that serves as the standard of length is kept at a fixed temperature to prevent it from shortening or lengthening with changes in temperature. 

Standards of Quality. One of the most interesting activities of this bureau is the development of standards of quality. These standards are specifications for goods that the 

Government plans to buy. Unless the merchandise measures up to these standards, the goods will not be purchased. The standards are prepared by a competent staff of chemists and engineers who comprise the Federal Specifications Board. 

Let us assume that the government wishes to purchase bed sheets for its prisons or armories. A sample of the cloth presented by the lowest bidder is sent to the bureau. The

- 

Courtesy W. L. Douglas Shoe Company DUPLICATE OF SHOE TESTING MACHINE USED BY THE U. S. BUREAU OF STANDARDS 

material is subjected to very rigid tests to determine its strength and durability. If the cloth meets with the specifications that have been made, it will be accepted. Otherwise, it is rejected and the sample submitted by the next lowest bidder will be examined until one is found that does meet the standard.

Many citizens have asked the bureau to inform them of these tests so that they might purchase the same kind of goods that had passed the Government tests.

Unfortunately, several difficulties prevent the bureau from divulging the information: (1) Congress has not seen fit to empower the bureau to tender such a service to consumers. (2) The bureau has not extended its tests to include whole lines of competing products. 

However, the Bureau will willingly let the consumer know what specifications it has set up. As the result of the work of this organization, a complete set of standards of quality have been adopted for many commodities. 

Unfortunately, the consumer would have difficulty in making use of information of this kind. It is highly technical in nature and it would not be understood. In addition, the consumer would have to purchase and use many intricate devices for testing the product. 

Certification Plan. Since the use of specifications is not practicable for most of us, the bureau has developed another plan known as the Certification Plan. Under this plan, the bureau has compiled a list of manufacturers who are willing to certify to the consumer that the product conforms with Federal specifications. These manufacturers then label their products so that the ultimate consumers will have the opportunity to purchase their goods, not by "feeling the merchandise," but by using a simple specification method. 

For example, an advertisement of the X Sheet Manufacturing Company may read, "The X Company certifies that every X sheet fulfills all requirements of the United States Government master specification #304 for high-count pillow cases... used by the Government as the standard for its purchases." In this way the consumer can be assured that he is getting merchandise that would pass the rigorous tests used by the bureau. If more manufacturers were willing to sell their products under these circumstances, the consumer would be more likely to get the quality of goods that he desires.

- 

This bureau has also been helpful in its attempts to standardize the sizes of containers. The U. S. Standard 

Container Act has reduced thirty-one sizes of grape baskets to three.

Other Contributions. Ada Lillian Bush, of the Depart ment of Commerce, sums up other activities carried on by the department in aid of the ultimate consumer. "In the routine task of buying a fish and frying it, for example, the home-maker is likely to benefit from various types of work carried out by the Department of Commerce or under its auspices. There are the surveys by the Coast and Geodetic Survey pertaining to coasts and streams; the lighthouse service that protects the ship or boat carrying the fish to port if it was obtained from other than fresh-water sources; the research of the Bureau of Fisheries to safeguard the adequacy of supply and the health of fish in our streams; the Bureau of Standards' scientific tests that enable the accurate weighing of the fish at purchase; the market research and other activities of the Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce relative to efficient movement of goods, retail and wholesale operating costs, credit relations, marketing practices, etc., that tend to expense reductions here and there, many of which are reflected in accrued consumer benefits." 

Public Utility Control. It has been found advisable to permit certain organizations to become monopolies, that is, to be the only ones to conduct a particular business in a given locality. Such companies as railroads, electric companies, gas, water and telephone companies can be more economically operated if but one of each serves within a given area. As a result, our Government has permitted and fostered this arrangement.

However, to prevent these organizations from charging rates that are excessive, such Federal agencies as the Inter-

state Commerce Commission and others must approve the rates being charged, thus helping consumers. 

It is interesting to note that recently the Interstate Commerce Commission instructed certain railroads in the eastern part of the country to lower the rates for passengers from 3 cents to 2 cents a mile. Although this was objected to by several companies, they were compelled to observe the order of the Interstate Commerce Commission. As a result, at least some of the companies increased their income considerably because more people made use of the railroad than ever before. This reduction was advantageous both to consumers and to the railroad companies.

Other Federal Agencies. In this very limited treatment of Federal aids to the consumer, we have omitted several agencies that serve the consumer. The U. S. Public Health 

Service of the Department of the Treasury, among others, frequently broadcasts information that is useful to consumers.

State and City Aid 

The activities of our Federal Government must, because of constitutional limitations, stop at the border of our state. 

As a result, it is necessary for the state government to set up a similar organization to carry on within our state borders. 

New York State also has a Pure Food and Drug Act for articles made and sold within the state. Its enforcement is in the hands of the State Department of Agriculture and Markets. It also has a Public Service Commission that performs tasks, similar to those of the Interstate Commerce Commission, within our own state, including those affecting intrastate railroads and gas, electric, and water companies. 

In New York City the work of enforcing the Pure Food and Drug Act is left to the Bureau of Food and Drugs of the Department of Health. Careful examination of these products, as well as of those who handle the products, insures

- 

Courtesy Federal Art Project, Board of Health, New York City GOVERNMENT INSPECTION OF MILK 

good quality in the food we buy. Before anyone may be employed in a store selling articles of food, the worker must have a foodhandler's certificate. 

Another important agency in our city government is the Department of Public Markets. This department provides for inspections of public markets so that consumers may get food products quickly and under sanitary conditions. The 

Bureau of Weights and Measures of this department, using the standard weights and measures of the Federal Bureau of Standards, tests all such instruments used in this city. Inspectors place a seal on any scale that meets the standard. 

Others are confiscated. More than 5,000 faulty scales and measures were seized in 1936. The department estimates

[Ch. 13 

that at least 250,000 faulty measures and scales are still in use.

Many other departments in our city and state governments co-operate to help the consumer. 

Semipublic and Commercial Aids 

In addition to those government aids to the consumer, semipublic associations have sprung up which also help. 

The American Medical Association, made up of more than 98,000 reputable doctors all over the country, maintains a Committee on Pharmacy and Chemistry, which investigates and reports on medicines used by physicians; a Committee on Foods, which investigates and reports on food products of a nonmedical nature; a Bureau of Investigation, which investigates and distributes information on quack medicines and quack doctors.
