SEALS OF THE AMERICAN MEDICAL ASSOCIATION 

Considering the type of product investigated, you will notice that these doctors are performing this service, not only to help the consumer but also to protect their own practice from unscrupulous people who prey upon the funds the consumer has set aside for medical expenses. 

Better Business Bureaus 

Similarly, other businessmen have banded together to protect their own interests in opposition to those unscrupulous businessmen who sell inferior products to the consumer. 

The Better Business Bureau of New York City is such an

Ch. 13] 399 

organization. This bureau has succeeded in weeding out many unscrupulous merchants. To illustrate their work, the following true case is given. A certain shop on Fifth Avenue, known as the "Linen Corner," misrepresented as linen, goods that were actually cotton. The Better Business Bureau investigated, gathered evidence, and then presented the case to the District Attorney's office. The owner of the store was arrested, found guilty, and given a $500 fine or sixty days in jail.

This organization also carries on "truth in advertising" campaigns. For example, it has sent the following recommendation to storekeepers: "It is specifically recommended that the phrases 'moth killer,' 'moth repellent,' and 'mothproof' be used only if adequate impartial tests of the product show full justification for such claims. Cardboard chests and closets, specially treated or used in conjunction with chemical preparations, cannot be depended upon to furnish sure protection." This and other interesting information may be found in their periodical Fair Practice.

Testing Services 

Because of the great variety of goods and the exaggerated claims made by advertisers, some stores, such as Macy's, have set up their own testing laboratories. Here, presumably, all goods sold by the store are first tested before any claim as to their qualities is made. Inferior merchandise is rejected. General Foods has a similar laboratory to test and encourage the improvement of good food. 

Of a similar nature are the testing laboratories conducted by magazines, such as Good Housekeeping. This magazine maintains that, before accepting the merchandisers' advertisement, its testing laboratory examines the product and, if it approves the product, issues a seal of approval. 

Unfortunately, however, the consumer cannot take the published results of these tests too much for granted. It

[Ch. 13 

must be remembered that, while this work is presumably carried on for the benefit of consumers, such laboratories owe their existence to the stores or magazines for whom they work.

Consumers' Own Aids 

Many consumers have begun to realize that, if they are to get their money's worth, they must help themselves. As individuals, however, they are powerless because a high degree of knowledge and great care are needed under modern buying conditions. As a result, consumers, like workers, have banded together for self-protection. Associations have been organized by groups of consumers for the benefit of consumers. These organizations may be divided into two groups, those which test the qualities of products, that is, independent laboratories, and those which test and buy products to sell to their members, that is, consumers' cooperatives.

Testing of Products by Independent Laboratories. Fol lowing the lead of scientific buyers in stores, many consumers have contributed sums of money to maintain testing laboratories for the purpose of finding out the quality of merchandise being offered for sale. Membership in the organization entitles the consumer to reports made by the scientific investigators. These reports are published in magazine form. Consumer's Research is an organization of this type. Other magazines, such as Consumer's Union and Consumer's Guide, may be purchased at newsstands. Membership is not required.

Consumer Co-operation. In order to remove the profits of the storekeeper, consumers have taken on his function themselves. A group of consumers organize a corporation, contribute a sum of money, open their own store, hire clerks, and buy and sell merchandise, as if they were the store-

Ch. 13] 401 

keepers. Such organizations are known as consumer's cooperatives.

The modern movement in consumer co-operation is believed to have begun in 1844, when 28 weavers organized the Rochdale Store in England. Since then the idea has spread all over the civilized world, although it is more popular in other countries than in our own. For example, in 

Finland 60 per cent of all retail sales are made through cooperatives. In the United States about 12 of 1 per cent are made in this fashion. According to the literature of the Cooperative League of the U. S. A., which is fostering this movement, there are "more than 1,800,000 members in the United States doing a nonprofit business of $365,000,000 a year (a million dollars a day)."

The principles upon which consumer co-operation is founded will explain how the organization operates. 

Open Membership. Every one who is willing to pay the nominal membership fee (in some cases as little as $5) is entitled to become a member.

Price of Merchandise. Goods are sold at prices prevailing in other stores. Payment must be made in cash. Nonmembers may purchase goods in this store. 

Dividends. Profits made are distributed to members on the basis of their purchases, not on the amount of stock owned. This, in effect, means that the consumer really pays less for his purchases than he would in another store since he receives in dividends part of what he has paid. 

Control. Each member has but one vote, no matter how many shares of stock he owns. Those who lend money to the business receive a small interest payment but in no way do they have more power to control.

Although many arguments have been advanced for and against this type of consumer activity, theoretically, at least, it should result in cheaper prices to the consumer. It has not, however, gained much headway in America.

[Ch. 13 

 The Economic Citizen Learns to Buy Wisely 

Now that we have enumerated the evils and attempted solutions of the consumer's problem, what factors can the economic citizen control directly or indirectly, in order to get his money's worth?

Additional Education. Many ways are open to permit the consumer to obtain more information concerning what things he should buy. He may avail hinself of the material that is being prepared by the government and other organizations. Many government publications on the subject are free of charge. Many books, such as those listed at the end of this unit, offer interesting information to the consumer. 

In school, courses in home economics are offered to students. Home economics is a science that deals, in part, with how to "buy a living." These courses are designed to help boys and girls by teaching them interesting facts concerning their purchases. They teach students how to purchase various articles of food, not on the basis of fancy name or package, or on advertising "blurbs," but on the more solid foundation of the nutritive value of food. In clothing, the values of good workmanship, durability, and comfort are stressed.

At the present time these courses are designed primarily for girls. Various estimates indicate that three-fourths of the nation's buying is done by women. In the future, perhaps, our educational authorities may make provision for boys to avail themselves of the benefits of such information. 

Legislation. In a country such as ours, beneficial changes may be brought about peacefully by the use of the ballot. 

However, since our lawmakers must be convinced that consumers are interested in bettering conditions, consumers must organize as a group and actively engage in bringing

Ch. 13] 403 

the matter to the attention of our legislators. If merchants have "lobbies" that influence the passing of laws, consumers, too, should have lobbies to protect their interests. 

It is interesting to note that already consumers have made their appeal so strong that under the old NIRA a special body was organized within the administration to protect the consumer.

Consumer's Code 

In view of the many obstacles that stand in the way of getting one's money's worth, the economic citizen should memorize and practice these rules: 

1. I will buy only what I need and can afford. 

2. I will buy according to my budget. 

3. I will insist upon good quality. 

4. I will make sure that the price is fair. 

5. I will buy on the installment plan only when I am able to meet my obligations and when the articles to be purchased are durable goods. 

6. I will buy only from reliable dealers and merchants. 

7. I will look for seals, stamps, and other marks of government protection. 

8. I will resist unfair selling methods, dishonest, highpressure advertising, fake sales, worthless guarantees, and any other efforts to prevent me from getting my money's worth. 

A careful observance of these rules will help the economic citizen really to get his money's worth.

[Ch. 13

CHAPTER XIV  

"Why doesn't Uncle work?" asked the little nephew. "Everybody else works." "You see, Son, Uncle's money works for him," replied the boy's father.

And thus an idea was born in the mind of a youngster who later became one of the leading financiers in the United States. Lest this story sound too simple, let us say at once that money can't work for you unless you or someone before you has worked to obtain the money. 

Factors Involved in the Investment of Savings. Saving is so important in economic life today that many governments legislate to bring about compulsory saving. In the 

United States, the Social Security Act forces many to save whether they wish to do so or not. This is the age of youthbut the young become old. If a youth has made provision for old age, he or she can sit back and enjoy retirement; but woe to him who has nothing saved. The importance of saving cannot be disregarded now any more than it ever could. Budgets must be balanced, as we illustrated in Chapter XI. But mere saving is not enough. If the greatest benefits are to be obtained, savings must be made to earn an income for their owner. In other words, they must be wisely invested. What, then, are the factors involved in the investment of savings?

Safety of Principal. When a person invests his hardearned savings so that they can earn an income for him, he wants to be sure above all that his principal will be safe. 

Safety should be the basic and fundamental requirement of 406

Ch. 14] 407 

every investment made by the average person. The need for safety cannot be too greatly emphasized. During periods of depression when investors are thinking about the possibility of loss, safety is usually given much attention. But in periods of prosperity when business seems to be good and the possibility of loss is not so apparent, the need for safety is often overlooked. At no time, however, should the average investor permit an offer of a high rate of interest or any other inducement to blind him to the need for safety. No promises of gain will compensate him for the possible loss of his principal.

Regularity of Income. The average investor needs an income that is dependable. Even though the rate may be rather low, a certain amount that is received regularly is more desirable than the promise of a larger amount that may never be received or, at best, that may be received only at irregular intervals.

Types of Investments. When a person has saved some money and has it ready for investment, he finds that he can invest it in a number of different ways. We shall study some of the more important forms of investments that should be considered by the average individual. 

Postal Savings System. One of the safest types of investment that are available for the average person is depositing in the Postal Savings System. Postal-savings depositories are conducted by the Government in connection with the post offices. The rate of interest is only 2 per cent, but the deposits are safe since they are guaranteed by the Government. Postal savings have been especially popular since 

1929, the beginning of the depression, at which time many other types of investments were found to be unsafe. 

United States Savings Bonds. If a person has saved $18.75, he can purchase a United States Savings Bond. In

ten years this bond will be worth $25 in cash. It can be redeemed at any time after sixty days from the date that it was issued, but if the greatest return is to be earned, it should not be redeemed until the end of ten years. If the investment is continued for that period, the rate of interest is approximately 3 per cent, or one-half more than the rate of interest paid on postal savings. Bonds with maturity values of $50, $100, etc., are also offered by the Government.

Savings Banks. Savings banks and the savings departments of commercial banks offer a high degree of safety. 

This is true, in recent years especially, because banks now

- 

have their deposits insured by the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation. The rate of interest paid by savings banks varies to some extent according to business conditions, because business conditions determine the amount that the savings banks can earn from the deposits. At the present time the rate of interest paid by such banks is about 2 per cent. No interest is now paid on Christmas or vacation funds because such deposits are temporary and cannot be loaned out by the bank to earn an income. A bank can afford to pay interest on amounts deposited only as it can make loans with those funds and thus earn an income. The

Life Insurance. Insurance is another means of providing funds for old age. Life insurance is essentially a protective, rather than an investment, measure. Even though a man is brilliant in his knowledge of finance, he is wise to invest in insurance. Frequently a worker earning $100 a month is heard to say, "I can't afford insurance on my pay." The answer is, "You can't afford not to have insurance because of your pay." For a poor man, life insurance is the only means of providing a lump sum of money to his family in the event of his death.

Unless right to change Beneficiary is reserved, the interest of any Minor Beneficiary must be released under an order of court by a legally appointed Guardian of such Minor before any change, surrender, or cash loan can be effected. 

If the premium is paid when the Application is signed, the Policy will usually bear the same date. If a different date is desired, indicate it clearly in the Application. 

If the Automatic Premium Loan Provision is selected, a Request, which includes assignment, must be filed in duplicate with the Company before a Policy can be issued. 

This Application is the property of the Company, to whom it must be returned after the Medical Examination is made and the Application completed. It must not be withdrawn or destroyed.

This illustration shows only one page of a four-page application blank. The complete application requires a report of the physical examination, a report of the agent, and other necessary information. 

Buying a Home. Buying a home provides another means of investing savings. Strictly speaking, you do not have complete assurance of safety of principal when you buy a home. The prices of real estate, like the prices of other

- 

things, are higher at some times than they are at others. 

A house bought at one time may, therefore, be worth less at some time in the future. But fluctuations in prices will not prevent your having a home, nor will they make any changes in the property that you actually own. If the property, was well selected, the chances are that there will be no serious decrease in value.

There are certain satisfactions in owning a home that a person can change or improve as he wishes. It should be recognized, though, that owning a home may or may not be a good investment. Whether or not it is a good investment depends partly on the price paid and partly on the price for which other property of that value can be rented. 

In investing in a home, one must consider the following expenses: taxes, which usually amount to about 2 per cent, of the value of the property; insurance, which amounts to about one-half of 1 per cent; and upkeep and depreciation, which usually amount to about 1½ per cent and 2 per cent, respectively. In addition, we must consider the amount that could have been earned by the money used in paying for the property if it had been invested otherwise. For example, assume that a man has purchased property for $9,000, paying $4,000 in cash and assuming a first mortgage of $5,000. The annual costs of owning this property are: (1) Taxes (2% of $9,000)..... (2) Insurance (2% of $9,000). (3) Upkeep (1½% of $9,000). (4) Depreciation (2% of $9,000). (5) Interest on Loan (52% of $5,000)... $180.00 45.00 135.00 180.00 275.00 (6) Interest on Investment (2% [the rate paid on deposits by savings banks] of $4,000)... 80.00

Total Annual Costs.. 

$895.00 

If a house of this type could be rented for $70 a month, the total annual rent would be $840, or $55 less than the cost of owning the home. If, on the other hand, such a

house could be rented for $80 a month, the total annual rent would be $960, or $65 more than the cost of owning the home. We can see, then, that in some instances owning a home may be cheaper than renting, while in others it may be more expensive. From this we may conclude that although owning one's own home is often a good investment, it is not necessarily a good investment if too much is paid for the property.

Stocks and Bonds. A person who buys stock in a corporation buys a part of the ownership of the business. If the corporation makes profits, the stockholder may receive dividends. If the corporation does not make profits, he will receive no dividends and may even lose the value of his investment. Usually the return on his stock will fluctuate from year to year according to the earnings of the corporation.

When a person buys a bond, he is loaning money to a corporation. His return is fixed because the corporation promises to pay a fixed rate of interest as well as to repay the face value of the bond at the time of maturity. The owner receives this fixed rate of interest unless the corporation fails and is unable to pay as it has promised. In the past, many corporations have failed so that bondholders, as well as stockholders, have lost money. Whether bonds and stocks are good investments varies according to the particular situation. At this time, it is sufficient to say that a person should not buy bonds or stocks unless he has much information about the businesses in which he is investing and has reasonable assurance that they will continue to earn profits from which they can pay interest and dividends. 

Building and Loan Associations. A building and loan association is an organization that accumulates funds by selling shares to investors. An investor may buy shares outright or may pay for them in installments. If he pays

for them in installments, he receives dividends on his shares, but he is charged interest on the unpaid balance until the shares have been completely paid for. 

A building and loan association loans its funds to those who wish to buy or build their own homes. Usually the association will lend from 50 to 65 per cent of the value of the whole property. The person obtaining the loan then agrees to purchase shares with a fixed value equal to the amount of the loan, and to pay for these shares in installments. In this way he accumulates funds for repaying the loan.

When one buys shares in a building and loan association, he is buying a part of the ownership of the organization, the same as if he were buying stock in any other business. 

Although building and loan associations are usually well managed and ordinarily earn satisfactory incomes, which they pay out as dividends, there have been, especially since 1929, many instances in which building and loan associations have failed, with consequent loss to shareholders. There are, however, at the present time, more than 6,000,000 depositors or shareholders who have more than $5,000,000,000 invested in building and loan companies in the United States. 

Effect of Inflation on Investments. When money is invested in savings accounts or in bonds, the investor has the promise that he will receive a certain rate of interest and, at some time in the future, the original amount of his investment. Even if he does receive the interest and the face value of his investment, it is possible that he may suffer a loss because of the decrease in the purchasing power of money. 

This situation is illustrated by the experience of a hardworking German carpenter who, in 1902, purchased a 20year endowment insurance policy with a face value of 20,000 marks. After paying premiums for 20 years, he re-

Ch. 14] 

415 

ceived a check for 20,000 marks. Was he happy? One look at the stamp on the envelope was enough to show that he was unhappy. The value of the stamp was 100,000 marks. 

In 1902, 20,000 marks were worth approximately $5,000. 

In 1922 the same number of marks were worth one-tenth of 1 cent. The change was brought about by inflation in the 

German currency that decreased the purchasing power of each mark.

This is an unusual case. The condition that caused it will probably never happen here. It is true, however, that from year to year the purchasing power of a dollar does change. In some years, prices are high; in others, prices are low. When prices are high, $1 will obviously purchase less than when prices are low. 

Investors sometimes attempt to avoid the danger of inflation by investing in homes and in the stocks of corporations, for such investments represent ownership in property that remains valuable even though the purchasing power of currency decreases or disappears. 

Investment and Speculation. 

When a person invests his money, he is primarily interested in safety of principal and regularity of income. He is interested in the amount that will be returned to him more or less uniformly over a period of time. When a person speculates, he is usually not so much interested in income, but rather in the profit that may be made by selling that which he has bought at a price higher than the purchase price. When a person places his money in a savings bank, in United States savings bonds, or in a building and loan association, he is said to invest. When he buys stocks with the hope of selling them at a higher price in the near future, he is said to speculate.

It is impossible to draw a definite line separating investments from speculations, for all investments have some risk and few speculations have no investment features. The

[Ch. 14 

difference between investment and speculation is, however, suggested by the old adage "A man invests when he gets all the safety he possibly can; a man speculates when he takes any risk he cannot afford." The average individual with a limited income, especially in view of the happenings during the last ten years, should invest and should avoid speculation. He should invest by depositing funds in savings banks, by buying Government bonds, by buying insurance in wellestablished companies, by buying a home, or in other equally conservative ways. Until he has become fairly well to do and has sufficient funds to invest to justify his making a careful study of different businesses, he should not invest in industrial stocks or bonds. He should avoid all speculation. 

It is well known that although speculators occasionally make large sums, most speculators die poor.

UNIT VI ALL WORK AND NO PLAY? 

INTRODUCTION 

So far in our discussion we have given our attention to the search for economic independence. Life would be dreary indeed if it consisted only of doing things one has to do. "Your grandfather worked fifteen hours a day; your father worked ten. You work eight hours a day, five days a week." So runs an advertisement of the Encyclopedia Brittanica. To it might be added the pending legislation for a six-hour day, five days a week. The trend is definitely toward a shorter working day. This means more time to do things one wants to do.

The invention of machinery is responsible for many evils in our economic life. To balance these evils, in part, the invention of machinery is responsible, too, for giving us increased leisure time. What are we doing with this blessing?

Are we taking fullest advantage of the freedom from the long hours of work that the use of machinery has given to us? 419

CHAPTER XV LEISURE 

Interest in Recreation. On one Saturday afternoon in the year 1937, the Army-Navy football game in Philadelphia was witnessed by 102,000 spectators. On the same day the elevens of Fordham and New York Universities staged their annual contest before 65,000 people in New York City. 

Twenty thousand more saw the Stamford-Columbia gridiron contest. Even of more importance was the fact that several millions of people throughout the country witnessed football contests among high-school boys. 

From October to December, Saturday is football day. A person may spend anywhere from 15 cents to $5 for a ticket to see a football game. 

In the spring, baseball, tennis, racing, and other sports draw millions of fans. Besides these spectators, many others who are younger participate in athletic activities.

The number of motion picture theatres is still increasing, and the names of cinema stars are better known to young and old than are the names of the chief executives of the states, cities, and even of the nation. If Saturday afternoon may be called football day, Saturday night may be called movie night.

In the summer, the beaches from the Battery to Montauk Point are filled with people. New York City and New York 

State have more playgrounds than ever before, but the bathing pavilions and places of amusement where admission payment is demanded seem to thrive. 

Municipal recreation centres were used by more than 

1,500,000 young people and adults in 1934. Five million children under 17 years of age played in public playgrounds 420

Ch. 15] 421 

LEISURE 

during the summer of 1934. Dramatics engaged 84,000 people, while 40,000 were enrolled in arts and crafts under public auspices. Tennis took care of 984,000; baseball, 612,000; and basketball, 400,000 children and young people.

These figures stand out, but there are ten million other urban children still without playgrounds. To meet the demand, recreation centers should be more than doubled. The need for proper use of leisure time is constantly growing and will continue to grow.

Source of Leisure. Why do so many people have so much free time? Our grandfathers were fortunate if they had to work less than fifteen hours a day. Today a forty-hour week prevails in many industries. A bill to limit work to thirty hours a week has been introduced in Congress. Unless a change comes about, the amount of leisure time is going to increase steadily.

Time for leisure is in its infancy, but it is a lusty infant. 

Some people think that too much leisure is not a good sign. 

We cannot turn back the clock. The trend of the times is toward longer and more frequent vacations as well as toward a shorter working week and working day. 

How did this situation come about? It is not difficult for us to put our finger upon the beginning of our present era of leisure. The advent of the machine was not much more than a century ago. Machines displaced men as early as 1812. In 

1813 thirteen workmen were hanged at York for the deliberate destruction of machinery. Not until 1929 did the American people realize that increased leisure time was a normal state of affairs. This spare time may have been thrust upon us, but it has come to stay.

We have been trained to work more efficiently than ever before. More leisure time is the result or accompaniment of increased working efficiency. At present, we need more training for leisure than for work. There is no need to go into

[Ch. 15 

the statistics of mental diseases, except to state that there are more patients in our hospitals in the United States suffering from insanity than from any other cause. In some cases, the condition arose because of the lack of something purposeful to do.

The problem of the wise use of leisure time is a direct challenge. This problem cannot be solved in a negative way. 

We cannot "leave it to George." There must be a positive, supervised, and planned program. This is the job not only of society, but of every last individual. 

Use of Leisure. A prominent United States Senator, in talking to a well-known publisher of a leading daily newspaper, brought up the question of success in life. The publisher is alleged to have said, "I have made a million dollars." 

Whereupon the Senator replied, "I haven't a thousand dollars, but I have a million friends." The publisher never did understand the concept of leisure, much less the wise use of it. 

High-school boys and girls must be educated to enjoy their hours of leisure, just as they must be educated to enjoy their work. To know how to earn a living is important. However, leisure must not be neglected during the period of school days. Maturity may bring more opportunities for using idle hours, but at maturity one's capacity for play may have been destroyed. One must learn to make a living, but that is not enough. One must learn to live not only when working but also during all the rest of the day. 

There are 168 hours in a week. Let us assume that forty of these will be devoted to work and fifty-six to rest and sleep. 

That leaves seventy-two hours for some form of leisure-the major portion of our time. The use of the seventy-two hours must be planned. Some leisure time should be used for entertainment, some for improvement, and a little for doing nothing just loafing. One may abhor the idea of so much time for anything but work and sleep. That is not the

Ch. 15] 423 

LEISURE 

40 HOURS 56 HOURS 72 HOURS WORK SLEEP 

cllb 

LEISURE Courtesy National Association of Manufacturers question. We are faced with this problem of time on our hands. Very often doing nothing is harder than doing our job. No one person can plan completely the use of another's time. However, we can make the following suggestions for entertainment and improvement: 

1. Plan your week ends as well as your longer vacations. 

Visitors to New York rarely miss seeing the Aquarium at

Battery Park. Have you been there? Many travelers from 

New York have spoken in glowing terms of the concerts at San Marco, Venice, but have they ever listened to the Goldman Band in Central Park? You are not compelled to travel to enjoy yourself. New York City has been called the greatest summer as well as winter resort in the world. "See New York

First" might be a good slogan for school children. A ferry boat trip around New York Harbor is cheap. The Board of

Education has organized and conducted free trips for many of the pupils in its high schools. 

The National Theatre Act (approved July 5, 1935) pro vides for the incorporation by Congress of the American

[Ch. 15 

RADIO CITY 

Courtesy National Broadcasting Company

Ch. 15] 

425 

LEISURE 

National Theatre and Academy, which is a nonprofit corporation, to present theatrical productions of the highest type and to advance public interest in the drama "as an art belonging both to the theatre and to literature and thereby to be enjoyed both on the stage and in the study." This is only one of the newly created opportunities for enjoying the best plays, interpreted by the best actors, at a minimum cost. 

2. Acquire a hobby. The collecting tendency crops out at a time when it may be useful not only in giving one an avocation, but also in establishing an interest that may continue throughout one's whole life. Young people today collect stamps, coins, shells, old silk stockings for the purpose of making quilts, cigar bands with which to ornament ash trays, and numerous other articles. 

Such movements as the Boy Scouts and the Girl Scouts, and the interest developed in nature study and outdoor life through these and other means, have not only had an effect upon the mental and physical health of the younger generation, but have also served as a means of recreation and a means of satisfying some primary tendencies. 

Some girls make a hobby of “minding” the neighbors' children while their parents go to the theatre, movies, or a bridge game at a friend's house. This is practical training for leisure and frequently a pleasant and profitable avocation as well. 

3. Avail yourself of all opportunities for getting out into the open. Tennis, baseball, golf, field hockey for girls, and archery can be enjoyed at small expense because of the number of free parks and recreation centers. Not only the gymnasiums but also the swimming pools of the high schools and other buildings in New York City are thrown open to all during the evenings in winter and during the days in summer. 

Central Park, in Manhattan, offers entertainment at little cost. Row boats can be hired for a small fee. The

menageries are free during most of the week. Outdoor dancing, carefully supervised by municipal officials, is offered without charge on many summer evenings. 

The Metropolitan Museum of Art is on the east side and the Museum of Natural History on the west side of the park. 

Both are open to the public without charge several days during the week. The Hayden Planetarium, adjacent to the 

Museum of Natural History, is free to school children at stated times. Adults may enjoy this wonder of the age for as little as thirty-five cents. In addition to all of this, one has the great outdoors of the park itself. 

4. Visit your out-of-town friends and relatives at intervals. "I must make it a point to visit Aunt Emma more often." 

One hears such an expression many times. Formerly it took a day to reach Aunt Emma's house. Today, however, Aunt 

Emma is right next door even though she is fifty miles from 

New York City. The trip costs less than two dollars. Today, people do not have to wait for a baptism, wedding, or funeral to visit friends. A few people object to visits, for they feel that visitors drop in merely to spend the time. It would be more accurate to say that today there is more time for cultivating and maintaining friendships. Longer vacations enable those in the Middle West to visit their eastern or southern friends. Leisure has not cheapened friendship; it has enabled many a person to increase his satisfaction of that priceless possession. 

5. Spend some leisure time in voluntary education of one sort or another. Extension courses, evening school courses, libraries, and museums are all free to the people of New York. Illustrated lectures give more satisfaction to some individuals than baseball or football. There are camera clubs, reading circles, and various civic societies. Finally, travel is a great educational factor.

There is much more enjoyment in participating in leisure activities than in simply watching. However, it is impossible

Ch. 15] 429 

LEISURE 

for everyone to be a player. Spectators aid the players in most lines of endeavor. Not every boy can hope to play on the varsity team at Notre Dame. Every boy who is physically fit, however, should take part in some sport. 

The American public is beginning to enjoy professional activities of every kind. As a result of universal high-school education, men and women know the fine points of baseball, football, radio performances, books, newspapers, and maga-

zines. A person may not have ability to write a column for the local newspapers, but he can appreciate and differentiate between serious and careless performances. Taking example from experts often increases one's own ability. This is as true in football or on the stage as it is in writing a composition. 

All of this leads us back to the connection between education and leisure time.

By-Products of Leisure. There is no law compelling us to use time unprofitably simply because it is leisure time. Time

[Ch. 15 

spent profitably is likely to be more interesting and enjoyable. Many men have gained fame through their accomplishments during spare time. Lincoln studied law at night, after a hard day's work as a clerk in a store. Edison worked as a telegrapher during the day. This didn't stop him from working at electricity during his spare time. Robert Zuppke, the football coach of Illinois, has achieved merit as a marine artist. Frank Wandle, trainer of athletes at West Point and later at New Haven, invented health-improving machines in his leisure time.

The success of George Eastman in photography is known to the great majority of school boys and girls. What may not be known is that Eastman studied photography while clerking in the Rochester Savings Bank. During the war England sought the man best fitted to give advice concerning optical glasses. Do you suppose that this man proved to be a physicist? an optician? a manufacturer of glass? No! J. E. Barnard was a hatter.

President Roosevelt has one of the finest American collections of ship models and ship pictures. Poincaré, late premier of France, was a mathematician of note. "The crime of the age is the waste of leisure time, for what we earn we put into our pockets, but what we spend in leisure time we put into our characters."

SELF-TESTING QUESTIONS 

1. Define leisure; use any standard dictionary. 

2. Leisure may be mental, moral, physical, social, or even economic. 

Locate the library nearest your home. Mention one recreation centre open to you. Cite any civic centre that you attend. Select a newspaper article from the "what to buy" column. How many times have you attended a block party or a dance on the mall in 

Central Park?

Ch. 15] 431 

LEISURE 

3. Find out the capacity of the Polo Grounds, the Yankee Stadium, Ebbets Field, the Lewisohn Stadium at City College, the Hayden 

Planetarium. 

4. Taking the year 1934, arrange in order the sports that mainly made up the recreation of young people. 

5. Why do we need more training for leisure than for work? 

6. Mention five suggestions for entertainment or improvement of leisure time. 

7. Cite three examples showing how wise use of leisure time brought about success in business. 

THOUGHT QUESTIONS 

1. "The crime of the age is the waste of leisure time, for what we earn we put into our pockets, but what we spend in leisure time we put into our characters." Explain the significance of this statement. 

2. The proposal has been made that the United States should add a "Minister of Leisure" to the cabinet. Argue for or against this proposal. 

3. Thorstein Veblin emphasizes the "bent for idle curiosity as well as the instinct of workmanship." Show how these instincts can be reconciled. 

4. The five following fields may be tentatively adopted as the minimum essentials of a well-balanced leisure time program: social relations, outdoor play, reading, the arts and crafts, and science. (a) Should scholarship be added? (b) What is the reason for your answer? (c)

Give one concrete illustration for each of the five fields. 

5. "In the vast possibilities of leisure, children do not have to be taught to play; we have only to propose some new game." (a) Under what conditions is this true? (b) Under what conditions is this false? 

6. "The amount of time spent in gladiatorial combats, gambling, drunkenness, and crime is the best evidence for the need of leisure that is planned and purposeful." Explain this statement fully. 

7. "Earning a living is important; but complete living involves more than earning a living." How does leisure affect complete living? 

8. "Guidance in education is sorely needed." "Guidance for leisure is a sine qua non." How does the second statement depend on the first?
